The point of philosophy is to start with
something so simple as not to seem worth
stating, and to end with something so
paradoxical that no one will believe it.
Bertrand Russell

Preface

This book starts with a provocative idea, namely, that the times when philosophers
could claim that philosophical problems are too complex to be solved, are over.
Artificial intelligence has reached, or will soon reach, such a level of performance that
computers can excellently and in a matter of seconds solve philosophical problems—
even problems that are vividly debated in the expert literature. This is not just a
prophecy, but the outcome of experiments the author has done, notably in the context
of causation. Identifying causes of events in somewhat complex situations is far from
easy, as witnessed by the plethora of theories proposed in the professional literature;
no overarching definition of the concept of cause exists. And yet advanced AI easily
solves cases over which hundreds of the best experts debate1 .
Anyone who has followed, even from a distance, the rapid evolution of AI will
surely agree that there is no reason why machines could not learn to answer philosophical questions in an entirely professional manner. Even if we just extrapolate
from the way current artificial neural nets (ANNs) work—in essence, by looking
for correlations in data—it is clear that they can, to the least, provide answers that
are an excellent ‘common denominator’ of expert positions published in the literature—of which there are often so many that a human philosopher easily loses track.
Thus, even if the functioning of Large Language Models—creating a planetary AIhype recently—is not entirely transparent, we should assume that they are capable
of proposing a (highly?, most?) probable solution to a vast array of philosophical
problems. Especially, disruptive innovations in the functioning of ANNs and AI in
general might lead to problem-solving capacities that are indistinguishable from, or
outperform, those of the best human philosophers. There are solid reasons to believe
that this is a highly likely evolution. To the point that one might wonder whether
the profession of philosopher will keep any relevance, or whether it soon will be an
occupation of a bygone age.
This book comes with an optimistic message. It argues that there is a way for
philosophers to keep ahead of machine intelligence. I will argue that this virtuous
1 Cf. Vervoort and Nikolaev (J. General Philosophy of Science 2025, published online, cf. https://

doi.org/10.1007/s10838-025-09735-7), see also arXiv preprint https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14239.
vii

viii

Preface

path takes us to a new method of doing philosophy, a method that can be aided by
AI. To help this approach make its mark I will put it at the centre of a new philosophy, baptised ‘synthetic philosophy’, which I consider the natural prolongation of
analytic philosophy. (I call the baby synthetic philosophy, because ‘synthetic analytic
philosophy’ sounds decidedly bad.) In times of an AI explosion, it seems clear that
analysis alone does not suffice anymore. In a nutshell, the main message of this book
is that, if problem-solving is their goal, philosophers should strive to build synthetic
theories, and derive solutions within such encompassing theories. Theory-building
may well be the last skill in which we outperform machines2 . The really good news
is that there is a semi-quantitative rule that allows us to know whether we are on track
of the best theory and the best solution to a given problem. ‘Best solution’ should
be understood here as the solution that solves the problems of preceding attempts.
Needless to say, synthetic philosophy lies in the continuation of existing philosophical methods and schools; but it takes theory-building more seriously than is usually
done.
As a side remark, initially I had subtitled this book “Introduction to synthetic
philosophy”. But I decided to change the subtitle when in 2023 commercial Large
Language Models made spectacular progress in a wide array of problem-solving
tasks. However, I wish to be clear: this book is much more about using a synthetic
methodology for problem-solving than about using AI. I claim that AI can, for the
time being, function as a great supporting tool, in a precise way to be specified, not
yet as the synthetic agent.
I believe there is a second important reason why problem-solving should receive
wider attention from philosophers than is habitual. It is difficult to deny that human
society presently faces problems of an unprecedented urgency: think only of the
climate change and the inequality in income and capabilities among people worldwide, exacerbated by depleting natural resources. The analysis (say, of causes and
remedies) of any societal problem beyond what science tells us brings us inevitably
to philosophy, to ethics and practical philosophy in the first place. But in philosophy,
as in scientific disciplines, all problems are related. This idea is nicely captured by
Quine’s concept of the ‘web of belief’, the interconnected network of theories and
disciplines in which revisions of one theory can have implications for all others, at
least in principle (we will have much to say about this beautiful idea in this book).
In sum, I believe that even the most practical problems have a strong link with more
fundamental philosophical questions, for instance related to truth, knowledge, causation/causality, probability, free will, etc. —some of which will be studied in the next
chapters. In other words, many practical questions cannot be answered with good
confidence if the theoretical ones are not satisfyingly answered first. As an example, a
theory of ethics that does not cohere with an adequate model of free will—a narrower
and solvable problem, as I will argue in Chapters 6 and 7 —, cannot be the real deal.
Therefore, not only practically oriented philosophers but also theorists can contribute

2 Cf. Vervoort et al. (2021) “Artificial consciousness, superintelligence and ethics in robotics: how

to get there ?”, Mind and Matter, 19(1), 7–43.

Preface

ix

to proposing solutions for the great challenges of our time: to basing solutions on
solid grounds.
Sure, this is a decidedly optimistic vision of our profession, one that contemporary
academics rarely proclaim, it seems, for a variety of reasons, one reason being,
perhaps, the fear of sounding quixotic. But is sounding quixotic not an inevitable
task of innovative philosophy? Another reason may be that philosophers have lost the
synthetic drive, the conviction that it is possible to connect their highly specialised
research to the greater web of knowledge. The overwhelming majority of publications
in contemporary analytic philosophy deals with problems that are studied on the
analytic micro-scale, detached from the broader view (surely for understandable
and not always unjustified reasons). But exclusively focussing attention on microproblems is leading philosophy astray from its vocation to be more immediately
relevant for society at large. Again, this book comes with a message of hope: it
claims that analytic philosophy is now mature enough for a natural next phase, i.e.
for synthetic steps; that it is possible to integrate smaller-scale problems into a more
synthetic whole. These synthetic, solid models and theories are then ready to be
used in practical philosophy and in science. Of course, this final step is not the
main focus of this book, which is dedicated to theoretical problems mainly. But in
the Epilogue, Chapter 9, I will indicate, as an example, how a coherent account of
free will can serve as a principle for practical ethics (the Epilogue is dedicated to
more speculative problems). And throughout this book I will give examples of how
fundamental philosophy could inspire and guide scientific research, in neuroscience
(Chapter 7§7), computer science (Chapter 7§7), and physics (e.g. Chapter 9§1). One
may even doubt whether some of the toughest problems of the foundations of physics
can be solved without a good understanding of the philosophical concepts involved,
notably those of cause, causation, and probability (more in Chapter 9§1).
Of course, philosophers will and should be eminently sceptical when hearing the
intrepid claim that philosophical problems can be solved. But I will substantiate
my claim by showing in detail how the method I recommend leads to answers to
concrete and well-known open problems; I will argue that these solutions maximise
an objective indicator of solidity. Thus, the readers will be able to judge for themselves
whether the promise is met. (Of course, the ‘problems’ I consider in this book are
tractable problems of analytic philosophy, not the questions that are sometimes called
the ‘big’ or ‘ultimate’ ones, say about the existence of God, the meaning of life, the
ultimate nature of the ultimate constituents of the universe, etc.)
This book is intended for a wide public; I hope to trigger the interest of expert
philosophers but also of philosophical neophytes and scientists. Most chapters in the
book will start with an introduction for beginners, where I have tried to phrase the
problem under scrutiny in an as simple and clear way as possible. I do this for two
reasons. First, to allow the newcomers to think for themselves. Few things are more
pleasurable than creative thinking; and people who have independently thought about
a problem are more apt to evaluate the soundness of a proposed solution. Second,
because I believe that expressing problems in the simplest way possible is the best
starting ground for solving them. This is an old wisdom, but it seems frequently
neglected in the contemporary professional literature, which often uses jargon of an

x

Preface

extraordinary sophistication. I would like to emphasise: the philosophy I promote
is one that uses transparent language, and that does not create new concepts when
notions that are known in the wider intellectual community largely suffice. I believe
that the task of philosophy is to make simple what seems complicated; certainly not
the other way around. Furthermore, clarity should be conducive to remain relevant
for society at large.
To put this book in context, an autobiographical note may be helpful. The method
of synthetic philosophy is, in its core, the same as that of theoretical science, physics
in particular. Before embarking on a Ph.D. in philosophy of science (at the Université
de Montréal), I did a Ph.D. (at the Université d’Aix-Marseille) and a post-doc (at
the École Normale Supérieure, Paris) in condensed matter physics. The reader is
warned: in a few chapters I will speak with some confidence of the methods (and
results) of physics. But this will not go beyond what presumably every physicist
and philosopher of physics knows. So I will not add much to what philosophers of
physics have excellently said about the methods of physics: my goal is to apply these
methods of physics and of synthetic philosophy to concrete problems.
Switching disciplines instilled in me the habit to cross perspectives when doing
research. When I studied philosophy in Montreal, I saw philosophical problems
through the lens of physics, in particular from the perspective of the difficult—and
still unanswered—questions raised by quantum physics. I saw connections between
the two disciplines literally in every corner of philosophy. Just as I had, before,
looked at physics through the lens of philosophy. Indeed, philosophy came first: in
my Belgian high school, a Jesuit college, we spent months reading Plato and other
philosophers. I found this exercise of crossing perspectives an invaluable source of
inspiration. It will then not be a surprise that there is one more credo that I would
like to transmit in this book: namely, that taking science seriously can be enormously
helpful for addressing philosophical problems; and that there is a symmetrical benefit
in using good philosophy to tackle scientific problems—of course, mainly problems
of a foundational type, in say neuroscience or physics, such as about the nature of
consciousness, of reasoning, of free will, problems of the interpretation of quantum
mechanics, probability theory, relativity theory etc. I hope this book will corroborate
at least the first part of my credo. (As said, in this philosophy book the readers will
not find much evidence for the second part, philosophy helping science. But I will
indicate a few lines of interdisciplinary research as examples.) Needless to say, I am
not very original in my claim, many have uttered a similar expression of faith in
interdisciplinary research. But, as far as I can judge, it seems not very often done,
and I believe that the effectiveness of interdisciplinary research is still very much
underrated.
Chapter 3 on Gettier’s problem and Chapter 7 on free will are reworked
and substantially extended versions of published articles3 . I wish to thank here
3 Namely: L. Vervoort and T. Blusiewicz (2020), “The CMT Model of Free Will”, in Dialogue,

the Canadian Philosophical Review, Volume 59, Issue 3 pp. 415–435; and L. Vervoort and A.
Shevchenko (2022). “Epistemic Relativism and the Gettier Problem: Insights from Philosophy of
Science”, in Epistemology and Philosophy of Science, Vol. 59, 1, p 58–81.

Marquis; to my post-doc advisor Yves Gingras from the Université du Québec à
Montréal; and to Mario Bunge from McGill: they opened a new world for me.
Subsequent encounters and discussions with other philosophers have inspired me

for philosophy and physics in me a long time ago.

9 Epilogue: Lines of Further Research . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .
9.1 On the Principle of Causation . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .
9.2 Agency in a Causal World. Consequences of the Non-existence
of Absolute Free Will . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .

About the Author

Louis Vervoort received a Ph.D. in philosophy of science at the Université de
Montréal in 2013 and worked as a post-doc at the Université du Québec à Montréal,
and since then as a professor in Russia, now at the Higher School of Economics
(HSE) in Moscow. Before switching to philosophy, he obtained a Ph.D. in physics at
the Université d’Aix-Marseille and did a post-doc at the École Normale Supérieure in
Paris. This bi-disciplinary experience often incites him to study philosophical problems from a crossed philosophical-scientific perspective; he has the firm belief that
there is a great potential of cross-fertilisation between these fields. Louis Vervoort
has worked on classic problems of analytic philosophy, such as Gettier’s problem,
causation, the interpretation of probability, the problem of induction, free will and
consciousness, and the interpretation of quantum physics—quantum nonlocality and
Bell’s theorem, notably. One of the key insights he would like to convey in this book
is that these problems are related, and that a synthetic approach is needed to address
them.

Maximally Coherent Theory

xxi

Chapter 1

The Method of Synthetic Philosophy.
Extending the Web of Knowledge
Through Problem Solving

Abstract In this chapter I describe the method of synthetic philosophy. Its key
thesis is that problems are solved within the maximally coherent theory, or MCT:
the theory that coherently answers the largest number of well-posed questions. This
postulate provides a semi-quantitative criterion for deciding whether a philosophical
problem is solved or not. I argue that the MCT can be hunted for and found by
exploiting the continuity between science and philosophy. I briefly make the link with
methodological credos of Quine and of Ladyman and Ross. The synthetic method
can already be aided by AI, as illustrated in first case studies. Moreover, it is likely
that future AI will work according to this method, in philosophy and beyond.

1.1 Introduction
Modern philosophy has a reputation of asking more questions than it answers, a
tendency that has been enhanced since the rise of analytic philosophy in the twentieth century. Compared to the sciences, this situation is somewhat disheartening,
since in the latter disciplines answers are found, and problems solved, at least provisionally; and the community usually knows when a problem is solved. In contrast, it
rarely happens that professional philosophers agree that an important philosophical
question is satisfyingly answered, or a problem solved. Of course, the great problems of philosophy are highly complex, for instance because they concern human
beings, societies, and questions of ‘sense’. But is there really a final ground for such
a profound epistemic difference between philosophy and natural science? Is there no
way to curtail the explosion of questions, of questions about questions, of problems
about theories, of worries about solutions?
One ground for the proliferation of unanswered questions in analytic philosophy
is, I believe, the fact that ‘logical possibility’ is ever more frequently considered as a
sufficient ground for deciding about ‘(ontological) reality’—where logical possibility
is usually assessed by common-sense logic. If something can happen in a ‘possible
world’, it is often considered a bona fide candidate for being real. But logic per se
says very little about reality: it deals in the first place with correct reasoning. To

© The Author(s) 2026
L. Vervoort, Problem Solving in Philosophy,
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-17756-8_1

1

2

1 The Method of Synthetic Philosophy. Extending the Web of Knowledge …

derive claims about reality—about our world, the one we study in this book, surely
the one that many philosophers still consider worth studying—one can use logic
and apply it to a set of hypotheses about the world (this is a well-known scheme,
studied by authors as Hempel and Quine, to mention only them). The (correctly)
derived statements about reality are then as certain as the hypotheses from which
they start. Hence the more solid these starting assumptions are, say because they are
in continuity with accepted science, the more likely it is that the derived conclusions
are solid. If a minimalistic logic is used as the sole basis for evaluating ‘possible
worlds’, virtually anything goes. But logical possibilities are vastly constrained by,
notably, laws of nature. In sum, it seems unavoidable to use science as input if one
wishes to regulate the eruption of possibilities that common sense and logic alone
offer to the creative philosopher. Whenever possible, it should be science that anchors
the web of knowledge into firm grounds.
I will argue in this book that philosophy is now mature enough to come to reasonably solid answers to key problems; and that there is one overarching method to
do so, in essence the same method as the natural sciences use to obtain answers,
in particular theoretical physics. I believe that this method is severely neglected
in modern philosophy, resulting in an ever-meandering path of investigations that
leads only in the best case to better-defined problems and partial answers, but rarely
to solutions that allow genuine progress. This method could be called the method
of ‘theory-building’ or the synthetic method, of which I will propose a pragmatic
variant. Let me cast my belief in the form of a hypothesis or principle, which I call
the MCT-hypothesis:
MCT-hypothesis: Answers/solutions for philosophical questions / problems can only
be given with reasonable certainty if they are coherent with, or derived within, the
maximally coherent theory (MCT); and for many important philosophical problems
this MCT can be constructed.
If we understand, for a moment, the MCT simply as the ‘best possible’ theory
(covering a certain problem-space), then most philosophers would presumably agree
with the first part of the MCT-thesis: genuine solutions to problems agree with, follow
from, the best theory about the problem-space of interest. But I suspect that many will
remain sceptical as to whether this best theory can be constructed, as is claimed in
the second part. In order to justify both parts of the thesis I will look in the following
in some detail at how problems are solved in science. This should explain why I use
the concept of ‘maximally coherent theory’; and also how such theories can be found
in a practical way. In Appendix 1.1 examples are provided showing that current AI
can already be used as a supporting tool for the synthetic method. It is to be expected
that this option will become very tangible in the near future. Indeed, at the end of
this chapter I will surmise that it is quite likely that, at some point, AI will be able
to apply the synthetic method in its entirety and thus solve philosophical problems
better than humans do. To the least, I claim we will need the proposed method to
verify the solutions and decisions that the AI of the future will submit to us—if we
wish to remain in control. This is far from a luxury: many of the risks associated
with superhuman intelligence have already been studied (Bostrom, 2014). (Clearly,

1.2 The MCT-Thesis, First Round

3

readers who do not believe that AI will one day help to advance philosophy should
not put this book aside: the synthetic method can be applied by humans without using
AI—which is essentially what I did in this book.)
But first, a word on the notion of ‘theory’. ‘Theory’ should be understood here, in
first instance, in the rather modest sense of a coherent set of assumptions/hypotheses/
principles covering a (smaller or larger) problem-space, i.e. providing explanations
in this space—not necessarily in the grander sense as one knows it for instance in
physics. In physics, theories describing a whole sub-discipline, say thermodynamics
or electrodynamics, are known and accepted; but this is rarely the case in philosophy,
except in standard logic. Yet, if one accepts the MCT-hypothesis, there may exist, for
instance, a sound ‘theory’ of probability or of free will (as investigated in Chapters 4,
6 and 7), while a full-blown theory of philosophy of science or of mind may still be
lacking or debated. In sum, the method I will describe here focuses on the construction
of theories/models/accounts that may refer to limited problem-spaces. Of course, as
in physics, there is no a priori reason why ‘the’ MCT, the theory unifying all theories,
could not exist as a matter of principle. Analogously, physicists do speculate about
a future ‘Theory of Everything’—there exist even theorems about it.1
In a scientific context, it suffices for our purposes to understand theories as
hypothetical-deductive systems of propositions closed under deduction, ideally
axiomatised, in any case based on a (small) number of base hypotheses, definitions, laws, principles, axioms (e.g. Hempel, 1965; Nagel, 1979; Popper, 1959).
Paradigmatic examples are Newton’s mechanics and Euclid’s geometry.

1.2 The MCT-Thesis, First Round
The MCT-thesis can be considered as a heuristic rule for philosophical problemsolving. My goal now is to show that it can be transformed into a practical, workable
methodology. It is a key assumption of this book, following Quine and many other
philosophers, that science and philosophy are in an epistemic continuum; that their
goals (roughly, the description of what is out there, of reality) and (therefore) also
their methods only differ in degree (of precision), but not in kind. Thus, in order to
understand the MCT-thesis it is obviously relevant to have a look at how problems are
solved in science. The following analysis is well-known and has been done in detail
by philosophers as Popper (1959), Hempel (1965), Nagel (1979), Quine (1951, 1960,
1969), Kitcher (1981, 1989), Schurz (1999) and several others. Here I will present
a condensed and simplified synthesis, where I will pay special attention to the key
role the notion of coherence plays in the scientific methodology, in particular in the
process of theory-building.
In this book I will often take inspiration in physics. There are good reasons to
do so. Physics is the most mature of the natural sciences, surely because it deals
1 For instance, such a Theory-of-Everything should satisfy Bell’s theorem, on which more in

Chapter 9.

4

1 The Method of Synthetic Philosophy. Extending the Web of Knowledge …

with, in a sense, the simplest systems: inanimate systems of which the behaviour
can often be reduced to that of one or a few elementary units (typically, particles).
Even if this is certainly not always so, say in fluid mechanics or the quantum theory
of condensed matter, which deal with aggregate systems, the corresponding theories
are (historically, practically) derived from investigating simplified ‘point particles’,
‘elementary fluid elements’, ‘atoms’, etc. Philosophy often deals with incomparably
more complex systems: for instance humans, which have a mind and associate in
complex societies, of which the laws are only beginning to be discovered. Now,
returning to our initial question, in physics and in other natural sciences it is clear
when solutions to problems are accepted by the community: namely, when they are
derived within, or in agreement with, the accepted theory. Mechanical questions
are answered by mechanics, electromagnetic problems are solved within Maxwell’s
theory of electromagnetism, etc.
It is important for my argument to note that accepted physical theories form a
coherent web, roughly as Quine theorised it. There are concepts that occur in several
sub-disciplines associated with different theories, and these concepts allow for a
coherent overlap between disciplines/theories. This web is also coherent with logic
and certain mathematical theories (but physical theories go beyond mathematics:
they have to explain how the mathematics in the theories can be empirically understood and ‘applied’; the mathematical concepts need to be interpreted). At any rate,
physicists do agree that there is no contradiction between their well-established
sub-disciplines, say between optics, classical mechanics, electromagnetism, thermodynamics, statistical physics, and even quantum mechanics. Not only do these
disciplines not contradict each other, there are concepts and principles that exist
in more than one, or most, of them; in this sense no discipline is isolated—they
form a web. For instance, notions as energy, velocity, momentum, position, time are
used in most of the sub-fields, and the same holds for the principles of conservation of energy, of linear momentum, of angular momentum, etc. Therefore, if results
involving these concepts and principles are empirically corroborated in one sub-field,
other sub-disciplines also acquire indirect evidence. In a slogan, there is a dynamics
of corroboration (or refutation!) that extends in principle over the whole coherent
web, as Quine had it.2
2 I will leave this notion of coherent web rather metaphorical here; we do not really need it, but it is

still a handy metaphor (we do need the concept of coherence). It seems that this web can loosely be
imagined as a dense network in which the nodes represent ‘knowledge units’. These knowledge units
can be conceived of as sciences (including logic and mathematics); sub-disciplines of a science;
or laws, principles within one sub-discipline. So the knowledge units can be imagined fine- or
coarse-grained; still in other words, each of the nodes can be envisaged as a web on its own (down
to the most fundamental principles). Thus, a coherent dynamics of corroboration extends over all
of physics, and in principle even over all of the sciences, which all overlap with physics (As Quine
has emphasised, collisions with reality, with experiment, can often be absorbed by modifying some
of the nodes/hypotheses, without affecting the sub-field as a whole. The web then gets upgraded; so
the web is dynamic. A dramatic collision would occur, for instance, if classical logic would appear
not to adequately apply to reality anymore; then it seems that the whole web might need profound
revision. But there is no convincing evidence for this possibility, also not in the light of quantum
mechanics).

1.2 The MCT-Thesis, First Round

5

Of course, at the forefront of science, there are problems for which no fullblown theory yet exists, let alone a generally accepted one. Solving such problems
would amount to a dynamic upgrading of the web. A case in point is the problem of
describing the physics of the Big Bang and the origin of matter. In order to describe
the Big Bang better than we can do now, it is widely accepted that one needs a ‘unification’, as it is called in physics, of quantum field theories and general relativity
theory. In other words, these theories need to be made coherent among each other:
at present, they are still in contradiction when applied to the Big Bang, for instance.
Since there is no unified theory yet, many questions about the Big Bang cannot yet be
answered with reasonable confidence. Even when there is not yet a full theory available, scientists proceed by extending existing theories by modifying only some of the
key hypotheses (e.g. laws) of existing theories. Typically, this leads, at the forefront
of science, to the proposal of several competing theories. The history and philosophy
of science show that among competing frameworks, the ‘winning’ theory is the one
that can unify, i.e. make coherent, the largest number of existing theories. In practice, this is the theory that allows deriving the largest number of formerly accepted
basic theories as special instances (i.e., instances valid in limiting conditions) of the
new more general theory. Returning to the previous example, one expects that the
questions that are now yet unanswered about the Big Bang will be answered once
quantum mechanics and general relativity are unified in a more encompassing theory
of quantum gravity.
To make full sense of the MCT-hypothesis, then, the most useful idea we can learn
from this little excursion in physics, is the following one. The ‘best’ or ‘winning’
(physics) theory that is known at a given epoch of history, is the one that can
adequately answer the largest number of questions, solve the most problems, in
a maximally coherent way—so in a manner that coheres, to start with, with logic and
the relevant mathematics, but also with the other accepted theories. Indeed, note that
if a theory can adequately answer the largest number of questions in its problemspace, then it should also cohere with a maximum number of other accepted theories
(if it does not cohere with all accepted theories it must be a wrong theory and cannot
correctly answer questions, old or new). So, we have found something useful; we
have identified a pragmatic interpretation of what the maximally coherent theory, or
MCT, is: it is the theory that coherently answers the maximum number of (well-posed
and meaningfully different) questions in the problem-space under consideration.
Before returning to philosophy, let’s recall a few other points. Of course, the
history of science has abundantly shown that the ‘best’ theory at a given time is
bound to be replaced by a more powerful one at a later time; non-trivial theories of
natural science are defeasible, provisional, not final (too high likelihood). We know
that natural science theories, however precise, usually end up as approximations
(only approximately true under certain conditions) of deeper and therefore more
precise theories. But in a given period, scientists usually well know which theory is
most powerful, most adequate for a given problem-space; it is always the one that

6

1 The Method of Synthetic Philosophy. Extending the Web of Knowledge …

allows them to answer the largest number of questions in a coherent way.3 Even at the
forefront of science, when a full theory for a complex set of interconnected problems
does not yet exist, one considers the potential of hypotheses to answer questions as
a sure guide for constructing the future theory.
A second point to consider is that scientific progress, understood as the construction of new efficient conceptual frameworks or, if one prefers, as the growth of the
web of knowledge, does not always proceed via a discontinuous process—as when
a full-blown theory is added to the web at one point in time. (Actually, the process is
always gradual, but some cases appear more discontinuous than others.) The discontinuous case is illustrated by Einstein’s construction of special relativity theory: the
core of his theory was largely proposed ‘in one piece’ at a given time, anno 1905,
and thus added as a finalised theory to the web. Usually, the body of scientific knowledge grows in a more continuous way, by gradually integrating concepts, laws, and
models into the web, before a greater solidification is achieved by full-blown theory
synthesis. A case in point is electrodynamics. Before Maxwell made the final theory
(unifying optical and electromagnetic phenomena, and allowing new predictions,
say the spectacular prediction of the speed of light) many local theories / sets of
hypotheses had been proposed that crucially contributed to the final theory. Think
of the discovery of the notions of electric current, magnetic field lines (by Faraday),
the laws of Gauss, Faraday, Ampère, and many more. These more modest theories
were vindicated a posteriori as sound; even before final synthesis, with them the web
had greater explanatory power than without them. Although distinguishing gradual
and discontinuous progress is somewhat artificial, it brings a useful idea to the fore:
usually the web grows via incorporation of small-scale theories. I will argue in a
moment that this is the case in philosophy, where full-blown theory synthesis seems
exceptional.
Let us now return to the MCT-thesis as applied to philosophy. Of course, in philosophy ‘generally accepted theories’ are rare. But still, this concept admits of degree
(there are semi-quantitative measures of acceptance, see further); and, especially, we
have concepts that lie in a semantic continuum with ‘theories’, as already mentioned
above. For many philosophical problems proto-theories, or at least coherent sets of
hypotheses can be constructed that have explanatory power, that can solve problems, answer questions. Just as scientific explanation proceeds from laws (or theories) according to Hempel’s celebrated scheme (1965), in philosophy explanation
also proceeds from coherent sets of hypotheses. Now, the most valuable lesson to be
drawn from the above comparison with physics is the construal of the MCT (covering
a given topic) as the theory with the maximal (coherent) explanatory power, the
theory that coherently solves the largest number of problems (related to the topic of
interest). I claim that this construal provides us with a practical method for problemsolving in philosophy too. Sure, the ‘maximal number of solved problems’ is only
3 ‘Coherent’ here means: 1) if a generally accepted theory covering the problem-space exists:

coherent within the relevant theory (i.e. logically deducible within the relevant theory, as in Hempel’s
‘covering law model’ of explanation); 2) in any case, even if no generally accepted theory exists:
coherent with logic and with mathematics if the theory in question uses mathematics; 3) in any
case, coherent with other accepted theories.

1.2 The MCT-Thesis, First Round

7

a semi-quantitative notion; but it appears to be an excellent heuristic guide, as the
following chapters will hopefully show. Indeed, for most important philosophical
topics analytic philosophy has provided a quite well-structured cartography of the
problems attached to these topics; these problems are approximately countable, at
least in principle.4 I maintain that (synthetic) philosophy should take this analytic
work by a large community seriously, and strive for constructing the synthetic theory
or proto-theory that has the maximal explanatory power with respect to these often
well-identified and published results; this theory is, by definition, the MCT. Synthetic
philosophy solidly rests on past achievements.
In sum, it appears that the soundness of philosophical hypotheses and theories has
in principle a measurable, quantifiable character. ‘Coherent’ in the above lines again
means, a minima: 1) coherent with logic (and with mathematics if the theory / problem
in question involves mathematics); and 2) coherent with established sciences, i.e. not
contradicting them. As in physics, this then also means that the theory will be coherent
with other maximally coherent theories for other, overlapping problem-spaces, i.e.
problem-spaces that involve shared concepts and hypotheses.
At this point a note on the potential for fruitful bi-directional interaction philosophy—science seems in place. The requirement of strict coherence with natural
science, and of using physics as the ultimate touchstone whenever possible, seems to
imply that philosophy can never suggest fundamentally new ideas to science, ideas
that would ultimately lead to fundamental revisions of it. But one should not forget
that the web of knowledge is dynamic: it is, in (almost) all its nodes, provisional to
all likelihood. Even theories of natural science with massive empirical support can
be, and indeed were, replaced by better ones. Although this is a speculative matter,
I believe that in some exceptional cases solid results of synthetic philosophy must
be taken seriously as candidates not only for inspiring and grounding new lines of
scientific research, but also for suggesting paradigm-changing programs; research
programs that would imply, if successful, a fundamental revision of physics, for
instance. This idea is briefly discussed at the end of Chapter 8. In the Epilogue,
Chapter 9§1, I argue that the results obtained in Chapter 5 on causation warrant a
different approach to the interpretation of quantum physics, and even to the unification of quantum mechanics and gravity. But this is speculative philosophy, i.e. philosophy that seems, at least at first sight, in tension with commonly accepted science
theories and their immediate interpretations. (Therefore Chapter 9 goes beyond the
main scope of this book, which is dedicated to solvable problems and tries to avoid
speculation as much as possible.)

4 To the very least, I believe that on the basis of this theoretical number some approximate metric

of explanatory efficacy should be decidable by the philosophical community.

8

1 The Method of Synthetic Philosophy. Extending the Web of Knowledge …

1.3 The MCT-Thesis, Second Round. The Methodology
in Somewhat More Detail
One may understand the MCT-thesis from a slightly different, corroborating perspective. We start from the (trivial) assumption that physics and philosophy share broadly
the same objective: in short, to explain the world.5 The privileged systems studied
in philosophy, humans, consist, ultimately, of physical building blocks. Alone these
two facts, or assumptions, already hint to one of the essential theses of this book,
namely that philosophy and science should cohere, form a coherent web of belief.
Recently this idea has been promoted by Ladyman and Ross (2007) in the context
of metaphysics and the ontology of the ultimate constituents of reality. I propose to
apply it to philosophy as a whole. Many concepts are shared in physics and philosophy, say those of object, probability, space, time, law, cause, locality, etc. Of course,
if the goals and objects of inquiry partly overlap, then it is to be expected that the
methodologies also overlap. Every chapter of this book can be seen as an illustration
but also as an (a posteriori) justification of this epistemic or methodological principle
of the coherence between philosophy and science. Its a posteriori justification lies as
usual in its problem-solving power, to be investigated in the subsequent chapters.
Now, the requirement of the coherence of the web is tantamount to the existence
of strict logical criteria for synthetic philosophical theses to count as ‘sound’ or
‘(approximately) true’: to start with, they should not contradict logic and science,
the most solid core of the web. But they should also not contradict other (proto-)
theories, beyond science, which are gradually integrated into the web. As we saw
above, the web usually grows in small steps. The first candidates to be integrated
with reasonable confidence are, arguably, certain statements of philosophy of science:
solid accounts of concepts as probability, which have a mathematical but necessarily
also an interpretational aspect beyond the mathematics.6 In sum, philosophical theses
can be part of the web; and must be part of it if they qualify as solid knowledge. In
this book I will study problems that in one way or another resonate with physics,
which makes it easier to decide about the solidity of proposed solutions. However,
let me emphasise that even problems that seem a priori far from natural science, say
ethical problems, may very well have a much stronger link than usually assumed. For
instance, an account of free will should be part of ethics; and free will has a strong
link with physics and neuroscience (Chapters 6, 7). Of course, this synthesis in a
grand unified theory of philosophy seems particularly challenging and demands a
much greater effort than attempted in this book. Still, for illustrative reasons I provide
some very preliminary ideas, certainly not definite solutions, for how to start such
a synthesis in Chapter 9, the Epilogue. I will in this book make a clear distinction
between solutions that have been obtained by the synthetic method at full scale, and
5 Or, in the case of some philosophical disciplines, say ethics, to prescribe how a better world might

look like.
6 Probability theory can describe, can be applied to, parts of the objective world (namely repeatable

experiments or ρ-systems, as is argued in Chapter 4). To do so, one has to interpret the terms of the
mathematical theory, which is a task of philosophy (of science).

1.3 The MCT-Thesis, Second Round. The Methodology in Somewhat More …

9

those that remain more conjectural. The Epilogue deals with such conjectural theses
that nevertheless strongly resonate with the results from the main chapters.
Thus, as many philosophers, I consider that the basic theses of scientific realism
are part of the reliable web. It is an implicit assumption of natural science that there
exists an objective, mind-independent physical reality; there is no evidence that the
fundamental laws and properties that are described by our physical theories are the
outcome of the activity of a (human or other) mind.7 That is why I adopt scientific
realism (coupled with material realism, as usual, even if none of the claims made
in this book depend on the material nature of physical reality). Anti-realist theories
elicit vastly more questions than they answer—a sure indication that they are on an
improbable track (more on scientific realism e.g. in Psillos, 1999). When necessary,
I will say more about other background assumptions in the following chapters.
If synthetic philosophy’s goal and methodology are comparable to those of natural
science, then also the epistemic certainty that can be reached by it should be comparable; the difference is one in degree, not in kind. Thus, I submit that the solutions
obtained by synthetic philosophy at full scale are comparable in certainty to solutions obtained in natural science; to the least, they are the solutions on which further
improvements can and should build. Sure, philosophy investigates more complex
systems, implying that formal theories, based on quantitative laws as in physics, are
usually lacking. Hence predictions and confrontation with observation do normally
not have a quantitatively precise character—but exceptions exist, e.g. many predictions derived from the interpretation of probability are quantitative. But to conclude
from this that synthetic philosophy cannot be corroborated or refuted by empirical
observations, is largely a fallacy, I believe. Let me start with a few examples coming
from the following chapters. A sound interpretation of probability contains statements that are verifiable / falsifiable with numerical precision; it solves paradoxes of
probability theory; it has empirical consequences for the interpretation of quantum
mechanics and for the application of probability theory to real-world problems in
general (cf. Chapter 4). A solid definition of the notion of cause can be tested in
neuron diagrams (Chapter 5), and could, via the principle of causation, guide new
explorative research in physics (Chapter 9). A sound definition of free will can
partially be corroborated by neuroscience, and can perhaps even be used to devise
new experiments (Chapter 7). Moreover, it could be integrated in a theory of ethics;
which in turn could be assessed, in principle, by semi-quantitative measures probing
for instance the overall wellbeing the theory ‘generates’ in a test group or society
adopting it. True, this is a particularly ambitious research program that surely is
difficult to implement, but doable in principle. In view of such examples, I would
conclude that refutation of philosophical theses by empirical observations is surely
7 There are interesting philosophical and religious systems that counter this claim. Of course, we

cannot exclude that they are right. But we can exclude that there is scientific evidence for such a
creating mind. In this book we look at what science tells us, and how we can bring philosophy in
harmony with it. Another caveat is this: when I say “laws are not created by a mind” I refer of
course to the ‘structures’ or regularities of nature that are immanent in it or constitute it, not the
law statements that are (approximate) descriptions of the objective laws (according to the scientific
realism I adopt). Law statements are human creations.

10

1 The Method of Synthetic Philosophy. Extending the Web of Knowledge …

much less immediate than in physics, but not impossible. In physics, in principle one
falsified prediction suffices to overthrow the theory. But as Duhem, Quine and others
have shown, this picture is quite simplistic: an accumulation of evidence is required. It
appears that in philosophy even a larger, longer accumulation of conflicting empirical
evidence is needed before the corresponding theories are revised—surely because
the theories deal with much more complex systems. But the (a posteriori) explanatory efficacy of philosophical models about real-world solvable problems remains
testable, even if it is in a subtler and more indirect manner. I hope that this thesis will
be illustrated by examples given in this book.
The above comment concerns the subtle a posteriori justification of solutions via
confrontation with reality or experiment. As argued above, the more practical method
of a priori corroborating/justifying conclusions and solutions derived by synthetic
philosophy is condensed in the MCT-hypothesis and resides in the establishment
of the theoretical problem-solving capacity of these solutions. This is an a priori,
theoretical method, in that it can be applied before one assesses the solution/theory
by empirical research, i.e. by deriving and testing empirical consequences of the
solution or the MCT in the problem-space of interest. (As recalled above, such a
priori theoretical development of solutions also exists in physics, of course. Relativity
theory is an example: it came before its experimental tests.) Note that the success of
this justificatory process in philosophy hinges on the relevance and solidity of the
questions and problems (usually studied in the literature) from which the synthesis
starts. If our discipline makes any sense, it goes without saying that we should
have a reasonable confidence that philosophers have studied meaningful problems
as reported in the literature. And meaningful and well-posed problems (of synthetic
philosophy) usually refer to reality, to this world, not only to logical possibility. I will
often have the occasion to illustrate this claim in the following chapters, for instance
those dealing with definitions, i.e. with models of what some useful concept precisely
corresponds to in reality. In conclusion, both the starting problems and the solutions
of synthetic philosophy should solidly be anchored in reality. There should be a way
to confront synthetic theses with reality, even if this process is less straightforward,
immediate, and precise than in physics.
In wrapping up, what then is, in a nutshell, synthetic philosophy? Synthetic philosophy is philosophy that aims at solving problems following the synthetic methodology, the core postulate of which is the MCT-thesis, stating that problems must be
solved by the MCT in the problem-space of interest. This is a local MCT, not some
MCT in a global sense, some theory that solves all problems of the book. (I did
not show how such a global, unified, parsimonious MCT would look like—even if
I suppose one might have an approximate idea about it by considering the various
local MCTs.) In general, the local MCT is, by definition, the theory that provides the
largest number of coherent answers in the problem-space of interest.
I am aware that this description of the synthetic methodology remains quite
abstract. We can try to be a little more explicit about the tools one can use to find the
MCT, as they follow from the above paragraphs; but the detailed process is problemdependent and can only be made fully concrete in the following chapters. Note, then,
that the definition of MCT provides two criteria, or tools, to find it: maximisation of

1.3 The MCT-Thesis, Second Round. The Methodology in Somewhat More …

11

coherence and maximisation of explanatory power. As we saw, coherence is coherence with science and coherence among local MCTs. It is somewhat artificial to
distinguish coherence and explanatory power, because both go hand in hand (from a
theoretical point of view); but in the heuristic process of problem-solving synthetic
philosophy can start with or privilege one tool rather than the other. The final criterion to judge the final result is coherent explanatory power; how one gets there is
not essential and depends, ultimately, on the psychology of the inquiring synthetic
philosopher. While Chapters 4, 5, 7 focus from the start on maximising explanatory
power, essentially via unification, Chapters 2, 3, 6 mostly use coherency (with science
and inter-MCT coherency) as the practical synthetic tool—which, in fine, should also
lead to maximal coherent explanatory power. This criterion of coherency with science
is sometimes expeditious for finding solutions. For instance, the outcome of Chapter 2
on the problem of induction is coherent with (the methodology of) science and can
be seen as a direct application of the MCT-postulate, stating, among other things,
that problems are solved within theories (hypothetical-deductive systems). Chapter 3
on Gettier’s problem provides an interpretation that coheres with the MCT-postulate
and with all other solutions of the book (which in turn cohere with natural science).
Chapter 6 almost directly draws on physics to propose a solution.
On the other hand, Chapters 4, 5, 7 focus on maximising explanatory power from
the start; coherency with science does not immediately suffice for inspiring solutions. Thus, for the problems of defining probability (Chapter 4), cause (Chapter 5),
and relative free will (Chapter 7) the MCT is the model/theory that unifies the
existing competing accounts for the problem considered, which then should be special
(limiting) cases of the MCT; at least, that is the intended result. Maximal explanatory
power can then rather straightforwardly be demonstrated. Note that for the topics of
Chapters 4, 5, 7, coherency with science seems less immediate as a problem-solving
tool, since they are somewhat farther away from it; but for these topics several quite
solid and well-documented theories exist that clearly have explanatory power—but
also a series of unsolved problems. But again, the distinction between focusing
on coherence or on explanatory power as heuristic tool is somewhat artificial; both
work organically at the same time. For instance, when attempting to make a synthesis
between existing theories, coherence with science and logic should obviously be a
permanent guiding rule, eliminating certain accounts from the start.
Perhaps the most detailed (and maybe surprising) illustration of the synthetic
methodology is given in Chapter 5, where we look for a unified definition of cause.
Most experts believe that an overarching definition does not exist. I will show in
detail how the MCT-method allows us to gradually solve this problem, and how
the most likely MCT is built in a step-by-step process. I will start by investigating
one problem-space, namely mundane causal situations represented by classic neuron
diagrams. These are covered in the literature by counterfactual accounts of different
guises. The goal of the first step is to derive an overarching definition for this space.
This is done by looking at as many problems as possible, which have handily been
compiled in a reference work by Paul and Hall (2013); they all are represented by
diagrams (cf. Table 1 in Chapter 5). For synthesis, it is important to consider a large
number of cases. Next, I will look at an overlapping problem-space, namely the

12

1 The Method of Synthetic Philosophy. Extending the Web of Knowledge …

identification of causes in physics, which is usually handled by the functional and
probabilistic accounts of causation. This analysis will reveal an essential principle
of causal identification, which I will term the ‘representation-relativity of causal
claims’, allowing us to solve several problems of the second space. Once this principle is found, the unification of the physical and counterfactual accounts of causation
will be almost immediate. The resulting higher-order account leads to a solution for
a surprisingly wide array of problems encountered by classic, less synthetic counterfactual accounts, e.g. the well-studied problems related to early and late preemption, double prevention, overdetermination, transitivity, token-versus-type causation,
black-box cases, etc. In the last step of unification, I will show how also the popular
manipulationist account of causation can be subsumed by the final model, solving
again more problems. Thus the chapter on causation offers a detailed illustration
of how synthetic philosophy works, in the step-wise manner that one also finds in
natural science. (Clearly, the fact that the final model applies to physics increases the
likelihood that it represents the MCT; even if the bulk of the arguments leading to
the model comes from philosophy.) In other chapters, e.g. Chapter 4 on probability, I
will propose a synthetic account from the start, and then show how it solves problems
and subsumes other accounts.
A disclaimer: I am of course well aware that the methodology for problem solving
that I propose—even if based on known ideas—is not always straightforward to
apply; that I have not produced here a detailed algorithm that allows one to generate
answers quasi-automatically. But, then, such a thing does not exist in science either:
there is no detailed theory for theory-building beyond the general strategy succinctly
recalled above. Revolutions in science, the revolutionary theories that ultimately
get accepted by the community, are made by their creative genitors in a heuristicpsychological process that remains largely mysterious. But one thing seems sure:
once made, these theories unify, synthesise, and by the same token answer more
questions than their competitors do.
Rather than further scrutinising the MCT-thesis and justifying it a priori, it seems
clearly more instrumental to show how it works in practice. This is the goal of the
next chapters. Especially the second part of the MCT-hypothesis, the claim that for
many problems the maximally coherent solution and theory can be found, needs to
be shown by construction.

1.4 A Note on AI and the Future of Philosophy
An essential feature of the synthetic method is that it should lend itself to fruitful interaction with AI—if not now already, then in the immediate future. Quite generally,
this method starts from dressing a cartography of problems related to a philosophical
topic. Certain types of AI, notably Large Language Models (LLMs), are already
instrumental in making such a cartography, a tendency that should be enhanced in
the future. Appendix 1.1 illustrates how a commercial LLM (a ‘chatbot’) can assist
in defining an overarching definition of the concept of cause, which is the topic of

1.5 A Few Related Views

13

Chapter 5, by listing problems encountered by different accounts of causation. For
what it is worth, I also asked the chatbot to propose a ‘best’ account, the one with
the widest applicability. It chose the candidate that comes out as my winning candidate in Chapter 5. Sure, the list of problems the chatbot proposed (see Fig. 1.1 of
the Appendix) is not necessarily the most complete one as the best experts would
compose it, but it is nevertheless a quite solid starting point. Of course, the AI does
not yet make human philosophical expertise redundant when it comes to synthesising new solutions (for instance, my version of the winning account comes with
essential modifications). But exercises as these show that the prospects of AI-aided
philosophical research are very real, which is also the conclusion of a deeper-digging
study (Vervoort & Nikolaev, 2025).
This seems not surprising. First, AI has the capacity to store and process enormous
amounts of data and texts, whereas humans are clearly limited here. Next, artificial
neural nets’ core capability is to hunt for correlations, for similarities—which is the
first step towards synthesis; and in the context of philosophical research, problemsolving is largely a matter of synthesis. More concretely, since LLMs work essentially
as statistical text completion engines, they should be capable of finding the most
probable answer to many questions—when these are discussed in enough texts and
do not require overly complex step-by-step reasoning (cf. e.g. Vervoort et al., 2023).
‘Most probable answer’ should not be understood in an absolute sense, of course,
but relative to the enormous corpus of (philosophical) texts the models received as
training input.
It is only natural to assume that, once AI will have become a genuine problemsolving tool also at the forefront of philosophical research, it will then be capable of
applying or emulating the synthetic method—somehow. Of course, that seems not
what LLMs are doing for the time being (Arkoudas, 2023; Vervoort et al., 2023).
One should assume that what they propose as solution to a problem is the statistically
most likely (or highly likely) answer as it is extracted from published text—not a
creatively generated new solution. But, then, creativity surely also works according to
rules, and not haphazardly. Given the current explosive evolution of AI, I believe it is
very likely that machines will implement or emulate such rules in the near future (for
first ideas along these lines, see Vervoort et al., 2021; for evidence of AI-creativity
in mathematics and other fields, see e.g. Bubeck et al., 2023).
Therefore, we must assume that, for better or for worse, the first artificial synthetic
philosopher is soon to appear in our midst. If only to assess the output of such engines
and remain in control, we will need very solid synthetic theories in all relevant
problem spaces. In sum, the future of synthetic philosophy should be bright.

1.5 A Few Related Views
Several philosophers have advocated using natural science and in particular physics
as a sure companion for philosophy; from the long list I will here only mention Quine
(1951, 1960, 1969, 1991) and recently Ladyman and Ross (2007). Clearly, I follow

14

1 The Method of Synthetic Philosophy. Extending the Web of Knowledge …

Quine’s credo of the continuity between science and philosophy, and his claim that
philosophy is constrained by scientific standards of clarity, evidence, and explanatory
relevance (Hylton & Kemp, 2023, § 2, § 7; for a detailed analysis of Quine’s developing views, see Verhaegh, 2018). Yet my approach differs from Quine’s philosophy
in several respects. First, Quine adopts a strongly naturalistic conception of epistemology as a chapter of natural science alone (e.g. Quine, 1969, p. 83, Verhaegh,
2018, Ch. 2). In my view such a chapter may of course be sought for and can surely
be interesting, but there are important philosophical matters beyond it, as illustrated
in Chapter 3 on Gettier’s problem. I do not consider epistemology to be a part of
natural science; both problem-spaces are meaningful and distinct even if connected.
Actually, it will be seen in several of the following chapters that for many a problem
it is essential to make the distinction between science and philosophy of science.
Making the distinction is the beginning of the solution (see notably Chapters 2, 7,
and 8). Next, Quine rejects the concepts of ‘meaning’ and ‘knowledge’ essentially
on the basis of their being vague (Quine, 1987, pp. 108–110, Hylton & Kemp, 2023,
§ 2.2). I agree that these notions are vague, but again, I believe epistemology has a lot
to contribute to the meaning of ‘to know’ and the essence of knowledge (Chapter 3).
Philosophy has its reason d’être as a discipline that is clearly distinct from science,
even if in coherence; it allows us to solve meaningful problems. As another example,
Quine rejects the ‘metaphysical’ notion of necessity (Quine, 1991, p. 270). Necessity
plays an important role in my philosophy: see e.g. Chapters 5 on cause and 9 on the
principle of causation. Finally, there are several touchy topics in Quine’s philosophy,
say his view on the analytic-synthetic divide, or the worries he had regarding belief
statements and counterfactual conditionals (e.g. Hylton & Kemp, 2023, §5.2): these
can be analysed and dissolved by the perspectival account elaborated in Chapter 3.
(I will not do the latter analyses in any detail in this book; they are a straightforward
application of the account developed in Chapter 3.)
In a recent thought-provoking book, Ladyman and Ross (2007) apply the scientific paradigm to philosophy; they focus their efforts on philosophy of science and
‘naturalised metaphysics’ or ‘metaphysics done as naturalistic philosophy of science’
(ibid. p. 7). They investigate, notably, the ultimate nature of reality and the unification
of the special sciences with physics. Several of their claims resonate well with some
of those I will present in this book, but I will not study problems of ontology in the
strict sense (even if Chapters 5 and 9 on causation are at the interface of ontology and
philosophy of science), but rather from philosophy of science (Chapters 2, 4, 5, 9),
epistemology (Chapter 3), philosophy of mind (Chapters 6, 7). Thus, in comparison,
I conceive of the objective to base philosophy on science more generally: I believe
philosophy in its entirety should agree with science in a maximally coherent way.
Via the MCT-postulate, this is a quite pragmatic stipulation that works as a guideline
for constructing a scientific philosophy for, in principle, all branches of philosophy8
8 Ladyman and Ross state (ibid., p. 28): “Metaphysics, as we will understand it here, is the enterprise

of critically elucidating consilience networks across the sciences.” I see an analogous role for
synthetic philosophy, namely, to paraphrase, as the enterprise of elucidating coherence networks
across the sciences and the classic branches of philosophy.

1.6 A Swift Flight Over the Remaining Chapters

15

(except perhaps logic). I further wholeheartedly agree that philosophy should aim at
unification and hence that “generation of arguments for disunity is not naturalistic
metaphysical inquiry” (ibid. p. 5). Arguments for disunity are largely predominant
in current philosophy of causation and probability, to name only these.
As said above, I will assume the usual non-sophisticated theses of scientific realism
(see e.g. the introductions of Psillos, 1999; Chakravartty, 2007), as surely many
philosophers of science and most scientists do. In Chapter 3 I will show that my
version of scientific realism has connections with the ‘scientific perspectivism’ of
authors as Giere (2006) and Massimi (2018). But I would like to emphasise that for
addressing the problems studied in this book, I do not need to delve into the extraordinarily complex question of the ultimate constituents of nature or the universe.
Obvious candidates are elementary particles, quantum fields, force fields or a combination of these. The appropriate reflex would be to look at what the experts of our most
fundamental physics theory, quantum field theory, have to say about this problem. But
at the present stage of physical theory, this problem is undecidable, as even experts
admit (Kuhlmann, 2023, §5.5). Ladyman and Ross advocate a version of scientific
realism called ‘ontic structural realism’, which holds that only structure and relations
exist, rejecting material objects as fundamental. Now, even if this vivid debate were
resolved (despite its many variants and issues, cf. Ladyman, 2023), it wouldn’t affect
my answers to the classic philosophical problems explored in this book: these are
independent of the debate over ultimate reality.9 In sum, I remain largely agnostic on
the question of fundamental ontology, a matter that seems undecidable at present.10
But this local agnosticism is of course not an obstacle to formulating answers to
classic problems, answers that are reasonably certain, perhaps not in some unrealistic ultimate sense, but answers that make progress and should be a safe basis for
a next cycle of synthesis. Other differences with Ladyman and Ross (2007) concern
the principle of causation (more in Chapter 9).

1.6 A Swift Flight Over the Remaining Chapters
Before concluding this chapter, let me present a brief overview of the remaining
chapters (this section can be skipped by readers who prefer their sense of suspense
not to be spoiled). Chapter 2 is a very short and straightforward illustration of how
the MCT-thesis can sometimes almost immediately solve classic problems of philosophy, in this case the problem of induction. In Chapter 3 I will show that insights from
epistemology intimately cohere with the MCT-hypothesis and that knowledge, the
object of epistemology, must inextricably be linked to sets of hypotheses (theories).
9 As long as we can remain in the paradigm of scientific realism.
10 Also for this problem, the synthetic method is in principle applicable. But applying this method

seems very difficult, notably because the cartography of interwoven and sometimes ill-defined
problems is very complex; and, especially, because physics does not seem to privilege any of the
possible answers to many or most of these problems.

16

1 The Method of Synthetic Philosophy. Extending the Web of Knowledge …

The MCT-thesis and these fundamental insights from epistemology corroborate each
other, form a coherent web. Logically Chapter 3 is prior to Chapter 2, because the
former lends credit to my initial hypothesis, the MCT-thesis; Chapter 2 is an immediate application of that hypothesis. But I prefer to start with a short and easy chapter,
to present the method in a gradual manner to the reader.
In somewhat more detail, Chapter 2 applies the MCT-hypothesis to Hume’s
problem of induction, sometimes called “one of the most profound philosophical
challenges imaginable” (Henderson, 2020). This problem has been studied by many
renowned philosophers, including Russell, Mill, Reichenbach, Carnap, Quine, and
many more. I have already mentioned that the MCT-hypothesis or methodology is
related to Quine’s holism. The latter view can quite precisely be re-interpreted as
stating that questions can only adequately be answered, and problems solved, by
whole theories—a claim made more precise by the MCT-thesis (cf. Chapter 3). My
account is also related to Aristotle’s philosophy of science, at least if we interpret
it somewhat charitably (for a detailed critical analysis of Aristotle’s philosophy of
science, cf. van Fraassen, 1980). Aristotle seems to have fore-felt that any solid
knowledge, scientific or philosophical, must be based on unprovable ‘first principles’, as he called them, which constitute the basis of a scientific (sub-)discipline. In
modern terms, these first principles are the axioms, postulates, basic laws, or basic
hypotheses of theories. If we accept this, and we must accept something, we almost
immediately solve the infamous problem of induction.
If solid knowledge is inextricably linked to theories, more generally sets of
hypotheses, one expects that epistemology, the theory of knowledge, should cohere
with the MCT-thesis. One expects a fundamental and ubiquitous manifestation of the
MCT-thesis in epistemology. Now, there is indeed compelling evidence from these
quarters that scientific and philosophical knowledge are embedded in theories in a
fundamental way.11 I show in Chapter 3 that one of the key problems of modern
epistemology, Gettier’s problem, is solved when one considers knowledge (as well
as justification and truth) as being relative to sets of hypotheses, in a sense to be specified. This chapter will lead us to conclusions that can conveniently be summarised
by simple and short phrases as: one can know something only if assuming some
theory—theory in the broad sense specified above, so including, more mundanely,
coherent sets of hypotheses. In a slogan: knowing needs assuming (to start with).
And something is true only relative to, within a set of hypotheses, a theory—a theory
which can, as a whole, be a more or less adequate representation of reality. Against
appearances, this position can easily be married to absolutist or objectivist views
about truth and knowledge, be it in a slightly subtle way. Similarly, justification is
justification within a theory, relative to a theory. Without such assumptions, pointing
to a type of epistemic relativism and a mixed coherence-correspondence theory of
truth, Gettier’s conundrum remains with us; with these assumptions, it becomes
transparent.
11 Note that this claim is not in contradiction with the idea that empirical observation and exper-

imentation are essential for the discovery and construction of theories of natural science and
philosophy.

1.6 A Swift Flight Over the Remaining Chapters

17

I suspect that at this point some philosophers will raise their eyebrows. Some
might find this a little simplistic. Those I would remind of Russell’s motto stated
at the beginning of this book: we have to start with something simple, and then
hopefully transform it into something less simple. All subsequent chapters heavily
draw on insights gained in Chapter 3. Others might object: why would (the truth of)
banal ‘observation sentences’, such as “The neighbour’s dog just bit me”, be relative
to a theory? As mentioned above, it is essential to keep in mind that I will use in
this book the notion of ‘theory’ often in a broad sense: it will, depending on the
context, also refer to (coherent) sets of hypotheses, belief systems, coherent bodies
of information (which may refer to sense data), etc. In this sense it comes close to the
more informal ‘perspective’ or ‘representation’. In several of the following chapters
(notably in Chapters 3, 5 and 7), in order to make the distinction between this looser
concept and that of scientific theory in the strict sense (T), I will often use ‘theory*’
or the symbol ‘T*’ for the broader concept. Then, it will be argued in Chapter 3 that
even the truth value, and hence the knowing, of a sentence as “The neighbour’s dog
just bit me”, depends on some—indeed rather trivial—set of hypotheses T*, such as
that the sentence-utterer is not deceived, that the animal that bit him classifies as a
dog according to biology (not as a wolf), etc. Philosophers of science will recognise
here the well-known ‘theory-ladenness’ of observation sentences. Of course, in such
mundane cases it is usually not necessary to specify the hypotheses T* one assumes;
but in sophisticated cases it is. It is reassuring that perhaps the most paradigmatic
topic of modern epistemology coheres so well with the MCT-hypothesis.
Chapters 4 to 7 concern definitions, namely of the concepts of probability, cause,
and free will. Definitions of metaphysics, philosophy of science, philosophy of mind,
etc. can fulfil the role of basic hypotheses of a philosophical theory, or of a coherent
proto-theory. They are of the type “X is Y” (e.g. “free will is Y”), where (i) Y describes
X in sufficient detail, sufficient for explanatory use, and where (ii) X is (implicitly)
assumed to be something of importance, of interest, something that exists in reality; or
to be a concept of a theory (say metaphysics or the interpretation of probability) that
usefully describes or analyses reality. Such a definition can be the basis of a genuine
theory about X; which, if it is a good theory, should be maximally coherent. Thus, I
agree with almost all analytic philosophers that defining concepts is a highly useful
task of philosophy. And indeed philosophy has done a fine job, not only in defining
concepts, but also in identifying a great number of problems for existing definitions
and accounts based on these definitions. This is precisely the fertile ground needed
to start the next task, that of constructing definitions/theories, ‘theory-definitions’ if
preferred, that can solve ideally all existing problems—in practice, the most relevant
ones published in the literature.
This is the approach I have followed for proposing the definitions in Chapters 4,
5 and 7. Regarding probability, there is a vast literature devoted to understanding
this notoriously subtle notion, beyond the relatively simple mathematics. The mathematical concept of probability can be applied, highly successfully, to real-world
problems; hence one needs to interpret the mathematical symbols in order to use the
calculus correctly. Here is where the problems start, as witnessed for instance by the

18

1 The Method of Synthetic Philosophy. Extending the Web of Knowledge …

vast number of paradoxes of probability theory, with which even famous mathematicians have struggled.12 These paradoxes arise because a precise definition of probability (one that interprets Kolmogorov’s axioms) appears to be surprisingly subtle:
there are implicit notions linked to it, and one needs to be aware of these if one wishes
to solve tricky problems. In Chapter 4 I will propose a definition that makes these
implicit notions explicit. This definition can be seen as an elaboration of von Mises’
frequency interpretation of probability (1928/1981, 1964). Alas, the frequency interpretation seems to have become a minority position in the philosophical community
(e.g. Hayek, 2019). This is regrettable, since the other interpretations, such as the
classical or logical or subjective interpretations, do not comply with the MCT-thesis:
they cannot solve problems in a maximally coherent manner. In Chapter 4 I propose
an account that unifies the original frequency interpretation, the classical interpretation, the propensity interpretation, and certain consistent versions of the subjective
interpretation. They all can be seen as special cases of the proposed model.
Regarding the notion of cause the situation is as complex as for probability.
Libraries are filled with works on causality/causation; again very many interpretations are on the market. In metaphysics Lewis’s counterfactual theory is popular
(1973). In philosophy of science the manipulability account (e.g. Woodward, 2003)
or the probabilistic theory of causation (e.g. Suppes, 1970) are favoured—and usually
believed to be incompatible with other accounts, Lewis’s in particular. Hence, just
as for probability, it is generally believed that one overarching definition of cause
is impossible. But this is a puzzling thought: in everyday speech people usually
know well how to use the concept—and indeed common-sense intuition is always
the touchstone for judging the adequacy of a theoretical account of cause –; but at the
same time philosophy would not be able to provide an overarching definition. This
sounds dubious, and indeed synthetic philosophy comes to a different conclusion
(Chapter 5).
Chapters 6 and 7 are dedicated to another classic of philosophy, namely the
problem of free will: what is free will, and do we have it? The debate between libertarians, determinists, compatibilists and many other schools goes on since millennia;
almost all philosophers of renown have expressed their opinion on the problem.
Clearly, whether something exists depends on how one defines it; once a definition
is given the existence is decided by the best available theory. Hence, in principle the
free will question can be studied from different perspectives. In order to understand
the enormous range of questions and positions this problem elicits, I will argue that
it is best studied from two perspectives: the first based on physics and its philosophy
(Chapter 6), the second based on (all) other cognitive fields, notably psychology,
sociology, and the attached theories of practical philosophy such as ethics, philosophy of law, etc. (Chapter 7). These two perspectives vehemently clash. The first one
must be expected to be the most solid view since it derives almost immediately from
physics (not from the assumption of determinism, as many believe). It leads to the
staggering conclusion that we do not have free will, at least not in the absolute sense
12 It is sometimes said that probability theory is the branch of mathematics against which it is easiest

to make mistakes (Tijms 2004).

1.7 Conclusion

19

in which it is usually defined. However, I will also argue (Chapter 7) that it is useful
to withhold a modified, relative notion of free will—if only to be able to engage in
discussions with… most people. Having free will, in this relative sense, is a necessary
condition for having moral responsibility and agency, which play such a preeminent
role in ethics, legal frameworks, philosophy of wellbeing etc. Hence, to jettison the
concept of free will seems risky. However, I cannot present a full-blown synthesis of
both perspectives. In other words, although the definition of relative free will given in
Chapter 7 solves many, and perhaps a majority, of the published problems, it surely
is subject to further improvement. Needless to say, just as in science, any position
in synthetic philosophy is perfectible. Until a full-blown MCT is proposed, I submit
that the two basic perspectives are complementary: it depends on the context which
one is best adopted.
Chapter 8 is the general conclusion, resuming some key findings. Finally, in
the Epilogue, Chapter 9, I will study more conjectural theses, not obtained by the
synthetic method at full scale: lines of further research that seem particularly interesting to me, and that can be based on the results derived in the main chapters. These
lines of research concern practical implications of my account of free will, and the
principle of causation.

1.7 Conclusion
In this chapter I have proposed a methodology leading to a criterion for judging the
quality/adequacy/soundness of philosophical explanations and solutions, a criterion
