that is measurable, at least in theory. The rationale is clear by now: answers are
adequate if they follow from the MCT (covering the relevant problem-space); and
identifying the MCT is based on counting the problems that it can coherently solve.
This method, which hinges on ideas that are well alive in philosophy of science, seems
transparent and pragmatic—something I will try to substantiate in the following. Of
course, in practice, in some cases deciding about the explanatory power of a theory is
not always entirely obvious, essentially because the cartography and counting of the
covered problems are not always entirely obvious; but at least we have a guideline
and are not moving blindly. At the same time, I am aware that the proof of the pudding
is in the eating: that I will need to illustrate and support the approach by case studies.
Notwithstanding the straightforwardness and transparency of the method, it would
be naïve to believe that it will be welcomed with open arms in all quarters. I am
aware that the synthetic method goes against the predominant habit of studying and
discussing problems in hyper-specialised communities, often focussing on problems
of purely analytic nature, and rarely attempting to make the link with other related
topics (let alone society and science). This modern habit surely comes with extrascientific benefits. But it obstructs the verification of the verisimilitude of the theses
proposed—the correspondence to reality that seems often of secondary concern (a
problem that does not only befall philosophy). Furthermore, the proposed method
is onerous: it requires the study of large ensembles of problems and theories, and,

20

1 The Method of Synthetic Philosophy. Extending the Web of Knowledge …

especially, their subsequent synthesis or unification—an activity that seems largely
expelled from contemporary analytic philosophy. I believe that this lack in systemapproach, in theory-based thinking, is the greatest hiatus in current philosophy. I
believe it is a ground for stagnation, and potentially for a loss of credibility of the
discipline in the eyes of many outsiders. And the problem is pervasive and multifarious. Many authors surely will have encountered reviewers of peer-reviewed journals
rejecting a model only because one of its starting hypotheses does not match the
beliefs of the reviewer—even if the model solves more problems than the competing
accounts. I cannot emphasise enough: the ultimate judge of a new theory should
be its explanatory power, not the a priori plausibility of its premises per se. A fine
analytic job is to identify errors in reasoning starting from a theory’s premises; but
not to reject bona-fide premises from the start, even if these are subjectively counterintuitive (an idea also stressed by Ladyman & Ross, 2007). Intuitive plausibility is
in the eye of the beholder. Explanatory power is much more objective, since it is
ultimately a quantitative criterion. A corollary is that it makes little sense to question
and scrutinise ad nauseam the premises of a theory ‘on their own’, if they lead to a
better theory.

Appendix 1.1. Case Study: AI as a Tool for Synthetic
Philosophy
In synthetic philosophy, in order to build the solution for problem X, it is essential
to keep an overview of (in principle all) the problems faced by accounts that aim at
addressing problem X. Normally such an overview is the starting platform from which
the creative task of synthesis proceeds. It seems clear that certain types of AI can
assist us in this effort, at least for dressing the initial cartography of problems. I tested
this idea using a currently popular LLM, ChatGPT, based on GPT-4 technology,13
for a small number of problems. I will here only present a few illustrative examples, a
mini case study. A systematic investigation of a specific AI’s problem-solving skills
in philosophy will, I believe, soon become interesting, but goes beyond the examples
shown here. A more detailed case study is given in Vervoort and Nikolaev (2025),
focusing on causation in neuron diagrams.
For instance, I asked the chatbot to provide a list of problems faced by existing
accounts of causation. Figure 1.1 displays my question (Q1) and the answer ChatGPT
gave. One will note that, although neither perfect nor complete, the vast majority
of listed problems are quite sensible and identify key concerns for the respective accounts of causation. I believe this is not a surprise, in view of how these
13 From the company OpenAI (https://openai.com/). ChatGPT, based on the Generative Pre-trained

Transformer (GPT) language model, is available in different versions (GPT-3.5, GPT-4 and several
others) (cf. OpenAI 2023). The experiment, done in December 2024, does not aim to be systematic;
also, more powerful models are bound to be released. For some insights on the working of LLMs,
I recommend a publication by the company DeepSeek (Guo et al., 2025). Finally, more complete
lists of problems can be obtained by interacting with LLMs in a dialogue about specific accounts.

Appendix 1.1. Case Study: AI as a Tool for Synthetic Philosophy

21

language models function: they should be excellent in summarising problems that
are frequently discussed in published texts.

Q1> In philosophy, the concept of ‘cause’ is highly debated. Philosophers generally doubt that there is
an overarching definition of the notion of cause, one that can be used in all contexts and disciplines.
Instead, there are different accounts of causation, such as the counterfactual account, the manipulationist
account, etc. However, all these accounts are debated and come with a series of problems. My questions
is: Can you make a complete list of problems that these accounts encounter?
GPT-4> Certainly! Below is a list of problems commonly associated with various accounts of
causation, organized by the account type:
1. Counterfactual Accounts of Causation
• Problem of Preemption: Counterfactual accounts struggle with cases of preemption, where
two potential causes exist, but one preempts the other. For example, a backup cause might not
occur because the primary cause takes effect, yet the backup cause still seems relevant.
• Problem of Overdetermination: If two independent causes are sufficient to produce an effect
(e.g., two people simultaneously pushing a car), the counterfactual account has difficulty
explaining which cause is primary or whether both equally qualify.
• Problem of Non-Actual Scenarios: Counterfactuals require evaluating non-actual,
hypothetical scenarios, which can be unclear or indeterminate. Determining the "nearest
possible world" can be subjective and context-dependent.
• Fragility of Events: Some versions assume that small changes to an event’s context make it
impossible to determine causation, leading to inconsistencies when the "smallest change" is
unclear.
• Asymmetry of Causation: Counterfactuals often assume causation is asymmetric (cause
precedes effect), but they sometimes fail to adequately explain why the counterfactual
dependence is directional.
2. Manipulationist (or Interventionist) Accounts
• Circularity Problem: Manipulationist accounts often define causation in terms of
interventions, but interventions themselves presuppose causal relationships, potentially leading
to circular reasoning.
• Scope Limitation: The account works best in scientific and experimental contexts but
struggles to apply to historical, metaphysical, or everyday causal claims where interventions
are impossible.
• Problem of Complex Systems: In systems with numerous interconnected variables,
distinguishing a specific intervention as the "cause" is often impractical.
• Non-Intervenable Causes: There are causes that cannot be intervened upon (e.g., laws of
nature or events in the distant past), yet they are still causally significant.
• Causal Relativity: The notion of causation becomes relative to the particular variables and
interventions chosen, raising concerns about whether causation is objective or merely
pragmatic.
3. Regularity Accounts (Humean Causation)
• Problem of Singular Causation: Regularity accounts explain causation in terms of patterns or
laws, but they fail to account for one-off causal relations (e.g., a meteor causing a crater).
• Distinction Between Correlation and Causation: Regularity accounts often conflate
correlation with causation, struggling to explain why one event brings about another rather
than merely accompanying it.
• Epistemic Problem: Regularity accounts depend on observing patterns over time, but this does
not explain causation in cases where patterns cannot yet be observed.
• Problem of Necessity: Regularity accounts do not capture the intuitive sense of necessity that
underpins causation (i.e., why the cause must bring about the effect).

Fig. 1.1 Dialogue with ChatGPT about causation and its problems (showing the literally transcribed
text produced by ChatGPT-4)

22

1 The Method of Synthetic Philosophy. Extending the Web of Knowledge …

4. Probabilistic Accounts
• Low-Probability Events: Probabilistic accounts fail to explain causation when causes lead to
effects that are very unlikely (e.g., smoking causing lung cancer, which remains
probabilistically rare).
• Epiphenomena: Probabilistic accounts may attribute causation to events that merely correlate
with the effect without actually bringing it about (e.g., barometer readings and storms).
• Problem of Context Dependence: Probabilities depend on background knowledge and
assumptions, making causation highly context-dependent rather than an objective relation.
• Ambiguity in Thresholds: These accounts often rely on arbitrary thresholds for determining
causation (e.g., how much an event must raise the probability of another event).
5. Process Accounts
• Vagueness of Processes: Process accounts (e.g., causal processes transmitting marks or
energy) face challenges in precisely defining what counts as a process versus a coincidental
chain of events.
• Non-Physical Causes: These accounts struggle to handle abstract or non-physical causation
(e.g., mental states causing actions).
• Over-Specification of Mechanisms: Some process accounts require detailed mechanisms to
establish causation, making them unsuitable for less well-understood causal contexts.
6. Mechanistic Accounts
• Scope of Application: Mechanistic accounts are suited for biological or physical systems but
do not generalize well to social, historical, or abstract domains.
• Granularity Problem: There is often ambiguity about the level of detail needed in a
mechanism to count as explanatory (e.g., molecular vs. macroscopic levels).
• Boundary Problem: Identifying the boundaries of mechanisms (what to include or exclude)
can be arbitrary or context-dependent.
• Absence of Mechanisms: There are cases where causes and effects occur, but the exact
mechanism is unknown or unknowable (e.g., early scientific theories).
7. Agency Accounts
• Anthropocentrism: These accounts, which define causation in terms of human actions or
intentions, are overly anthropocentric and do not apply to non-human or non-intentional
causes.
• Problem of Involuntary Causation: They fail to account for causes that do not involve
deliberate agency, such as natural disasters.
• Circularity in Agency: Causation by agents is often explained in terms of causal relations
between mental states and actions, potentially leading to circular definitions.
8. Pluralist Accounts
• Conflict of Criteria: Pluralist accounts admit multiple types of causation but face difficulties
reconciling conflicting criteria across contexts (e.g., counterfactuals vs. mechanisms).
• Loss of Explanatory Power: By allowing multiple accounts, pluralism risks losing the
coherence and unifying explanatory power that a single account provides.
• Ambiguity in Selection: It may be unclear which account of causation to apply in a specific
context, leading to arbitrariness or inconsistency.
This overview demonstrates that no account of causation is without significant challenges, and much
philosophical debate stems from attempting to address these problems. Let me know if you would like
further details on any specific issue!

Fig. 1.1 (continued)

Immediately after ChatGPT gave its answer to Q1 (in seconds), I asked it to pick
the ‘best’ account (Q2): see details in Fig. 1.2. For what it is worth, it chose the
account that comes closest to the one proposed in Chapter 5—based on painstaking
human research.

Appendix 1.1. Case Study: AI as a Tool for Synthetic Philosophy

23

Q2> Can you make now an assessment of what is the ‘best’, the most widely applicable, of all accounts
of causation proposed in the philosophical literature? Please choose only ONE account.
GPT-4> If I must choose only one account as the "best" and most widely applicable in the
philosophical literature, the Counterfactual Account of Causation stands out as the most viable
option.
Why the Counterfactual Account?
The Counterfactual Account, most famously developed by David Lewis, defines causation in terms of
counterfactual dependence: an event AAA causes an event BBB if, had AAA not occurred, BBB would
not have occurred. Despite its limitations, this account is the most widely applicable for several
reasons:
1. General Applicability
• Diverse Domains: The counterfactual account applies broadly across a wide range of fields,
including:
o Everyday reasoning ("If I hadn’t watered the plant, it would have died").
o Legal and moral contexts ("Had the defendant not acted, the victim would have
survived").
o Scientific reasoning, particularly in hypothesizing causal links.
• Metaphysical Flexibility: Counterfactuals are conceptually flexible and can be adapted to
describe causation in abstract scenarios, including non-physical or historical contexts.
2. Intuitive Appeal
• Closely Matches Human Reasoning: Counterfactual reasoning mirrors how humans think
about causation in practice. It aligns with our natural way of assessing responsibility, blame,
and impact.
• Simplicity and Clarity: The basic structure of counterfactual dependence ("if not AAA, then
not BBB") is straightforward and accessible.
3. Relationship with Other Accounts
• Compatibility: The counterfactual framework can incorporate or complement insights from
other accounts:
o It connects with manipulationism, as interventions often depend on counterfactual
reasoning.
o It can be extended to probabilistic contexts by considering how AAA changes the
likelihood of BBB.
o It aligns with mechanistic explanations when used to identify which processes are
essential to producing the effect.
4. Addressing Limitations
While the Counterfactual Account does face challenges, such as those posed by preemption and
overdetermination, philosophical refinements and hybrid approaches have mitigated many of these
issues:
• Preemption: Advanced versions of the account (e.g., incorporating causal chains or structural
equations) handle preemption better than the basic version.
• Overdetermination: Probabilistic and dependence-based refinements help resolve cases where
multiple sufficient causes exist.
Even with these challenges, the counterfactual account remains versatile and broadly applicable. Its
issues are often more tractable than those faced by competing theories.
Conclusion
The Counterfactual Account is the "best" and most widely applicable account of causation because it
provides a general, intuitive, and flexible framework that spans diverse contexts and fields. While no
single account can solve all problems, the counterfactual approach strikes the best balance between
simplicity, theoretical power, and adaptability.

Fig. 1.2 ChatGPT proposes its preferred account of causation (the dialogue shows the literally
transcribed text produced by ChatGPT-4)

1 The Method of Synthetic Philosophy. Extending the Web of Knowledge …

Whether one qualifies these results as surprising or not (and even whether one gives
a high or low score to the answers’ quality) ultimately depends on one’s perspective.
But a few years ago few people would have suspected that an AI could answer the
above questions in a coherent manner. It is not possible to know in detail how GPT4 comes to its answers; it is a ‘black-box’ technology.14 But one can assume that
any given answer, e.g. to Q2, must be the statistically most probable answer—or
a highly probable one—as it is synthesised from published texts; in this sense it
might well correspond to the most popular answer in the philosophical community.
Of course, that does not mean it is the correct or ‘best’ solution in the absolute.
Note that the problem-list of Fig. 1.1 could probably be made more complete by
asking the chatbot questions targeting individual accounts; it is well-known by now
that chatbots as ChatGPT often have an increased performance when judiciously
prompted in a dialogue.
Let me admit that the problem-list of Fig. 1.1 would have come in handy when
I started working on causation several years ago (I did not have it at hand then). At
any rate, it remains an interesting exercise to show that the new account of causation
derived in Chapter 5 does indeed solve all the problems listed in the figure (see many,
but not all, answers in Chapter 5).
For comparison, Fig. 1.3 shows an example in which ChatGPT’s solution is clearly
diverging from mine: the interpretation of probability, studied in Chapter 4. Another
example of helpful use of AI is given in Chapter 2, Appendix 2.1 on the problem of
induction. I asked another LLM, DeepSeek (Guo et al., 2025), what the problem of
induction is, and whether Popper or Quine solved it. I estimate that—for this question—the AI’s answer is quite satisfying and again a good example of a ‘common
denominator answer’: one that probably expresses the received view in the philosophy community. Indeed, since answering the question does not require complex
reasoning, and since much material about it must exist in the literature, one can
expect that the LLM will give such a consensual answer.
A cautious conclusion of case studies as the above is that AI is well on track
to serve as an assistant for philosophical problem solving. Going a little further, in
(Vervoort & Nikolaev, 2025) it is shown that ChatGPT and other LLMs can already
correctly answer questions that are highly debated by causation experts. I believe that
exercises as these make it abundantly clear that we, human philosophers, urgently
need to develop powerful synthetic theories, if we wish to keep ahead of machines
and be capable of assessing their outcomes and decisions. And it seems that other
disciplines, especially theoretical ones, face the same challenge. Clearly, this matter is
also essential for safety and control, which might not be guaranteed if AI’s cognitive
capacities cross a certain threshold (Bostrom, 2014).

14 Interesting but partial information about the computational techniques used can be found in Guo

et al. (2025) which discusses a similar language model.

Q3> In philosophy and science, there is a debate about how to interpret the notion of probability.
Several accounts/interpretations are discussed in the literature, for instance the limiting frequency
interpretation, the subjective interpretation, the logical interpretation, the propensity interpretation, etc.
If you would have to choose ONE of these interpretations as the most universal one, the most widely
applicable one, which one would you choose (choose only one)?
GPT-4> If I were to choose just one interpretation of probability as the most universal and widely
applicable, I would select the subjective interpretation. Here's why:
The subjective interpretation, often associated with Bayesian probability, defines probability as a
degree of belief or confidence an individual has in the occurrence of an event, given the information
available. This interpretation is extremely flexible and universally applicable for several reasons:
1. Wide Applicability Across Domains:
o It applies not only to repeatable experiments (like rolling a die) but also to unique, oneoff events (e.g., the probability of a future war or the probability of a specific historical
event having occurred).
o It is indispensable in fields where uncertainty is a result of incomplete knowledge,
such as decision theory, economics, and medicine.
2. Consistency with Rational Decision-Making:
o The subjective interpretation aligns closely with Bayesian updating, a method that
allows beliefs to be systematically updated in light of new evidence, which is a
cornerstone of scientific reasoning and decision-making.
3. Handles Singular Events:
o Unlike the limiting frequency interpretation, which requires repeatable experiments,
the subjective interpretation handles singular, non-repeatable events without issue.
4. Bridges Objectivity and Subjectivity:
o While inherently subjective, the approach becomes more "objective" when individuals
with shared information converge to similar probabilities. This makes it a practical tool
for scientific inference and collaborative decision-making.
While other interpretations, like the propensity or frequency views, have strong applications in specific
contexts (e.g., physical sciences or long-term experiments), they are often less flexible or struggle to
address cases involving uncertainty due to lack of repeatability or incomplete knowledge.
Thus, the subjective interpretation, with its foundational flexibility and coherence in a wide range of
philosophical and scientific contexts, emerges as the most universal.

Chapter 2

The Problem of Induction Unmasked

Abstract This short chapter is meant as an immediate application of the key postulates of synthetic philosophy, exposed in Chapter 1. I argue that the problem of
induction can be unmasked as a pseudo-problem by using Aristotle’s insights on the
working of science and his notion of ‘first principle’. These insights are corroborated by centuries of scientific practice. Therefore, if philosophy wishes to employ
epistemic standards comparable to those at work in science, it should adopt a welldefined principle of induction as a first principle, in other words as an unprovable
basic axiom, and recognise that asking for its logical justification makes little sense.
This result can be seen as an application of the MCT-thesis, implying that problems
are solved by theories; some of these theories’ assumptions cannot be derived from
higher-order theories; and must therefore remain unjustified in this sense. But in
another sense they can be maximally justified, especially the principle of induction.

2.1 Introduction for the Lay Person
Can I infer with certainty from the fact that the sun has always risen, that it will rise
tomorrow? Can I infer from the fact that all the swans that I saw are white, that the
next swan I will see will be white? (No: there are black swans.) Can I, in general, infer
certainties about the future from what I experienced in the past? More dramatically:
can scientists legitimately propose laws by induction, i.e. by formulating general
principles based on a body of particular observations? Or is it better to revert to
science-scepticism?
These questions were famously studied by Hume, one of the most popular philosophers of all time, in his Treatise of Human Nature. There he wrote: “there can be
no demonstrative arguments to prove, that those instances, of which we have had
no experience, resemble those, of which we have had experience” (Hume, 1739).
Hume concluded that there is no satisfactory justification for inductive inferences.
The “problem of induction” has since attracted a huge amount of work, some philosophers going as far as calling it “one of the most profound philosophical challenges
imaginable” (Henderson, 2020). Bertrand Russell claimed that if Hume’s problem

© The Author(s) 2026
L. Vervoort, Problem Solving in Philosophy,
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-17756-8_2

29

30

2 The Problem of Induction Unmasked

cannot be solved, “there is no intellectual difference between sanity and insanity”
(Russell, 1946). Prominent philosophers as Mill (1843), Reichenbach (1938), Russell
(1948), Carnap (1952), Popper (1959), Salmon (1966), and Quine (1969) all proposed
their solutions (more on Popper’s and Quine’s solutions in Appendix 2.1). But no
consensus about its interpretation has emerged (for a recent review with updated
references, see Henderson, 2020).
I ask the reader to pause a moment, and to try to come up with an answer to Hume:
is there really no satisfactory justification of induction?

2.2 Introduction for the Professional Philosopher
Hume’s question of induction can generally be stated thus: how can we justify inductive inferences, i.e. inferences from the observed to the unobserved, in particular to
general laws? I will consider this question here in the context of natural science,
to be able to tackle the problem in its most precise (and most dramatic) form. The
‘problem of induction’ in science, or the scientific problem of induction, can then be
stated as follows: there seems to be no justification for inferences from the observed
to general scientific laws. It is useful to further rephrase this in a slightly more definite
guise as follows: there seems to be no (conclusive) justification for the fact that any
given theory of natural science can ‘work’, i.e. can have predictive (and explanatory) power, so, can predict future observations.1 Note that I said ‘can work’: not
every theory works, not even approximately. I also emphatically only assume that
theories have predictive and explanatory power in a defeasible, improvable manner.
(This assumption is coherent with the history of science; and we want to reason
within a maximally coherent framework, resonating with science.) Introducing in
an obvious manner the notion of ‘principle of induction’, one can phrase things in a
condensed way as follows. The (scientific) principle of induction says that some laws
and theories can work. Hume’s problem concerns the justification of this principle.
In the following I will argue that Aristotle’s work provides all the insights required
to show that the problem is, in fact, a pseudo-problem. I show how this can be done
in Sect. 2.4, after having recalled Aristotle’s notion of ‘first principle’ in Sect. 2.3. In
Sect. 2.5 I compare my analysis to the one that might come closest, due to Strawson
(1952), and conclude. In Appendix 2.1 I succinctly assess the capacity of an LLM,
DeepSeek (Guo et al., 2025; Liu et al., 2024), to answer a non-trivial question about
the problem of induction. I asked it whether Popper or Quine solved it, and estimate
that it gave an adequate consensual answer.
1 Note that the definitions of the problem of scientific induction in terms of laws or of theories are

largely equivalent. If general laws would not exist, natural science theories could not exist, at least
not as we know them; and laws are embedded in theories. Note also that few people would contest
that well-established theories do predictive work. Electric circuit theory can predict what a current
in a given resistance in a given circuit will be; thermodynamics can predict what the volume of a
given gas in defined conditions will be; etc.

2.3 Aristotle’s Notion of ‘First Principle’

31

2.3 Aristotle’s Notion of ‘First Principle’
The details of Aristotle’s philosophy of science, developed notably in his monumental
Metaphysics, Physics, Posterior Analytics and Nicomachean Ethics, are debated
among experts (e.g. Aydede, 1998; Irwin, 1988; Lesher, 1973). My aim here is
certainly not to present an exegesis of Aristotelian philosophy of science, but just
to point out some of his theses that I need in the following sections. It seems not
difficult to recognize ideas in Aristotle’s philosophy that have stood the test of time.
I refer here to passages as these from Posterior Analytics (Chapter 10, Book Alpha,
transl. Barnes, 1993 p. 15): “I call principles in each kind those items of which it
is not possible to prove that they are. Now what the primitives and what the items
proceeding from them mean is assumed; but that they are must be assumed for the
principles and proved for the rest”. Famous is following passage (Chapter 19, Book
Beta, transl. Barnes, 1993 pp. 72–73; my interpretations between brackets are, as far
as I know, the usual ones):
I have said earlier that you cannot understand anything through a demonstration unless
you know the primitive immediate principles. As for knowledge of the immediates, one
might wonder whether it is the same or not the same [as deductive knowledge], and whether
there is or is no [scientific] understanding in each case, or rather understanding in the one
case and some other kind of knowledge in the other; and also whether the states [first
principles], not being present in us, come about in us or rather are present in us without
being noticed. It is absurd to suppose that we possess such states; for then we should possess
pieces of knowledge more exact than demonstration without its being noticed. But if we
get them without possessing them earlier, how could we come to acquire knowledge and to
learn except from pre-existing knowledge? This is impossible, as I said in connection with
demonstration. It is clear, then, both that we cannot possess these states and also that they
cannot come about in us when we are ignorant and possess no state at all. We must therefore
possess some sort of capacity but not one which will be more valuable than these states [first
principles] in respect of exactness.

The capacity that allows us to intuit first principles is famously called ‘nous’ by
Aristotle (Aydede, 1998; Irwin, 1988; Lesher, 1973).
Combining ideas from his Metaphysics, Physics and Posterior Analytics, and interpreting them perhaps slightly charitably, it seems well possible to extract conclusions
from Aristotle’s philosophy of science that are nowadays generally accepted by scientists and philosophers alike. I would summarise these ideas as follows. Any science
is based on ‘first principles’; the degree of certainty or accuracy or ‘truth’ of these
principles depends on the science in question; and the certainty or truth of a scientific
discipline as a whole follows from the certainty or truth of the first principles of its
foundational theories. A theory cannot be truer than the first principles on which
it rests, one could say using the modern word of theory. In the course of history
scientists have named the first principles of their favourite theories ‘hypotheses’,
‘postulates’, ‘premises’, or ‘axioms’—all names for Aristotle’s first principles. Aristotle also makes the distinction between the degree of certainty that can exist within
mathematics and logic, and within the natural sciences (e.g. Shields, 2023, §4). While
the propositions that are correctly derived from the axioms of, say, Euclidian geometry, count as absolutely (mathematically) certain, the explanations or predictions

32

2 The Problem of Induction Unmasked

following from the founding principles of any theory of natural science count as
more or less adequate, more or less likely,2 but in any case as fallible, defeasible,
improvable—to use again modern terms.
Presumably, few scientists or philosophers of science would disagree with these
well-known theses. In a slogan, the history of the natural sciences is the history of
theories that have been considered, in some epoch and by some people, as adequately
/ truly describing elements of the universe, but that appeared to be in need of revision
and improvement at a later time. The above insights, initiated by Aristotle’s work,
are all I need to make my point.

2.4 Dissolution of the Problem
Should philosophy of science and science be considered to be on a par as to their
epistemic precision and degree of certainty? Presumably most philosophers would
see the difference between these knowledge fields as one of degree rather than of
principle; that is the position defended here, resonating with the synthetic approach to
philosophy and the MCT-thesis (cf. Chapter 1). At any rate, there is no known reason
why philosophy of science should escape from the fate described by Aristotle, a
fate that governs any enterprise that aims at acquiring sound knowledge: that is, any
philosophy of science necessarily has to adopt some starting assumptions, definitions,
hypotheses, principles, axioms. In short: one cannot start from nothing. As repeatedly
pointed out by Aristotle, first principles are unprovable in the logical sense––i.e., they
cannot be deduced from other, more fundamental, principles. But they can be strongly
corroborated a posteriori by very many empirical observations (or even by theoretical
findings) that are predicted or explained by these principles or by the theory based
on these principles. (I use ‘corroboration’ here in Popper’s sense.)
I believe that the conclusion imposes itself by now. Let me first go over the
essence of the argument, and then elaborate a little. There is arguably no principle
that suggests itself more forcefully as a candidate for a first principle of philosophy of science as the principle of induction—the assumption that laws of nature
exist, and that, therefore, some theories can work. Indeed, this principle counts, for
obvious reasons, among the most strongly corroborated principles of the whole scientific enterprise, or, more precisely, of philosophy of science. It is a principle that is
corroborated by all working scientific theories; other principles are corroborated by
specific theories. Hence, it may well be the best-corroborated principle of philosophy of science. The better a principle is corroborated, the surer we can be it is a first
principle of the theory in question (and indeed we can be pragmatic: the principle
of induction has never been derived from more fundamental principles). And finally,
2 In the case of well-confirmed theories, say thermodynamics, the likelihood that predictions

(correctly derived from the postulates) are true, i.e. correspond to reality, can be considered as
‘provisional quasi-certainty’. Only if in the future a more precise theory would be developed, more
precise predictions would be possible.

2.5 Conclusion

33

as pointed out by Aristotle, there is no sense in asking for a justification of a first
principle. QED.
The basic postulates of specific theories, take quantum mechanics, can be corroborated by vast amounts of empirical observations and reach the status of ‘building
block of physics’ (as in the case of quantum mechanics nowadays). But the principle
of induction has been corroborated by all the established theories of all of natural
science: countless predictions of these theories have been proven right, theories can
work—and can work even with an astounding precision, as for instance quantum
mechanics. From this point of view, one might rightly call the principle of induction
‘the law of laws’. In that respect, it is even less warranted to ask of induction whether
it can be justified than of any postulate of a natural science that works.
We might look at the problem from an intimately related angle, by drawing
following comparison. Much as it makes little sense for elementary particle physics
to question its basic assumption that elementary particles exist, it makes little sense
for philosophy of natural science to ask whether natural sciences, and hence the principle of induction, exist. If philosophy of (natural) science exists as a discipline, as a
meta-theory of (natural science) theories, then such theories necessarily must exist;
hence the principle of induction (as I defined it) must exist, must be valid: if general
laws would not exist, natural sciences could not exist, at least not as we know them.
From this point of view, rejecting the principle of induction amounts to rejecting
the possibility of philosophy of science: we enter here in the realm of self-defeating
claims.
Sure, particle physics might eventually be replaced by another theory, say string
theory (although such a theory would not make the notion of particle redundant).
So the present-day laws of particle physics, the so-called standard model of particle
physics, could one day be shown to be derivable (as approximations) from more
fundamental laws of another theory. But what would replace philosophy of science?
Synthetic philosophy takes the (established) results of past philosophy seriously:
no-one has formulated a theory from which the law of induction, the law of laws,
could be derived. As long as one has no principle (part of a working, maximally
coherent theory) from which it can be deduced, Aristotle’s stipulation is clear: it
must be considered a first principle.

2.5 Conclusion
I am not aware of other authors solving or rather dissolving Hume’s problem by
referring to Aristotle’s now accepted notion of first principle; but it is improbable
that no at least somewhat similar analyses exist. The analysis I am aware of that
comes closest to mine is due to P. Strawson (1952, p. 257). In a nutshell, Strawson
argues that, just as it is odd to inquire about the legality of the law, it makes little sense
to ask whether induction is justified. Just as the law is the standard against which
the legality of actions is judged, the principle of induction is one of the standards we
use to decide whether claims about the universe are justified (for a discussion of this

34

2 The Problem of Induction Unmasked

argument as phrased here, see Okasha, 2002, p. 28 and Henderson, 2020 § 5.3). But
I feel this argument can be made considerably sharper, as proposed above.
Perhaps one explanation of why our analysis has not emerged earlier, is that people
might use a too stringent conception of the principle of induction. The principle of
induction, if well understood, definitely—and obviously—does not say that induction
always works, that all laws always perfectly work. As I emphasized from the start,
my analysis hinges on a correct understanding of (scientific) induction, as stating
that some laws can work, can have (approximate) predictive or explanatory power.
If not, natural science would be a void enterprise.
Let us give the last word to Aristotle. In his Nicomachean Ethics he concludes
(Chapter 3, Book 1, transl. Crisp, 2004 p. 5): “it is a mark of an educated person to
look in each area for only that degree of accuracy [I said: certainty] that the nature
of the subject permits”. I suggest that a similar attitude is recommendable regarding
the problem of induction. From all we know about how science works, and from all
we can learn from philosophy, induction must be assumed as a first principle. And
already Aristotle told us that from first principles we cannot ask a justification in the
sense of deductive proof . But principles can be justified by corroboration—by all the
verified instances that comply with them. In this sense, the principle of induction is,
or counts among, the best justified principle(s) of philosophy.

Appendix 2.1. DeepSeek’s View on Popper’s and Quine’s
‘Solutions’
Here I briefly assess the capacity of an LLM to answer a question about the problem
of induction. I asked DeepSeek-V3 (Guo et al., 2025) what the problem of induction
is, and whether Quine or Popper solved it. Note that answering this question does not
require complex multi-step reasoning (neither a detailed exegesis of the literal words
of the authors): synthesising from published texts suffices, which is what LLMs
should excel at. Figure 2.1 shows a transcription of the dialogue. To the best of my
knowledge, and based on the way in which such language models function (Guo
et al., 2025; Liu et al., 2024), I estimate that DeepSeek gave a satisfying ‘common
denominator’ answer—with an enthusiastic touch. Even if I did not do a systematic
study, another language model, ChatGPT (also in its free version), gave essentially
the same answer qua content. This is coherent with the fact that both are statistical
text-completion engines trained with comparable bodies of text. For what it is worth,
if students would give me this answer in a test without using AI, I would give them
a 10/10. (This is not to say that the answer is the best expert’s answer; the final
assessment can only be done by the best expert, presumably still human. But, then,
the notion of ‘best expert’ is somewhat ethereal.)

Appendix 2.1. DeepSeek’s View on Popper’s and Quine’s ‘Solutions’

35

Question>: What is the problem of induction? Did Popper solve it? Did Quine solve it?
DeepSeek>:
Of course. This is a fantastic and fundamental question in the philosophy of science. Let's break it down.
1. What is the Problem of Induction?
The problem of induction, most famously articulated by the 18th-century Scottish philosopher David
Hume, is the philosophical question of whether inductive reasoning (reasoning from specific
observations to general principles) can be rationally justified.
* What is Induction? It's the process of making generalizations based on observed patterns.
o Example: You observe that every single swan you have ever seen (thousands of them, across
different countries) is white. You therefore conclude (inductively infer) that "All swans are
white."
* Hume's Problem: Hume pointed out that such reasoning relies on a crucial, unproven assumption: that
the future will resemble the past (or that nature is uniform). We assume that the patterns we've observed
will continue to hold.
* Why is this a Problem? Hume argued that any attempt to justify this assumption is circular.
1. You could try to justify it inductively: "The future has always resembled the past, so it will
continue to do so." But this is itself an inductive argument, assuming the very thing it's trying to prove
(that past patterns will continue).
2. You could try to justify it deductively (through pure logic/reason): But there is no logical
contradiction in the idea that the laws of nature could change tomorrow. It's logically possible that all
swans you see from now on could be blue.
Therefore, induction is not a logically valid form of reasoning. We use it constantly and it's essential for
science and daily life, but we have no ultimate, non-circular rational foundation for it. It's a matter of
psychological habit, not logical proof.
2. Did Karl Popper "Solve" It?
Popper's Answer: Yes, but not by justifying induction. By eliminating the need for it altogether.
Popper agreed with Hume that induction is unjustifiable. His famous response was to argue that science
does not actually use induction.
* Falsificationism: Popper claimed that scientists don't start with observations and generalize them into
laws (induction). Instead, they start with bold, imaginative conjectures (theories and hypotheses). They
then subject these conjectures to rigorous tests, attempting to falsify them.
* The Key: A single counter-example (e.g., finding one black swan in Australia) can logically falsify a
universal theory ("all swans are white"). This is a deductive process (modus tollens: If P then Q; not-Q;
therefore not-P).
* Did He Solve It? In a very specific sense, Popper "solved" it for his own description of the scientific
method. He replaced the problem of justifying induction with a deductive process of conjecture and
refutation.
* The Criticism: Most philosophers think Popper's solution is incomplete. The biggest problem is that
it ignores the role of induction in the application of science. We accept a well-tested theory not just
because it hasn't been falsified yet, but because we inductively believe it will hold in the future. When
an engineer builds a bridge using principles of physics, they are making an inductive bet that those
principles will continue to hold. Popper's system doesn't account for why we prefer one non-falsified
theory over another, which seems to rely on their past empirical success—a form of induction.

Fig. 2.1 Dialogue with DeepSeek-V3 (in September 2025) about Popper’s and Quine’s ‘solution’
to the problem of induction. The dialogue is literally transcribed

36

2 The Problem of Induction Unmasked

Verdict: Popper didn't solve the logical problem of induction; he attempted to dissolve it by arguing that
good science doesn't need it. This is a brilliant and influential move, but most consider it an elegant
evasion rather than a full solution.
3. Did W.V.O. Quine "Solve" It?
Quine's Answer: Not by "solving" it in the traditional sense, but by radically redefining the problem and
showing why it's the wrong question to ask.
Quine, a 20th-century American philosopher, took a completely different approach, grounded in his
naturalism (the view that philosophy is not a privileged "first science" but continuous with empirical
science).
* A Naturalized Epistemology: Quine argued that the traditional project of trying to rationally justify
science from scratch, from a foundation outside of science, is a hopeless failure (as Hume showed).
Instead, we should treat epistemology (the theory of knowledge) as a branch of psychology. We should
study how we, as physical creatures in a physical world, actually develop successful theories about that
world.
* Induction as a Natural Fact: For Quine, induction isn't a flawed logical rule to be justified; it is a
natural phenomenon. It's a habit hardwired into us by natural selection. Animals that successfully
inductively predicted that a certain rustle in the grass meant a predator survived and reproduced. We are
the descendants of successful inducters.
* The Problem is Misdirected: The question is not "Is induction justified?" but rather "How do we, as a
matter of fact, use induction to successfully navigate and predict the world?" This becomes a question
for psychology, neuroscience, and evolutionary biology, not for a priori logic.
* Pragmatic Justification: The "justification" for induction is pragmatic: it works. It has allowed us to
build successful scientific theories that make accurate predictions. To demand a further, transcendental
justification is meaningless.
Verdict: Quine did not "solve" the problem in the way Hume framed it. Instead, he argued that Hume's
framing is a dead end. He dissolved the problem by naturalizing it, moving it from the realm of pure
logic to the realm of empirical science. For Quine, the continued success of science is the only
"justification" we need or can have.

Conclusion: The problem of induction remains a central and unsolved problem in philosophy in the
traditional sense. No one has provided a non-circular logical justification for it. However, the responses
from Popper and Quine have been immensely influential in changing how we think about the problem,
shifting the focus from justifying induction to understanding how science and human cognition actually
work.
