Chapter 3

Solving Gettier’s Problem.
Knowledge, Truth and Justification are
Relative to Theories*

Abstract In this chapter I show that one of the paramount problems of epistemology,
Gettier’s problem, is solved if one assumes that knowledge, truth and justification are
relative to representations, theories, or, if one prefers, to coherent sets of hypotheses.
This idea comes from practice first: in many somewhat complex cognitive contexts,
people use the words ‘know’, ‘true’ and ‘justified’ in exactly that way: by (explicitly
or implicitly) referring to some set of hypotheses or theory. Phrased slightly more
precisely: these persons’ conversations, when they use these words, can be made fully
coherent if one assumes that there is an (implicit) reference to a theory (understood
in a general sense, denoted by ‘theory*’). Notice how well this claim agrees with the
MCT-principle, implying, among other things, that coherent answers to non-trivial
questions—knowledge—are given only by whole theories. I show that this relativistic
account strongly resonates with Quine’s acclaimed holism, and solves other wellknown problems of epistemology. If we combine the results of this chapter with
other theses of this book, and acknowledge that it has proven difficult to find other
solutions to Gettier’s problem, we arrive at the conclusion that knowledge, truth and
justification must be relative to theories in a fundamental sense. Thus, representationrelativity is the core principle of my epistemology. All in all, the results obtained
in this chapter lend credit to a type of moderate epistemic relativism, call it ‘theory
relativism’ (not, of course, in contradiction with realism), in which the notion of
‘absolute truth’ can be recovered as a natural limit case.

3.1 Introduction for Neophytes
When do you know something? How can one analyse the concept of knowledge; what
does it mean ‘to know’? In what sense is knowledge related to truth—can one know
things that are not true? The most celebrated analysis of ‘propositional knowledge’,
of which the basic ideas are due to Plato, interprets knowledge as ‘justified true
belief’. More precisely, according to this analysis, a person, S, ‘knows that p’, where
‘p’ is any proposition, iff the following three conditions are satisfied:
(i) S believes that p;

© The Author(s) 2026
L. Vervoort, Problem Solving in Philosophy,
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-17756-8_3

39

40

3 Solving Gettier’s Problem. Knowledge, Truth and Justification are …

(ii) p is true;
(iii) S is justified in believing that p.
In one of the most cited articles of contemporary philosophy, E. Gettier is widely
believed to have shown that the above analysis (let us call it Plato’s analysis) is
not always adequate (Gettier, 1963). Gettier claims to exhibit cases in which the
conditions (i)–(iii) are satisfied, while subject S does not know that p. The most
famous Gettier case goes as follows. Suppose Smith and Jones are both interested in
the same job, and suppose that Smith has good reasons to believe that Jones will get
it—for instance, because the recruiter told him so. Suppose also that Smith knows
that Jones has exactly ten coins in his pocket (he has inspected Jones’s pockets).
Suppose further that, unbeknownst to Smith, he himself also has ten coins in his
pocket. And finally suppose that, to everyone’s surprise, the recruiter changes her
mind, and appoints Smith for the job. If we now identify S = Smith and the statement
p = ‘the person who gets the job has ten coins in his pocket’, then we see that all
conditions (i)–(iii) above can be true. And yet S does not really know that p; Smith
only knows that p by sheer luck. In sum, real knowledge seems to be something
more than ‘justified true belief’. At least, that is the conclusion that Gettier and
philosophers in general have drawn from Gettier’s analysis.
Readers who would like to elucidate Gettier’s counterexample should pause now,
and think independently for a moment. What is happening here? How does the
counterexample arise? (A hint: are all concepts of Plato’s analysis used in exactly
the same sense throughout the counterexample? Analytic philosophy often starts
with careful analysis of concepts.)

3.2 Introduction for Professionals: Relativism Versus
Absolutism
The debate between relativism and absolutism is vivid in several fields of philosophy, such as philosophy of language, ethics, and epistemology (for reviews, cf.
Baghramian & Carter, 2020; Boghossian, 2006; Harré & Krausz, 1996). Relativism
comes in many flavours even within each field, but a common feature of all relativistic models is that they consider their central object of interest (be it concepts,
propositions, ethical values, truth, knowledge, or even reality) to be relative to something else—to some referent, or parameter. Following Harré and Krausz (1996),
Boghossian (2006), and Baghramian and Carter (2020), perhaps the most general
types of referents are ‘conceptual frameworks’ or ‘evidential systems’. But there
seems to be a consensus that this general relativizing parameter is determined by
something else, and that one gains in narrowing the ‘conceptual framework’ down
to ‘language’, ‘culture’, ‘historical epoch’, ‘time’ etc. In modern epistemology, a
popular type of relativism is contextualism, so called because it takes the essential
referent (for knowledge, or rather knowledge attributions) to be a ‘conversational

3.2 Introduction for Professionals: Relativism Versus Absolutism

41

context’ (cf. e.g. Cohen, 1998, 1999; DeRose, 1999, 2009; Lewis, 1996; Rysiew,
2016).
One goal of the present chapter is to take one step back, and to argue that, for
solving key epistemological problems, one gains in keeping as referent ‘conceptual
frameworks’, rather than contexts. Now, instead of ‘conceptual framework’, I will
argue that the better notion is ‘(coherent) body of information’ or ‘(coherent) set
of hypotheses’ or ‘theory’; note that there is a semantic continuity between these
concepts. ‘Set of hypotheses’ and ‘theory’ are the more precise notions, at least in a
scientific or philosophical context, since they are familiar from philosophy of science;
some may find ‘body of information’ more appropriate in everyday contexts, as will
become clear later. As mentioned in Chapter 1, I will gather all these related notions
under the concept of ‘theory*’ and often refer to them by the symbol ‘T*’.
The origin of these ideas lies in the simple observation that in scientific discussions
relativising to hypotheses and especially theories is an everyday, even inescapable
practice. It is therefore a priori worthwhile to investigate whether a similar practice
is instrumental in epistemology; especially, whether it solves problems. To start, I
will study this practice from a straightforward linguistic angle. Actually I could,
for my initial purpose of elucidating Gettier’s problem, remain essentially on a
pragmatic-linguistic level. But linguistic habits may have deep-digging epistemological grounds, and by applying this linguistic practice to Gettier’s problem I will bring
the discussion inevitably on the grounds of epistemology and its core assumptions.
Indeed, my interpretation of Gettier’s problem points to a form of epistemic relativism that connects with a wide variety of positions in epistemology and philosophy
of science—and with the heuristic principle of Chapter 1. I will make a comparison with Quine’s holism (1951, 1960, 1991), and succinctly with contextualism and
with scientific perspectivism; the latter is a position in philosophy of science (Giere,
2006 and Massimi, 2012, 2018, 2021). The ultimate a posteriori justification of the
proposed solution to Gettier’s problem is that it is in full coherence with other key
findings of this book.
In an article entitled ‘Realism and Relativism’, Field (1982) proposes a research
program that comes close to what I have in mind: he takes as relativising parameter
an ‘evidential system’. Field defines epistemological relativism as follows (p. 563):
I understand it as the doctrine that the basic epistemological properties are not such properties
as that of belief B being justified, but rather such relativized properties as that of belief B
being justified relative to evidential system E. (An evidential system is, roughly, a bunch
of rules for determining under what conditions one is to believe various things; a belief is
justified relative to an evidential system in certain circumstances if the rules license the belief
under those circumstances.) To make relativism a believable doctrine, we have to show that
all legitimate epistemological reasoning can be reconstructed with such relativized notions
and without the absolute or unrelativized ones.

I will argue in this chapter that the basic notions of epistemology, namely truth,
knowledge and justification, should be considered as being relative to theories*, in
a well-defined sense to be spelled out below. In the narrow sense ‘theory’ should

42

3 Solving Gettier’s Problem. Knowledge, Truth and Justification are …

be understood in its usual meaning in philosophy of science1 ; so not exactly in the
sense of ‘evidential system’ as Field defines it—but there seems to be room for
overlap between the two views. Many authors have defended variants of relativistic
epistemology (for a recent review, see Baghramian & Carter, 2020, notably § 1.3.
on the ‘hidden parameter’ view of relativism, perhaps closest to mine). But, as far as
I know, none of them has claimed that the concept of ‘theory’ or its generalisation
‘theory*’ should be put at the heart of the matter.
It seems a good idea to emphasise from the start that the results of this chapter,
combined with those in other chapters, support a moderate epistemic relativism—
call it ‘theory relativism’—moderate, because well compatible with realism. Indeed,
as many authors (e.g. Field, 1982; Harré & Krausz, 1996) have shown, relativism
of the type I have in mind is of course not contradictory to realism. We need to
adopt (scientific) realism if we wish to extend the web of knowledge in the most
risk-free way starting from natural science, as argued in Chapter 1. Thus, relativity
of propositional truth has very little or nothing to do with extreme types of relativism
as subjectivism—the position that all opinions are valid—neither with the constructivism aptly rebutted by Boghossian (2006). My account is compatible with the idea
that some theories describe a mind-independent reality better than others; moreover,
with reference to a given theory, it is of course possible to distinguish true propositions from false ones.2 Concomitantly, I will show that the concept of ‘absolute’ or
‘objective’ truth can be recovered as a natural limit case of my account.
The chapter is organized as follows. I will start with a study of a language practice that is especially popular among scientists (Sect. 3.3), but that is also sometimes
needed in everyday contexts (Sect. 3.4), and that amounts to embedding propositions,
more precisely an expression as ‘p is true’, in theories. The efficacy of this practice
will incite me to adopt (Sect. 3.3) a mixed coherence-correspondence view on truth,
i.e. a basic epistemological position, which will fully be justified only in the subsequent sections. In Sect. 3.5 I will apply the mentioned relativistic language practice
to Gettier’s problem (1963) and propose a solution. I will show that this analysis
refines a compelling result due to Zagzebski (1994) (Sect. 3.6). Finally, I will further
solidify my account by arguing that it overlaps with Quine’s epistemological holism
(1951, 1960, 1991) and solves problems of contextualism (Sect. 3.6).

1 For my purposes it suffices to assume, following a long tradition in philosophy of science (Hempel

1965; Popper 1959), that theories are hypothetical-deductive systems of propositions closed under
deduction – one could add that they are based on a (small) number of base hypotheses, laws,
principles, axioms. Note that at the most basic level theories can be considered as (coherent) sets
of hypotheses – this follows from the definition I just gave –, so ‘set of hypotheses’ is likely
the most general interpretation of ‘conceptual framework’. Or should one say ‘explanatory set of
hypotheses’?
2 My position is also at odds with the relativism of Kuhn and his ‘incommensurability’ of rival
scientific paradigms (I will have more to say about rival theories below).

3.3 How the Words ‘True’, ‘Know’ and ‘Justified’ Are Used in Natural …

43

3.3 How the Words ‘True’, ‘Know’ and ‘Justified’ Are
Used in Natural Science. On a Mixed
Coherence—Correspondence Theory of Truth
Let us start with having a look at a conversational habit that is most widespread in
natural science, physics in particular.3 Suppose that a physicist measures the speed of
a fast object by using optical techniques, and finds x meters per second, or rather x ±
Δx, where Δx is the measurement precision; x is measured with respect to an agreedupon reference frame. Suppose that other physicists confirm the measurement, and
that all agree that the speed of the object during measurement was x ± Δx (expressed
in m/s, and relative to the frame). Is it ‘true’ that the speed of the object was x ±
Δx? If we would ask the physicists, insisting that we indeed are inquiring about
the ‘absolute’ truth of the measurement value, the question might spark hesitation.
One of them might remember, for instance, that the measurement technique neglects
relativistic effects; that spurious phenomena (e.g. due to unstable atmospheric conditions) perturbing the optical measurement can never be fully excluded; and (more
speculatively) that effects due to the moving of the reference frame with respect to
the sun, the gravitational centre of our galaxy etc. are perhaps an issue. I believe
that after some reflection they would agree on an answer as the following: “within
our hypotheses (by applying a non-relativistic model calculation on the experimental
data, and as far as our measurement assumptions are valid), it is indeed true that the
speed of the satellite was x ± Δx.” That is a different answer than “it is absolutely
true that the speed was x ± Δx”.
A more realistic example is given by electron microscopy, which can, under
certain stringent conditions, provide images of what seem to be individual atoms4
(e.g. Eigler & Schweizer, 1990). Suppose that a sample reveals under the microscope
that an atom A is located at a distance of (about) three nanometres from a distinguishing point P—say another atom—on the substrate B. Typically, distances are
estimated by counting periods of the crystal lattice of the substrate. If there are three
periods between A and P, each period having a width of one nanometre—as known
for instance from X-ray diffraction experiments –, the distance A-P is three nanometres. Is the empirical statement ‘the distance A-P is three nanometres’ absolutely true
(within some specified range of precision Δx)? It is well-known that such an estimation hinges on several assumptions, in fact full-blown theories. The atomic images
are of course not obtained by usual vision, but by using a sophisticated technique
that is based on (that assumes the correctness of) sophisticated physical, e.g. electrooptical, and mathematical theories, Fourier transform theory notably (Williams &
3 The linguistic analysis of this section is rather detailed, following the standards of analytic philos-

ophy. Readers, especially if accepting the main idea of this section, might go quickly over the
examples and the linguistic analysis. However, the mixed coherence-correspondence theory of
truth in this section is important for the whole book.
4 One condition is that the atoms, of element A, are deposited on a quasi-perfect and ultra-clean
crystalline surface of another chemical substance, B. It is impossible to use electron microscopy to
visualise free-floating atoms.

44

3 Solving Gettier’s Problem. Knowledge, Truth and Justification are …

Carter, 2009). The atomic image on the microscope’s screen is a very indirect reconstruction of something that is assumed to be related to the atom (a cloud of electrons
associated with it). This example illustrates the theory-ladenness of observation: even
observation sentences depend on the truth of a set of assumptions.
In many fields of physics and science professionals doubtlessly find it quite
clear which theory describes a given phenomenon best. For instance, for mechanical phenomena such as the flight of a satellite, it is generally agreed that relativity
theory does a better job than classical Newtonian mechanics (even if the difference
in the predicted velocities may be small). But at the forefront of scientific research,
it happens often that it is not yet decided which theory is most efficient: competing
frameworks are proposed to explain the same data. For instance, for the phenomenon
of superconductivity, i.e. the disappearance of all electric resistivity in metallic materials at low enough temperatures, several competing theories exist, of which it is not
yet clear which one will prove most adequate—if any. Suppose now that two theories
allow one to calculate a same variable, for instance the superconducting transition
temperature for a given material, ts . Theory 1 predicts ts = x1 (expressed in degrees
Celsius), while theory 2 implies that ts = x2 ; suppose that x1 x2 . Finally, suppose
that ts has not yet been measured, at least not with enough precision to discriminate
between the two different numerical values. It is clear, then, that in certain contexts
it is ambiguous to state that “it is true that t s = x 1 ”—as ambiguous as it is to say that
“it is true that ts = x2 ”. No-one really knows. Both values are inferred from theories
that experts consider as valuable but competing potential candidates for explaining
superconductivity. What could be said with less ambiguity in this context is a phrase
like: “within theory 1 (i.e., while assuming the validity of theory 1), it is true that ts
= x1 ”.
Some further examples: “Within the ideal gas model, molecular collisions are
elastic”. Or: “The measured chemical equilibrium constant is K, assumed that one
can neglect temperature effects”. Notice that if we would take the authors of above
written sentences aside and ask them whether “p is true”, they should reply for full
clarity something like: “p is true within theory T” or “p is true within hypotheses T”.
Within another theory T† , p might not be true at all.
In principle, the only point we need for our subsequent discussion, is that it is a
frequent language practice in the scientific community to state that “p is true within
T”, instead of “p is true” simpliciter. I will term this a perspectival (or relativistic)
language practice for using the word ‘true’ as qualifying a proposition ‘p’. According
to this usage, one adds information to the word ‘true’ by specifying a perspective
(hypotheses T) within which p is supposed to be true, or which is at least compatible
with p. This perspectival practice is needed for removing ambiguity from speech, as
is illustrated in above examples. I will abbreviate ‘p is true within T’ as ‘p is true_
T’.
Now, even if I could remain on the linguistic-pragmatic level for my purpose
of elucidating Gettier’s problem, the language practice just illustrated resonates
strongly, not only with the heuristic-epistemological rule of Chapter 1, but also with
well-known debates about theories of truth (and of knowledge, cf. Sect. 3.6). Indeed,
this practice points to the idea that there are two aspects to the truth of a proposition

3.3 How the Words ‘True’, ‘Know’ and ‘Justified’ Are Used in Natural …

45

p, a coherence and a correspondence aspect (e.g. Kirkham, 1992, Ch. 2). These are
the two angles from which theories of truth are usually discussed. This double nature
of truth is at least pertinent in science; but since I have assumed from the start that
philosophy and science are in continuity, we should seriously consider the possibility that it also holds for philosophy, i.e., in general. The first, coherence, aspect
concerns the truth of p as logical consistency of p within a theory T. So this aspect
asks whether p is logically consistent with T, specifically, whether p can be logically
deduced5 from T. The second (correspondence) aspect is the ‘truth’ or adequacy or
verisimilitude of T (as a whole) for describing aspects of reality, pointed to by p.
In sum, the highlighted perspectival parlance is coherent with a mixed coherence—
correspondence theory of truth. I will argue in Sect. 3.6 that this account strongly
resonates with the essential ingredient of Quine’s holistic epistemology.
Note that the scheme of this truth account, as it applies to theories T in the
narrow sense, relevant for science, can formally easily be generalised to general
sets of hypotheses or sets of information T*, the perspectives relevant for mundane
observation sentences of daily life. It suffices to generalise ‘logical deduction’ to
‘coherent inference’:
p is true_T* (i.e., according to T*) iff p can coherently be inferred from T*.
Whereas in physics theoretical propositions can usually mathematically be deduced
from a theory, mundane observation sentences do not necessarily follow from some
T* by strict deduction; coherent inference, as in abduction for instance, has a broader
meaning. To give an example, take p = ‘it rains here and now’, and T* = some body
of information to which I have access, i.e. some (sentences describing) sense-data
and some (rather trivial) hypotheses (such as that no-one is sprinkling water from the
roof and that I am not otherwise deceived). Thus, on my relativistic account, I can
conclude that it really rains here and now iff this statement can be coherently inferred
from some sense-data and some hypotheses, and if these data and these hypotheses
have verisimilitude (correspond to reality). It is not important to detail this mixed
truth account further; we can well remain on a somewhat coarse level for the time
being.
Sure, especially in mundane contexts this seems a complex logical construal of
propositional truth. But I will nevertheless adopt this mixed coherence—correspondence theory of truth as general. The real justification will come later: this account
resonates best with my solution of Gettier’s problem and with a variety of other problems and theories discussed in this book, starting with those dealt with in Sect. 3.6.
In sum, a rather innocuous language habit in science points to a fundamental epistemological result, namely an account of the concept of propositional truth. (We will
see in a moment how one can construe the notion ‘really true’ in the above scheme.)
Going back to science, it thus appears that one can make a good case for the idea
that scientists consider a proposition p as being ‘true’ if p is logically consistent
5 Or ‘rationally/coherently/probabilistically inferred’, which is a less strict notion. Here the ‘logical

deduction’ is (of course) the same as for instance at work in Quine’s holism (cf. Section 6). In his
review article of (1991, p. 268) Quine says: “I give logic a special status: logical implication is the
link between theory and experiment”.

46

3 Solving Gettier’s Problem. Knowledge, Truth and Justification are …

with a theory T, and if T (as a whole) is believed to be an adequate description of (a
part of) reality. Note that this claim holds not only for theoretical propositions, but
also for observation sentences, due to the theory-ladennes of observation. Clearly,
within scientific realism, I assume that there is a degree of adequacy or verisimilitude
associated with theories T (and by extension with bodies of information T*). The
efficacy of theories varies from theory to theory.
It appears that a similar perspectival language practice exists in discussions among
scientists when they use the words ‘know’ and ‘justified’. Once again, contexts in
which several competing theories co-exist are most relevant. Suppose that ‘pc ’ is a
proposition from classical mechanics, such as “the kinetic energy of this object is
E”. Also suppose that a calculation using the relevant formula of classical mechanics
indeed allows to infer that the kinetic energy of the object in question is E—in
other words: pc is true within classical mechanics. First note that, in general, pc
is not true within relativistic mechanics: the latter theory will in general predict a
different energy E* for the object in question. Relativistic and classical mechanics
are ‘co-existing’ theories: some physicists use on a daily basis the relativistic variant,
while others (the majority) use the classical one, since the latter is often an excellent
approximation of the former. Now, one can easily imagine contexts in which it is not
a priori clear which theory is used or referred to. Suppose, then, that in such a context
the two physicists Alice and Bob are discussing the calculations of their colleague
Cecil, who just submitted to Bob the result ‘pc ’ (“the kinetic energy of this object
is E”). If Bob would ask Alice “Is it true that pc ?”, Alice might find this question
ambiguous: all depends on whether one refers to classical or relativistic mechanics.
Luckily she can bring some clarity into the debate and answer in an unambiguous
manner: “pc is true within classical mechanics” (she would be right to top). If Bob
would ask “Am I justified to believe that pc ?”, Alice, decided to keep the debate as
clear as possible, could answer: “You are justified to believe that pc if you assume (the
validity of) classical mechanics”, or shorter “You are justified to believe pc within
classical mechanics”. And finally, to Bob’s question “Does Cecil know that pc is
true?”, Alice would have to answer something like: “Cecil knows that pc is true if
one assumes the validity of classical mechanics”, in short “Cecil knows that pc within
classical mechanics”.
As another example, consider two psychologists, Carl and Anna, discussing
prehistoric cave paintings. One of them, Carl, is a fan of Jung, Anna hardly. Carl
suddenly says: “Archetypes play an essential role in prehistoric art. But would Brian
know that?” The latter question may seem awkward, at least to some (it does to
Anna). Can one really know that Jung’s archetypes play an essential role in prehistoric art? That depends on whether it is true that pa , where pa = “Jung’s archetypes
play an essential role in prehistoric art”. On usual interpretations, one cannot know
a proposition that is false. And whether pa is true is certainly a matter of debate
among the expert anthropologists. In sum, one can only avoid confusion if one says
something like: “Brian knows that pa if one assumes the validity of Jung’s theory”. In
order to be coherent with the language use I highlighted before, one can abbreviate
this expression by “Brian knows that pa within Ta ”. I will again formally rewrite this
as “Brian knows_Ta that pa ”.

3.3 How the Words ‘True’, ‘Know’ and ‘Justified’ Are Used in Natural …

47

Similar remarks hold for ‘justified’ in these contexts. Justified is a notoriously
vague word—how justified is justified? Is one justified to believe that the theoretical
value of the superconductive transition temperature ts = x1 ? That depends on the
theory one adheres to or uses. Proponents of T1 may agree, proponents of T2 may
not. Is one justified to believe that Jung’s archetypes play a role in prehistoric art?
All depends. My point is, again, that there exists a practice, a shorthand expression,
by which the use of the word ‘justified’ can be rendered unambiguous in certain
contexts. As the reader will have understood, this practice is to reference ‘justified’
to a theory, or at any rate to talk about ‘justification within a theory’, rather than justification simpliciter; so to use ‘justified_T’ rather than ‘justified’. An expression as “S
is justified to believe that ts = x1 within T1 ” is in agreement with a common language
practice of science (and philosophy), and avoids above indicated ambiguity. Similarly, one could say that “S is justified, within Jung’s theory, to believe that archetypes
play a role in prehistoric art”. Once more, ‘within’ is shorthand for ‘by assuming the
validity of’, or ‘if one assumes the validity of’ (or equivalent expressions). We write
as before: “S is justified_T to believe that p”.
In conclusion, in certain contexts, notably in contexts where several theoretical
frameworks can co-exist and be referred to, it appears that an ambiguity linked to
the use of the words ‘know’, ‘true’ and ‘justified’ can be dissolved by specifying
a perspective or representation. Actually, the representation-relativity of reasoning
will be a leitmotiv of this book, and a principle that we will encounter in almost
every chapter. One community in which this practice, or if one likes this ‘reasoning
within a theory or model’,6 is particularly widespread is computational science.
This surely is due to the fact that researchers in these areas are used to manipulate
different computational models, and jump from one model to another (within one
discussion, or within one research project) in order to compare the (numerical or
other) results to which these different models lead. By stating the model they use
for a given research effort (expressed in propositions p), they ipso facto indicate
the assumptions or approximations they made to perform their calculations. By the
same token, they can indicate standards of accuracy; different models/theories are
usually associated with different degrees of accuracy. (This remark immediately
suggests a link with contextualism, for which context-dependent epistemic standards
are essential; the comparison is made in Sect. 3.6.) Thus, stating the theory used is the
most economical way to allow other persons to verify these results. Formally, under
the convention introduced, this perspectival practice amounts to replacing ‘true’ by
‘true_T’, ‘know’ by ‘know_T’, and ‘justified’ by ‘justified_T’, where T is a to-bespecified theory, or, more generally, a set of hypotheses. For my immediate purposes,
the only claim we need in the following is that this practice is sometimes useful for
rendering conversations less ambiguous.

6 Or still more precisely, this awareness that expressions as ‘p is true’ are, in certain contexts, in

need of specification of a theory.

48

3 Solving Gettier’s Problem. Knowledge, Truth and Justification are …

3.4 Usage in Daily Conversations and Philosophy
Some readers may still doubt whether the above discussed language practice is
useful for tackling epistemological problems. Note that there are a priori reasons
why this could well be the case. First, it is widely believed that clarifying language
is a key function of analytic philosophy. Next, one of the main goals of this book
is to show that theorising in philosophy and natural science are on a par; hence
general epistemological practices in science have a high likelihood to be relevant for
philosophy.
To start with, there is no obstacle to use ‘true’, ‘know’ and ‘justified’ in a perspectival manner in everyday speech. Consider for instance following case. Ann and Bill
are visiting Cyrus, who is an illusionist and a notorious fan of the latest, most sophisticated holographic imaging techniques. While Ann and Bill are waiting in Cyrus’s
house for him to appear, they nervously discuss whether Cyrus will play a trick on
them. Suddenly they see a small alligator entering the room. Bill shouts: “That’s an
alligator”. Ann whispers: “That’s true if we assume he’s playing no tricks on us”. Or
“That’s true if we can trust our eyes”. Or “That’s true on / within the usual assumptions”. Abbreviated: “That’s true_T*”, with T* = the set of normal assumptions, or
the hypotheses one starts from during normal visits. Other examples are easily found.
Is Kosovo an independent country after its declaration of independence? That is true
if accepting the now predominating ‘international’ viewpoint, not if one adheres to
the position of many Serbians (who some time ago would have been right at any
rate). “It is true within the usual political viewpoint” (or on the usual viewpoint)
is a legitimate expression. Of course, in everyday discussions, the perspective T*
will rarely represent a full-blown theory; people rarely relate their propositions to
scientific theories. T* will rather be a (more or less coherent) body of information,
e.g. provided by common sense, or a set of hypotheses/assumptions.7 One would
think that these hypotheses are rarely explicitly stated, except if this omission entails
ambiguities, or more generally if explicit referencing helps the understanding.
Finally, ‘to know’ and ‘justified’ can also be relativised in the same way in daily
contexts—as will presumably be clear by now. (The examples in this paragraph are
somewhat repetitive and meant for sceptical readers.) Consider the sentence “Aldo
knows that p”, with p = “Aldo’s horse Pegasus will win in the races next Sunday”.
Can one know that p? Common mortals surely cannot. But Aldo may know, for
instance if he has a long tradition of administering judiciously chosen injections
to Pegasus’s competitors before the races. Within the information set he has access
to, he may well know that p. Am I today justified to believe that it will rain next
Wednesday? I am, if assuming the validity of the latest meteorological predictions.
Abbreviated, “I am justified_T* to believe it will rain next Wednesday”, with T* =
7 ‘Coherence’ again refers here to logical consistency within the body of information, but also to

‘external’ coherence with generally accepted information. This type of coherence is also demanded
of scientific theories, be it in a more logically stringent guise. From this point of view, the concept
of ‘coherent body of information’ could be seen as a mundane version of ‘theory’; at least, there is
a semantic continuity between the concepts.

3.5 Application to Gettier’s Problem

49

the latest meteorological information. Notice that it is well conceivable that I was
justified_T† to believe it will not rain next Wednesday, with T† = the meteorological
information that was at hand yesterday.8 Thus, this approach allows to elucidate a
conundrum, debated in the literature, that can succinctly be formulated as follows:
subject S1 may be justified_T1 to believe that p, while another subject S2 is justified_
T2 to not believe that same p. In sum, a certain type of perspectival language may
be helpful in daily conversations too.
We have now set the linguistic stage to make the step to philosophy, in particular Gettier’s problem. Linguistic studies as entrance for epistemological investigations are not abundant, but exist. Ludlow (2005) presents arguments in favour of
such a linguistic support for more fundamental interpretations, building on work of
e.g. Vendler (1967) and even Wittgenstein. In particular, Ludlow provides linguistic
evidence showing that ‘knows’ has an “implicit argument position for standards of
knowledge” (a phrasing that is reminiscent of authors in the contextualist tradition).
Along similar lines, Bach (2005) mentions an indexed or relativised form of using
‘know’, which he represents as: “S knowsD that p”, where D is again a standard
of knowledge (what these standards precisely are is not further detailed). The latter
formal representation comes close to my interpretation above, according to which D
is best interpreted, at least in certain contexts, as a theory or set of hypotheses T. By
specifying a theory T one also specifies epistemic standards, notably the accuracy of
the observational propositions one can infer from T.9 In sum, the studies by Ludlow
(2005) and Bach (2005) are well resonating with my view on the use of ‘know’. A
difference is that in my account ‘know’, ‘true’ and ‘justified’ are treated on the same
footing: this allows us to analyse Gettier’s problem.

3.5 Application to Gettier’s Problem
Gettier’s analysis (1963) can be seen as a critique of Plato’s analysis of knowledge.
On Plato’s account following equivalence holds:
S knows that p ↔ (p is true & S believes that p & S is justified in believing
that p). (P1)

8 Notice that this body of information can, in a sense, largely be considered as a set of assumptions /

hypotheses. Meteorological models start from 1) sets of data (measurements), and 2) mathematical
equations or algorithms, which are assumed to correctly predict the weather. The weather forecast
is based on the latter assumptions – it represents hypotheses itself, in a sense.
9 For instance, specifying that T = classical mechanics implies that the accuracy of a certain
prediction will be lower than if T = relativistic mechanics.

50

3 Solving Gettier’s Problem. Knowledge, Truth and Justification are …

The three ‘right hand side’ (RHS) conditions of Plato’s thesis 1 (P1) are each a
necessary condition for S knowing that p, and they are jointly sufficient for S knowing
that p. “S knows that p” is the left hand side (LHS) of the equivalence. One sometimes
abbreviates (P1): K = JTB.
Gettier is widely believed to have shown that this analysis is not always valid,
by constructing examples in which the three RHS conditions are all fulfilled, and
yet S does not know that p. Take for instance Gettier’s ‘case I’ in which Smith (S)
detains information by which he is justified to believe 1) that Jones will get the job
for which they both are applying, and 2) that Jones has ten coins in his pocket. Then,
according to Gettier, Smith is justified to believe that “the man who gets the job has
ten coins in his pocket” (= p) is true. Suppose, further, that in reality not Jones but
Smith gets the job, and that Smith also has, by pure chance, ten coins in his pocket.
Then by chance p is true. Thus, the three RHS conditions of (P1) are satisfied and
yet Smith doesn’t detain real knowledge, only chancy knowledge; he does not really
know p—one might say.
Let us now apply the perspectival language rule introduced before; we first apply
it as such, and leave a fuller justification to the next section. So, we replace in (P1)
‘true’ by ‘true_T1’, ‘know’ by ‘know_T2’ and ‘justified’ by ‘justified_T3’. (I could
introduce here the notation T1∗ etc. for general sets of hypotheses, but I will drop
the asterisk in this chapter for simplicity.) A priori, T1, T2, T3 could be different
conceptual frameworks, bodies of information, beliefs, etc., but (P1) relates the three
terms to each other, so explains one concept by using the others, and therefore it
seems we should take T1 = T2 = T3—else our expression (P1) cannot be given a
meaning, as will soon become clear. Thus, according to a natural application of our
perspectival rule, (P1) should be replaced by (P2):

S knows_T1 that p
↔
(p is true_T2 & S believes that p & S is justified_T3 in believing that p),
where T1 = T2 = T3.
(P2)
This expression has a clear meaning; it is shorthand for: S knows that p within a set
of hypotheses or a body of information T1, iff he believes that p, and p is true within
that same T1, and S is, within T1, justified to believe p. After the examples given
in the previous sections an expression as this should sound familiar and meaningful.
The a priori justification of (P2) is clear. (P1) contains three terms that are potentially
ambiguous, and that can be clarified as in (P2), by specifying a body of information
or a set of hypotheses (T1). (P2) is a more precise version of (P1).
(At this point one could wonder: why not relativise also the verb ‘to believe’ to
a conceptual framework? If K = JTB, and if one assumes that K, J, and T can be
relativised or perspectivised, why not B? There is a categorical difference in these
concepts: K, J, and T can be subject to epistemic standards, while B is a psychological

3.5 Application to Gettier’s Problem

51

concept, in essence. This being said, one could also relativise B; but this needlessly
complicates the analysis.)
The essential point now is that Gettier’s analysis does not satisfy (P2), in particular
it does not satisfy the condition T1 = T2 = T3. This symbolic expression simply
implies that, when analysing ‘know’ as in (P1), and introducing in (P1) for clarity
three references each defining a perspective, these references should be the same. In
other words, this condition stipulates that one can only compare ‘knowledge within a
perspective T1’ to justified true belief within that same T1. Let us call the condition T1
= T2 = T3 the condition of ‘constant or consistent referencing’. At least two attitudes
are possible towards this condition at this point: either one may already accept it as
reasonable; or one may remain sceptical and ask what its heuristic benefit is. To the
least, I intend to show that the analysis (P2) allows for a coherent analysis of Gettier’s
problem.
But first, as an another example of an application of (P2), notice that (P2) provides
an explanation of following well-known problem in epistemology: how can one
know something that is not really true? For instance, it is common to say that physicists know propositions (pc ) of classical mechanics, even when these are not really
true. This case can be analysed within model (P2): S knows that pc within classical
mechanics, if S believes pc , if pc is true within classical mechanics, and if S is justified, within that theory, to believe pc . Such a statement, even if cumbersome, does
certainly not conflict with scientific language practice neither with scientific logic.
Hence the analysis (P2) answers the question above, if one accepts that knowledge is
knowledge within a theory*, that partial knowledge is knowledge within a partially
true theory*, and that partial knowledge can be more valuable than no knowledge at
all. Most theories are only partly true.
Back to Gettier: as we just saw, Gettier’s analysis is not in agreement with the
analysis of ‘know’ condensed in (P2). Closer inspection shows that in Gettier’s
counterexamples T1 T3. When Gettier uses ‘to know’, he means ‘to really know’,
as is clear from the examples he gives. If we wish to represent this in our (P2), T1
would be the ‘best possible theory*’ or ‘the best possible information/hypotheses
set at hand’. In the story of Smith and Jones, this best possible information is the
information that is at hand after the job is attributed—when one indeed really can
know who got the job. But the ‘justification’ of Gettier’s examples is only justification
within a truncated perspective or information set T3. Indeed, Smith was justified to
believe that Jones would get the job based on preliminary information (T3) about the
boss’s future decision10 —but information that turned out to be inadequate: the boss
changed his mind. T3 is in any case not the same information or set of hypotheses
as T1; T1 is a revised and improved body of information with respect to T3 (one
could write T1 > T3). In short, T1 T3, in violation of the condition of consistent
referencing. (Let me remind the reader that it is a key assumption of scientific realism
10 Thus, I understand T3 here as a body of information that includes, for instance, a promise of

the boss regarding his future decision. Or, one could consider T3 as a set of hypotheses including,
for instance, the hypotheses “The boss said that Jones would get the job” and “The boss is usually
trustworthy”.

52

3 Solving Gettier’s Problem. Knowledge, Truth and Justification are …

that different theories usually have a different degree of adequacy or verisimilitude.
Therefore one can in principle introduce a metric (<) to compare theories in the same
problem space.)
An identical analysis is valid for Gettier’s ‘case II’. Now p = “Either Jones owns
a Ford, or Brown is in Barcelona”. If Smith has no clue where Brown is, but has
evidence that Jones owns a Ford (he always had one in the past), he is justified
to believe that p, based on the hypothesis that there is constancy in Jones’s car
possession. If in reality Jones doesn’t own a Ford any longer, but Brown is by chance
in Barcelona, p would be true and yet Smith would not really know that p. In our
formalism: Smith is justified_T3 to believe that p, with T3 = the body of information
that includes the hypothesis of constancy in Jones’s car possession. At the same time p
is true_T2 with T2 = the best possible body of information (including the information
that Jones has no Ford and that Brown is in Barcelona). Smith does not know_T1
that p, with T1 = T2 = the best possible theory*. But the latter conclusion is only a
counterexample to (P1), not to (P2), since, again, T1 T3.

3.6 Interpretation. Comparison with Zagzebski, Quine,
and Contextualism
In this section I will further elaborate and justify my above claims, notably by
comparing them to Plato’s theory of ideas, to Zagzebski’s analysis of the inescapability of Gettier cases (1994), to Quine’s holistic theory of meaning, and to epistemic
contextualism.
To recapitulate, in the preceding sections I have shown that in certain conversations it is useful to specify the body of information or theory or perspective one
relates to. Obviously, explicit referencing towards a theory or information set is an
uncommon practice in daily conversations: it is not only cumbersome, but often also
unnecessary. Explicit referencing is often redundant because in a non-ambiguous
context the ‘background information’ or ‘background set of assumptions’ is broadly
shared by all parties of the conversation: it is the body of information and assumptions that is available to common sense, or that is shared by people engaging in
a ‘usual’ or ‘normal’ conversation—a conversation that is not in need of special
epistemic precautions. At the same time, it seems at least worthwhile to consider
the possibility that explicit referencing is as natural and helpful in epistemology
(for solving problems) as it is in other contexts. I submit that this is the case for
Plato’s and Gettier’s analyses of knowledge. On this view, then, (P2) is the more
precise variant of (P1). This assumption allows for a transparent interpretation of
Gettier’s counterexamples to (P1): these counterexamples are based on a shift in the
reference or perspective. If to the left and right of the iff -arrow in (P2) one uses a
different measure or reference of knowledge and justification (i.e. if T1 T3), it is
not surprising anymore that the equivalence does not hold. (P2) contains in particular
the condition of consistent referencing (T1 = T2 = T3), which emerges as a direct

3.6 Interpretation. Comparison with Zagzebski, Quine, and Contextualism

53

consequence of perspectival language use and the fact that (P2) relates ‘know’, ‘true’
and ‘justified’. If one uses a reference for these words, they should be the same if
one defines one concept by the others; without this condition (P2) would make no
sense.
In short, what Gettier has shown is that real knowledge is not the same as partially
justified belief (see Cabbolet (2024) for a similar conclusion). (P2) also suggests an
answer to the question: under which conditions is justified true belief equal to knowledge? Formally: if T1 = T2 = T3 in (P2)—so if the condition of consistent referencing
is satisfied. For several reasons, all related to the desideratum of maximising explanatory power, I said above that I would jump to conclusions and take this perspectival
solution to Gettier’s problem as the basis for a perspectival epistemology—which
aims at telling us something fundamental about the theory-relative nature of truth
and knowledge. In the following I will corroborate this position by arguing that it
can subsume other well-known accounts.
To start, Plato’s wider epistemology can be understood as a special case of (P2).
Indeed, on Plato’s classic view, real knowledge (episteme) is knowledge of the eternal
truths, the Ideas; an important part of Plato’s philosophy was aimed at distinguishing
it from ‘common belief’, or ‘opinion’ (doxa). Now, it is natural to associate Plato’s
episteme to (fully) justified belief in (absolutely) true propositions. Thus, Plato’s
thesis (P1) can be seen as a natural limit case of (P2), with T1 = T2 = T3 = the best
possible theory or information set, or the final or ‘divine’ theory if one likes.
Interestingly, it seems that when one uses ‘to know’ and ‘true’ in everyday speech,
one uses them in general in this platonic sense: one means ‘to really know’, and ‘really
true’ (recall also how Gettier interprets his examples). Not only people in general
but also philosophers and scientists seem to find this concept of absolute knowledge
natural, if we can rely on the frequency with which it appears in theology, philosophy,
science (think of the related notion of ‘Theory of Everything’ used by physicists).
But on my account this unrelativised or natural or ‘purely factual’ interpretation of
‘know’ should be seen as an idealisation, in other words as an oversimplification: it
throws philosophy into unsolvable conundrums as Gettier’s problem if not properly
re-construed. Natural language is full of such idealised concepts; cases in point
are the notions studied in the next chapters: free will (Chapters 6 and 7), cause
(Chapter 5), and probability (chapter 4). Philosophers having studied these concepts
professionally will presumably agree that there is a hidden part in their meaning. On
the other hand, it is reassuring that the notion of objective or absolute truth can be
given a clear meaning on our account: it is the truth as described or covered by the
best possible theory/set of assumptions. In sum, our type of epistemic relativism is
well compatible with a belief in objective truth, even if in a slightly subtle way.
Of the many investigations that have contributed to elucidate Gettier’s analysis,
the well-known account of L. Zagzebski (1994) seems particularly pertinent. In ‘The
inescapability of Gettier problems’, Zagzebski proposes a condition that is imposed
on all usual accounts of knowledge, whether of Plato’s type (P1) or involving a fourth,
fifth etc. RHS condition. The conclusion of the article is that all counterexamples
to (P1) à la Gettier arise when “an accident of bad luck is cancelled out by an
accident of good luck” (Zagzebski, 1994, p. 66). And such a cancelling-out can only

54

3 Solving Gettier’s Problem. Knowledge, Truth and Justification are …

occur when there is a type of ‘difference’ or ‘independence’ between knowledge and
justification. In the author’s words (Zagzebski, 1994, p. 73):
The notion of knowledge requires success, both in reaching the goal of truth, and in reaching
it via the right cognitive path. The notion of justification or warrant is less stringent, requiring
only that the right path is one that is usually successful at getting the truth. It is this difference
between the notion of knowledge and the notion of justification that is responsible for Gettier
problems.

Zagzebski concludes (ibidem, p. 71):
It appears, then, that no account of knowledge as true belief plus something else can withstand
Gettier objections as long as there is a small degree of independence between truth and the
other conditions of knowledge.

Zagzebski does not explain in more detail what this difference or independence
amounts to. Now, it seems clear that her claim can straightforwardly be recast in
terms of a perspectival interpretation. Within my model, the counterexamples arise
when T3, the reference for justification, is different from, less adequate than T1,
the reference or perspective of knowledge (and truth); I wrote T3 < T1. This nicely
fits to the above quotes. I also argued that in order to be ‘Gettier-proof’, one should
‘consistently reference’: T1 must be equal to T2 and equal to T3, where equality is
measured in degree of verisimilitude. Clearly, this condition of consistent referencing
corresponds to the condition of absence of independence Zagzebski refers to. In sum,
both analyses overlap: even if there is only a small degree of independence, even if
the (implicit) references of the words ‘know’, ‘true’ and ‘justified’ are almost the
same (T1 ≈ T2 ≈ T3), “justified true belief will never be sufficient for knowledge”
(Zagzebski, 1994, p. 69). Thus, the analysis in Sect. 3.5 provides an interpretation of
Zagzebski’s difference or independence between knowledge and justification. The
latter is a difference in the information / hypotheses (the T’s) that are implicitly
referred to, in particular a difference in adequacy or verisimilitude of the latter.
It seems important to also look beyond the horizon of Gettier’s analysis. I find
it particularly relevant to make the link with W. Quine’s acclaimed epistemological holism, which he developed, notably, in his seminal article ‘Two Dogmas of
Empiricism’ (1951). (Quine withheld his view on holism in his later review paper
(1991), cf. Verhaegh, 2018 §6.3; see also similar ideas by Hempel (1950)). Quine
famously discusses the meaning and verifiability of scientific statements, criticising
the analytic-synthetic distinction and reductionism—“the belief that each meaningful
statement is equivalent to some logical construct upon terms which refer to immediate
experience” (1951, p. 20). He says:
The dogma of reductionism survives in the supposition that each statement, taken in isolation
from its fellows, can admit of confirmation or infirmation at all. My countersuggestion,
issuing essentially from Carnap’s doctrine of the physical world in the Aufbau, is that our
statements about the external world face the tribunal of sense experience not individually
but only as a corporate body. (1951, p. 38)

And:
The unit of empirical significance is the whole of science (1951, p. 39).

3.6 Interpretation. Comparison with Zagzebski, Quine, and Contextualism

55

Thus, Quine argues that propositions cannot be verified or tested individually by
direct empirical observation; what one tests or verifies is a whole web of hypotheses,
a whole theory one could say, or perhaps even science as whole (a generalisation
I would not make11 ). Clearly, this epistemological/verificationist holism resonates
remarkably well with the claims presented in this chapter. Quine’s theory of verification could be seen as part of a truth theory, providing information about when (observation) statements (in science) are true. My main point is therefore the following: I
believe that Quine’s verificationist holism can be subsumed under the mixed coherence—correspondence theory of truth I adopted in Sect. 3.3 (coming from a wholly
different linguistic angle); or, perhaps both are overlapping construals of the same
idea.
Indeed, Quine’s thesis can be restated, perhaps slightly more clearly and generally,
in theory-relativised parlance, not only in a scientific but in a general context. Namely:
the truth-value of propositions is attributed via the truth of a whole theory (in the
broad sense); propositions are solidly embedded in theories T/sets of hypotheses
T*. More explicitly, a statement (whether an observation sentence or a theoretical
statement) is true if it can (logically/rationally) be inferred from T* and if T* as
a whole is an adequate theory* (i.e. if T* corresponds to reality, if it describes
reality well, notions that clearly come with a degree). This is the mixed coherencecorrespondence account of truth of Sect. 3.3, resonating with my solution to Gettier’s
problem.
Thus, there seems to be a strong overlap with some of Quine’s celebrated views.
I believe it would be interesting to go deeper into the similarities and differences
in my view and Quine’s, but this would go beyond the scope of this book (some
topics of tension were mentioned in Chapter 1). This being said, I do not know
how Quine’s philosophy would solve Gettier’s problem. For addressing this problem
one needs, I believe, the relativity of statements with respect to theories*, not to
science as a whole. Also, as shown above, several other epistemological riddles
are solved by making the distinction between true_T1 and true_T2. On the other
hand, just as for the problem of induction (cf. Chapter 2), Quine could perhaps deny
that there is an interesting problem. In his (1987, pp. 108–110) he claims that the
word ‘knowledge’ is imprecise and unscientific. He might therefore dismiss Gettier’s
problem as not being a real (scientific) problem.12 I agree that ‘to know’ is vague,
but not that there isn’t a relevant philosophical problem. It seems to me that Quine
11 True, in a paper written forty years later, Quine invokes not science as a whole, but rather “chunks

of [science], clusters of sentences just inclusive enough to have critical semantic mass. By this I
mean a cluster sufficient to imply an observable effect of an observable experimental condition”
(1991, p. 268). Nevertheless, Verhaegh argues that Quine remained faithful to his radical holism
(Verhaegh 2018, §6.3). On my view ‘theory’ is the better reference unit: if a cluster of sentences
of a theory is definitely shown to be in contradiction with experiments, then the whole theory will
ultimately be rejected (at least under certain conditions). Next, in the history of science as we know
it, disciplines centred on theories have changed independently on countless occasions, leaving the
rest of the network untouched. Finally, taking ‘theory’ as the reference unit is most instrumental for
solving a series of other problems studied in this book.
12 I thank Sander Verhaegh for having drawn my attention to (Quine 1987) and Quine’s likely
interpretation of Gettier’s problem.

56

3 Solving Gettier’s Problem. Knowledge, Truth and Justification are …

is caught in an overly restrictive canvas of seeing epistemology only as a chapter of
natural science—a position that tends to reject philosophy as a genuine, theory-based
discipline, and that has a self-contradictory touch to it. My account allows one to see
in what sense ‘to know’ is vague, and how to remediate this problem.
It seems one can now make essential generalisations: once we have adopted the
above relativistic coherence-correspondence account of truth, it is then also quite
compelling to adopt following key thesis: namely, that the meaning of propositions
and concepts derives from their meaning within a T*. We will encounter essential
illustrations of this thesis throughout this book (cf. the general conclusion, Chapter 8,
also Chapter 6). This claim agrees again with a view that can be retraced to Quine,
it seems (1960, 1969): epistemological/verificationist holism and semantic holism
are inextricably related—ultimately one and the same thing.13 I will not elaborate
on this idea here any further; but I hope that at the end of this book the reader
will agree that many of my conclusions in the subsequent chapters corroborate the
perspectival claims presented above. I believe they find their clearest expression in the
theory-relativity or representation-relativity of truth, knowledge, and justification.
(If, by the end of this comparison with Quine’s ideas, some readers conclude that
I have merely re-derived some of his theses, I would gladly accept that verdict. Let
us then hope that I have elucidated some lesser-known connections, and that this
effort serves to illustrate the very Quine-compatible idea that one can reach the same
nodes in the web of belief by different routes.)
Let us finally compare the above account to a well-known position in contemporary epistemology, namely contextualism, a type of epistemic relativism. As
mentioned in the introduction, my initial goal was to follow Field’s injunction
to inquire whether key concepts of epistemology can be relativised to ‘evidential
systems’. I will only compare my model here to some of the most salient claims
of contextualist epistemology. Note that one alleged solution to Gettier’s problem
within contextualism was offered by D. Lewis (1996), but his solution relies on a
complex set of unintuitive rules; it is now considered as controversial (e.g. Cohen,
1998; Heller, 1999).
In its paradigmatic form epistemic contextualism states that the (truth value
of) propositions expressed by ‘knowledge sentences’ (such as ‘S knows that p’)
depend(s) upon the conversational context in which they are uttered (e. g. Cohen,
1999; DeRose, 1999; Lewis, 1996; Rysiew, 2016). Now, it is trivial that the key ideas
of contextualism and of the relativistic view based on (P2) can be translated into each
other. In short, if knowledge attribution depends on the context, it depends on a given
body of information: a conversational context is trivially linked to a body of information—without which the conversation would not be possible. Conversely, if the
verb ‘know’ depends on a given body of information T*, knowledge sentences (KS)
indeed depend upon context, because the information set T* in which one embeds a
knowledge type depends on the context of conversation.
13 I will justify it this way: on the logical-epistemological level, the meaning of something, say X,

can be construed, it seems, as a body of essential statements about X that are (supposed to be) true.
If truth-about-X is ultimately relative to a T*, then meaning-of-X also is.

3.7 Conclusion

57

Let us make a slightly more detailed comparison. Since there are infinitely many
theories*, and since in most contexts several of these are likely to have some pertinence, the attributor of a KS can, a priori, refer to a few of these theories when she
utters the (often ambiguous) phrase ‘S knows that p’. In context 1 she may mean
‘S knows_(T1) that p’, and in context 2 she may want to say ‘S knows_(T2) that p’
(or ‘S does not know_(T2) that p’).14 These and other epistemological riddles can
straightforwardly be handled by my model (see one more in Appendix 3.1). Here,
then, is one argument in favour of theory-relativism as compared to contextualism:
in one and the same context several theories* can be discussed or referred to, and
in that case the expression ‘to know that p’ must be accompanied, if full clarity is
required, by a specification of the theory*, not the context.
In sum, there seems little doubt that context-relativism and theory-relativism at
least broadly overlap in several respects. I submit that the added value of my model
lies in the following: (i) its consistency with (language) practices in science and
philosophy at large; (ii) the fact that it can offer a precise and straightforward interpretation of Gettier’s problem, as well as of the other epistemological problems and
theories dealt with above; (iii) especially, the fact that it resonates favourably with
the solutions to other key problems studied in this book.

3.7 Conclusion
In order to solve Gettier’s problem, we could have remained essentially on the level
of linguistic practice, if we wished to play it safe, but we wanted to get to the
core of things, and investigate what is to be learned from this particular problem
for general theories of truth and knowledge—the core of epistemology. I proposed
that the key principle to be derived is that of a fundamental theory-relativity or
representation-relativity of knowledge, truth, and justification—in any context, scientific or mundane. My main arguments are based on 1) the fact that Gettier’s problem
is a key problem of contemporary epistemology (so a coherent solution should be
considered with particular attention); 2) the fact that the relativistic account of truth
(a mixed coherence—correspondence view) that is most naturally compatible with
the above solution to Gettier’s problem strongly resonates with Quine’s acclaimed
holism; 3) especially, the fact that representation-relativity will turn out to be the

14 See the examples given above, and see Cohen (1999, p.58) for a well-known example, which can

easily be analysed in relativised parlance. Following the example of Cohen, Smith may know that
Mary’s plane stops in Chicago based on common sense and the assumption that official schedules
are usually reliable. So Mary might say (or mean) that Smith knows the fact within the body of
information offered by common sense and that assumption. But the same Mary might also say (or
mean) that Smith doesn’t know the fact for sure if one takes potentially more reliable data into
account (the same body of information plus the information gathered by checking with an airport
manager). Relative to a potentially more complete body of information, Smith doesn’t know for
sure.

58

3 Solving Gettier’s Problem. Knowledge, Truth and Justification are …

main tool for problem-solving for a majority of problems studied in this book (see
notably Chapters 5–8).
Some particular results were the following. The truth-value that one is (or should
be) willing to attribute to any utterance depends on the (truth-value or the degree of
truth of the) hypotheses/theories one necessarily must assume15 when making the
utterance. Even (the truth-value of) a banal statement as ‘this is a tree’, or ‘the dog
Droopy just bit me’ depends, in a sense, on (the truth-value of) a set of hypotheses—
including for instance the assumption that one’s senses are not manipulated, that
a certain dog really is called Droopy, etc. Needless to say, being aware of theoryrelativity is mostly useful for solving complex problems: one does not need to be
constantly aware of it in daily life! An inextricably and obviously related conclusion is
then that scientific knowledge is solidly embedded in theories alone (on the logicalepistemological level); but more generally, that any knowledge, even of everyday
things, is embedded in sets of hypotheses. Thus, our analysis of Gettier’s problem
does not only suggest an account of truth, but also of knowledge; I will not elaborate, but it is clear that it corroborates ancient wisdoms about knowledge-gathering.
These wisdoms can pleasantly be condensed in slogans, perhaps platitudes, such as:
(advanced) knowledge is all about theory building—which is a variant of the MCThypothesis of Chapter 1. And: the atoms of knowledge are theories. I hope that such
claims are self-explaining by now.16
To repeat, and to defuse once more charges of radical rationalism, theoryrelativism can well be brought in harmony with the idea that many theories and
sets of hypotheses are, in the process of discovery, abstracted by the human mind
from empirical observations—and therefore that observations and experiments play a
primordial role for knowledge gathering too, besides theory building. But theories*
are the key ingredients at the level of a logical-epistemological analysis of what
knowledge is. As emphasised above, theory-relativism can also well accommodate
the notion of absolute or objective truth—as a limit concept.
My account is a moderate form of epistemic relativism, and is related to a form of
perspectivism in philosophy of science. In philosophy of science, theory relativism
could be compared to, for instance, the scientific perspectivism of authors as Giere
(2006) and Massimi (2012, 2018, 2021), which are perspectival realisms (see discussions of perspectivism also in, e.g., Chakravartty, 2010 and Callebaut, 2012). Indeed,
the following view is a natural companion to my epistemological account: what we
can scientifically know are (ultimately) not absolute objective truths but theories,
which offer perspectives to the objective universe—hence a connection with Giere’s
15 On a logical level, the necessity of the hypotheses/theories one assumes reveals itself in the

following way: these hypotheses/theories must necessarily be announced or stated when someone
asks ‘why?’, when someone asks ‘why is p true?’. This is the logical / rational way to justify the
assumed truth of p.
16 To be explicit, once more: the truth (value) of statements depends on, is decided by, the theory one
adopts. More obviously, the justification of statements is done via theories, and therefore hinges on
the latter: propositions are justified if they derive from a justified theory (and we know the criteria
for theory justification, based on prediction, explanation, problem solving). It is then also natural
to say that the building blocks of knowledge are theories: not concepts or propositions in isolation.

Appendix 3.1. On the Factuality of Knowledge

59

and Massimi’s views. Again, this position is not in conflict with the concept of objective truth; on the contrary, it is consistent with it. I will not study here in detail how
deep the connection with Giere’s and Massimi’s accounts goes, but just note that
many types of (scientific) perspectivism exist. To the best of my knowledge, none of
them has put ‘theories’ or ‘theories*’ at the centre of the vantage point.

Appendix 3.1. On the Factuality of Knowledge
This appendix is intended for experts in epistemology, and can safely be skipped by
other readers. At the risk of being redundant, I would like to respond to a worry I often
heard, and that was phrased by an anonymous reviewer of (Vervoort & Shevchenko,
2022). Some readers keep finding it problematic that the above account asserts the
following. If it is the case that p, according to one theory, call it T1, and it is the case
that not-p according to another theory, call it T2, then one could truthfully assert (S1)
‘John knows that p and Mary knows that not-p’, if (S1) is understood as (S2) ‘John
knows_T1 that p and Mary knows_T2 that not-p’. However, so goes the objection,
given the factuality of the verb ‘know’, no-one can assert (S1) without contradicting
themselves. It is added that our proposal boils down to an attempt to provide the verb
‘know’ with an unnatural interpretation, and that we need to show how this verb’s
factual component can be accounted for in terms of our account.
I would answer the following. Natural language is rife with imprecisely defined
concepts, and ‘know’ is one of them; it is precisely the task of philosophy to elucidate
them. In a nutshell, statement (S2) is more precise than (S1), and avoids Gettier’s
problem and the problem just raised. As explained in more detail in Sect. 3.3 by my
mixed coherence—correspondence view of truth, the factuality of knowledge can
well be retrieved in the proposed model: factual knowledge is knowledge within a T
that adequately describes the facts. “I know that p” [where p corresponds to a fact]
means, really, “I know_T that p where T describes the facts adequately”, in full, “I
know that p relying on hypotheses T that describe the facts adequately”. I agree,
this may sound contrived; but it is a way out of several conundrums—and note, I
do nothing more than generalising insights that are very alive in philosophical and
scientific practice. Can epistemology afford to close its eyes on an epistemic reality
in a realm that is reputed to employ high standards of knowledge, and therefore
presumably language practices doing justice to these standards? Finally, in Sect. 3.6
I have argued that in everyday / natural language ‘know’ usually corresponds to ‘really
know’ or ‘know within the absolute T’, which is a natural, but often imprecise, limit
case of my model. In conclusion: knowing implies assuming; and sometimes one
has to declare what one assumes.

Chapter 4

The Scientific Concept of Probability

Abstract In this chapter I revisit the frequency interpretation of probability of von
Mises, and argue that it contains the key elements of any coherent interpretation
of scientific probability—notwithstanding the heavy criticisms von Mises collected
from mathematicians and philosophers alike. I propose a modified definition that
is not based on von Mises’ ‘infinite collectives’ but retains an essential ingredient
of his interpretation, namely that probability can only quantitatively be defined for
events that are, or can be, repeated in similar conditions and that exhibit frequency
stabilisation. New is that the mentioned ‘conditions’ should be ‘partitioned’. Thus,
I will partition probabilistic systems into object and environment, or in object, initiating, and probing subsystem, and show that such partitioning solves a series of
classic problems. Other consistent interpretations can be unified with frequentism,
e.g. Bayesianism à la Jaynes. I explore in detail the tantalising subjective or operationalist touch of probability, and argue that it can be put on objective grounds. One
further upshot of the model is that several quantum puzzles can be seen as deriving
from an imprecise interpretation of probability.

4.1 General Introduction
Philosophers are since a long time interested in the meaning, or interpretation, of the
concept of probability, as witnessed by the enormous literature that exists on the topic.
When people with a scientific training hear about this ‘problem of interpretation’,
their first reaction is often one of surprise (as was my case). Why is there a problem?
Are all problems of probability not adequately solved by the well-understood calculus
of probability, based on Kolmogorov’s axioms? That there is an interesting problemspace can be recognised by following two considerations—to which usually not much
attention is paid in science curricula (regrettably, I now believe). First, in textbooks
of applied probability, the science student learns that two or more interpretations
of probability exist, notably the ‘classic’ interpretation of Laplace, applicable to
chance games (using dice, cards, urns with balls, etc.), and the ‘frequency’ interpretation, applicable to basically anything else. Especially modern textbooks add other

© The Author(s) 2026
L. Vervoort, Problem Solving in Philosophy,
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-17756-8_4

63

64

4 The Scientific Concept of Probability

interpretations, notably the subjective interpretation. Rough definitions of these are
the following. According to the classic Laplacian view, the probability of event E
occurring in a probabilistic experiment is the ratio NE /N, where NE is the number of
outcomes in which E occurs, and N the total number of different outcomes. (This is a
rough definition; it presupposes for instance that the outcomes are equally likely and
mutually exclusive.) According to the frequency interpretation, the probability of E is
the relative frequency with which E arises in long (ideally infinite) series of repeated
experiments. Both these interpretations are ‘objective’. According to the ‘subjective’
or Bayesian interpretation of probability, probability values are subjective measures
of belief; different persons may attribute different probabilities to the same event.
Several other interpretations exist, and each in several variants. As a consequence,
a philosophically-inclined student, consciously or unconsciously driven by an urge
for unification, might wonder: Why there isn’t one overarching interpretation?
I will argue, following von Mises, that probability can be seen not only as a
mathematical theory, but also as a physical one. In a sense, probability theory is the
most encompassing physical theory we have. All physical systems comply with it:
obviously all probabilistic systems comply with it1 ; and deterministic systems are
a special case of probabilistic systems characterised by probabilities 0 or 1. This
fact already seems to suggest that there should be one fundamental interpretation
of (scientific) probability as a basic physical property or category. But can one
really assert this intuition in view of the plethora of counterarguments and pluralistic
positions that thrive in the literature? Isn’t that a thought too bold to entertain?
Possibly, but according to the MCT-thesis we should take it seriously.
A second eye-opening consideration as to whether there is a real problem, stems
from the intriguing chapter of the paradoxes of probability theory. Applied probability is rife with such paradoxes: problems the solutions of which were disputed
even among famous mathematicians, including the fathers of probability theory.
Many mathematics books on probability contain at least a few: Bertrand’s paradox,
the Borel–Kolmogorov paradox, the Monty Hall problem, etc. Together with the first
consideration, these paradoxes show that the application of measure-theoretic probability theory (Kolmogorov’s axiomatic theory) to real-world problems is non-trivial.
But it can of course be applied to real-world problems, for instance physical problems, say die throwing, chance games, or countless problems of statistical physics.
In sum, these two considerations show that the interpretation of probability—What
is probability beyond the mathematics? How to apply the theory?—is a genuine
problem at the interface of mathematics, physics and philosophy. I now believe it is
a particularly fascinating one, and a uniquely subtle one to boot. People who have
delved into this question will not need to be convinced. Novices may now pause, and
try to come up with their own answer to the above questions.
Following the heuristic rule of Chapter 1, I believe the approach to tackle the
problem of interpreting probability should be one of unification, as always: one
should try to unify existing interpretations (as far as it is possible), and show that
1 I mean that the probabilities predicted by probabilistic physical theories, say quantum mechanics,

obviously comply with probability theory.

4.1 General Introduction

65

the unified theory can solve more problems, including the mentioned paradoxes
and the very many problems discussed in the literature. I found it instrumental to
guide this quest by following additional questions: What is a probabilistic system (in
the physical sense)?, and: How does one extract probability values from empirical
data? These questions put the search on firm empirical grounds, which seems not a
luxury. Indeed, I believe it is not enough emphasised in the philosophical literature
that in science any experimental probability is determined, measured, verified as a
relative frequency. Recalling the verificationist principle of meaning of the logical
empiricists, and the credo of synthetic philosophy that science is the ultimate judge of
philosophy (at least for problems that have such an immediate link with science), this
seems to give a head start to the frequency interpretation. But again, the real question
is: does the latter allow one to unify and concomitantly to answer the largest number
of well-posed questions? That is what will be studied in this chapter.
Some people immediately object that theories such as quantum mechanics may
predict probabilities as real numbers that are not obviously ratios or frequencies
(think of the square of a modulus of a quantum wave function, a transition amplitude, etc.). But to verify these numbers one always determines relative frequencies.
And as most empirically determined quantities (say the magnitude of speed, energy,
magnetic field, etc.), these relative frequencies necessarily have an experimental
error or precision; they lie within a numerical range ΔQ. As emphasised in the next
section, it is the key assumption of my von Mises-style model, and one of the best
corroborated facts of science, that ΔQ can be made as small as practically needed
by increasing the number of trials. In other words, the measured probability Q = P
± ΔQ, where P is the ‘real’ or theoretical probability value and ΔQ the experimental
precision or uncertainty. Any real number P between 0 and 1 (as predicted say by
quantum mechanics) can be written under the form P = Q ± ΔQ with Q rational; and
ΔQ can be made arbitrarily small by increasing the trials. In sum, one can determine
ratios that arbitrarily closely approximate2 the theoretical P.
Several authors have scrutinised the frequency interpretation (for reviews on this
