Chapter 5

A Unified Definition of Cause

Abstract The aim of this chapter is to show that an overarching definition of cause
can be constructed, at least in a deterministic setting. Several well-known accounts,
notably the counterfactual, manipulationist, and functional accounts can be unified.
The functional interpretation is generally accepted in the physics community, but
is unfortunately largely absent from contemporary analytic investigations—yet it
is essential for understanding the ontology of the universe. The greatest tool for
unification will appear to be the ‘representation-relativity of causal identification’
(this is a relativity not of causation as such, which is best understood as an objective
feature of the world, embedded in the laws of nature). This perspectival relativity is
akin to the one encountered when investigating Gettier’s problem. On the basis of
this epistemological principle I will propose a modified counterfactual account, and
show that it solves a remarkably wide array of problems. Finally, I will argue that
there are good reasons to believe that this model, perhaps with minor modifications,
can englobe probabilistic causation.

5.1 Introduction
A series of fascinating questions of philosophy and science involves the concept of
cause. Does every event, every phenomenon, have causes? Or rather, are there some
events that are not determined by causes but happen by pure chance, pure hazard?
Is the principle of causation/causality true? Should every mature physical theory be
causal? Is the difficulty in unifying relativity theory and quantum mechanics linked
to the fact that the first is a causal theory, while the second an a-causal one? What
is the link between the concept of cause and that of probability? Can we have a free
will even if everything, including our thoughts and choices, is caused by preceding
events?
If we wish to seriously address such questions, we need, first of all, a good
definition of the concept of cause, beyond our intuitive notion. Interestingly, in daily
conversations people know quite well how to use the word ‘cause’. I am definitely
sure that the cause of the pain in my leg is the bite from my neighbours’ dog. The

© The Author(s) 2026
L. Vervoort, Problem Solving in Philosophy,
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-17756-8_5

113

114

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

cause of this broken window is my daughter having thrown a stone at it. A physicist
may confidently say that the cause of the increase in electric current in a given
circuit is the increase in voltage in a given potential source. So, we do seem to have
an excellent command over the meaning of cause. From one point of view, this is
obvious: without a grasp of the cause-effect relations that we encounter literally
everywhere, we would surely not survive. Is it then not surprising that there is no
consensus in the philosophy community about a good definition of cause?
One of the most famous attacks on the notions of cause and causation/causality
(besides Hume’s) is due to Bertrand Russell (1912), who claimed that physics does
not only not need the concept of cause, but that such a concept cannot be defined
in a physics context. (There are a few immediate objections to make to Russell’s
verdict in his seminal paper: 1) Russell only verified the solidity of definitions found
in encyclopaedia; 2) It is not while the word ‘cause’ does not appear in the most
abstract physical theories that it cannot be defined in a meta-theory of physics; 3) In
their daily practice, physicists use the word cause all the time; 4) On closer inspection,
Russell defends in this paper a specific definition of cause, namely the ‘functional’
one scrutinised below.)
Since then, it has become an utterly popular idea that cause is a multifarious
concept, that different contexts require different definitions of cause. J. Norton puts
it this way (2003):
How do we know which terms in the science to associate with the cause and effect? There
is no general principle. (2003, p. 16)
The proliferation of different accounts of causation and the flourishing literature of counterexamples suggests no general agreement even on what it means to say that something is
a cause. So perhaps we should also give up the search for a single principle and allow each
causally well-behaved science to come with its own, distinct principle of causality. (2003,
p. 6)
At a fundamental level, there are no causes and effects in science and no overarching principle
of causality. (2003, p. 23)

N. Cartwright states in a similar vein (2004):
We currently have on offer a variety of different theories of causation. Many are strikingly
good, providing detailed and plausible treatments of exemplary cases; and all suffer from
clear counterexamples. I argue that, contra Hume and Kant, this is because causation is
not a single, monolithic concept. There are different kinds of causal relations imbedded in
different kinds of systems, readily described using thick causal concepts. Our causal theories
pick out important and useful structures that fit some familiar cases—cases we discover and
ones we devise to fit.

In the following I will greatly profit from detailed case studies done in a recent
compendium on causation, written by L. A. Paul and E. J. Hall (2013). At the end of
their reference book they conclude (p. 249):

5.1 Introduction

115

After surveying the literature in some depth, we conclude that, as yet, there is no reasonably
successful reduction of the causal relation. And correspondingly, there is no reasonably
successful conceptual analysis of a philosophical causal concept. No extant approach seems
able to incorporate all of our desiderata for the causal relation, nor to capture the wide range of
our causal judgments and applications of our causal concept. Barring a fundamental change
in approach, the prospects of a relatively simple, elegant and intuitively attractive, unified
theory of causation, whether ontological reduction or conceptual analysis, are dim.

These are just a few excerpts illustrating the widespread agreement among experts
that a unified, universal concept of cause does not exist. In this chapter I will argue
that such pessimism is not warranted. True, to solidify my claim I will have to
go through an elaborate and somewhat painstaking analytic-synthetic program; but
without this effort any revision of the accepted wisdom would be illusory.1 The
proposed unification is restricted to deterministic causation,2 which takes the lion’s
share in the literature, but I will suggest that a strong overlap with probabilistic
causation seems to loom just around the corner.
Regarding the notion of ‘reduction’ in the last quote, Paul and Hall stipulate (2013,
p. 7–8): “We will also assume a broadly reductionist outlook, according to which
facts about which events cause which other events are fixed, somehow, by (i) the
facts about what happens, together with (ii) the facts about the fundamental laws that
govern what happens. […] We will assume that a successful philosophical analysis
of the causal relation ought to show exactly how facts about what happens, together
with the laws, fix the facts about event causation.” And on p. 256: “Thinking carefully
about the different ontological stances one could take brings up another central issue
for the metaphysics of causation: whether or not the project is reductionist, that is,
does it reduce the causal to the non-causal. […] Moderate and conservative realism
lend themselves most naturally to reductionist interpretations: causal relations are
reduced to the other, more fundamental entities that the realist admits in the world.
On such views, causal theories are to be given in noncausal terms, and our ability
to determine whether there is causation is not dependent on having independent
cognitive access to any irreducibly causal facts.”
It is useful to have the notion of reduction defined from the start. Indeed, the
reductive/ontological demand, as just phrased by Paul and Hall, is one more challenge our effort of synthesis should take up; we will come back to it in Sect. 7, once
the definition is constructed. Pleasingly, this chapter will show that building a precise
definition goes hand in hand with uncovering the ontological/metaphysical grounds
1 An older publication, which foreshadows the results derived in this chapter and which makes the

link with the principle of causality, is (Vervoort 2013a).
2 In the literature on causation, the causal relata (events, say) are usually considered to be either

deterministic or probabilistic. Events happening in mundane contexts, say someone throwing a stone
at a bottle or breaking a window or killing an opponent, are considered part of this ‘deterministic’
realm, to which definitions of cause should be applicable. Note that many of the events we encounter
in daily contexts could also be called ‘spontaneous’ or ‘self-caused’ or ‘free’ (think of spontaneous
decisions of humans); but still they usually fall in the literature under the class of events that should be
covered by a definition of deterministic cause. I adopt this convention. A more thorough assessment
of seemingly self-caused events is presented in the chapter on absolute free will, Chapter 6.

116

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

of causal claims. The ‘conceptual analysis’ goes hand in hand with the ‘ontological reduction’; the ‘descriptive project’ goes hand in hand with the ‘metaphysical
project’, using terms from (Frisch, 2023). Thus, even if I will be rather summary when
it comes to ontological questions about causation—as a classic scientific realist I a
priori simply assume that causation is embedded in, emergent from, the laws of
nature—, my definition will make the link with the ontology quite obvious, and
indeed plainly corroborate the classic realist credo. (One reason why this can be
done in a straightforward way is that our synthesis will encompass the notion of
cause that is most effective in physics; then one only needs, for this argument, very
familiar hypotheses of scientific realism, essentially that the laws of nature have
ontological primacy.)
Before scrutinising the professional literature, I would ask the readers to halt a
moment, and think for themselves: what would you consider a good definition of
cause? Perhaps we should, at first, simplify the question and look for a definition that
works in one field, say physics. Mind that this exercise should be conceived as follows,
in accordance with the reductive demand above. When trying to construct a definition
of cause, we could or should imagine communicating with a physicist who knows
physics but not what ‘cause’ means; once we have given her our definition she should
be able to identify the right causes of any given physical event or phenomenon—
rather, of any description of such event that does not contain the notion of cause and
its kin.
Now, what the ‘right causes’ are is supposed to be given by intuition, or by the
intuition of the expert in the field. Clearly, such a methodology gives a huge weight
to intuition. But this is justified by several facts:
(i) Causal intuitions of experts generally coincide; a group of physicists or philosophers usually can agree about what the causes of an event/phenomenon are—at
least if it is part of a reasonably well understood sub-domain of physics.
(ii) Human brains are genuine ‘cause hunters’, much more so than equation hunters
(and especially more so than philosophical insight hunters). This has an obvious
evolutionary ground. Identification of causes is vital for human survival from
our very first days on earth; we need to be able to identify cause-effect relations
in order to guide our lives in all (new) situations encountered. Unsurprisingly,
infants can identify causal relata from very early on (e.g. Leslie & Keeble,
1987).
(iii) An encompassing definition can be built that coincides with intuition in simple
causal situations, as I will show below.
In other words, in this heuristic process of testing whether a tentative definition of
cause works, the philosopher’s practice cannot do without relying on her (or an expert
scientist’s) intuition about causation in concrete situations. Now, even if we start in
this construction process from an intuitive, pre-theoretic concept, success would
come with an important pay-off: we would obtain a solid theoretical concept, one
that can solve concrete problems transcending our intuition. Therefore, once we have
identified a definition that works in the numerous and wide-ranging test situations
investigated, and that moreover fits into a larger theory unifying the concept of cause

5.1 Introduction

117

with other essential concepts3 (such as theory, law, probability, explanation, etc.), we
can have sufficient confidence in the definition we found; we can then legitimately
apply it to situations where mere intuition is insufficient or helpless. Even more
importantly: we can then hope to be armed for addressing the questions mentioned
at the beginning of this chapter—considered by some as among the most profound
of philosophy.
(As a side remark, it seems that the above epistemic procedure reflects nothing
more than what is done in any cognitive field deserving the attribute ‘scientific’. Intuitive concepts gain epistemic power through theory building, through logical integration in a synthetic theory. In physics, key concepts as force and energy also started
their career as intuitive and imprecise notions. So, one might find here an example
showing that the difference between natural science and philosophy is smaller than
often thought, not only qua method but also qua verification. Both scientific models
and causation models are tested via confrontation with empirical data: in the case
of science produced by experiments, in the case of causation by intuition-based-onexperience; or one might say, by the brain, a machine fine-tuned over thousands of
years to generate empirical cause-effect predictions.4 Sure, there is a difference in
degree in precision and conclusiveness. But, as argued in Chapter 1 §3, this discrepancy can further be reduced using the synthetic method, here applied to published
accounts of causation.)
Less intuitive situations occur for instance in probabilistic models such as the
example studied in Appendix 5.1: there I will show that we can use our privileged
model to do calculations and come to quantitative conclusions, going beyond intuition. Less intuitive situations also occur for instance in quantum mechanics—say
in the famous Einstein–Podolsky–Rosen experiment in which two correlated electrons simultaneously acquire a definite spin value, even when only one is measured.
What causes what in such an experiment? Is quantum mechanics compatible with the
notion of cause (many physicists, the mythical Niels Bohr in the first place, believe it
is not)? Of course, for addressing such difficult questions we need a solid definition.
As already mentioned in Chapter 1, another excellent reason why constructing a
unified concept of cause is indispensable in our time, is that we urgently need it for
assessing artificial intelligence (AI). AI already answers questions as “what is the
cause of X in this neuron diagram” in a surprisingly correct manner, even in debated
cases (Vervoort & Nikolaev, 2025). If philosophers want to keep ahead of their field,
they need solid models to assess, train, and use AI for causal analysis—and surely
soon for many more philosophical tasks.
Finally, we need the concept of cause for investigating the principle of causation,
once veneered as a queen of natural philosophy, then vehemently decried by a host of
3 In this chapter I will elaborate in particular on the unification between the notions of cause,

probability, and theory, as will become clear in the following. Since the link between (scientific)
theory and law is quite clear, we have one more connection in the coherent network for free.
4 Moreover, many indirect empirical tests for our account of causation are conceivable, cf. e.g.
Appendix 5.1; and consider the many inferences we make at different places in this book from our
model of causation; several of these have empirical connections.

118

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

renowned philosophers, Hume, Mach, Russell, Popper, Bohr, van Fraassen, Norton,
Cartwright to name a few. That is something we leave to the Epilogue, Chapter 9.
The chapter is organised as follows. A multitude of definitions of cause exists in
philosophy (for recent reviews, cf. e.g. Paul & Hall, 2013; Psillos, 2014; Woodward,
2003). I will base my synthesis on prominent models, namely the counterfactual
account (e.g. Lewis, 1973; Menzies & Beebee, 2020; Paul & Hall, 2013), the manipulationist account (e.g. Menzies & Price, 1993; Woodward, 2003, 2023), the physical or functional model (e.g. Mach, 1960; Russell, 1912; Schlick, 1949), and the
probabilistic account (e.g. Cartwright, 1979; Hitchcock, 2021; Reichenbach, 1956;
Suppes, 1970). I will start in Sect. 2 with the counterfactual definition and make a
first synthesis for neuron diagrams (see also Vervoort & Nikolaev, 2025). Then I
will focus on the definition of deterministic cause prevalent in physics (Sect. 3). In
Sect. 4 I will revisit the manipulationist definition in order to disentangle it of its
worries of anthropocentricity and circularity. In these two last sections I will introduce representation-relativity and show the key work it does, notably in unifying
the functional and manipulationist definitions. Although the present chapter deals,
strictly speaking, only with deterministic cause, in Sect. 5 I will propose an analysis
of probabilistic causation, essentially to argue that under broad conditions it overlaps
with deterministic causation. With the knowledge gathered in Sects. 3 to 5, we can
then return to counterfactual causation, and make the second and final synthesis in
Sect. 6.
A word on notation: I will denote with capital letters the definitions of counterfactual (DEF-CF), functional (DEF-F), manipulationist (DEF-M), and probabilistic (DEF-P) cause, and with lowercase letters the definitions restricted to neuron
diagrams (def-sn and def-mn). These definitions constitute the core of the respective models. ‘DEF-CF’ etc. denote the mature versions of these definitions, while
‘DEF-CF0’ etc. are earlier and less mature versions. I will clearly indicate when
additional clauses (e.g. a time clause) are implicitly understood in these definitions.
(These implicit clauses are less essential and it is likely that they only play a role in
folk applications of the notion of cause.)

5.2 A ‘Counterfactual’ Definition for the Neuron Diagrams
of Paul and Hall (2013)
Let us first introduce the basic counterfactual definition of cause, which seems, in its
essence, quite powerful. In most academic texts, this definition is assumed to concern
event-causation; I will show in Sect. 6 how to generalise it to type-causation. Unless
otherwise stated, I will assume that ‘events’ happen in definite space–time points;
with caution this assumption can be generalised to include space–time regions,5 but
5 To be precise, whether events refer to space–time points or rather regions will be seen to depend on

the context of inquiry, in other words the perspective the ‘cause-inquirer’ assumes when considering
the event of which (s)he desires to know the cause (see following sections). Examples of events

5.2 A ‘Counterfactual’ Definition for the Neuron Diagrams of Paul and Hall …

119

we better start with the simple case. Note that this convention about what ‘events’
are corresponds to its use in physics (where events can be associated to properties
assuming a value in a given space–time point or region), but also in probability theory
as applied to physics.6 Suppose, then, that two events X and Y occur; we want to
find out whether X is a cause of Y. According to the basic counterfactual account, in
order to answer this question one has to evaluate what would happen if X would not
occur. If in this counterfactual situation also Y does not occur, then X is a cause of
Y.
This idea seems to correspond to deeply entrenched causal intuitions. I consider
the cause of the fire in the forest to be the fact that I threw a match, because I know
from experience that if I had not thrown the match, the forest would not have been
set on fire. I believe that the cause of the premature death of my flowers is the fact
that I did not water them, because experience tells me that if I do water them, they
flourish. Thus, in such causal assessments, we imagine a counterfactual scenario
about ‘not-X’ happening. But not just any scenario. A little thinking quickly makes
clear that we change X to not-X, so to speak, and we evaluate the consequences, but
at the same time we control our imagination and we do not change other events and
conditions of the real scenario we are assessing besides those that are a consequence
of not-X. In the example of the forest fire, the counterfactual scenario I consider is
one without my match being thrown, and in which ‘all other things that can be held
constant are held constant’, so in which all other things, not related to not-X, are
happening as they happened in the real scenario—not a scenario in which someone
else throws a match, or a lightning sets fire to the forest etc. We should carefully
control our imagination.
It may well be that an imaginative process like the one just sketched goes on in our
mind (often automatically, unconsciously) when we assess (new) causal situations;
more to the point, that something like this lies at the basis of a definition of the
concept of cause. For now, let me just emphasise that it is far from trivial to make
this clause ‘all other things held constant’, in short ‘ceteris paribus’, precise. Great
efforts have been dedicated to it; most famous is D. Lewis’s theory based on his
‘possible world semantics’ for counterfactuals (Lewis, 1973). But these efforts have
been unsuccessful, according to now widespread agreement (Menzies & Beebee,
2020; Paul & Hall, 2013). I will argue in this chapter that if we modify the basic
counterfactual definition judiciously, notably by making the ‘ceteris paribus’ clause
precise, it becomes solid and can solve the existing problems. This task will ask quite
some synthetic work: we need to scrutinise very many case studies on counterfactual
causation, and make a detour over competing definitions.
Let us formalise the rudimentary model (DEF-CF0) as follows (in this section X
and Y are events on token level; both are assumed to occur):
in space–time regions are: ‘the drought in region R in summer 2023’; ‘the magnetic field H in
space–time region (T, R) assumes the values H0 ’, where T is a given time interval and R a given
region in space and H0 is a range of values each corresponding to the field in the space–time points
in (T, R); ‘the revolution in (T, R)’, etc.
6 Recall from the chapter on probability that a probabilistic ‘event’ corresponds to some experimental
outcome – a property of a system – assuming a certain value (in some space–time point or region).

120

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

X is a cause of Y
iff
if X were not to occur, Y would not occur.
(DEF-CF0)
We have just seen hints that DEF-CF0 might well need upgrading by making the
ceteris-paribus clause explicit:
X is a cause of Y
iff
if X were not to occur, Y would not occur (ceteris paribus).
(DEF-CF0’)
For the time being, I leave the ceteris-paribus clause in DEF-CF0’ undefined,
or loosely defined as done above. And I specify that X should happen before Y
(or at least, should not start after Y): there is an (implicit) time-clause in DEFCF0 and DEF-CF0’. In all my subsequent definitions I will assume this temporal
requirement to be included. The reason is straightforward: our intuition commands
it, and without this clause, a vast number of problems can simply not be solved.
Thus, one conclusion of this chapter will be the very unspectacular thesis stating that
effects never come before their causes. I believe (and will argue below) that there is
no successful theory of bona fide causation/causality, be it in philosophy or physics,
that concludes otherwise.7 Let us then bite the bullet and start solving problems.
There is a vast literature arguing that the simple DEF-CF0 does not work in
many cases: it leads to results that go against strong causal intuitions; the same is
true for DEF-CF0’. Paul and Hall (2013) have compiled and discussed a majority
of such problems, building on the work of many. They treat in great detail topics as
‘early preemption’, ‘late preemption’, ‘tampering’, ‘overdetermination’, ‘causation
by omission’, ‘double prevention’, transitivity, etc. In a majority of cases, the authors
study these problems with the help of ‘neuron diagrams’, which represent causal
situations in a compact visual form.8 The book by Paul and Hall is of great utility,
for I can use it to attempt a first synthesis: to propose a definition that works for all
the cases studied in this book (or at least a great majority).
A representative neuron diagram is given in Fig. 5.1.
In the diagram of Fig. 5.1 five subsystems, represented by five neurons, are in
causal interaction in the following way. Neuron C emits a stimulating signal (represented by an arrow) towards neuron D, which subsequently fires; D likewise sends a
stimulating signal to E (all firing/ ‘on’ neurons are shaded). A stimulates B but B is
inhibited to fire due to the inhibitory signal from C, represented by a line with a black
7 I exclude retrocausality, since there is too little evidence that it can solve problems in coherence

with physics; on the contrary, it leads to well-known logical paradoxes that constitute a weighty
no-go argument (more in Chapter 9).
8 Even if these diagrams can represent a sizeable portion of the problems discussed in the analytic
literature, Paul and Hall emphasise that they surely do not capture all relevant features of causation.
I agree; hence the importance of a further synthesis.

5.2 A ‘Counterfactual’ Definition for the Neuron Diagrams of Paul and Hall …

Fig. 5.1 Typical neuron
diagram (reproduced from
Fig. 1, Paul & Hall, 2013, p.
ix), explained in the text.
‘Firing’ or ‘on’ neurons are
shaded

dot or blob.9 The temporal order goes from left to right: C and A fire at t1, B and D
react at t2, E reacts at t3, where t1 < t2 < t3. Note that the (causal) connections, so
the functioning or meaning of the diagram, can be described without using the word
‘cause’. Therefore, if we manage to construct a definition of cause that allows us to
extract the cause of neuron events (like the firing of neuron E), we have a reductive
definition.
Looking at Fig. 5.1, most people would agree that the cause(s) of E’s firing are the
firing of C and/or D. Or if they wish to be more precise, they could say that (the firing
of) C is the ‘root’ cause at t1 and D the ‘proximate’ cause at t2. Intuitively, the firing
of A would not be identified as a cause of E’s firing, because its action is blocked by
C. In the literature, the firing of A is called a ‘preempted back-up cause’. From now
on, I will use following convention: capitals as X (Y, A, B, …) will in principle refer
to events, i.e. the X-neuron firing or non-firing, which I will sometimes abbreviate for
clarity as X+ and X-, respectively. When no confusion can exist I will occasionally
also indicate the X-neuron (the neuron at which the event X occurs) simply with ‘X’.
Actually, Fig. 5.1 represents one of the most discussed cases in the recent analytical
literature, called early preemption. It is singularly interesting, because it constitutes
a challenge to the basic counterfactual model; and, especially, we will see in the
following that a great number of problems studied in Paul and Hall (2013) can be
solved if one solves this case. Why does DEF-CF0 run against intuition in this case?
It works well when it comes to identifying D+ as a cause of E+: if D would not fire,
E would not. Similarly, DEF-CF0 leads correctly to the conclusion that A+ is not
a cause of E+. But looking at the diagram and recalling what it represents, it seems
clear that if C would not happen, E would still happen, due to the back-up neuron A.
This scenario corresponds to Fig. 2 in Paul & Hall, 2013, p. ix, reproduced as diagram
4 in Table 5.1 below. Even if we call DEF-CF0’ and its ceteris-paribus clause to the
rescue, and examine what would happen if C would not happen ceteris paribus,10 E
would still happen due to A. So the basic definitions DEF-CF0 and DEF-CF0’ lead
to the wrong conclusion that C is not a cause of E.
9 In the words of Paul and Hall: “Arrows represent token-level stimulatory connections between

neurons; lines ending with black dots represent token-level inhibitory connections” (2013, p. 10).
10 One notices here that the ‘ceteris paribus’ clause is C-relative (or, if one prefers, relative to the

time at which C occurs, namely t1 in Fig. 5.1). Indeed, what this clause exactly means seems
clear: ‘all events occurring at the time of C and before, except C, remaining fixed’. That is how
the counterfactual reasoning is usually done, for instance very often, even if not systematically, in
(Paul and Hall 2013). I will define the ceteris-paribus clause along this line in the following.

Here is a concrete example of early preemption (Hitchcock, 2007): “Assassin
poisons Victim’s coffee. Victim drinks it and dies. If Assassin hadn’t poisoned the
coffee, Backup would have, and Victim would have died anyway. Victim would not
have died if there had been no poison in the coffee.” In this example Assassin’s action
preempts Backup from poisoning Victim.11 Intuitively, Victim’s death (Y in DEFCF0) is caused by Assassin’s action (X), not by Backup’s, since that is preempted.
But DEF-CF0 gives the wrong result.
To solve this and other cases, we need to introduce some definitions. First, notice
that there is something remarkable about Fig. 5.1, a feature that is absent from most
diagrams from (Paul & Hall, 2013). There is an ‘ambivalent loop’ starting at the
active neuron C: along the path C-D-E only stimulating signals are emitted towards
the final event E+; along the path C-B-E, also starting at C and ending in E, there is an
inhibitory signal—one that is backed-up by A. So, from C to E there is, so to speak,
an ambivalent converging fork, consisting of one stimulating path, one inhibiting;
with the extra feature that the inhibited neuron is backed-up (many philosophers
11 To make the correspondence with Fig. 5.1, one could take, for example: C + = Assassin decides

to put poison in Victim’s coffee (and notifies Backup); D + = Assassin puts poison in Victim’s
coffee; E + = Victim drinks the coffee and dies; A + = Backup decides to put poison in Victim’s
coffee; B + = Backup puts poison in Victim’s coffee (unless he gets a notification from Assassin).

128

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

noticed this feature, of course). Now, I will show in the following that only when
such an ambivalent loop is present in a neuron diagram, one needs to go beyond
simple counterfactual ideas. Moreover, I will argue that these exceptional cases can
further be unified with all other diagrams, and with other major definitions through
a higher-level theory.
Let us make this precise with a few definitions, all relative to neuron diagrams
as just defined (all this could be done slightly more formally, but at this point there
seems nothing to gain with it):
def-an (ambivalent neuron): A ‘Y-ambivalent’ neuron is: (i) either an active
neuron from which two paths start towards neuron Y (i.e. converge on Y or on
an active neuron that connects to Y in the forward direction), of which one path
contains only stimulating arrows, and one path one inhibiting connection towards
a (necessarily inhibited) neuron, which receives a (back-up) stimulating signal
from an active off-path neuron; (ii) or an active neuron that stimulates (directly
or via other stimulating neurons) an active neuron that complies with (i).
In Fig. 5.1 the C-neuron is an E-ambivalent neuron. Other Y-ambivalent neurons
are the C-neurons in diagrams 8, 10, 17, 19, 20 and 24 in Table 5.1; ‘Y’ corresponds
to the last neuron in these diagrams (usually E). In case (i) a ‘neuron that connects to
Y in the forward direction’ is a neuron from which there is path to Y via connections
that only go forward in time. Case (ii) in def-an is illustrated in diagram 24 (this is
the diagram of Fig. 38 of Paul & Hall, 2013).
Using def-an we can propose a definition for cause in neuron diagrams. Actually,
we can and should distinguish single-neuron causes and multiple-neuron causes;
in (Paul & Hall, 2013) almost exclusively single-neuron causes are considered.
Multiple-neuron causes refer to sets of events that are all simultaneous, so happen at
the same time (concretely, in Table 5.1 I list causes at t1 only). As will be seen in the
following, when hunting for causes, by far the most useful clause in def-sn below
is the counterfactual clause ‘¬X implies ¬Y, ceteris paribus’: most of the time it is
this ‘simple counterfactual’ (SCF) clause one needs, not the one about ambivalent
neurons. It is stipulated that these definitions refer (exclusively) to neuron diagrams,
which are assumed to represent actual situations, and that the causal identification
has to be done by a (mental/theoretical/counterfactual) operation involving inverting
(‘¬’) X, to be specified in the definition of the ceteris-paribus clause infra. Here is
the definition:
1) def-sn (single-neuron cause): A single-neuron event X is a single-neuron
cause of Y (also a single-neuron event) iff either the X-neuron is a Y-ambivalent
neuron, or ¬X implies ¬Y (ceteris paribus).
2) def-mn (multiple-neuron cause): A multiple-neuron event {X1 ,…, Xn },
consisting of n ≥ 2 simultaneous single events, is a multiple-neuron cause (a
set of ‘joint’ causes) of the single-neuron event Y iff none of X1 ,…, Xn is a
single-neuron cause (in the sense of def-sn) and the conjoined {¬X1 ,…, ¬Xn }
form a minimum set of events that together imply ¬Y (ceteris paribus).

5.2 A ‘Counterfactual’ Definition for the Neuron Diagrams of Paul and Hall …

129

Here ‘¬X’ can be read ‘not-X’ and represents event X not happening, i.e. ‘¬’
operates the switching of X+ and X-. In def-mn the multiple-neuron event X consists
of n simultaneous single-neuron events X = {X1 ,…, Xn }; ¬X = {¬X1 ,…, ¬Xn }
corresponds to the set of events in which each Xi is switched. The ceteris-paribus
clause stipulates how exactly the inverting has to be effectuated, in other words, how
the associated counterfactual model has to be built. Here goes its definition12 (X
can be single or multiple events): given a (factual) neuron diagram (representing a
scenario of factual interactions), the inverting of neuron event X corresponds to a
counterfactual scenario, starting at the time of X, in which X+ and X- are switched and
all ‘forward’ consequences of this switching implemented ceteris paribus, so while
keeping all other events, not related to X, fixed. ‘Forward consequences’ are those
occurring after, not before, the time of occurrence of X; in any case, the counterfactual
scenario does not take into account what happens before X. (How to implement
consequences of switching and which events are related to each other is simply
stipulated by the rules that come with the meaning of the diagrams.13 )
Thus, this clause or rule implies that in the counterfactual scenario (used to decide
whether X is a cause of some posterior event Y) one can neglect all events that happen
before X; this is a mental, imagined scenario, in that it is a variant of the (model of)
reality one wishes to causally dissect. As one example, the ceteris-paribus clause
implies that in diagram 5 of Table 5.1 F- is a cause of E+: F+ implies E- if one keeps
fixed all F-unrelated events in the path A-E.
One will note that these definitions certainly share some commonalities with other
published accounts of causation. First of all, if one disregards ambivalent neurons,
def-sn is quite similar to DEF-CF0 and DEF-CF0’—at least under a broad interpretation, to be elaborated. Next, the idea of a ‘minimal set’ in def-mn resonates with
‘minimal sufficiency’ accounts14 (e.g. Paul & Hall, 2013, p. 16). Finally, the way we
build the counterfactual scenario resembles to some extent the way it is done by other
proponents of the counterfactual interpretation (e.g. passages in Paul & Hall, 2013,
p. 20, 21, etc.) So def-sn and def-mn are inspired by ingredients of other models.
But, to the best of my knowledge, these ingredients have not been combined in the
way here proposed.
Using these quite straightforward definitions—they become in any case straightforward with some practice –, I have identified in Table 5.1 the causes of 26 representative diagrams, many of which are discussed in Paul and Hall (2013); the table also
contains new diagrams not discussed there. Specifically, I have indicated the root
cause(s) (so the causes at time t1) of the last event of each diagram, as definitions
def-sn and def-mn deliver them.

12 I will make it very explicit; interestingly, it sounds a bit clumsy, presumably because our brain is

trained to do this operation very quickly and very routinely. We do counterfactual thinking all the
time, when planning, conceiving, deliberating, researching, and fantasising.
13 To take the simplest example, if two active neurons X and Y are only connected via a stimulating
connection, ¬X will imply ¬Y, a scenario in which both X and Y are off.
14 Actually, the requirement that the set be minimal is only needed for diagram 16 in Table 1.

130

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

These results should be the correct ones: they comply with intuition, and correspond often, but not always, to the ones favoured by Paul and Hall. Most importantly, while in (Paul & Hall, 2013) they are left as un-unified problem cases, here
they all follow from one unified model. This is the main result of this section. As
a consequence of this first synthesis, the proposed model can solve a majority of
problems discussed in Paul and Hall (see R1–R4 below), but not all; for some, I will
need a second synthesis: a model unifying def-sn and def-mn with other powerful
definitions.
As an example, the table indicates that in diagram 1 the root cause of E+ is C+,
that in diagram 3 the root cause of E- is C-, etc. A third column displays agreement
or disagreement with the verdict given in Paul and Hall (2013). Note that in the
literature the causes at the initial time t1 are usually discussed, rather than at t2 etc.,
since causes closer to the last event are much easier to retrieve, since more intuitive—
accordingly, the SCF rule applies to them. So I focus on the most difficult and most
discussed case, even if our definitions work for all times, as can easily be verified.
Several points deserve to be highlighted before I comment on some interesting
corollaries. First, the vast majority of causes of single-neuron events in these diagrams
are themselves single-neuron causes (especially if one considers also causes at times
t > t1, not indicated in Table 5.1). So def-sn is more needed or useful than defmn, which can explain why multiple-neuron causes are much less discussed in the
literature. Yet, it will be seen that def-mn is definitely needed. And indeed, in real-life
situations it is often as important to know joint causes as single causes, as any medical
doctor, scientist, engineer or detective will confirm (see examples further on). Next,
the table shows that there are only seven ambivalent neurons (the archetype being
neuron C in diagram 1). Therefore, the vast majority of causes are identified with the
‘simple counterfactual’ (SCF in Table 5.1) clause stipulated in def-sn (namely: ¬X
implies ¬Y, ceteris paribus). Simple counterfactual inverting, much as the basic DEFCF0 has it, leads most of the time to correct causes! I will explain and corroborate
this point in Sect. 6.
Let us now highlight a few further results (R1–R4) that follow from the above
definitions. A full discussion of these and other problems discussed in the literature
will have to await Sect. 6, after the final synthesis.
R1. Double prevention and transitivity. Diagrams 5 and 12 are examples of ‘double
prevention’ (e.g. Schaffer, 2000), considered a particularly problematic case in the
literature. Our model corroborates the intuitions of many philosophers (e.g. Hall,
2000; Hitchcock, 2001; Paul & Hall, 2013, Ch. 5) that transitivity is violated in
neuron diagrams (as defined above and in Paul & Hall, 2013). Our model confirms
that in diagram 5 C+ is a cause of D+, which is a cause of F-, which is a cause of E+
(all this follows straightforwardly from the definitions). Yet C+ is not a cause of E+,
as again follows from the model. Other well-known accounts struggle with this case
(Paul & Hall, 2013, p 82ff ). I will come back to this key problem in Sect. 6: actually,
the violation of transitivity appears to be an artefact of the neuron diagrams, rather
than a fundamental property.

5.2 A ‘Counterfactual’ Definition for the Neuron Diagrams of Paul and Hall …

131

Note that my model also solves more complicated cases of multiple prevention as
in diagram 20. A different and more complex route (‘de facto dependence’) to double
prevention has been taken for instance in Hitchcock (2001), discussed in Paul and
Hall (2013, p. 222ff ). My model is robust against similar variants, some gathered in
Table 5.1.
R2. Multiple-neuron or joint causes. Definition def-mn identifies in three diagrams
(13, 16 and 24) multi-neuron or ‘joint’ causes. First of all, and interestingly enough,
diagram 16 (a variant of 13, but not discussed in the literature) offers a weighty
argument that such joint causes must exist. Indeed, it is intuitive that in this diagram
there are no single-neuron causes of E- at t1. The simple counterfactual rule implies
that neither A-, nor G+, nor C- are causes, in agreement with intuition. However,
that same rule, the essence of def-mn, does identify {A-,C-} and {A-,G+} as joint
causes, so sets of causes that together, in cooperation, cause E-. There must be a cause
of E- at t1: these diagrams are conceived in such a way that if something happens
at t2, t3, or t4, it must be because of something happening (or not happening) at t1.
So if there are no single-neuron causes, the only possibility left is that joint causes
exist; in diagram 16 these are {A-,C-} and {A-,G+}. Thus def-mn gives a unified
solution to problem cases as diagrams 13, 16 and 24 (Note that, in diagram 16, the
SCF rule on its own would imply that {A-, G+, C-} are joint causes; in all diagrams
of Table 5.1, if X and Y are causes according to our definitions, {¬X, ¬Y} also invert
the last event, and could, if one so desires, also be called a multiple-event cause. This
is in agreement with the intuition that doubling causes can only guarantee the effect
even more. But the minimality requirement of def-mn comes to the clearly better
conclusion that one should look for minimal sets, in this case {A-,C-} and {A-,G+}).
Similarly, in diagram 13 (Fig. 41 in Paul & Hall, 2013) one finds that {G-, C+}
is a multi-neuron cause of E+, a possibility not envisaged in (Paul & Hall, 2013).
A+ is a separate cause, in a clear sense. Here is a further strong argument that such
joint causes, as I defined them, should correspond to something real: the diagrams in
Table 5.1 can be implemented electronically, using light-emitting diodes as neurons.
In such electronic diagrams stimulating and inhibiting signals are carried by electric
current, and the on and off states of a neuron correspond to the LED being on or off,
so emitting or not. One can imagine circuits that have, next to each neuron-LED,
a switch that allows an experimenter to deliberately switch the LEDs on and off—
corresponding to the mental switching (‘¬’) of the definitions. Intuitively, hunting
for causes (at t1) would also here be hunting for those events or event sets that,
(deliberately) inverted, invert the state of the last LED. And it is quite intuitive, and
could be tested in the lab, that in such a circuit materialising diagram 13, A+ and
{G-, C+} are separate causes. A mundane but somewhat contrived example of this
diagram can also be found, and comes to the same conclusion.15
15 A + represents the evil A setting fire to a forest, leading to the burning of a house (E + ),

the intended effect of A and his accomplice D. The other neurons represent fireguards that can,
in principle, ‘activate’ (+) colleagues to extinguish fires, for instance by calling them by phone.
Suppose only G and C sit in watchtowers and can, in principle, see the fire; F is the fireguard
responsible of the zone of the fire and the house. D + stands for D, the accomplice, deciding to put
