5 A Unified Definition of Cause

Finally, diagram 24 is also discussed at length by Paul and Hall (2013, p. 187ff ).
With def-sn and def-mn one finds that the causes are C+ and {F-, A+}, which seems
a different verdict than the one proposed by Paul and Hall (2013, p. 188), who seem
to leave the case undecided. (Diagram 24 is the only case in Table 5.1 for which one
needs clause (ii) of def-an of ambivalent neuron.)
R3. Late preemption. This type of causal relation, schematised in diagram 25, is
highly debated in the literature since D. Lewis’s work, and considered one of the
most problematic cases (e.g. Paul & Hall, 2013, p. 99, Menzies & Beebee, 2020).
The typical example is that of C throwing a rock (C+) at a bottle and shattering it
(E+); A also throws a rock but a little later, so that A’s rock sails through empty
space but would have shattered the bottle if C’s hadn’t. The intuitive cause of E+ is
C+. Our model solves it in coherence with the previous cases: suffices to recall that
events are located in space–time points, an assumption I made from the start, for
reasons explained at the beginning of this section. Note that this assumption is well
in accordance with the common-sense interpretation of the ‘events’ in the bottleshattering scenario just sketched. Thus E+ stands for the shattering of the bottle at
a given time t2 (and at a given place); the shattering would not have happened at
t2 if A’s throw would have shattered the bottle, since it comes late. Thus def-sn
works. (My last word on late preemption will be given by a model based on a further
synthesis.) The definition similarly solves the example of a bell ringing twice (Paul &
Hall, 2013, p. 101ff ).
Note how simple this solution to late preemption is in comparison with other
proposals, such as Lewis’s and several other experts’ (reviews in Paul & Hall, 2013,
p. 99, Menzies & Beebee, 2020). Of course, I am only entitled to so much optimism
because these solutions are consistent with results obtained within the next-level
synthesis, and thus presumably with the MCT, the maximally coherent theory, as
shown in Sect. 6.
R4. Overdetermination. A self-explaining example of the reputedly ambiguous
‘overdetermination’ (cf. detailed discussion in Paul & Hall, 2023, Ch. 3 §5) is given
in diagram 26. Using the SCF clause of def-mn, one finds that {C+, A+} are joint
causes of E+. So, for this diagram, as defined in the literature and above, neither C+
nor A+ are separate causes. This is also the solution obtained with my final model.
Tampering, black-box cases and other problems discussed in the literature within
the neuron diagram paradigm will also be seen to be handled correctly by the final
model.

F asleep (F-), but only if he receives a call from C confirming the fire, so that his evil action is not
in vain. C does call (C + ), thus deciding D to get active (D + ). C also calls B, her boss, who calls
F, in vain. G is an absent-minded guard, who normally should have called B. In this scenario A +
is clearly a cause of E + ; the only other scenario (besides A-) in which E + would not happen is
when G would be active (G + ) and at the same time C would not call D (C-) (so that D remains
inactive). Hence, intuitively, A + and the joint set {G-, C + } are causes of E + , as the model
stipulates.

5.3 ‘Functional’ Definition of Cause in Physics. Representation-Relativity

133

For all diagrams of Table 5.1, the problem-solving capacity and relative simplicity
of def-sn and def-mn, and, especially, their compatibility with an even more encompassing definition (Sect. 6), confer confidence that the solutions they deliver are the
correct ones—to the point that one can rely on them in less intuitive cases, as represented in say diagrams 13, 20 or 24. There are several ‘experimental’ ways to further
corroborate these definitions (independently of the absorption in a stronger model).
As said, the definitions could be verified by electronic realisations of the diagrams.
Next, neuron diagrams and these definitions can be programmed by a computer code.
Assuming that the ‘ambivalent neuron’ clause can definitely be proven to be valid
also for diagrams that are more complicated than those of Table 5.1, the definitions
could then allow solving diagrams that are much more complicated than those in
(Paul & Hall, 2013), say containing thousands of neurons instead of the 14 or less
in the diagrams in Table 5.1. There must be diagrams that can only be solved in
an impractically long time by humans, but in a fraction of a second by a computer.
Finally, in (Vervoort & Nikolaev, 2025) and (Vervoort et al., 2023) it is shown that
AI based on artificial neural nets (so-called Large Language Models, LLMs) already
goes quite far in correctly identifying causes in neuron diagrams (as verified using
def-sn and def-mn); unfortunately it is not fully transparent how LLMs do this.16
At any rate, assessing the evolution of AI’s causal reasoning skills is both a strategic
and fascinating effort, where philosophy has a key role to play: only synthetic efforts
can provide the ‘gold standard’ to evaluate AI’s causal verdicts against.
One obvious point to observe regarding definitions def-sn and def-mn, is that
they have, of course, a touch of ad-hoc-ness. It seems clear that they cannot serve as
general definitions of cause for general contexts—even if this is the ambition of the
original DEF-CF0 and DEF-CF0’ from which they originate. It is the ‘ambivalent
neuron’ clause that is responsible for this ad-hoc-ness. Therefore, one hopes that
this clause can be disposed of in a second synthesis. To see how this could be done,
let us look at other important accounts of cause: they might bring us the necessary
inspiration.

5.3 ‘Functional’ Definition of Cause in Physics.
Representation-Relativity
Within synthetic philosophy it is mandatory to be coherent with physics—with
current physical theory—since physics deals with the ‘purest’, simplest systems
and is therefore more easily systematised. Hence it is most likely to be true, most
likely to represent something real, at least to some degree — according to the realist.
To put it in romantic words, physics is philosophy’s best friend.

16 LLMs function in essence by statistical text completion, not by logical reasoning. But such models

could be enhanced with logical reasoning models.

134

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

In physics it appears often easy to identify causes, whether on token (event) or
type (property, variable) level.17 The cause of the increase in volume V of a gas in
such-and-such conditions may be the heat H transferred to the gas; in short H causes
V. (On token level this sounds less congenial: event e1 causes event e2, where e1 =
the event consisting of an amount of heat h being transferred to a gas in a certain
time lapse, and e2 = the event consisting of a gas assuming a volume v at a certain
time; h and v are numbers.) The lasing of a semiconductor diode may well be said to
be caused by the electrical current injected in the diode, causing first a ‘population
inversion’ in the electronic energy levels, and subsequently the emission of laser
light. When evaluating a particle reaction, a physicist could say something in this
style: “the production of particle X is caused by the collision of particles Y and Z”
(for instance, X may be a gamma ray, Y an electron and Z a positron).
Now, as several philosophers of physics have noted (e.g. Schlick (1949), Mach
(1960) p. 325, Suppes (1970) p. 46, p. 67 and references therein, Bunge (2009)
Chapter 4.1 and references therein), there are good reasons to believe that in physics
deterministic causal relations are functional relations. Indeed, the volume V of a
gas is a function of its temperature and therefore of the heat H transferred to it (in
symbols: V = f (H)). This idea well parallels the intuitive idea that H causes V. The
laser light intensity is a function of the injected current. The energy of particle X is
a function of the energy of particles Y and Z.
And indeed, associating causes with arguments in functions is precisely what
physicists do in the only chapter of physics from which a definition of cause can immediately be extracted. There is one chapter that explicitly deals with the notion of cause,
namely the foundations of quantum mechanics, more precisely Bell’s theorem and
its ancestor, the Einstein–Podolsky–Rosen paradox (Jammer (1974), Bell (1964)).
There are a few other sub-disciplines that use the concept of causality, notably relativity theory, as we will see in Chapter 9. But no definition of cause can be distilled
from them, at least not as immediately as from Bell’s seminal paper. (John Bell’s
paper of 1964 is one of the most cited articles in physics—which is highly remarkable, since it has at least in part a philosophical content, related to the question of
causation. Bell’s quasi-philosophical work laid the basis for new branches of science,
quantum cryptography and quantum information theory.) Note again that the scarcity
of the concept of cause in theoretical physics textbooks contrasts with the practice
of physicists—in their daily work they use it all the time.
Let us have a quick look at the article in which Bell derives his famous inequality.
[A brief intermezzo for those interested: Bell’s inequality allows for demarcating
quantum from classical physics. Classical physics is causal physics, in a sense defined
below. Bell has shown that (i) all classical theories must satisfy the Bell inequality in
a certain experiment—at least if they are ‘local’, i.e. not relying on faster-than-light
interaction/communication, as any known theory; (ii) quantum mechanics violates
the inequality by a certain factor. In 1964 it was not clear what the experimental
outcome would be: would the inequality be violated or not? The experiment, quite
17 The somewhat subtle interplay between definitions of cause on type and token level is well

explained in many texts, e.g. in Woodward (2003), e.g. p. 39–41 and p. 74ff .

5.3 ‘Functional’ Definition of Cause in Physics. Representation-Relativity

135

sophisticated, was done many years after Bell’s analysis, and it demonstrated that the
quantum prediction is correct—the inequality was violated by the amount calculated
by Bell. The precise upshot is still debated, but the usual conclusion is that ‘nature is
not classically local’ (cf. e.g. Myrvold et al., 2024). This interesting chapter of physics
goes of course beyond the scope of this book, but we will briefly return to it in the
Epilogue—discussing the principle of causality. My own preferred interpretation of
Bell’s results can be found in Nikolaev and Vervoort (2023) and Vervoort (2013b).]
Bell’s article begins as follows: “The paradox of Einstein, Podolsky and Rosen was
advanced as an argument that quantum mechanics could not be a complete theory but
should be supplemented by additional variables. These additional variables were to
restore to the theory causality and locality.” For us, the question is: how do physicists
believe that ‘causality can be restored to the theory’, or rather, how exactly do they
conceive of the notion of cause? Bell does not give a fully explicit definition, but it
is easy to make it explicit from his use of a few simple mathematical expressions: a
physical variable or property Y is described in a deterministic manner, or is caused,
if it can be expressed as a function of some other variable(s), say the set or n-tuple
of variables X; in symbols,18 if Y = f (X).
So, for the physics community the question of what (physical) causes are seems
largely unproblematic—at least in a deterministic setting. The secondary literature
on Bell’s paper is enormous, but as far as I know no physicist nor philosopher
of physics has ever contested Bell’s definition of (deterministic) cause. Of course,
things are arguably simpler in physics than in other cognitive domains and in everyday life—where functional dependences usually cannot be established (in any case
not with certainty, and even less by a precise mathematical expression as in physics).
Now, since within synthetic philosophy there is such a huge incentive to be coherent
with physics whenever possible, the aim of the remainder of this chapter could be
summarised thus: to look for a general definition, one that can also be applied in
real life when functional dependences are not known or not known to exist, and that
reduces to the definition of cause in physics.
In this context, it seems interesting to note that the concept of ‘function’ is one
of the most essential and most frequently used concepts of mathematics, where it is
given a precise definition (e.g. Rudin, 1976; Royden, 1988, Ch. 1). Functions from
a set X to a set Y associate (‘map’) to each element of X precisely one element of
Y. In this sense, if the element of X and the function are known, the element of Y
is also known—hence determined, one might say. This notion of determination is,
of course, strongly resonating with that of causation (which explains Bell’s interpretation sketched above). Functions are literally everywhere in mathematics, but also
in physics; to the point that a majority of physics problems can be said to be chases
for functions. This is quite obvious: solving the (deterministic) equations of physics
allows one to discover how some quantity Y varies as a function of other variables
X—how Y is determined by X. If the time-domain of these other variables does not
18 Bell actually writes A = f (λ), where λ are the so-called ‘hidden variables’: yet unknown variables

that would, if real, ‘complete’ quantum mechanics and make a causal theory of it. The expression
A = f (λ) is used in Bell’s derivation of his inequality.

136

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

come after that of Y, physicists often call the variables X the causes of Y. In a slogan,
hunting for functions is hunting for causes. The ubiquity of functional dependencies
in physics (and mathematics) is then an intuitive argument for the ubiquity of causation, for the reality of the causal principle. But I will stop this digression here, even
if I believe it is revealing and interesting (my last word on it in Chapter 9).
Let us now make the above ideas more precise, and construct an explicit definition.
The simplest variant of functional definition of cause would be, on type level:
X is a cause of Y
iff
∃ function f : Y = f (X).
(DEF-F0)
(As said, in this section on physics, X and Y denote physical properties, more
precisely variables.) For reasons to be explained, I will not include so-called
‘physical constants’ among the variables, just as is usually done in physics (hence
their different name). For instance, the gravitational constant g is usually not
considered a variable but a constant, for self-explaining reasons. Constants occur in
the equations used to derive functional expressions, so appear in the functions
resulting from solving the equations, so could a priori be included among the
causes; but this is not what is usually done. More on this point in a moment, when I
discuss Maxwell’s equations as an example.
On token level DEF-F0 becomes: “The event represented by variable X assuming
a value x is a cause of the event represented by the variable Y assuming a value y
iff Y = f (X) (and hence in particular y = f (x))”. (Note that such an expression only
makes sense if x is one of the possible values of the property X and y of Y as they
describe the system under scrutiny; so this is assumed.) One may write the definition
on token level thus:
Xx is a cause of Yy
iff
∃ function f : Y = f (X) (and hence y = f (x)).
(DEF-F0’)
To complete DEF-F0 and DEF-F0’, which really are one and the same definition,19
a few add-ons are due, two rather trivial, and one essential. First, as already stipulated
in the previous section, this definition has an implicit time-clause, stipulating that the
time-domain of X (or more precisely of Xx , i.e. of X assuming the value x) should be
anterior, or at least should not start after, the time-domain of Y (or Yy ). Without this
clause, DEF-F0 would imply that causation is symmetric. Mathematically speaking,
functions (as they occur in physics) can usually be inverted, so that from the bare
DEF-F0 would follow that X is also a function of Y, and therefore caused by Y. But
this goes against the usual intuitions: for instance, in the above example we accept
that heat causes volume increase, but not the reciprocal.
19 Because, in physics, it makes no sense to consider properties X that would have no range of

values x assigned to them; the two definitions imply each other.

5.3 ‘Functional’ Definition of Cause in Physics. Representation-Relativity

137

It deserves some further study whether it is judicious to define the time-clause in
its less stringent variant, stating that the time-domain of X (or Xx ) ‘should not start
after’ the time-domain of Y (or Yy ), rather than ‘should be anterior’. This allows
for the possibility of simultaneous cause and effect. But in many, and perhaps all,
physical contexts the so-called initial conditions that define the time-evolution of the
system under scrutiny allow one to identify an unambiguous and asymmetric causeeffect relation. These initial conditions are needed to solve the equations. Consider
e.g. following case: what is the cause of the fact that this ball remains motionless on
this table? We are in a physics context; we are looking at a physical event consisting
of the movement of a ball on a table; more precisely we ask what is the cause of the
event consisting of this ball having a vanishing 3-dimensional velocity on this table
at time T or in time interval ΔT. Physics teaches us that the movement of the ball at
time T depends on the forces being exerted on it at time T (in this case gravity and
the equal reaction force of the table, adding up to a total force equal to zero) and on
the velocity at some arbitrary moment before T (since the force and velocity at time
T are zero the preceding or ‘initial’ velocity of the ball also must have been zero,
according to Newton’s second law of motion). So one could say the causes of the
considered event (the non-moving of the ball at T) are the absence of total force on
the ball and the absence of initial velocity. The vanishing force could be considered
a simultaneous cause, but the vanishing initial velocity is a preceding cause; so as a
whole the cause is anterior to the effect.
In quantum mechanics, there exist cases where simultaneous cause and effect
seem, a priori, to make sense. If two particles are in a so-called ‘spin-singlet’ state,
the spin X of one particle is always opposite to that of the other (Y), so that X = −
Y, a particularly simple functional expression trivially implying that Y = −X. Do
both spins cause each other? The other option is to stick to causes that come before
their effects. According to the latter version of the definition, and according to the
current status of quantum theory (judged to be not fully mature by Einstein and
fans!), there would be no causes of X and Y: we do not know any function f and
property Z such that X = −Y = f (Z), where Z is anterior to X and Y. Many physicists
even accept that there is a fundamental theorem that prohibits the existence of such
a f and Z: Bell’s theorem. Now, events having no causes is also strange, and the
orthodox no-cause verdict may be due to the provisional nature of quantum theory.
However, a more widely acceptable solution to this conundrum exists. Spins are
quantum so probabilistic properties, and therefore the analysis in terms of functions
may not be adequate. As we will see in Sect. 5, there should be a different definition
of cause (DEF-P) that applies to probabilistic properties. If one analyses the abovesketched anticorrelation of the singlet-state using DEF-P, one comes to the satisfying
conclusion that there are antecedent causes to X and Y: see Chapter 9 §1.
Now, I believe that the temporal aspect of causation is most interestingly discussed
within the context of the most fundamental physical theories, as highlighted by
Ladyman and Ross (2007, §5.4). The first point to observe is that most fundamental
equations of physics are time-symmetric, as recalled by Russell (1912) and Ladyman
and Ross. This indeed suggests that the most general time clause in our definitions
of cause should be: “the time domain of Y should not start before that of X”. General

138

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

relativity, for instance, offers a cogent case where causation is mutual, and where
cause and effect exist simultaneously and are interchangeable, so to speak. Einstein’s
field equations express the deterministic link between the spacetime curvature X and
the energy-matter distribution Y. Both X and Y exist simultaneously, and it is not
obvious what comes first, curvature or energy-matter; this is reflected in Wheeler’s
famous slogan “Spacetime tells matter how to move; matter tells spacetime how to
curve” (Thorne et al., 2000). So at the most fundamental description of reality, the
safest move is to provide for mutual causation. Note, on the other hand, that future
research may come up with theories that do explain ‘what came first’ (on some
natural interpretation). Note also that even the most fundamental theories, when
they are applied to solve concrete problems, are normally supplemented with initial
values that dictate ‘which variable(s) come(s) first’, exactly as in above mechanical
example. Similarly, in electromagnetism concrete problems normally contain the
time sequence that determines the solution: e.g., first an electrical field is switched on,
then the magnetic field is created—even if Maxwell’s equations are time-symmetric
and both fields seems to act simultaneously in these equations. I will come back to
some finer details about the time-clause in Chapter 9—which is, at any rate, not the
essential ingredient of causation as noted at the end of Sect. 1.
The second somewhat trivial implicit clause in DEF-F0 and DEF-F0’ is that X
and Y should refer to, describe, different systems, or at least different properties if the
same system is scrutinised. Without this clause one would find that, for instance, the
speed V of a system at time t1 is a cause of its speed at t2 > t1, which sounds awkward.
Indeed, in deterministic systems V(t2) = f (V(t1)) for some function f . Here is another
example. Suppose a stone falls in a pond, causing circular surface waves to emanate
from the point of impact. What is the cause of these circular surface waves? A more
precise question is: what is the cause of this water surface profile (the height H of the
wave crest) at space–time point20 (x,y,z,t) ? Even more precise is: what is the cause of
the event consisting of the wave assuming a height H at space–time point (x,y,z,t) ?
According to DEF-F0’, supplemented only with the time-clause, any surface profile
H’(x’,y’,z’,t’) taken at a time t’ before t would be a cause of H, because in principle
the theory of fluid mechanics allows for calculating H as a function of H’. But this
is again against our intuition: we usually do not say that an earlier part of the wave
causes a later part; we would of course say that the wave and its parts are caused
by the (impact of the falling) stone. This and other examples seem to indicate that
the causal relation is between different entities, or, more generally, between different
properties, whether these describe different entities or one and the same (as, perhaps,
in the above example from general relativity). That the causal relation usually exists
between different entities complies with the common and ancestral intuition that
things are not self-caused. (This compelling conviction can surely find a scientific
justification in the law of conservation of energy: energy, things, and changes are not
created out of nothing, they come ‘from something else’.) Thus, a second clause that
I will assume to be implicit in DEF-F0/F0’ is that the properties X and Y pertain to
different entities; minimally, that property X property Y. Note that in definitions
20 Or one could consider as event a set of wave heights at a region in space at a certain time.

5.3 ‘Functional’ Definition of Cause in Physics. Representation-Relativity

139

as def-sn for neuron diagrams (or related definitions in the literature) it is also trivial
that X
Y; one doesn’t even bother to mention it. In sum, I believe this second
clause is less essential; it seems that intuition easily takes care of it.
Let us now come to the third specification that needs to be included in DEF-F0/
F0’—and this one is essential; it will be the key to the final synthesis in Sect. 6. One
can uncover this extra specification via two angles of analysis. Here is angle 1.
According to the functional definition DEF-F0, the stone is indeed the cause of
the wave, more precisely the characteristics of the stone (X, including for instance its
diameter) are the cause of the wave characteristics (Y) (fluid mechanics predicts Y
= f (X) for some function f that can be calculated), in accordance with our intuitions.
As always, this functional relation can be calculated from the equations governing
fluids—an essential remark we will come back to later. But which characteristics
exactly are ‘the’ cause(s); to what detail should one go? As is well-known, causal
analysis can be done at different levels of precision. For instance, physicists may
include among the causes of the precise wave profile the precise way in which the
stone falls (e.g. the height X’ from which it falls on the water surface), as well as
the depth of the water basin (X”). They then need to use more precise equations that
include these extra variables. X’ and X” are co-determining the detailed shape of the
wave; fluid mechanics can predict their influence (if one takes these variables into
account the calculations become quite involved). So X alone, or all three of X, X’
and X” can come out as causes, according to the same DEF-F0, depending on the
precision one asks. Higher precision demands more precise, ‘truer’ model equations.
Thus, as we will corroborate in a moment, it seems that what one counts as cause
depends on the details of the effect-event ‘one’ considers. Let me first make a remark
on the use of ‘one’ in the above sentence. In principle, although this is slightly
tedious and not really necessary, one could distinguish the person who inquires
about the cause of effect Y (the ‘cause-inquirer’) from the person who identifies or
assigns a cause X in response (the ‘cause-assigner’); often these are one and the
same person. This distinction is sometimes helpful in conversations about causeeffect identification: there is a (trivial) logical sequence. Indeed, in a sense, causes
are not, as objects, ‘ostentatiously out there’ in the world. For causes to be explicitly
identified, obviously someone has to inquire about them before the same or another
person can venture an answer. Now, the point is that this inquiry imposes standards
of precision, more generally a context. It seems that this precision or this context
needs to be specified in DEF-F0, else it seems ambiguous and could give rise to
different cause-assignments to the same Y. [Instead of stating “this inquiry imposes
standards of precision” one could, in principle, say that the cause-inquirers impose
standards of precision; and that the cause-assigners have to take these into account,
if they wish to avoid identifying ‘wrong’ causes. But this seems obvious; I will put
remarks along this line between square brackets.]
Let us briefly elaborate on the above example, since it is quite representative of
physical practice. If one looks for the causes of the experimental event “the wave
height Y assumes numerical value y”, where y has been measured with a precision
instrument (e.g. using laser light) and thus has a small experimental uncertainty or
error-interval δyexp (a small fraction of y), the causes will include all variables X

140

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

= (X1 , X2 ,…) that allow21 one to write: y = f (x) ± δyexp for some f (with x the
corresponding values of X). If one only takes X1 into account, it may be that from the
equations follows that the calculated f (x1 ) does not correspond to the measured y: say
f (x1 ) = y+ δy1 , where δy1 > δyexp . The fact that the calculation does not correspond
to the experimentally measured value suggests that one has to include other variables/
properties X2 , X3 ,… until calculation shows that f (x1 , x2 ,…) ∈ [y –δyexp ; y+ δyexp ].
Then all causes are satisfactorily identified. In sum, to first approximation X1 is the
cause of Y, but to a better approximation X1 , X2 ,… are the causes. All depends on
the precision or details with which one considers Yy .
Importantly, it seems that physics is not different from other sciences, or even
common contexts. A non-expert police officer might be satisfied with identifying ‘a
mental disorder’ as the cause of M’s act of murder. An expert in criminal psychology,
perhaps one who knows M, might list more detailed causes. In a discussion among
physicists, the cause of a certain substance igniting might be identified as the heat
transfer between the substance and a flame; chemists might find that the causes are
to be found in a list of detailed chemical reactions. “What caused this war?” might be
answered differently by a historian, a political scientist, an economist, a sociologist,
or a psychologist. Actually, that the truth of a causal claim depends on the context
within which it is under discussion, has been argued by several philosophers—not
only in the case of scientific causal claims (see e.g. Menzies & Beebee, 2020, §4).
There is convincing evidence that people’s causal judgements are sensitive to contextual factors (e.g. Clarke et al., 2015; Hitchcock & Knobe, 2009; Icard et al., 2017;
Kominsky et al., 2015).
In physics, ‘context-dependence’ often, or perhaps always, boils down to
‘precision-dependence’, as illustrated above. Now, we can come to this conclusion—
that something has to be added to DEF-F0 to make it precise—also from a different
angle of inquiry, angle 2, which is even more compelling.
When DEF-F0/F0’ stipulates that, for X to be a cause of Y, a function f needs
to exist that connects them, one may ask: how do we know that the function exists?
Who or what tells us? More precisely, one might object that it happens that different
theories/models exist for describing a given property Y or effect-event Y y —we have
studied such cases in some detail in Chapter 3 on Gettier’s problem –, different
theories from which different functional dependencies, and hence causes, could be
derived. At any rate, the function necessarily comes from, is derived within, a theory/
model, essentially a set of equations. [Namely, the theory/model that is assumed—by
the cause-assigner—to describe the context of inquiry about the effect Y, a context
imposed, in principle, by the cause-inquirer.]
Indeed, knowing that a functional relation between X and Y exists is almost
always theory-based, or at least theory-laden (this thought is also entertained, but
ultimately dismissed, by Suppes, 1970, p. 45; we will corroborate it forcefully later
on). It happens extremely rarely that people establish the existence of a functional
relation by direct empirical research—except in cases when scientists explore a fully
21 One could write: “that correctly allow one to write” etc. But this is understood. The expression
y = f (x) is correct if it follows from the appropriate theory.

5.3 ‘Functional’ Definition of Cause in Physics. Representation-Relativity

141

new problem space. I will illustrate this rare case in the next section with an example
of LEDs (light-emitting diodes) materialising neurons. But even then they need to
make some assumptions, which one may construe as part of a rudimentary theory/
model. The point is that if (the context of) Y is not unambiguously specified, people
[cause-assigners] may adopt different theories/models, for instance corresponding to
different sub-disciplines within physics or to different scientific disciplines. Recall
the examples just mentioned, e.g. the case of physicists and chemists using different
theories/models to analyse the same phenomenon. Remaining in physics, there may
be a debate about which theory/model describes Y and in particular an event Yy best.22
As discussed in Chapter 3, theories/models may ‘coexist’ in physics to describe
Yy . As we saw above, in physics it is usually the precision with which an event
(Yy ) is considered [by the cause-inquirer] that determines the context. But there
are exceptions: it may happen that different co-existing models lead to numerical
predictions with the same precision. My conclusion from these insights from science
is straightforward: the most precise way to specify the context of Y is to explicitly
define the model/theory that is considered [that is of interest to the inquirers, and
that therefore should be used by the assigners, if they aim at consistency].
Of course, in daily conversations, even among physicists, such a specification
is often not made. Cause is an imprecise, pre-theoretic concept, loosely used by
people (we are trying to make it a theoretical concept). When one asks “what is the
cause of Y?”, I believe it is usually understood, it is usually implicit, that the best
available theory/model should be the basis for the causal hunt—this seems quite
obvious. Now of course, if the question is asked in an absolutist state of mind it is
perhaps understood that the best possible theory in the absolute should be used—to
identify The Causes (this idea is elaborated in Vervoort, 2013a). But this notion of
best possible theory in the absolute is a tricky one, since, to all likelihood, we do
not have access to it; alas, we do not know The Theory of Truly Everything. The
specification of which theory/model is meant is therefore often not superfluous. Note
also that the demand to specify the theory/model can be seen as equivalent to the
demand that Y be specified unambiguously enough (the specification of the theory
can be considered as included in the specification of Y). These simple remarks echo
the platitude that causes can be identified as precisely as the effects are described.
Thus, we come to the conclusion that, at least in physics, we can reach full clarity if
we simply specify, when identifying a cause, what the theory/model is from which the
cause and its corresponding function f is derived. This, then, leads straightforwardly
to the following definition of functional cause, where T stands for a given theory or
model:
X_T is a cause of Y_T
iff
∃ function f, T: Y = f (X) according to T.
(DEF-F)
22 For instance, superconductivity has still no fully agreed-upon theory (see Chapter 3), several

theories are being studied and have proponents. In such a case, cause-assigners may come to
different causal claims.

142

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

Or more simply (equivalently if well understood):
X is a cause of Y (according to T)
iff
∃ function f : Y = f (X) (according to T).
(DEF-F)
This definition, which may look a bit awkward to some, is actually very simple, and
can be read thus: “X is a cause of Y according to theory/model T iff T implies that Y =
f (X) for some function f ”. On token level the definition is equally straightforward.23
In sum, by this move we have made explicit the last, and indeed essential, specification that has to be added to the bare DEF-F0: in order to avoid confusion, or
identifying wrong causes, one has to specify the theory/model/set of hypotheses T
that is used to derive the functional relation between X and Y. DEF-F is my final
definition of functional cause. Recall that it has two more implicit and rather trivial
clauses: a time-clause and an entity-clause. Note that ‘Y_T’ in DEF-F indicates the
effect as understood, described, in the framework T; and similarly for X_T.
At this point I would like to propose a conclusion that goes widely beyond
physics alone, one that concerns causal claims in any context. Readers who have
read Chapter 3 on Gettier’s problem will have noticed that there is close parallel
between that chapter and the present section on functional/physical cause. It is often
a good idea to start from physics, the most basic of the natural sciences, to try and see
whether conclusions valid there can be generalised to other contexts. In Chapter 3
it was argued that this move is the right one, and that the representation-relativity
or theory-relativity of concepts as truth and knowledge should be generalised to
everyday contexts—at least if one wants to solve somewhat sophisticated philosophical problems. This demand of generalisability is just a requirement that follows from
the key desideratum of synthetic philosophy, namely to strive for maximally coherent
philosophical frameworks, frameworks that work coherently in as many contexts as
possible, and that are continuous with physics. We saw in Chapter 3 that this generalisation works if one understands ‘theory/model’ (well-defined concepts in physics
and its philosophy) in a looser, broader sense, applicable to any context: namely
essentially as ‘coherent set of hypotheses/assumptions’ or ‘conceptual framework’
or ‘coherent set of beliefs’ or sometimes even just ‘common-sense beliefs’. These
are all terms denoting what one could call an explanatory perspective on, or a representation of, a piece of reality, of a collection of real events or facts (in this chapter I
will denote them all by ‘T’, not ‘T*’ as in other chapters—since we need subscripts
in the following).

23 It reads: “X is a cause of Y according to theory/model/hypotheses T iff T implies that Y =
x
y

f (X) for some function f (and hence y = f (x))”. Note that to determine, on event-level, whether
x = f (y), where x and y are numbers, one does in general not only need a theory (equations), but
also some contingent values of variables that describe the context of the contingent event Yy . These
values are usually called ‘initial conditions’ in physics (see the above example of the ball on a
table); without them one cannot, in general, solve equations and calculate numbers. We may simply
include these values as assumptions of the model T. (Note the analogy with Hempel’s scheme of
scientific explanation, which also involves laws and particular facts.)

5.4 ‘Manipulationist’ Definition of Cause. A Preliminary Synthesis

143

This rationale leads then to the quite straightforward conjecture that the clause
‘according to theory/model T’ of DEF-F might well be needed for a general definition
of cause, not only in physics. I will call this feature the representation-relativity of
causal claims, a notion of which the full importance will become clear soon. We will
also encounter this notion when studying in the next sections the manipulationist and
probabilistic interpretations of cause.
A last remark about DEF-F is implicit in what we said above, and essential
for the ontology of causation. DEF-Fallows us to understand the inextricable link
between causation and the fundamental ontology of nature—by an argument that is,
I hope, obvious by now. Indeed, by definition of the physicalism we have to adopt,
all events are ultimately governed by the laws of physics. But, as we have seen, the
corresponding law statements embody the functional relations between the variables
of the corresponding theory T; solving the law-equations amounts to discovering the
precise functional relations between these variables.24 As another example, solving
Maxwell’s equations in a given concrete situation in which a current J is switched on
enables one to derive a functional relation between J and the magnetic field B that is
created or caused: B = f (J). One can always imagine or assume temporal domains
of these variables (e.g. first comes the current, then the field); these assumptions
can be considered as included in T. DEF-Fthen immediately allows us to extract
cause-effect relations directly from the laws, fundamental or less fundamental, that
constitute the core of T .
We will later come back to this essential idea, which highlights the key role DEFF plays in a synthetic understanding of causation. One must therefore regret that the
functional account seems underrepresented in contemporary scholarship.

5.4 ‘Manipulationist’ Definition of Cause. A Preliminary
Synthesis
In this Section I will discuss a definition of cause that has become very popular in
the last decades, the manipulationist theory, as developed by authors as Menzies and
Price (1993), Woodward (2003, 2023), and Hausman and Woodward (2004). The
standard reference I will follow is the well-known work of Woodward (2003, 2023).
A lot of research has been invested in this theory, to which I cannot do full justice
here. I will only treat selected aspects of the manipulationist theory, since I believe
its problems, several of them summarised in Woodward (2023), can be solved by
a more synthetic model. Also, I criticised some aspects of it elsewhere (Vervoort,
2014). Yet, it is an interesting model and, if properly understood, it resonates with
the one I will ultimately adopt.

24 Similar remarks hold for probabilistic laws: these allow for extracting correlations, which, as we

will see in the section on probabilistic causation, are the key input of the definition of probabilistic
cause.

144

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

Woodward’s theory was, at least initially, developed for systems from physics
and other disciplines based on structural equations, involving properties/variables25
X, Y,…. The intuitive idea is straightforward: X is a cause of a Y iff X allows to
manipulate Y. The key definition of this account, which I will call DEF-M0, is the
following (Woodward, 2003, p. 51):
X is a cause of Y
iff
There is a possible intervention on X that will change Y [or the probability
distribution of Y]
(DEF-M0)
I will neglect for the time being the probabilistic case in brackets, to which I will
return in the next sections. Actually, Woodward calls the above causes ‘total’ causes,
and introduces also the concept of ‘direct’ and ‘contributing’ cause. However, I
have argued elsewhere that for typical physical systems the notions of direct and
contributing cause are inconsistent (Vervoort, 2014). Therefore, I believe that at
least in physics the legitimate definition of ‘cause’ (simpliciter) is DEF-M0. Also
beyond physics it captures the essence of the manipulability account; so I will focus
on it. Several comments are due to get a clearer understanding of this definition.
First, in the manipulationist literature there is a quite involved debate as to what
‘intervention’ actually means (Woodward, 2023, §§4, 8, 10, 13). Woodward states
(2003, p. 47): “More generally and slightly more precisely, we may think of an
intervention on X with respect to Y as an exogenous causal process that changes X in
such a way and under conditions such that if any change occurs in Y, it occurs in virtue
of Y’s relationship to X and not in any other way.” The problem with this definition (as
well as with its more elaborate versions, cf. Woodward, 2003 p. 98, Woodward, 2023
§5 (M1–M4)) is that for the typical systems studied in physics the system equations
are coupled, meaning that each equation contains at least one variable that also
belongs to at least one other equation of the set. This creates a causal link between
all variables, which renders it impossible that “if any change occurs in Y, it occurs in
virtue of Y’s relationship to X and not in any other way” (details in Vervoort, 2014).
Indeed, Woodward is inspired by authors as Pearl (2009) who consider systems that
have decoupled system equations. Here “each equation represents a distinct causal
mechanism which is understood to be ‘autonomous’ in the sense in which that notion
is used in econometrics; this means roughly that it is possible to interfere with or
disrupt each mechanism (and the corresponding equation) without disrupting any of
the others” (Woodward, 2023, §5). But this does not work for typical systems studied
in physics, and ‘intervention on X’ must be defined differently for coupled systems in
general.26 Before doing so, I will make a brief detour about coupling; this also gives
me the opportunity to illustrate another important feature of equations, or rather their
25 Therefore, it is useful to remember that X and Y range over value domains, minimally {0, 1}, in

physics normally intervals of the real numbers.
26 The original definition may remain valid for systems/disciplines based on sets of decoupled

equations, say econometrics etc.

5.4 ‘Manipulationist’ Definition of Cause. A Preliminary Synthesis

145

interpretation by physicists: the difference between dependent/independent variables
and system constants.
A paradigmatic example of coupling is given by Maxwell’s equations of electromagnetism, describing a potentially infinite class of systems belonging to such
subfields as electrostatics, magnetostatics, and electrodynamics (Jackson, 1999, Ch.
1). These are a set of four ‘coupled partial differential equations’, expressing the relation between the electric field (E), the electric displacement field (D), the magnetic
field (B), the magnetic field intensity (H), and their so-called sources, namely the
electric charge (ρ) and current density (J) (underlined variables are vector fields):
∇ x H = (4π/c) J+ (1/c) ∂D/∂t.
∇ x E = − (1/c) ∂B/∂t.
∇. B = 0.
∇. D = 4πρ.

(The equations are only shown here to illustrate a typical feature of system equations—there is no need to go in any detail about the meaning of the variables.
The operator ‘∇’ effectuates derivation in the space coordinates, ∂/∂t is the timederivative.) In above equations, ‘c’ is the speed of light. One notices that the set
contains variables (D and B) that belong to different equations; more precisely, all
equations contain variables that also belong to at least one other equation. This
coupling makes mathematical solution difficult: none of the equations can be mathematically manipulated ‘on its own’ to derive a solution for a variable; they have to
be handled in parallel.
One also sees that the role of the ‘variables’ and ‘constants’ in the system equations is clearly distinct. For any given physical system the constants are fixed, i.e.
they keep the same numerical value independently of the values of the variables. In
above example, ‘c’ is such a system constant: its value is assumed to be given (π
is a dimensionless numerical constant without physical interpretation—still another
case). ‘Variables’ play a very different role: within one system with fixed constants,
the variables may assume different values. If in electromagnetic systems the source
(or independent) variables J and ρ change (for instance due to human intervention,
or due to changes in the environment), the other (dependent) variables will also
change (always for fixed constants)—as the equations show. Interestingly, the terms
‘dependent’ and ‘independent’ variables used in physics (and mathematics) have a
distinctly causal flavour; so scientists do seem to implicitly use them with a causal
meaning in mind. Physicists usually call ‘independent variables’ those variables the
values of which are chosen by experimenters or have another causal connotation
(notably because these variables assume their values, in the real system represented
by the equations, before the dependent variables do); while ‘dependent variables’
are those that then depend as effects on the independent ones (through the mechanisms described by the equations). In many experimental situations J and ρ are the
independent variables (charges and currents are switched on first; this then creates/
causes the fields). But in other situations the role of dependent/independent variables is reversed: e.g. a changing magnetic field can induce a current in a conductor,

146

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

which is then considered the dependent variable.27 Notice that this cause-effect relationship between variables seems to correspond to the same basic intuition as the
one underlying the manipulationist model, but even more conspicuously the functionalist interpretation of cause: the equations allow deriving functional relations
between independent and dependent variables.
Let us now return to DEF-M0 and the interpretation of intervention. In the first
manipulationist theories the notion of cause was explicitly linked to human agency,
but authors as Menzies and Price (1993) and Woodward (2003, 2023) strived at
making the theory non-anthropocentric—so that causal connections can also exist
and be identified when human manipulation is impossible, e.g. in geological or
cosmological contexts (cf. e.g. Woodward, 2023 §§8, 10, 11, 12). But then one
wonders: why not bite the bullet and rephrase DEF-M0 in explicitly objective, nonanthropocentric terms? Objective means here: referring directly to what is supposed
to happen in reality, without the detour over human intervention. If successful, it
seems that such a move would greatly clarify the model.
Intuitively, and from the many examples given (Woodward, 2003, 2023), I believe
it is clear how to interpret ‘intervention’ in a consistent way—consistent with physics,
notably—while keeping a trace of manipulationist intuitions. I propose this interpretation: an intervention on X is an operation that changes the value of a variable
X—full stop. Note now that the charge of anthropocentricity can at once be taken
care of by simply stipulating that this ‘change operation’ can occur under the influence of humans or spontaneously, ‘in the course of events’ in which no human is
involved (in nature system properties change all the time without any human being
involved). Let us also stipulate that the change operation can happen in the real world
or in thoughts: physically in the lab (or in the wild), or mathematically if the equations are at hand, or, most of the time, theoretically, i.e. in our mental representation
of the system. As an example: using theoretical mechanics and its equations, I can
in thoughts consider what would happen if the gravitational force X between earth
and moon would change; I can numerically change X and see what the equations
predict; this a theoretical change operation on X. This counterfactual, mental operation would immediately lead me to such conclusions as that X causes the movement of
the moon, or of tides—an example tentatively considered in (Woodward, 2023, §10).
With this objective interpretation of intervention, DEF-M0 becomes transparent and
is equivalent to DEF-M0’:
X is a cause of Y
iff
A (possible) change in X will change Y [or the probability distribution of Y]
(DEF-M0’)
27 Of course, this naming does not really help solving the equations, so these names are not strictly

needed. But they are still helpful to picture what is happening. Humans seem to automatically
picture the world in cause-effect relations.

5.4 ‘Manipulationist’ Definition of Cause. A Preliminary Synthesis

147

In this definition the thorny notion of ‘intervention’ has been abstracted away, while
one can still see a trace of it in the notion of change. Note that it would be quite
amazing that humans’ efficient causal intuitions would be based on a mental process
involving such a complex notion as intervention given by (M1-M4) in Woodward
(2023, §5). But DEF-M0’ is simple and user-friendly for causal inferences: mentally,
theoretically imagining what Y would (roughly) do if one changes X is often immediate. And indeed, it is with this simpler interpretation that the examples of causeeffect relations given in Woodward (2003, 2023) make sense; I will mention a few
below.
DEF-M0’ is now a definition that directly concerns the objective world, in which
the realist believes. But also anti-realists may accept it: for them the ‘change’ would
be in thoughts. More precisely, DEF-M0’ refers to an agent-independent reality,
which can in principle be analysed via our theoretical representations; that is also
how definitions in natural science work. Interestingly, note that the revised DEF-M0’
does not only solve the anthropocentricity problem, but also the well-known problem
of circularity to which manipulability theories are vulnerable (e.g. Woodward, 2023,
§6). Causation is now defined in terms of change, which can mathematically be
defined (if X and Y are variables), and which (therefore) could be seen as logically
or metaphysically prior to the notion of cause. As so often happens, unification leads
to problem-solving on several fronts.
In DEF-M0’ I have left the ‘possible’ that figures in the ‘possible intervention’ of
DEF-M0. As said, various authors debate about the question of which interventions
are possible (Woodward, 2023, §10). Now, with what we have learned in the previous
section on functional cause, it seems clear what is a possible intervention or a possible
change: that is of course determined by the theory/model/set of hypotheses one uses to
think about Y and its potential causes (see an example below). At first sight ‘possible’
might seem superfluous in DEF-M0’—a change is a change and is always possible
in thoughts—but not so fast, the clause is still useful to avoid false causal claims.
For instance, without the clause, the speed of light ‘c’ could be identified as a cause
of the field values in electromagnetic problems: changing c in Maxwell’s equations
would change the fields, as the equations show. But the theory of electromagnetism
(especially if combined with relativity theory) teaches us that ‘c’ better be treated as
a constant, so that a change of c does not really qualify as a realistic possibility—
c is one of the few ‘universal constants’, to boot, with a touch of sanctity! Hence
c is no cause after all, according to DEF-M0’, as it should be. (But everything is
relative to the perspective, rather theory, one uses: those few theoretical physicists
who study theories in which the speed of light can vary—such heretics do exist, in
small numbers—would presumably have nothing against the statement that ‘c causes
the fields’…).
So, we see here again how the representation-relativity that emerged in the functional model comes to the surface. It becomes even more manifest and unavoidable
from another angle: the truth of ‘a change in X will change Y’ (or analogous counterfactuals) can usually only be ascertained if we have access to adequate hypotheses/
theories describing X and Y. In practice, one very rarely evaluates whether X changes
Y by direct manipulation of reality—I will give an example in a moment –; one

148

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

almost always does this in thoughts, in other words, in a representation of reality. I
made a similar remark about the functional account: the existence of functions is a
theory-laden question. As always representation-relativity is most patently seen in
complex contexts. For instance, a medical doctor may only know whether a change
in X (say a blood parameter) will be followed by a change in Y (another biomedical
property) if he knows the relevant theory describing the patient’s condition and the
relation between X and Y. In sum: cause identification is representation-relative, or
T-relative.
Thus, we end up with following final definition, DEF-M, my modified manipulationist definition (T stands, as always, for a given theory/set of hypotheses):
X is a cause of Y (according to T)
iff
A (possible) change in X will change Y [or the probability distribution of Y]
(according to T)
(DEF-M)
Only in rare, trivial cases can one identify causes without relying on some representation T, so by direct manipulation; but the identification will then be tentative,
speculative, unconvincing. For instance, suppose a test person is given electric circuits
that materialise the neuron diagrams of the former section; neurons are LEDs that
are ‘on’ if the corresponding neuron is ‘on’, similarly for the ‘off’ state. Suppose the
test person knows nothing about the working of the circuit. She is just told that she
can push buttons next to the diodes to switch their on/off state, potentially followed28
by on/off switches in other LEDs on the right side, and she is asked: “what do you
believe to be the cause(s) at t1 of the on or off state of the rightmost LED?” She
might notice after a few experiments that there is a constant relation between the
on/off-switching of some LEDs at t1 and that of the last one, so she might, surely
hesitatingly, identify the activation or non-activation of these specific left-side LEDs
as the cause(s)—essentially on the basis of DEF-M0’. Her assumptions T are here
rather trivial, and include perhaps only the hypothesis that ‘nothing more fundamental or contrived is going on’, or that her senses do not betray her, or that the
constant relation she noted is indeed constant also beyond her trials, so the usual
assumptions of someone who is moving in the dark. Luckily, we normally do not
experience such uncanny situations; we usually have access to a vast network of
trustworthy hypotheses—knowledge—to navigate the world.
What is interesting in this LED-neuron experiment, is that the tentative causal
inference is based on the observation of a constant relation between putative cause
X and effect Y. Thought experiments about direct manipulation of reality in an
unknown context as the above strongly suggest that this observed lawful or quasilawful relation is a prerequisite for causal identification, and a general feature of
28 So I suppose that the functioning of the circuit is implemented by LEDs and electronics, according

to the causal connections of the corresponding neuron diagram. One can perform the on/off switching
by pushing knobs next to each neuron/LED.

5.4 ‘Manipulationist’ Definition of Cause. A Preliminary Synthesis

149

causation as a fact of the world. That is an old idea, and one further reason why the
idea of theory-relativity is natural: theories can describe (quasi-)lawful behaviour.
Many problems of the manipulability account can be solved with the
representation-clause in DEF-M, as will become fully clear in Sect. 6, when I will
generalise it further. But here is already a flavour of a few of these solutions.
First of all, manipulationist causal claims necessitate the evaluation of counterfactuals. Woodward asks (2023, §5): “how should one understand (what is the appropriate interpretation of or semantics for) the counterfactuals in question? Without
attempting to answer this question in detail, it seems plausible that if interventionist
counterfactuals are to be useful in elucidating causal claims, their semantics must
be different from the familiar Lewis/Stalnaker possible world semantics”. I decidedly agree, and as is hopefully clear from the above paragraphs, the only definite
way to evaluate manipulationist counterfactuals is to use/apply the appropriate set of
assumptions/theory—if one wants to identify appropriate causes. Specifically, when
using DEF-M for identifying causes within a specified T, the manipulationist counterfactual must be evaluated, inferred, using this T. (Recall e.g. the example of the
counterfactually changing gravitational force, the effect of which is inferred, evaluated, within Newtonian mechanics.) This resonates well with what Woodward states
at places.29 I will give a more formal analysis of this matter in Sect. 6. In a slogan:
theories are evaluation machines for counterfactuals. Let me admit: I know of no
other way to coherently evaluate them.
As another example, Woodward analyses a classic case of preemption: “Gunman
one shoots (s1) victim, causing his death d, while gunman two does not shoot but
would have shot (s2) also causing d, if s1 had not occurred” (Woodward, 2023, §7).
The author deals with this case by referring to two interventions. But using DEF-M
one readily comes to the (correct) conclusion that s1 is a cause of d, simply because
even our common-sense hypotheses/knowledge tell us that if s1 would not have shot,
victim would not have died the same way or at the same time30 in the given situation.
At least under a ‘ceteris paribus’ assumption (we will see in Sect. 6 that this clause
should indeed be added to the definition for full disambiguation). Instead of referring
to two interventions, one can simply apply DEF-M, where now T = common-sense
hypotheses/knowledge (about how people are killed by shooting). This example may
sound a bit contrived, but recall that the manipulability account is best restricted to
situations described by variables from scientific theories.

29 In (2023, §5): “We are making use of a possibility constrained notion of intervention. Use of this
notion raises the difficult question of how the relevant notion of possibility should be understood.
I will suggest below that the best way of making sense of this notion is in terms of some notion of
conceptual or mathematical (or if you like, “metaphysical”) coherence—roughly speaking, the issue
is whether there is an appropriately empirically grounded theoretical/mathematical apparatus that
allows for a coherent description of the possible intervention in question and allows us to determine
what would happen if the intervention were realized.”
30 Recall that events are best conceived as being located in space–time (a suggestion coming from
physics).

150

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

At this point we are in the position to make an important step of unification.
Indeed, by now it will be clear that there is a strong link between the manipulationist definition DEF-M0 (a fortiori my modified version DEF-M) and DEF-F,
the functional definition dear to physicists. It is not a surprise then that the notion
of functional dependence is implicit in a large part of Woodward’s manipulability
theory. For instance Woodward (2003, p. 39):
(…) on a manipulability account of causation, it is most perspicuous to think of causal
relationships as relating variables or, to speak more precisely, as describing how changes in
the value of one or more variables will change the value of other variables. This is also the
way that many scientists think about causal relationships.

It is transparent that the ‘relationship between variables’ of this quote is covered by
the definition of function: if one knows the functional relation between X and Y, one
also knows how a change in the value of X relates to a change in the value of Y.
Functional dependence occasionally appears explicitly (Woodward, 2003, p. 42):
“(…) I generally focus on the deterministic case, in which (…) if variables X1 …Xm are all
of the direct causes of Y, then Y may be written as (2.2.1) Y = FY (X1 …Xm ).”

So, Woodward’s definition of total cause DEF-M0 and DEF-F strongly resonate. If
an explicit argument is needed: a cause that satisfies DEF-F is (often or always) a
cause on the manipulability account. If a functional relationship exists between X and
Y, it can in principle be exploited to manipulate Y by manipulating X: that follows
from the definition of function, and see the many illustrations in Woodward (2003).
Conversely, if there is a possible intervention on X (i.e. an operation through which
X is set/changed to a certain value) that changes Y, i.e. if X is a manipulationist cause
of Y, and if we restrict X and Y to deterministic systems, the system variables X and
Y should be functionally related, even if one does not know the formal expression
of that function. How else would they be related?
If Woodward’s DEF-M0 is already strongly resonating with DEF-F, my modified DEF-M, if properly understood, is indeed equivalent31 with it in the case of
deterministic systems, be it that DEF-F is then more precise.
It is important to note that DEF-M has the advantage that it naturally applies to a
broader class of contexts and systems than DEF-F, say from sociology, economics,
everyday life etc. In such contexts functional relations may not be known, neither
scientific theories in a strict sense, but empirical co-variation between X and Y
may be known—e.g. from experience or from tests involving manipulation of X. If
there are good reasons (in principle condensed in a conceptual framework, a set of
hypotheses T) to believe that this co-variation is not spurious, not an artefact, but can
31 A cause that satisfies DEF-F satisfies DEF-M. If a functional relationship exists between X and

Y, a change in X will (almost always) be followed by a change in Y (for functions occurring in
physics, a change Δy = 0 for Δx
0 arrives with probability equal 0, except in the rare cases
where Y lies in a horizontal plateau as a function of X). So the right-hand-side (RHS) of DEF-F
implies the RHS of DEF-M. Conversely, if there is a relation à la DEF-M between X and Y in
deterministic systems it must be functional; hence the RHS of DEF-M implies the RHS of DEF-F.
(This argument assumes that relations between variables of deterministic systems are functional
relations; but that is widely accepted.)

5.5 Definition of Probabilistic Cause. Another Preliminary Synthesis

151

be repeated, i.e. has some constant character, then DEF-M allows one to conclude
that X is a cause of Y (according to T). I will make this more precise in my final
synthesis.32 As mentioned, in some cases T is rather trivial, for instance in the above
experiment involving LEDs representing neurons.
Another hint to the generality of DEF-M is that it could, perhaps, be applied to
probabilistic systems. Let us chiefly investigate probabilistic causation in the next
Section—even if it is a priori not part of the subject of the present chapter, focusing
on deterministic cause. (Since Sect. 5 remains conjectural to some degree, I might
have relegated it to the Epilogue, but it fits best in the present chapter.)

5.5 Definition of Probabilistic Cause. Another Preliminary
Synthesis
Let us then briefly treat the probabilistic definition of cause, as studied by authors
as Reichenbach (1956), Suppes (1970), Cartwright (1979), Hitchcock (2021). The
analysis will be lighter than those presented for other definitions, and I do not claim
that a final model of probabilistic cause is given here; more synthetic work is needed
to claim this with full confidence. Still, I will propose an account that synthesises
the models of several authors; most relevant is that my account can be unified with
the other definitions of cause in this chapter under one widespread interpretation
of probability. This is the deterministic interpretation, which is widely even if not
universally accepted for classical systems, but is under debate in the quantum realm.
The key idea from which we start is H. Reichenbach’s well-known analysis of the
connection between causation and probabilistic correlation or dependence (Reichenbach, 1956). Interestingly, with his analysis Reichenbach answered a question raised
by the father of modern probability theory, A. Kolmogorov, in his reference work
Foundations of the Theory of Probability (1933/1956, p. 9):
In consequence, one of the most important problems in the philosophy of the natural sciences
is – in addition to the well-known one regarding the essence of the concept of probability
itself – to make precise the premises which would make it possible to regard any given events
as independent. This question, however, is beyond the scope of this book.

Kolmogorov asks whether there exist general conditions allowing one to know in
advance (without doing a statistical test) whether two variables X and Y are independent (de-correlated) or not; in symbols, whether their joint probability satisfies
following equation, for all values of X, Y:
?

P(X , Y ) = P(X )P(Y |X ) = P(X )P(Y ).

(Ind)

Or equivalently:

32 I assume, as always, that the appropriate temporal relation holds between X and Y.

152

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

P(Y |X ) = P(Y )

?

(Ind)

?

(Ind)

P(X |Y ) = P(X ).

An equality sign corresponds, by definition, to probabilistic independence. (In the
following X and Y stand for variables. In order to define independence on event-level
it suffices to replace in (Ind) X by Xx and Y by Yy as introduced in the previous
section. Note that on type-level (Ind) should hold for all values of the variables,
values that are defined by the system/situation under scrutiny.)
When dealing with a probabilistic question related, for instance, to the tossing
of two dice, or in general to the joint occurrence of two stochastic events, one has
to assume or guess whether the events/variables/systems are independent or not.
Generally speaking, in real-world situations there is no rule; the only way to be sure
is to experimentally verify (Ind). (Note that we find here an argument in favour of the
objective interpretation of probability: the factorability of P(X , Y ) is an experimental
matter, not one of subjective guesses.) Now, even if no rule exists, one often correctly
intuits whether events/variables are dependent or not. For instance, two die throws
can under normal circumstances safely be treated as independent. The interesting
question is: on what basis do we intuit this? Surely by our intuitive feeling that these
events are not causally connected. This is the basis of Reichenbach’s acclaimed
analysis.
According to Reichenbach, then, X is statistically dependent on Y if and only
if either (i) X causes Y (or v.v.), or (ii) there is a common cause Z of X and Y, or
(iii) both (i) and (ii) occur. The second part of Reichenbach’s thesis states that such
a common cause (case (ii)) has the property that it ‘screens off’ the correlations
between X and Y. More precisely: if the correlation between X and Y is exclusively
due to a common cause (there is no direct causation), and if Z is a complete common
cause for X and Y (i.e., Z is the set of all variables acting as common causes), then X
and Y must be conditionally independent given Z (Hitchcock & Rédei, 2020; Allen
et al., 2017). Mathematically, under the condition stated:
If P(X , Y ) = P(X )P(Y ) then ∃ Z : P(X , Y |Z) = P(X |Z) P(Y |Z) ,

(Reich1)

where Z is a complete common cause. (Recall that the variables X, Y, Z range over
value domains, denoted {X} etc.; the consequent in (Reich1) holds for all values
of the variables.) One says that ‘Z screens off X from Y’. The screening-off in the
consequent in (Reich1) also occurs in the case of a non-common-cause causal scheme
of the type: X → Z → Y (as Reichenbach noted).
Let us write Reichenbach’s thesis in a compact form (condition (iii) above is
understood):
X is statistically dependent on Y
iff

5.5 Definition of Probabilistic Cause. Another Preliminary Synthesis

153

Fig. 5.2 Causal connections
of type (i) and (ii) in
(Reich2)

either (i) X causes Y (or v.v.),
or (ii) there is an earlier common cause Z of X and Y.
(Reich2)
Figure 5.2 symbolizes these conditions (i) and (ii).
Reichenbach’s principle, sometimes called the ‘common cause principle’, seems
to hold true, at least for classical systems: it has widespread empirical support33 (cf.
Pearl, 2009; Spirtes et al., 2001).
Note now that from (Reich2) one can immediately deduce a definition of probabilistic cause (if one assumes, as usual, that the appropriate temporal order exists
between X and Y):
X is a cause of Y
iff
(i) Y is probabilistically dependent on X
and (ii) there is no earlier common cause Z of X and Y.
(DEF-P0)
In mathematical terms, using the definitions (Ind) and (Reich1):
X is a cause of Y
iff
(i) P(Y|X) P(Y),
and (ii) Z(earlier) : P(Y|X,Z) = P(Y|Z).
(DEF-P)
Trivially, for DEF-P to be applicable we need to assume that a probability function
P is defined for variables X, Y, Z. Note that (i) should hold for at least some value of
X and Y, and (ii) for all values of X, Y, Z (an application to a concrete case is given
in Appendix 5.1). The key specification that one only considers ‘earlier’ variables Z
prohibits the vacuity of the definition: without it, one could take Z = X so that (ii)
would never be satisfied. ‘Earlier’ means ‘taking its values earlier than X and Y’, as
33 Some authors have contested the universal validity of Reichenbach’s principle in classical systems

(cf. Hitchcock & Rédei, 2020). In quantum systems it might not hold, because ‘common causes’
of quantum properties might be precluded by no-go theorems as Bell’s theorem. But that is only so
under certain heavy assumptions; e.g. nonlocal or superdeterministic common causes would still
be allowed by the theorems. For readers interested in the philosophy of quantum mechanics, my
view on this topic may be found in e.g. (Vervoort 2013b, Nikolaev and Vervoort 2023, Sect. 6); and
see Chapter 9. My position surely is speculative, but there are at present no non-speculative views
available on this matter.
