5 A Unified Definition of Cause

Fig. 5.3 Model linking lung
cancer to smoking and other
factors

one demands of a common cause. This specification also allows one to consider the
causal chain X → Z → Y as a case of X–Y causation, as intuition tells us: in this
chain Z does screen off X from Y but Z is not ‘earlier’ than X.
A definition closely related but not identical to DEF-P has been proposed by
other authors, notably P. Suppes (1970) and Cartwright (1979). One difference is
that these authors use ‘>’ instead of ‘ ’ in (i), according to the intuition that ‘causes
raise the probability of their effects’. But this creates several unnecessary problems,
as also seems to be the conclusion of Hitchcock (2021, §2.5). Cartwright (1979)
proposes following definition: “X is a cause of Y iff P(Y |X , Z ) > P(Y |∼ X , Z ) for
every background Z” (where ∼X is the complement of X). But as noted by Hitchcock
(2021, §2.4), this would lead to the wrong conclusion that smoking is not a cause of
lung cancer in a model, depicted in Fig. 5.3, linking smoking (S), lung cancer (L),
the presence of tar in the lungs (T), causes of tar in the lungs other than smoking (Z).
This model assumes that lung cancer is only caused by tar in the lungs, which can
have different origins (causes), one of them being smoking.
Since T screens off L from S, P(L|S, T ) = P(L|∼ S, T ) so that we get the wrong
result (Hitchcock, 2021, §2.4). Using DEF-P we obtain for condition (i): P(L|S)
P(L), and condition (ii) is also satisfied: P(L|S, Z)
P(L|Z) and P(L|S, T ) =
P(L|T ) but T is not ‘earlier’ than S. Ergo S is a cause of L. Definitions à la Cartwright
(1979) also get Simpson’s paradox wrong (Hitchcock, 2021, §2.4), while it is again
solved in a straightforward way by DEF-P.
My goal in this Section is not to provide a complete analysis of a definition of
probabilistic cause à la DEF-P0 or DEF-P; I will restrain myself to showing that
DEF-P can be unified with other definitions. This is a new result and it shows that
DEF-P has, to the least, the potential to lead to a final solution for characterising probabilistic causation. This synthesis can be done under one widespread interpretation
of probability, namely the assumption that probabilities emerge from a deterministic
stratum, as for instance Laplace believed, one of the fathers of probability theory.
I would emphasise that this assumption is actually a ubiquitous guidance principle
in the statistical theory of causal modelling, elaborated, notably, by mathematician
and philosopher J. Pearl (2009). On this view, if a probability P(Y |X ) P(Y ) exists
(satisfies Kolmogorov’s axioms and, I will add, the criteria exposed in the previous
chapter), then there must exist a function from (X, Λ) to Y, where Λ are unobserved/
uncontrolled (and possibly unknown) variables. (X, Λ) are termed the causes of Y: this
is in agreement with our functional definition DEF-F. To justify this interpretation,
Pearl states (2009, p. 26):
In this book, we shall express preference toward Laplace’s quasi-deterministic conception of
causality and will use it, often contrasted with the stochastic conception, to define and analyze
most of the causal entities that we study. This preference is based on three considerations.

5.5 Definition of Probabilistic Cause. Another Preliminary Synthesis

155

First, the Laplacian conception is more general. Every stochastic model can be emulated by
many functional relationships (with stochastic inputs), but not the other way around; functional relationships can only be approximated, as a limiting case, using stochastic models.
Second, the Laplacian conception is more in tune with human intuition. […] Finally, certain
concepts that are ubiquitous in human discourse can be defined only in the Laplacian framework. We shall see, for example, that such simple concepts as ‘the probability that event B
occurred because of event A’ and ‘the probability that event B would have been different if
it were not for event A’ cannot be defined in terms of purely stochastic models (Pearl, 2009,
p. 26).

As is well known, this deterministic interpretation of probability is appropriate for
many classical probabilistic systems, systems that are known to be deterministic
under the surface.34 So, in this interpretation probability theory is an (immensely
useful) tool needed because of our ignorance of certain uncontrollable variables that
affect stochastic systems and processes; and probabilistic properties are (in principle)
deterministic functions of these hidden variables.
Now, under this interpretation it appears that DEF-P is essentially equivalent to
DEF-F. Here is a proof sketch. First, let us ignore for a moment the clause ‘according
to T’ in DEF-F. Under the deterministic interpretation of probability, if P(Y |X ) is
defined:
∃ f 1 , Λ: Y = f 1 (X, Λ) for all values of X, Y.
Condition (i) in DEF-P states then that there is no function f 2 such that Y = f 1 (X,
Λ) = f 2 (Λ). One might say, Y is an ‘irreducible’ function of X. Condition (ii) states
that, if there are ‘earlier’ variables Z such that Y = f 3 (X, Z, Λ), there is no function f 4
such that Y = f 3 (X, Z, Λ) = f 4 (Z, Λ). Again, Y must be an irreducible function of X
(and possibly of Λ and other variables). But that is what DEF-F tells us, be it slightly
less explicitly. Conversely and analogously, we can reinterpret DEF-F, i.e. add some
trivial clauses, so that it implies35 DEF-P for probabilistic systems—always under
the deterministic interpretation of probability.
Finally, what about the clause “according to T” in DEF-F? After what we learned
in the previous sections, the first hypothesis that comes to mind is to simply add it
to DEF-P—if it would make it more precise. There are indeed excellent reasons to
do so. Which variables Z one considers in condition (ii) of DEF-P always depends
34 Examples can be found e.g. in fluid mechanical systems (see the previous chapter on probability).

Fluid mechanics is governed by deterministic equations, essentially the Navier–Stokes equation;
yet there exists an enormous variety of probabilistic features in fluids, gases, eddies, diffusive
systems etc. Another example are the probabilistic properties of statistical mechanics simulated
by the Monte-Carlo technique mentioned in the previous chapter: all these properties, reproducing
experimental probabilities, are simulated with pseudorandom number generators which are actually
based on deterministic functions.
35 In some detail, introducing the set of hypotheses T: suppose that according to some T X and Y
are stochastic variables that are ultimately reducible to functions, and suppose DEF-F and T imply
that X is a cause of Y. Then ∃ f 1 , Λ: Y = f 1 (X, Λ) for all values of X, Y considered in T, which
is condition (i) of DEF-P. We can now add the trivial assumption that T stipulates that Y has a
genuine functional dependence on X, not one that gets cancelled when one fixes some other variable
Z. Then one must have (ii) of DEF-P. Ergo, a cause on DEF-F in T is also a cause on DEF-P in
T; DEF-P is more precise.

156

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

on some hypotheses T (a model, a theory,…) about the piece of reality under causal
scrutiny. In general, not all models contain the same variables, of course. Furthermore,
whether the two probabilities in (ii) are equal or not is, ultimately, an experimental
matter that can be tested—according to the frequentist interpretation elaborated in
the previous chapter. But as I highlighted there, such a test is subject to a certain
precision; and this precision should be specified, at least in principle, in T: one must
include it in the assumptions leading to the verdict ‘X is a cause of Y’.
The fact, then, that DEF-F appears to be a special case of DEF-P, namely when
DEF-P is applied to systems that are deterministic (above or under the surface),
confirms that DEF-P is more general, as it should be. Deterministic properties are
a special case of probabilistic properties, having probabilities equal to 1 or 0. Moreover, note that there is no principled reason why DEF-P could not work for irreducibly probabilistic systems (assuming that these exist; quantum systems are usually
believed to be of this type). An illustration of the utility of this definition is given in
Appendix 5.1, where a stochastic variant of an emblematic neuron diagram is studied
(without any assumption of hidden determinism). Interestingly, DEF-P again leads
to results that coincide with those found by other definitions, in the limits where the
latter apply.
A further advantage of DEF-P, pending its final corroboration, seems to be that
it could be used for defining precise measures of the causal influence of one variable
on another, or between sets of variables. In complex systems with many variables it
may be very useful to know which variable has the strongest influence on another
one. Such a measure of the ‘influence’ of X on Y could be defined as follows:
|P(Y |X ) − P (Y )|.

Inf(X → Y) =
Y

X

Here the sums run over all values of X and Y. Inf(X → Y) is only equal to zero
if each term is equal to zero, in other words, if X is not a cause of Y according to
DEF-P. In Appendix 5.1 this proposal is tested on a concrete example.
In conclusion, although more work is needed to compare DEF-P with other theories of stochastic causation, I believe that arguments exist to conclude that: (i) DEF-P
has a genuine potential for problem-solving (I leave it as an open question whether
it still has to be upgraded in a further synthesis); and (ii) that probabilistic and
deterministic causation can be unified.
Pleasingly, DEF-P is objective, has an immediate connection to reality: according
to it, causal connections are ‘measurable’ in a scientific way! At least, if one knows
the relevant probabilities with a sufficient precision to verify conditions (i) and (ii) of
the definition. That is not only dear to the heart of the realist, but is also needed for a
synthetic philosophy resonating with science. At the same time, DEF-P (and DEFM and DEF-F) only work for contexts that can be formalised (to some degree) and
described by variables (DEF-M was at least originally developed for such contexts).

As said, DEF-P only works concretely36 for systems for which one can define probabilities with a reasonable precision. The conditions under which that is possible are
given in the previous chapter.
But in many real-life situations such formalisation is not possible, and yet one
would like to find causes there too. The model that can most straightforwardly be
applied to daily-life contexts is the counterfactual one (DEF-CF0’); it has therefore
the potential to be the most general variant. Let us therefore return to the counterfactual definition, and see whether we can make it compatible with the other definitions,
in a final synthesis. The ideal would be that a modified counterfactual definition
would turn out to be the encompassing model, from which the other definitions can
be derived as special cases.

5.6 Final Synthesis. Representation-Relative
Counterfactual Cause
In their most robust formulation, the definitions we encountered in this chapter are
gathered in Table 5.2 (only def-mn is not given, since similar to def-sn).
I have already argued that DEF-F, DEF-M, and DEF-P strongly resonate, and
coincide under rather broad conditions, notably for deterministic (and formalisable)
systems. A priori DEF-M and DEF-P have the potential to have a wider applicability
and encompass probabilistic systems. But DEF-M cannot be a good definition for
probabilistic systems, as it stands. Indeed, “a (possible) change in X will change the
probability distribution of Y” can be interpreted as equivalent to clause (i) in DEF-P.
But it is well known that correlation (as in (i)) is not sufficient for causation; therefore
36 One might still consider that a definition as DEF-P holds true for all systems and events – a

hypothesis that assumes that probability theory describes ultimately all events in the universe. That
might well be a valid assumption, but it is a metaphysical one; it cannot directly be verified; and,
as argued in the chapter on probability, it doesn’t help to define objective, numerical probability
values for most events.

158

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

DEF-M needs another clause, such as clause (ii) of DEF-P (supposing this definition
can be validated as an overarching definition for probabilistic systems). In any case,
DEF-M must be upgraded for probabilistic systems. More importantly, one senses
that it should be possible to establish a close link between DEF-M and DEF-CF0’
(and def-sn/mn) for deterministic systems. The key difference is that in DEF-CF0’
there is an essential ceteris-paribus clause, while in DEF-M representation-relativity,
i.e. the ‘according to a given theory/model T’ clause, plays a fundamental problemsolving role; this clause also appears in DEF-F and DEF-P. Allow me to insist,
without these clauses these definitions lose their problem-solving capacity.
The problem we encountered with the counterfactual model, was that for neuron
diagrams it leads to the contrived definition def-sn, which definitely loses some of
its esthetical attraction due to the ambivalent-neuron clause. How can we get rid
of this clause, by using what we learned from the other models? Could it be that
we can eliminate the ambivalent-neuron clause by simply adding the representationrelativity clause to DEF-CF0’, and unify by the same token DEF-CF0’with the
other definitions? In our effort of synthesis, this is the first idea that comes to mind;
it is the simplest to try; and it works! (This is a patent example of how unification
often solves problems on different fronts.) In the following I will show that using
the representation-relativity clause alongside the ceteris-paribus clause in DEF-CF0’
directly leads to the desired final synthesis, so that we do not need def-sn and def-mn
anymore: they will be subsumed under the final counterfactual definition (DEF-CF
below).
Recall that I have already hinted in previous sections, notably at the end of Sect. 3
on functional cause, that the representation-relativity or theory-relativity of causal
claims might be fundamental also beyond science. Just as in Chapter 3, I argued in
Sect. 3 that in everyday contexts ‘theory/model’ should be understood in a broader
sense, namely as ‘coherent set of hypotheses/assumptions’ or ‘conceptual framework’ or ‘coherent set of beliefs’ or sometimes even just ‘common-sense beliefs’
(in other chapters I often designate all these notions by ‘T*’, but in this chapter I
will use ‘T’ for simplicity). Also beyond science sets of hypotheses are needed to
derive causes; examples abound. A detective trying to solve a murder case better have
a ‘theory’ about it: perhaps not always a set of scientific theories, but in any case
a more or less detailed and interconnected set of assumptions, describing more or
less detailed and interconnected mechanisms, processes, motives etc. Without such
a coherent set of hypotheses, the detective’s identification of causes (the culprit) will
likely be wrong. Similarly, a medical doctor, when looking for causes of complicated health problems, needs to know a set of theories and hypotheses representing
mechanisms etc.; without these her diagnoses will be doubtful at best. The better
the theories she knows, the better—more adequate and precise—is her identification
of causes. And different doctors often state different causes: one more hint to the
representation-relativity of causal claims. As already noted, representation/theoryrelativity (in the broad sense) resonates with the context-relativity discussed in the
literature (e.g. Menzies & Beebee, 2020 §4, cf. Section 3). But it makes it more
precise, since it is decidedly better at problem-solving.

5.6 Final Synthesis. Representation-Relative Counterfactual Cause

159

In sum, the investigations in this chapter suggest to upgrade DEF-CF0’ to DEFCF in the following way:
X is a cause of Y (according to T)
iff
if X were not to occur, Y would not occur (cet. par., according to T).
(DEF-CF)
DEF-CF is my final definition of representation-relative counterfactual cause. Let
us now study its efficacy. T is, as always, some given set of hypotheses/model/theory
about a factual situation involving X and Y. As in most counterfactual theories, X and
Y are most easily understood as actual events; but I will argue that the generalisation
to types is well possible. Here are some specifications (S(i)–S(iii)) completing the
definition:
S(i). The definition stipulates, simply, that in order to identify the causes of Y (according to
the representation or perspective T of Y), one needs to use T for inferring (mentally, counterfactually) which X, if not occurring or occurring differently than in the factual situation
under scrutiny, would lead, according to T, to Y’s not occurring or occurring differently. So,
it should be understood that ‘if X were not to occur’ must be interpreted broadly enough,
notably as ‘if X were to happen differently than it actually happens’ (note the link with DEFM). What ‘different’ is depends ultimately on the perspective T (see illustrations below, e.g.
when we deal with ‘tampering’ in RR6).
S(ii) (compatibility-with-T). Definition DEF-CF can be interpreted as a neutral definition
of cause-within-T, where T might be any theory, even implausible, fanciful, etc., but also
as a methodological rule of how to identify ‘(sufficiently) true/correct/reasonable’ causes –
which is what we are normally after. In the latter case T is supposed to be an appropriate
representation, a reasonably adequate model of the reality in which X and Y occur or are
supposed to occur; T should be explanatorily compatible with, applicable to, X and Y. One
might also say: T should ‘cover’ X and Y, much as in Hempel’s covering-law model of
scientific explanation; I will make the parallel with Hempel’s model explicit below. T should
also be compatible with the counterfactual situation in which X does not occur (not-X or
¬X); not-X should be possible or at least analysable within T; else we cannot derive correct
causes of it. (It seems harmless to stipulate that a theory T being compatible with Z implies,
among other things, that the theory allows to infer what would happen if Z would not happen
or would happen differently. As an example, take the neuron diagrams: one can imagine each
neuron being on or off, but not being blue – that is a hypothetical fact Z that is not covered
by, not compatible with, the model of what neuron diagrams are. Take any ‘theory-about-Z’.)
In other words, in order to identify realistic causes, once presented with an actual Y, one
should consider a T that contains realistic hypotheses/models of the situation in which Y
occurs. One should ‘apply’ T to Y/not-Y and X/not-X. This is what happens in real life:
when inquiring about a cause, one studies the situation within some (hopefully adequate)
conceptual framework. In a scientific context, T may be a (reasonably efficient) scientific
theory. In daily contexts, T is often simply the usual (and very large) set of assumptions
that people normally have about reality, or a subset of it that applies to the causal situation
under scrutiny: an explanatory story about the causal situation. For future use, let us call this
set TC , the ‘common beliefs’. (The notion ‘explanatory story’ is particularly helpful to deal
with the ‘switching’ problem under RR1 below, for instance.) TC might also be enriched
with scientific theories. It might be enriched with particular knowledge/hypotheses about the

160

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

particular actual situation. All that matters is that the perspective T be specified. [It is again
easy to cast this in the inquirer-assigner parlance. As argued in detail in Sect. 3 on functional
cause, in the logical sequence of causal conversations it is the cause-inquirer who comes
first, explicitly or usually implicitly, with a perspective T in mind; in logical principle the
cause-inquirer imposes T, one might say. T is reflected in the way the effect Y is phrased.
Sure, the representation T is often basic and implicit, semi-conscious, perhaps. For maximal
coherence, the cause X, as proposed by the cause-assigner, should then be within the domain
of application of T. But in conversations maximal coherence is not always respected: the
cause-assigner might come with her particular, different T; that surely happens in real life.
The ultimate requirement of a precise use of the word ‘cause’ is that T be specified.]
S(iii). The ceteris-paribus clause has the same meaning as given in Sect. 2 in def-sn for the
neuron diagrams. As discussed there, this clause is of key importance when investigating
whether ¬X leads to ¬Y: in this (in practice almost always) mental process of investigation/
deliberation one should keep fixed what can be kept fixed (according to T) when changing
X. Many examples of the ceteris-paribus application are given below and in Sect. 2 on the
neuron diagrams.

Although straightforward, some readers might ask for an explicit account of how
to evaluate the counterfactual in DEF-CF and of the precise meaning of the clause
‘according to T’. Here the following equivalent variant of DEF-CF might be helpful:
“X is a cause of Y according to T iff T implies that ¬X leads to ¬Y (cet. par.)”, where
‘¬X’ stands for ‘X not occurring or occurring differently’, much as it was defined
in def-sn for neuron diagrams. As mentioned above, the implication in ‘according
to T’ or in ‘T implies’ or in ‘leads to’ that is needed for using DEF-CF is akin
to the implication in Hempel’s scheme of scientific explanation. While Hempel’s
scheme is based on subsumption under laws (in short: laws and particular facts
imply the explanandum), in DEF-CF the subsumption is under T. But as illustrated
by the examples provided, there is no essential difference; for that matter, nontrivial Ts often contain laws, quasi-laws, generalisations based on regularities, rules,
etc. This being said, the implication-from-T must be interpreted more widely than
as ‘necessary logical deduction’: ‘most probable inference’ as in informal logic is
fine (see e.g. the example in RR3 below). The latter type of inference resembles
abduction, which is not a type of logically necessary deduction.
If one wishes to go in greater detail, one needs to distinguish event and type
causation. Event causation needs the representation T to be applied to particular
events; type causation to types. Also Hempel’s model of explanation works for events
and types. According to Hempel, in order to explain event Y (why does Y occur?),
one should subsume Y under at least one law L and some particular event(s) X. For
instance, Y = my flowers died; L = plants need water to survive; X = I did not water
my plants (neither did someone else). Hempel’s scheme says, in short: L and X imply
Y. One might say: one has to apply the law L to the event X and draw (according
to the laws of reasoning) the conclusion, which is Y. Very similarly, in my account
of ‘X causes Y according to T’, one has to apply T to the effect Y, and verify or
infer (complying with the laws of reasoning) which events X of the supposedly real
scenario would, if not occurring, imply the non-occurrence of Y. Subsuming under a
representation T (a set of coherent hypotheses, a model,…) is just a generalisation of
subsuming under the laws L of Hempel. Again, this procedure necessarily involves

5.6 Final Synthesis. Representation-Relative Counterfactual Cause

161

perspectives T that apply to Y and X; T must tell a coherent story about Y and X;
else one finds incoherent causes.
In conclusion, we have the following correspondence:
(Hempel) X explains Y (according to L) then X and L imply Y
(DEF-CF) X causes Y (according to T) iff ¬X and T imply ¬Y.
(In Hempel’s scheme I use the implication ‘then’ only, since the reverse implication is problematic.) Some might prefer the latter expression as the final version
of DEF-CF, if a time and ceteris-paribus clause are included, and if implication
is understood as specified above. The close correspondence between both accounts
resonates with the idea that causation and explanation are closely related, and that
one can characterise T as an explanatory story involving X and Y.
A priori, if we can corroborate it, DEF-CF would have vast advantages over
classical counterfactual definitions, in which the truth conditions of counterfactuals
remain vague (Paul & Hall, 2013)—recall e.g. the complex metaphorical account
of D. Lewis. In DEF-CF the truth of ‘¬X leads to ¬Y according to T’ is usually
unambiguously decided: it simply depends on whether within T it is true that ¬X
implies ¬Y. In principle this is objectively verifiable by anyone knowing T. Logical
or at least rational inference and reasoning are verifiable, decidable. A next important
advantage is that DEF-CF works not only on token level, as the original counterfactual models, but as easily on type level, just as Hempel’s model of explanation works
in both cases. I will illustrate this claim by examples, and come to a conclusion about
this matter in RR7 below.
I now show how DEF-CF solves problems of existing accounts (results RR1RR7); in particular, how it subsumes def-sn and def-mn (RR2).
RR1. Context-relativity of causes. Representation-relativity is related to the
context-relativity discussed in Sect. 3 and in the literature (e.g. Hitchcock & Knobe,
2009, Paul & Hall, 2013 p. 183ff , Menzies & Beebee, 2020 §4). A problem case
discussed under different guises deals with the cause of my flowers having died (Y)
(e.g. Paul & Hall, 2013 p. 183, Menzies & Beebee, 2020 §4). My gardener did not
water them (X1), but also the Queen did not (X2). Under DEF-CF0’ and the basic
counterfactual models, X1 is a cause, but so is X2, counterintuitively. How does
DEF-CF solve this case? One has to start with representing the situation/scenario
in which X and Y occur. Suppose, then, that the roses died (Y) and Mr. Vervoort’s
gardener did not water them enough (X1). Normally, one ponders about this tragic
situation through the lens of our common beliefs, TC (let us stipulate this). At any
rate, notice that X1 is a realistic possibility according to TC , the common beliefs
about the habits of people and gardeners. We all know that people, even the most
conscientious, occasionally omit to do their usual tasks. Next, it straightforwardly
follows from TC , containing also some folk biology, that if Mr. Vervoort’s gardener
would have watered the roses enough (ceteris paribus), they (probably) would not
have died. Our usual beliefs license us to conclude that watering roses enough is
vital for their flourishing, and therefore that not watering enough is often lethal. So

162

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

DEF-CF comes to the conclusion that X1 is a likely cause of Y according to T C .
TC is applicable to, covers, can be used to assess, the situation involving Y and X1.
However, TC does not cover X2. TC cannot coherently conclude that if X2 would not
have happened, so if the Queen would have watered Mr. Vervoort’s garden, the roses
would not have perished, because according to that same TC the Queen will, alas,
never water Mr. Vervoort’s garden. It is specification S(ii) of DEF-CF (‘compatibility’) that stipulates that (correct) causes can only be identified by X-compatible
conceptual frameworks T, and that in such Ts also ¬X must be realistic (else X
cannot be a realistic cause). As stipulated in S(ii): obviously, X cannot be a cause
according to T if ¬X cannot realistically happen according to T.
In conclusion, according to DEF-CF the Queen not watering Mr. Vervoort’s roses
is not the cause of their perishing. For maximal disambiguation of causal claims, it
is necessary to add here ‘according to TC ’ or something along this line. It could be,
after all, that according to the goofy story of the popular writer W, the cause of Mr.
Vervoort’s roses’ untimely death is the fact that the Queen did not water them. All
that matters is that T be specified.
We can now relate to another discussion in (Paul & Hall, 2013): as they and
several other authors have observed, causal investigations are often ‘infected by the
normative’, especially when causal omissions are involved (see examples on p. 183ff ,
including the above queen-and-gardener case). Paul and Hall conclude: “It remains
an open question how best to give a precise characterization of the phenomenon
these examples illustrate” (p. 184). Within my account the answer seems straightforward: they illustrate representation-relativity. More explicitly: causal investigations,
especially when omissions are involved, are often assessed through the lens of representations that include normative models. In the queen-and-gardener story we look
at things through the lens of a TC that surely includes some normative considerations
(the ‘cause’ is interpreted as the ‘culprit’, after all): common-sense beliefs about
the tasks and habits of gardeners and queens. It seems quite possible that people
engage in causal investigations relatively more frequently when norms are at stake
and culprits need to be found; at any rate, these norms can well be part of the perspective through which one assesses causal scenarios. This then automatically infects
the causal claims, in accordance with DEF-CF.
Here is another example (Paul & Hall, 2013, p. 183): “Billy and Suzy are playing
soccer with each other, but on rival teams. One of Suzy’s teammates successfully
kicks a goal. Both Billy and Suzy were nearby, and either one of them could easily
have blocked the goal. But we credit the goal in part, at least, to Billy’s failure to
block it, and not to Suzy’s failure to block it”. To answer the question “what caused
the goal?” we look at the game through the perspective of our usual model TC of how
soccer is played, which contains the rule or norm that only adversary players try to
block goal attempts of a given team. Hence according to TC , only Billy’s ineffective
blocking is part of the causes37 ; according to that same TC , the option ‘Suzy blocks’
makes no sense (in the given situation).
37 Because if Billy had blocked efficiently – an option compatible with (the norms of) T

would not have been made.

C – the goal

5.6 Final Synthesis. Representation-Relative Counterfactual Cause

163

A last subtle example is a case of ‘switching’ (Paul & Hall, 2013, p. 232): “A train
rushes along a track towards the point where the track forks. Suzy flips a switch,
directing the train along the left-hand side of the fork. If Suzy hadn’t flipped the
switch, the train would have taken the right side of the fork. A few miles later, the
left and right tracks converge, and a mile after this point Billy robs the train. Assume
that taking the left side of the fork rather than the right makes no difference to the
train’s manner or time of arrival. Does Suzy’s flip cause Billy’s robbery?” Paul and
Hall ask whether, due to the so plausible principle of transitivity, Suzy’s flip might
be a cause, since it causes the train to be at the place of robbery, which in turn is
necessary for the robbery to be possible. Within DEF-CF, Billy (or his act) is the
cause according to TC , more specifically TC focused on/restricted to common, indeed
trivial, knowledge about robberies. We all agree that if Billy hadn’t stolen stuff, there
would have been no robbery (ceteris paribus, in the situation depicted). On the other
hand, Suzy’s flip is the cause of the train being at the place of crime, according
to a different T C ’ focused on/restricted to the simple facts of railway transport and
switches. Note that TC = TC ’, more precisely, our usual beliefs about robberies (TC )
do not cover facts of railway transport, are not applicable to the event: “the train
was at place P”. Put differently, in the situation depicted, the explanatory story about
the train trajectory differs from the explanatory story about the robbery. Note that
TC surely has a specific normative dimension about human responsibility, not about
train locations.
This example suggests an important conclusion: if one wishes to appeal to transitivity between consecutive causes, one should consider causes that are relative to
the same perspective, that are causes within the same perspective. I will corroborate
this idea under RR6 below (it is somewhat reminiscent of the consistent-referencing
principle introduced in the chapter on Gettier’s problem). The example also suggests
that the neuron diagram in Fig. 45 in (Paul & Hall, 2013, p. 232) is not a good representation of the real-life scenario, since in the neuron diagram the switching does
seem to come out as a cause. That neuron diagrams (as usually defined) are often
indeed not a good model of reality will convincingly be shown in RR2 and RR6
below.
It is remarkable how many problems in the literature somehow hinge on contextdependence, subtle or less subtle. With some exercise, one can straightforwardly
solve these through representation-relativity.
RR2. Early preemption, bis. Final verdict for all cases in Table 5.1. Let us look
again and in detail at the surprisingly complex problem of early preemption, as treated
already in Sect. 2. We will see in the following that this problem is subtle indeed, and
tells us a lot about the pertinence of neuron diagrams and their interpretation. Let us
start with the example from (Hitchcock, 2007): “Assassin poisons Victim’s coffee.
Victim drinks it and dies. If Assassin hadn’t poisoned the coffee, Backup would have,
and Victim would have died anyway. Victim would not have died if there had been
no poison in the coffee.” As we saw in Sect. 2, DEF-CF0 gives the wrong result
(namely that Assassin is not the cause of Victim’s death). What does DEF-CF say?
I will first swiftly go over the solution, and then elaborate in detail.

164

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

Fig. 5.1bis The neuron
diagram believed to
represent real cases of early
pre-emption (reproduced
from Fig. 1, Paul & Hall,
2013, p. ix).

Let us assume that we have no extra knowledge, and that we assess Victim’s death
through the common beliefs, TC . Actually, on close inspection, TC with DEF-CF
give us the correct result. TC tells that if Assassin had not poisoned the coffee ceteris
paribus, Victim would not have died. ‘Ceteris paribus’ means: keeping fixed literally
everything that can, according to TC , be kept fixed, including Backup’s action, in
this case: his not acting. Indeed, common beliefs tell us that the scenario in which
Backup does not do his evil deed even if he had an agreement with Assassin is well
conceivable; Backup has free will, after all; he can change his mind. So thanks to the
ceteris-paribus clause we come to the right conclusion; more precisely, we come to
the conclusion that believers in TC prefer. Or so says my model.
Why is this solution not more transparent, why has it been discussed so often in
the literature, as witnessed by the large number of proposed solutions in Paul and
Hall (2013)? I believe that part of the explanation is that the neuron diagram that is
used to analyse the case (Fig. 5.1 repeated as 5.1bis), as well as the succinct wording
à la Hitchcock, are too crude, not really appropriate to describe the factual situation
under investigation. Let us have a closer look.
Hitchcock’s version (version-1) is a condensed wording using a conditional (‘if
Assassin hadn’t poisoned the coffee, Backup would have, and Victim would have
died anyway’). This is not a sequential description of (supposedly) actual events.
In the facts, either Assassin acts or Backup (or both); the rest is speculation, so
to speak. Things can be rephrased as a description of what actually happens in the
following way, for example (version-2, where A = Assassin etc.): “A and B conspire
to kill V by poisoning his coffee. They agree that normally A will do the job, but if
A’s husband would be around at the fatal time tm they have chosen for the murder,
she will send a message to B so that he can replace her. Around tm no husband is to
be seen, and A administers the poison, killing V.” Now, the fact that B would have
done the job if A hadn’t is, for ordinary people with limited knowledge, a speculative
assumption. But in Hitchcock’s story this assumption is declared from the perspective
(TH ) of an all-knowing cause-inquirer—a story-teller—who has unfailing access
to B’s thoughts and knows that B would necessarily do the job. That is far from
what humans normally can know. (Recall Chapter 3 on Gettier’s problem, where
we found out about the representation-relativity of ‘to know’.) Arguably, ordinary
people assessing the situation and looking for the culprit do not rely on TH and the
description in version-1 (even if verbally presented with version-1), but on TC and a
description in the style of version-2. TC and a story à la version-2 give at least minimal
information about events and mechanisms in the real world. At any rate, according to
my account of causal identification, when people identify ‘sufficiently true’ causes,

5.6 Final Synthesis. Representation-Relative Counterfactual Cause

165

they rely on sufficiently realistic descriptions of Y and sufficiently realistic conceptual
frameworks, say TC ; and they use the ceteris-paribus clause skilfully (or at least their
causal inferences are in logical agreement with such a skilful use). Now, according to
TC it is perfectly conceivable that A’s inaction leads to a living V and to a non-acting
B. In symbols with obvious interpretation, we have the supposedly real scenario
A & ¬B & ¬V; inverting A ceteris paribus leads, according to TC , to ¬A & ¬B &
V. Here ¬B is kept fixed while it can be held fixed; this is the minimally different
version (from the supposedly actual scenario) that complies with realistic ‘laws’ of
TC . Again, according to our usual beliefs there is no link of necessity between two
people’s actions in the situation sketched; for instance because they have free will
(or have so according to usual beliefs!). In sum, it is quite realistic that B remains
inactive even when A doesn’t do the job.
On the contrary, in TH , Hitchcock’s representation, there is an implicit link of
necessity. For cognitively limited beings as we are, it is difficult to assess who is the
cause/culprit assuming a link of necessity and TH (suffices to re-read TH a few times).
Perhaps A and B are joint causes according to TH : that is indeed the conclusion I
will come to in a moment, by using DEF-CF applied to TH . One can imagine a
far-fetched scenario in which there is a link of necessity between A and B, say via
wireless-connected implants introduced in their brains (with their accord) by an evil
surgeon. Suppose this mechanism ensures that if A does not kill V, B necessarily
does. Then it is much more intuitive to call both A and B causes: they have sealed
their fate in unison and have become one automat, so to speak. Interestingly, in court,
in the normal interpretation of version-2 it seems likely that the main responsibility
would be attributed to A, who did the fatal act; in court free will surely is considered
real. But in the case with brain implants a court might well conclude that B’s role is
equivalent to A’s, or nearly so.
Thus, it seems it is only this kind of scenario-cum-necessity that is well represented by the diagram in Fig. 5.1 and Fig. 5.1bis, under the usual rules (TN ) given
in Sect. 2 (caution: in Fig. 5.1 and Fig. 5.1bis A and B do not correspond to the
A(ssassin) and B(ackup) of above story). By definition, TN (or ‘T-neuron’) contains
the mechanical, abstract rules of the working of the diagram as given in Sect. 2.
Indeed, the mechanisms at work in the neuron diagrams are such that there is also a
link of necessity: in Fig. 5.1 C- necessarily implies B+ and E+, ceteris paribus. That
is how the abstract diagram and its connections are defined; that is what TN stipulates; in the abstract diagram there is no further ‘real-life’ interpretation given to its
elements. So we concluded in Sect. 2 that DEF-C0’ containing the ceteris-paribus
clause did not deliver the intuitive answer (that only C+ is the cause). DEF-C0’
(perhaps enriched with what we learned38 with def-mn) leads to the conclusion that
{C+, A+} are joint causes. But I would argue, along the above lines, that the intuitive
cause is the cause according to TC , namely C+. This is an illustration of how manifest
representation-relativity is.

38 Namely that ‘X’ in the definition can correspond to sets of single-neuron events.

166

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

In conclusion, DEF-CF can well help to interpret this case—and at the same time
explain why it is complex. Indeed, for analysing early preemption and the diagram of
Fig. 5.1, DEF-CF, which is at first sight very similar to DEF-C0’, leads to following
much deeper analysis:
(i) If one [first the cause-inquirer, in principle] interprets the diagram in abstracto,
i.e. according to T N , the strict mechanistic prescription of how the diagram works,
the causes of E+ are {C+, A+} (according to TN ).
(ii) If one interprets this diagram in concreto, e.g. as representing version-2 above,
i.e. according to T C , then the cause of E+ is C+ (according to TC ).
It is key to note that, while I needed in Sect. 2 the clumsy ‘ambivalent loop’ clause in
def-sn to come to the correct conclusion, DEF-CF leads to it in a simple, and above
all, unified way. One can further corroborate this analysis in a quantitative way using
DEF-P, the definition for probabilistic systems, as shown in Appendix 5.1.
Another conclusion I draw is that the neuron diagrams are, against appearances,
a too idealised model of most realistic cases of early preemption! (If true, philosophers would have fallen prey to a fallacy frequent also among scientists, namely to
misrepresent a complex reality by overly simplistic models. Isn’t that the history of
science, and of philosophy, in a nutshell?) The diagram interpreted with TN is a good
model of TH but not of the more relevant TC .
To model concrete real-life cases of early preemption as version-2 above, one
could propose a new type of connection and diagram as in Fig. 5.4, replacing Fig. 5.1:
a double connection that ensures that one interprets the diagram as it should according
to intuition (TC ).
In Fig. 5.4 the double connection with dot from C+ to B- indicates that it is
‘maximally blocking’, i.e. remains blocking even if C+ is inverted to C-. It is this
diagram that is a good model of a real-life case of preemption as version-2 above: it
leads to the conclusion that only C+ is the cause of E+.
Can one solve all diagrams with ambivalent neurons in Table 5.1 (diagram 1, 8,
10, 17, 19, 20, 24) in the same way, i.e. by putting a maximally blocked neuron in
the blocking branch (see another example in Fig. 5.5) ? Yes, to the extent that these
diagrams are intended to represent real-life cases, as it always seems to be the case
in (Paul & Hall, 2013). Just as in realistic cases of early preemption, we can assume
that for real-life cases involving ambivalent loops intuition and TC tell us that the
blocked neuron in the blocking branch of the ambivalent fork can remain blocked

In (Vervoort & Nikolaev, 2025) it is shown explicitly that this ‘maximal blocking
interpretation’ leads to the intuitive causes in all these diagrams; the definition given
by Vervoort and Nikolaev is a slight reformulation of def-sn in terms of this notion
of maximal blocking; it leads to the same results as in Table 5.1.
Another example with an ambivalent loop is given in Fig. 5.5, which is the
upgraded version of diagram 10 of Table 5.1 (Fig. 14 in Paul & Hall, 2013). With the
interpretation we gave to the double connection, one does not need the ambivalentneuron clause and def-sn to find the intuitive answer (that A+ and C+ both are separate
causes of E+); one simply needs the ‘simple counterfactual’ rule of DEF-CF… and
stipulate that these are the causes under TC for instance or under TN’ , where TN’ is
TN combined with the mentioned interpretation of the double connection. (Under
TN {C+, A+} are the joint causes, which is in this case only a marginally different
answer.)
Of course, drawing double (maximally blocking) connections in all ambivalent
loops is just a trick; we do not need this visual tool to come to the desired result; we just
need DEF-CF, just as for early preemption. So the important news is that the intuitive
results of Table 5.1 can be derived from DEF-CF applied to the diagrams, with T =
TC for instance, more precisely, any perspective that does not presuppose a link of
necessity as in TN , the mechanistic interpretation. As in early pre-emption, it is the
ceteris-paribus clause combined with the representation-clause that does the work
to understand what happens in ambivalent loops.39 Diagrams without ambivalent
neurons were already solved by the SCF-clause of def-sn and def-mn; but that is
part of DEF-CF; which therefore solves all cases of Table 5.1.
RR3. Double prevention, bis. We had already shown in Sect. 2 that def-sn gives the
right conclusion for diagrams 5 and 12, cases of double prevention (corresponding
to Fig. 10 and 29 in Paul & Hall, 2013). Then DEF-CF should also come to the right

39 When applying DEF-CF to the diagrams, one should also recall what we learned from def-sn

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

conclusion: this is immediate for diagrams that are solved by the simple counterfactual rule (SCF in Table 5.1) of def-sn, since that is also the basis of DEF-CF. As
we have learned, it is wise to apply DEF-CF to real-life cases, and be cautious with
the diagrams. Paul and Hall give following example (2013, p. 175), corresponding
to diagram 12: “David makes the coffee (A), and fills his cup (E). Meanwhile, Steffi
scoops up James the cat (C) as he lashes his tail wildly (B); her quick action prevents
a disastrous spilling (D). Most of us want to say that Steffi saved the coffee—that is,
C was among the causes of E.” DEF-CF applied with TC comes to the same conclusion: we all can well imagine that if Steffi had not scooped up James on the loose
(ceteris paribus), the coffee would have been spilled. That is a logical consequence
of TC , containing some folk physics and biology, applied to the situation depicted—
not a logically necessary conclusion perhaps, but at least a very likely one. (This
type of inference resembles abduction, which is different from logically necessary
deduction. But note that we can rephrase the example and trim down and specify TC
in such a way that that conclusion would be a necessary conclusion, in Hempel-style
deduction. So there is no essential difference.)
We still have to solve the transitivity problem related to diagram 5 (see Sect. 2).
We can best do that once we have studied ‘tampering’ below.
RR4. Late preemption, bis. Definition def-sn already solved this problem, since it
applies to events located in space–time points. Here is the example of Sect. 2 again
(cf. diagram 25 in Table 5.1): C throws a rock (C+) at a bottle and shatters it (E+);
A also throws a rock but a little later, so that A’s rock sails through empty space
but would have shattered the bottle if C’s hadn’t. The intuitive cause of E+ is C+.
This is also the verdict one obtains with DEF-CF (applied with TC and recalling
specification S(i)): if C would not have thrown a rock (ceteris paribus), the bottle
would have shattered differently (at a different time, with a different momentum of
impact, with a different pattern of shattering, etc.). That is a consequence of TC ,
which includes some rudimentary physics. If, on the other hand, A would not have
thrown a rock (ceteris paribus), the bottle would still have shattered in the same way.
Hence C’s throw is the only cause (according to TC ).
Again, we come to a verdict in a straightforward way, to be compared to the much
more complex accounts described in the contemporary literature.
RR5. Overdetermination, bis. We had already solved overdetermination using defmn, using its SCF-clause. Hence DEF-CF must also solve it in a straightforward way,
with as additional advantage that a subtle explanation can be given, differentiating
between different causal perspectives people may have. In this case, both a reallife description in terms of bullets and bottles (within TC ) and a purely mechanistic
description (within TN ) based on diagram 26 would lead to the conclusion that {C+,
A+} are joint causes of E+, since only if both do not occur E does not occur (according
to TC and TN ). Neither C+ nor A+ are separate causes.
RR6. Tampering and transitivity. ‘Tampering’ is a very interesting case discussed
in detail by Paul and Hall; it is diagrammatically represented by Fig. 5.6 (reproducing
Fig. 15 of Paul & Hall, 2013, p. 92ff ). This is a variant of early preemption, in which

5.6 Final Synthesis. Representation-Relative Counterfactual Cause

169

Fig. 5.6 ‘Tampering’, a
variant of early preemption
(idem as Fig. 15, p. 92,
Paul & Hall, 2013). Neurons
can fire with different
intensities, indicated in the
diagram

now neurons fire with different intensities, indicated in the diagram, according to
a sum/subtraction-rule40 (subtraction when the signal is inhibiting). For instance, B
fires with intensity 5 because it receives a +10 intensity from A and a -5 intensity
from C. Let us add that the neurons can only fire with intensity 5 and 10 (intensity =
0 corresponds to being off). With this, Fig. 5.6 is self-explaining. What is the cause
of E firing?
Paul and Hall state that “C and A are joint causes”, which they consider the
obvious intuitive verdict (there is no real justification given). Sure, on the one hand,
one intuits that somehow C also ‘does something’ in the firing of E. Moreover, if
transitivity holds—and this is considered by many an obvious feature of causation—,
then C must be a cause (since E’s intensity is caused by D and B, and D can only be
caused by C, intuitively).
DEF-CF gives again a finer analysis. Just as in the case of early preemption, one can
look at the question from at least two perspectives, TN and TC . First, let us consider
the matter under TN , the mechanistic rules of the neuron diagram as just defined in
detail. Then actually only A+ is a cause of E+, against above verdict, and against
transitivity! Indeed, note that C, as a kind of ambivalent neuron, contributes to E in
its upper branch always exactly as much as it subtracts from the signal from A in
the lower branch; this happens for any of its possible intensities (0, 5, 10); in this
sense it always exactly neutralises itself. (An analogous remark holds for the classic
early-preemption diagram.) Indeed, we can write (iX is the intensity of neuron X):
iE = iD + iB ; iD = iC ; iB = iA – iC ; hence
iE = iC + (iA – iC ) = iA .
Since this holds for any value of the intensities, as specified in TN , the intensity of E is
always equal to that of A, whatever the intensity of C is. DEF-CF gives, accordingly:
A is a cause, but not C, since if C would fire differently (ceteris paribus), E would
fire in the same way (according to TN ). Thus, transitivity does again not hold in the
mechanistic neuron model TN , just as it is violated in diagram 5 (cf. R1 in Sect. 2).
Now, we already saw when treating early pre-emption that intuition does not
always fit to TN , and that the neuron diagrams must be treated with caution when
it comes to concrete real-life situations. I believe we can now, finally, expose the
crux of the problem. I believe the heart of the matter lies in the idea that ‘perfect
ambivalence’, ‘perfect causal self-neutralisation’ as in Fig. 5.6 is rarely a good
40 This rule is reminiscent of how artificial neural nets in AI work.

170

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

Fig. 5.7 A variant of tampering (described by model TN ’), itself a variant of early preemption.
Neurons can fire with different intensities; and stimulate or inhibit with different strengths, the
numbers in brackets. In this example, the strength of the emitted stimulating signal is equal to the
intensity of the emitting neuron, while inhibiting signals can be weaker (see neuron C)

representation of what happens in the real world.41 The same remark applies to the
original diagram Fig. 5.1, the emblematic interpretation of early preemption. Perfect
self-neutralisation means: annihilation with numerical precision. To better see the
severe limitation perfect ambivalence implies, we can generalise TN and upgrade it
to TN ’ to take into account numerical differences in the causal action of C in the
upper and lower path. In TN ’ we simply stipulate that iD = iC , and iB = iA – i’C (iC
i’C ): C emits a different intensity to D than to B; it stimulates with a different
strength than it inhibits. Does this not look much more natural? To the least, it is
more general: mathematically, TN is a special case of TN ’ (when taking iC exactly
equal to i’C ). One obtains as the defining equations of TN ’ (neurons can fire with any
numerical intensity, see details in the caption of Fig. 5.7):
iE = iD + iB ; iD = iC ; iB = iA – i’C ; hence
iE = iC – i’C + iA iA .
And now DEF-CF comes to the conclusion that, in general, both A and C are causes
(according to TN ’), as intuition has it, and in accordance with transitivity. In sum,
TN ’ surely is a better model than TN for many realistic situations, and therefore
more likely to be compliant with TC (of course, all depends on the realistic case in
question).
A numerical example that operates according to the rules of TN ’ is given in Fig. 5.7.
Here the inhibiting strength of C = i’C = 4. In the factual diagram iE = 11, but if iC
= 0, iE = 10, and if iA = 0, then iE = 1. In general, if A or C fire differently (cet.
par.), E will fire differently, as stipulated by the equations: DEF-CF concludes that
A+ and C+ are causes (according to TN ’).
This conclusion is corroborated by DEF-M and DEF-F,42 which also arrive at this
intuitive verdict. That is excellent, even if not a surprise anymore since we already

5.6 Final Synthesis. Representation-Relative Counterfactual Cause

171

know that they are equivalent in formalisable deterministic cases. Note also how
this case of tampering illustrates how ‘occurring differently’ (mentioned in DEFCF) depends on the model (recall specification S(i) of DEF-CF). A neuron firing
differently means ‘firing with a different intensity’; to the least, this may be what the
cause-inquirer has in mind. Which intensities are allowed must be specified in the
representation T.
At this point a few important conclusions can be drawn. A key finding of our
investigation is that D. Lewis’s neuron diagrams, with their original rules (TN ), are
often not a good representation of reality. The problem appears when the diagrams
involve ambivalent loops, but also in other cases, cf. ‘switching’ in RR1 above.
I came to this conclusion when discussing early preemption and tampering, and I
proposed more functional variants of these diagrams (using blocking connections
or variable-strength connections) that do conform to intuition (TC ). Concomitantly,
we have seen that the double-prevention diagram 5 and the tampering diagram in
Fig. 5.6, with the usual interpretation, violate transitivity—at least when applying
DEF-CF (cf. Sect. 2, R1 on double prevention). This should not be a surprise, if these
diagrams are not realistic models. Something unnatural must have slipped into them.
It seems that the problem stems from the ambivalent loops, from the perfectly selfannihilating processes involved. These might indeed well represent only a theoretical
possibility, but not one that maps to (most) real processes. (For what it is worth, in
physics self-annihilation is anathema, at least in all systems I know of.) Perfect
self-annihilation implies, or is another interpretation of, the unnatural link of perfect
necessity we suspected to be the key problem when discussing early preemption
(RR2).
This analysis coheres with the subtle analysis DEF-CF provides of the notion
of transitivity.43 From this definition it follows that transitivity should hold, at least
when the causal events are covered by the same T. This is a logical consequence of
the definition, namely of following reasoning, consisting of applying DEF-CF to the
causal chain X1 → X2 → X3 considered from perspective T. Loosely, this leads to:
if X1 does not occur, X2 does not occur (according to T); if X2 does not occur, X3
does not occur (according to T); therefore, if X1 does not occur, X3 does not occur
(according to T). Thus transitivity must hold, at least in a context in which all events
in the transitive chain are described by the same model T. Ergo, the neuron diagrams
in which transitivity is violated (under TN ) are not realistic, are not good models
of real processes. Or put differently, the violation of transitivity is an artefact of
certain neuron diagrams, as originally defined. This conclusion is in agreement with
a conjecture made under result RR1 about switching, where we already suspected
that transitivity only holds within the same T. Here the diagram under TN implied
transitivity, while the real-world scenario did not, due to the fact that two different
perspectives are involved.
43 I will do the analysis of transitivity here solely on the basis of DEF-CF (in view of its problem-

solving capacity this may suffice). To increase the robustness of my conclusion, it would be interesting to compare these findings to other philosophers’ work on the topic (e.g. Hitchcock 2001); I
leave this to further work.

172

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

RR7. Token versus type causation. There is a long-standing discussion in the literature as to whether causation relates singular events (tokens) or general types (properties, variables, …). According to DEF-CF the answer to this classic question of
metaphysics seems clear: both are possible, since perspectives/representations can
be applied to both events—we have seen many examples—and general types. We
have already argued so when discussing the correspondence between our account
and Hempel’s scheme of scientific explanation, which also applies to events and
types. As an example of type causation, we saw in Sect. 4 that ‘gravitation causes
tides’ according to the manipulability account, DEF-M. DEF-CF arrives at the same
conclusion, since if the (strength of) gravitation would be different (ceteris paribus),
the tides would be different, for all we know from the theory of mechanics. ‘Smoking
is a cause of lung cancer’ is true (according to usual beliefs and medicine), since if
one does not smoke, the chance of lung cancer is reduced (ceteris paribus). Sure, if
statistics are available the latter example might be better dealt with using a definition
like DEF-P; but we have seen that this definition correlates with DEF-CF—to the
point that unification seems a very realistic possibility. Other examples are easily
constructed, and corroborate that DEF-CF covers type causation. [As before, the
cause-inquirer decides, in principle, whether event or type causation is meant by the
way ‘Y’ is phrased.]
RR8. Black-box cases. A last example I will mention are the black-box cases
discussed in (Paul & Hall, 2013, p. 161ff )—cases for which there is no clear outcome.
These become transparent if one realises that, to come to an unambiguous causal
verdict, one needs to specify a model, a representation; if that is absent or overtly
ambiguous as in black-box cases, one cannot make univocal causal claims. Clearly,
this is one of the main lessons to draw from DEF-CF.
In conclusion of this section, we see that DEF-CF solves a majority of cases
discussed in (Paul & Hall, 2013) and in other reference works. Importantly, it does
so in resonance with other definitions of cause discussed in this chapter. If compared
to the other accounts existing in the literature, DEF-CF derives these solutions in
a simple, unified way. This gives reasonable confidence that other problems, not
discussed here, can also be solved by our modified counterfactual account.

5.7 Conclusion
The goal of this chapter was to propose an overarching definition of cause by synthesising prominent accounts of causation. I focussed on the deterministic case—in
principle all causal situations that are not described by probability theory. Table 5.3
summarises (sanitised versions of) the definitions encountered and indicates in the
last column their application space, i.e. the nature of the causal relata. I have included
my tentative definition for probabilistic causation.
The first fact to observe is that these definitions—based on intense research efforts
by very many philosophers—have a pleasing homogeneity: broadly speaking, they
