express the same idea, be it in different guises. This is obviously an important sign
that unification might well be possible. In scientific contexts, DEF-F and DEF-P,
supposing the latter definition can be validated as a general definition for probabilistic
systems, are the most precise, when applied respectively to deterministic and probabilistic systems. But surely DEF-CF is the most encompassing definition, which
is clear from its scope: it applies most widely, as indicated in the last column in
Table 5.3. By the same token, it has the largest problem-solving capacity, as exposed
in detail in the previous section. It is therefore my privileged candidate for a solution
of the problem of defining cause.
To be applied with precision, the other definitions need a formalised model T
of a certain part of reality, at least in principle. Such models are often not known,
say in social and human sciences and in everyday contexts (even if they could be
hypothesised to exist). For instance, in a complex interpersonal situation, one may
suspect that a certain fact or factor pertaining to person A (an act, a trait of character,
a habit, …) has causal influence on the behaviour of person B; but usually we do
not have a formal (deterministic or probabilistic) model at hand describing such a
mechanism. Then DEF-F and DEF-P cannot be used with confidence, or become
speculative. On the other hand, DEF-CF is broad enough to apply.
In some more detail, I have argued that DEF-F, DEF-M, and DEF-P strongly
resonate, and coincide under broad conditions, notably for deterministic formalisable

174

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

systems described by variables. DEF-M is a definition of manipulationist inspiration
that I introduced to disencumber the original version of its anthropocentricity and
circularity worries; this makes its proximity to DEF-CF obvious. DEF-M and DEFP have the potential to have an applicability beyond deterministic variables, if they
can be shown to work for probabilistic systems44 ; but they still are in principle, as
originally understood, restricted to formalisable/scientific contexts T. DEF-CF, our
champion, can go beyond and be applied to daily-life contexts.
Accordingly, all three other definitions, at least when restricted to deterministic
systems, can be seen as special cases of DEF-CF, i.e. applications of DEF-CFto
restricted domains of reality. For instance, it is easy to show that for deterministic
variables X and Y DEF-F and DEF-CF are equivalent: the proof is similar to the
one given for the equivalence of DEF-F and DEF-M at the end of Sect. 4. That then
also makes DEF-CF and DEF-M equivalent under the condition stated.
Even if these investigations focus mainly on deterministic causation, it is fascinating that they show that a close connection with probabilistic events/properties/
systems seems at least a cogent possibility. I have already argued that DEF-P coincides with DEF-F, and hence with DEF-CF, when it is restricted to probabilistic
systems with a deterministic (hidden) basis. But note that there seems no obstacle
to interpret, or define, DEF-CF as reducing to DEF-P for probabilistic systems.
Indeed, one can for instance generalise DEF-CF thus: “if X were not to occur or
to occur differently (ceteris paribus; deterministically or probabilistically), Y would
not occur or occur differently (according to T)”. The specification “deterministically
or probabilistically” simply stipulates that there are two classes of events/types each
corresponding to a slightly different interpretation of the definition. We already know
how to interpret it for deterministic systems. Now, for (formalisable) probabilistic
systems this definition is most naturally interpreted as DEF-P, it seems. Indeed, if
probabilities exist for X and Y (according to some assumptions or model T), then the
fact that “if X were to occur differently, Y would occur differently” can naturally be
interpreted (on type level) as meaning that P(Y |X ) P(Y ), which corresponds to
clause (i) of DEF-P.45 Next, it is the ceteris-paribus clause in DEF-CF that points to
clause (ii) of DEF-P (!) Indeed, “all other things being equal” means, in formalisable
models: “all other earlier46 variables (Z) being fixed”. In other words, “if X were to
occur differently (ceteris paribus), Y would occur differently” means: “if X were to
occur differently (keeping all other earlier variables Z fixed), Y would occur differently”. In a probabilistic context, fixing variables means conditionalising on them,
so the latter expression means in probabilistic language “P(Y |X , Z) P(Y |Z) for
all earlier Z”; which is equivalent to (ii) in DEF-P.
44 DEF-M, as it stands, cannot be a good definition of probabilistic cause, as shown in the previous

section.
45 On token level, “if X = x were to occur differently (probabilistically speaking), Y = y would

occur differently (probabilistically speaking)” can, if X and Y are stochastic variables, naturally be
interpreted as: P(Y = y|X = x) P(Y = y), since an equality sign would mean that Y = y would
occur independently of the value of X.
46 Recall from the discussion of DEF-CF0’ and def-sn that the ceteris-paribus clause is defined as
stipulating that earlier events are to be kept fixed.

5.7 Conclusion

175

In sum, it seems that DEF-P is a natural interpretation of DEF-CF for probabilistic
systems; one could then define the probabilistic reading of DEF-CF to mean DEF-P.
I will leave these ideas here as avenues of future research. In order to further analyse
probabilistic causation and test DEF-P I introduce in Appendix 5.1 stochastic neuron
diagrams—perhaps they are an interesting tool for further research. The most pressing
open question is of course whether DEF-P already represents the MCT as it stands.
It is interesting to note that the unification achieved in this chapter is derived
by a methodology akin to the paradigmatic unification of theoretical physics. There
genuine progress is ascertained if a theory with wider applicability can be shown to
encompass other efficient models as special cases. That is essentially the outcome
of the present study; as in science, the wider model then has a greater problemsolving capacity than the local models. This logical-epistemological state of affairs
parallels the chronological steps in the research method used in this chapter. We
started with investigating the widest-scope model, the counterfactual one, and found
a partial solution applicable to neuron diagrams. However, this solution (def-sn and
def-mn) looked contrived and seemed to beg not so much for further analysis as
for further synthesis. This was done by looking at other contexts and accounts:
we found that the physical model DEF-F and the modified manipulationist model
DEF-M were crucially representation-relative, a feature hidden in neuron diagrams,
since, at first sight, ‘the representation is the diagram’. We could then return to the
counterfactual model with this knowledge. The first and simplest move, just adding
the representation clause to naiver counterfactual models, appeared to work.
Surely the newest idea in this chapter is the introduction of the notion of
representation-relativity or theory-relativity (or theory*-relativity if we re-introduce
the wider notion of theory* used in the other chapters of this book). As a postulate or rule, representation-relativity of causal identification and of causal claims
states that causes are necessarily relative to conceptual frameworks, perspectives,
theories*; or one might say: the identification/ascription of causes is representationdependent. (Even if causes are relative, causation is best seen as absolute, as ontological: see arguments above and below.) This postulate was used in the solution of
almost all problems, related to context-relativity (RR1), early (RR2) and late (RR4)
preemption, double prevention (RR3), overdetermination (RR5), tampering (RR6),
transitivity (RR6), token-versus-type causation (RR7), black-box cases (RR8) and
others. This is remarkable, if one considers the plethora of divergent solutions for
each of these problems proposed in the literature. I believe that this situation illustrates the need for a synthetic reflex in future philosophical practice: if philosophers
only propose analytic solutions to problems without a holistic approach, taking the
whole problem space into account, there seems no limit to the proliferation of ever
new problems.
As already stated, many philosophers believe that cause is an absolute, ‘invariant’
concept (Menzies & Beebee, 2020): that there is only one true cause (or one set
of true causes) for a given effect. I admit that this is a quite compelling intuition;
people normally use the word ‘cause’ in this sense, without specifying anything
more, say a theory. As argued in great detail in this chapter, I believe this intuition is
not really adequate from a precise philosophical perspective. But notice that such an

176

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

invariantist understanding can well be subsumed by my model: it corresponds to the
belief that causes are always, in all contexts, identified within the best available or
best possible theory/model/set of hypotheses. If this is (implicitly) understood, there
is no need to consider representation-relativity. Fair enough, but to solve problems,
one better make the implicit explicit.
Menzies and Beebee (2020 §4) state that “an advantage of invariantism is that
it allows us to conceive of causation as fully objective, mind-independent”. Not
only invariantism: as mentioned in the Introduction of this chapter, I conceive of
causation as objectively embedded in the (laws of) the universe and therefore mindindependent; I have to make this assumption to be in accordance with scientific
realism. The discussion under DEF-F might help sceptical readers to convince themselves that there are good arguments for this embeddedness-in-laws. As we saw in
Sect. 3, causes can directly be extracted from the laws; they are immanent in them. So
not causation is mind-dependent; but causal identification is representation-relative;
and one could say that causal identification, and the causes-according-to-T, approach
objective ‘truth’ to the extent that T is an adequate representation of reality. The better
T, the better the causal identification can be. In sum, if one looks at the small print,
representation-relativity characterises causal identification (and psychological and
logical aspects of causation), rather than causation as an ontological feature of the
universe.
These ideas connect immediately to the ‘reductive challenge’ discussed by Paul
and Hall, as already mentioned in the Introduction. Recall their statement (2013,
pp. 7–8): “We will also assume a broadly reductionist outlook, according to which
facts about which events cause which other events are fixed, somehow, by (i) the
facts about what happens, together with (ii) the facts about the fundamental laws that
govern what happens. […] We will assume that a successful philosophical analysis
of the causal relation ought to show exactly how facts about what happens, together
with the laws, fix the facts about event causation.”
This challenge is met by having shown that DEF-CF reduces to DEF-F in deterministic physical systems (Paul and Hall consider deterministic causation, cf. 2013,
p. 8). Indeed, DEF-F implies that causes can directly be extracted from the laws,
the fundamental equations (recall the discussion in Sect. 3, especially at the end).
Usually, in any case when dealing with event causation, one also needs particular
facts about what happens, the ‘initial’47 and/or ‘boundary’ conditions in technical
terms, in order to solve the equations and determine causal, i.e. functional, relations
with numerical precision. In sum, the equations, together with contingent temporal
relations, together with DEF-F enable one to determine cause-effect relations. Just
as Paul and Hall demand, “facts about what happens together with the laws fix the
facts about event causation”.
47 In some detail: a differential equation of order n for a property Y(X, t) considered as a function

of X and time t needs n initial values (at time t0 ) to be solvable, namely: Y(X, t0 ),…, Y(n−1) (X,
t0 ), where Y(n−1) is the (n-1)th time-derivative of Y. Thus, these initial conditions contain the
information about the time domains of the variables X, Y,…: this information is needed for causal
identification.

5.7 Conclusion

177

Therefore, as announced in Sect. 1, it indeed seems that building a precise definition goes hand in hand with uncovering the ontological/metaphysical grounds of
causal claims; that the ‘conceptual analysis’ goes hand in hand with the ‘ontological
reduction’; that the ‘descriptive project’ goes hand in hand with the ‘metaphysical
project’ (Frisch, 2023). If one prefers: the latter is just in reach of the former. Now,
to many, it is of course not a surprise that a solid definition immediately connects
with what goes on in reality, with the deeper ontology! (Think also of the chapters
on probability and free will.) I hope that those who are in doubt will find in this
chapter a counterargument to their doubts. At the same time, a full discussion of
this parallelism is not in the scope of this chapter, but we will come back to it in
Chapter 9, dedicated to lines of future research.
I believe that the above ideas and results inevitably imply that we should have
a new, more open-minded look at the old principle of causation/causality—let us
interpret it here as stating that everything happens according to laws of cause and
effect. Clearly, by showing that a unified notion of cause is a very real possibility,
we have resolved one crucial objection to the principle (namely, that it cannot be
consistently defined). Next, if everything happens according to the laws of nature
(the laws of physics), and if, as DEF-F ensures us, the laws of nature inherently
embody cause-effect relations, then it immediately follows that everything happens
according to laws of cause and effect. Ergo, the principle of causation would be
true, after all! (More in Chapter 9 §1.) Clearly, one should include in this argument
stochastic laws; but here DEF-P ensures us that they too, via the correlations they
embed, are causal laws.
Indeed, if the ontology of causation and the causal principle are now in reach,
we better start with distinguishing a probabilistic and a deterministic principle of
causation. But these seem to work largely analogously, just as deterministic and
probabilistic cause seem so prone to unification. To lift one tip of the ontological
veil, and looking first at deterministic systems, DEF-CF immediately points to the
ontological necessity of (deterministic) causation, the necessity that is usually considered an ingredient of the principle of (deterministic) causation. Indeed, according
to DEF-CF, a cause is necessary for its effects in the precise sense that without the
cause, the effect would not happen, ceteris paribus, i.e. in the situation considered.
This is so at least according to some representation or theory T; and if T is the best
theory we have, we might as well conclude that the cause (or the corresponding
event/property) is ontologically necessary. One could also say: causes produce their
effects, in a sense that seems slightly more metaphorical. In probabilistic systems,
and recalling the chapter on probability, it is collections of objects, probabilistic
ensembles, ensembles of ρ-systems that necessarily comply with the necessary laws
of probability theory. (The natural next thought is that this necessity is induced by
underlying deterministic laws governing the individual systems. But let us not digress
too far from the topic of this chapter.) Concomitantly, my conjectured generalised
DEF-CF implies, for probabilistic systems, that a cause is necessary for its effects in
the sense that the probability distribution of the effects would be different if that of
the cause would be different, ceteris paribus. In this precise sense, there is ontological
necessity in probabilistic causation too.

178

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

Fig. 5.8 The neuron
diagram believed to
represent real cases of early
preemption

But I will leave it to future work to delve deeper into the perspective that DEF-CF
and scientific realism open to the ontology of causation. First ideas can be found in
Chapter 9 §1. Let us end by recalling that the identification of causality and lawfulness
has, of course, a very long tradition. Specifically, the causal principle stating “all
events occur by laws of cause and effect (because the laws of nature can be construed
as laws of cause and effect)”, which follows from our synthetic analysis, has been
defended in different guises since antiquity, by the amazing atomists Democritus and
Leucippus among others, and by several authors since, Helmholtz, Mach (perhaps)
to name only them48 (for a condensed history, cf. Bunge (2009), pp. 252–253).

Appendix 5.1. Stochastic Neuron Diagrams and Test
of DEF-P
In this Appendix I propose a probabilistic interpretation of neuron diagrams. I will
illustrate the procedure using the diagram of Fig. 5.8.
If one interprets this diagram (diagram 1 in Table 5.1) in a probabilistic manner,
one can test DEF-P for probabilistic systems, and at the same time corroborate its
coherence with DEF-CF. In stochastic neuron diagrams symbols have a different
meaning: A, B, C, … are now variables that can assume different values each with a
given probability. The simplest case corresponds to all variables being dichotomic; it
is this case that can directly be compared to the original neuron diagrams as defined
in Sect. 2. For instance, C = 1 corresponds to the C-neuron being on, and C = 0 to
being off, idem for the other neuron-variables. In the probabilistic interpretation one
attributes a probability to each value, e.g. P(C = 1) = c1 , P(A = 1) = a1 , etc. (Then
P(C = 0) = 1 – c1 , etc.) The deterministic case can be modelled as a special case of
48 Helmholtz wrote (around 1881): “The principle of causality is nothing but the assumption of the

lawfulness of all natural phenomena” (translated by Bunge (2009), p. 252). Mach put it this way
(around 1883): “The business of physical science is the reconstruction of facts in thought, or the
abstract quantitative expression of facts. The rules which we form for these reconstructions are the
laws of nature. In the conviction that such rules are possible lies the law of causality. The law of
causality simply asserts that the phenomena of nature are dependent on one another” (quoted in
Bunge (2009), pp. 252–253). As Bunge remarks, from this passage one could infer that Mach does
not consider laws as being immanent in nature but as rules of procedure, which is at odds with the
position we defend.

Appendix 5.1. Stochastic Neuron Diagrams and Test of DEF-P

179

a general probabilistic scenario, by taking all probability values equal to 0 or 1. For
instance, in Fig. 5.8 P(E = 1) = 1 if the diagram is interpreted deterministically and if
P(C = 1) = P(A = 1) = 1. (One can imagine this as the outcome of measurements on
a series of identical diagrams, where C, A, D, and E are always on.) As we have seen in
Chapter 4, probabilistic modelling is only appropriate if the real situation represented
by diagram 1 can be repeated or occurs sufficiently many times in sufficiently similar
conditions. Therefore, strictly speaking, the probabilistic diagram 1 is not really a
good description of, for instance, the Assassin-Backup case, which is not really a
probabilistic scenario, but a deterministic one-time murder case. We do not have
statistical data about such a gloomy scenario; but let us imagine we have.49 (At any
rate, I believe the best interpretation of the Assassin-Backup case is provided by
DEF-CF as applied to the deterministic scenario discussed in the above sections.
Here I only wish to illustrate DEF-P, which I conjecture to be the privileged account
for genuinely probabilistic situations.)
This being said, it seems we can build a probabilistic model TP that avoids some
of the problems we encountered with TN , the usual interpretation of diagram 1.
We saw that TN is not appropriate when applied to many realistic scenarios—say
the Assassin-Backup case. According to the usual interpretation (TN ), diagram 1
stipulates that neuron B always fires if C is off and A on. But as we argued in
Chapter 5, Backup’s decision B, which he makes as a free being after a message from
Assassin, might change (for a variety of reasons) even if he had initially decided to
use the poison (A = 1). So let us associate a probability to Backup’s decision B after
the message: P(B = 1|C = 0, A = 1) = β, which may differ from 1, its value in
the original interpretation TN . This is the key parameter of the model: it represents
Backup’s ‘reliability’, i.e. his readiness-to-poison after Assassin’s message that she
cannot do the job, and after his initial agreement. To keep the calculations simple,
let’s only attribute probabilities to A, C and B; D and E react deterministically as
in the original TN . The probabilities P(B|C, A) are chosen so as to remain close to
the original deterministic interpretation. For instance: P(B = 1|C = 1, A = 1) = 0
expresses that Backup never acts if C acts, just as in TN .
In detail, the model TP is defined by following assumptions:
(A1) P(C = 1) = c1, hence P(C = 0) = 1 – c1 ;
(A2) P(A = 1) = a1, hence P(A = 0) = 1 – a1 ;
(A3) P(B = 1|C = 1, A = 1) = 0;
(A4) P(B = 1|C = 0, A = 1) = β;
(A5) P(B = 1|C = 1, A = 0) = 0;
(A6) P(B = 1|C = 0, A = 0) = ε, assumed to be a very small number, normally
0
From (A3)–(A6) follows that we also know the complements, e.g. from (A4):
P(B = 0|C = 0, A = 1) = 1 − β.
49 It seems one can justify the probabilistic interpretation by assuming that there is some theoretical

even if unknown probability that C is active, that B acts as promised, etc.

180

5 A Unified Definition of Cause

We now wish to answer the question: what is/are, according to DEF-P, the cause(s)
(at t1) of E = 1 in model TP ? Recall DEF-P: “X is a cause of Y iff (i) P(Y |X )
P(Y ), and (ii) Z (earlier) : P(Y |X , Z) =P(Y |Z) ”. Since we are interested in the
causes at t1, clause (ii) is not needed, or, rather, vacuously satisfied. Simply applying
the definition, one finds that (the variable) C is a cause of (the variable) E iff P(E|C)
P(E) for some values of E and C ∈{0, 1}. From the rules of probability theory
follows that the test-condition P(E|C) = P(E) is equivalent to:
(A7)

{A} P(E|C, A) P(A)

=

{C,A} P(E|C, A) P(C)P(A)

{C,A} P(E|C, A) P(C, A)

=

Here the sum {A} · runs over A = 0 and A = 1, and {C,A} · stands for the sum
over the four possibilities {C = i; A = j, with i,j ∈{0, 1}}. To verify (A7), we
need P(E|C, A) for all values of the variables, for instance P(E = 1|C, A) , where
P(E = 1|C, A) stands for any of the four probabilities P(E = 1|C = i, A = j) with
i,j ∈{0, 1}. This can be calculated as follows, using the fact that E = 1 can only occur
if C = 1 (and hence D = 1) or if B = 1:
(A8) P(E = 1|C, A) = P(C = 1 or B = 1|C, A)
= P(C = 1|C, A) + P(B = 1|C, A) – P(C = 1, B = 1|C , A)
= P(C = 1|C, A) + P(B = 1|C, A) – P(B = 1|C, A) P(C = 1|B = 1, C , A)
= P(C = 1|C, A) + P(B = 1|C, A) − P(B = 1|C, A) P(C = 1|C )
With (A1–A8) and the other inputs of the model one finds, taking ε = 0 for simplicity:
(A9) P(E = 1|C = 1) = 1
(A10) P(E = 1|C = 0) = a1 β
(A11) P(E = 1)= c1 + (1-c1 ) a1 β
These results can be interpreted (and can also be calculated without using the fullblown formula (A7)). (A9) is obvious: if C is on E is by assumption always on. (A10)
gives the probability that E fires given that C is off, which is equal to the probability
that A fires (a1 ) times the probability that, in these conditions, B fires (β). (A11)
gives the probability that E = 1 as a sum of upper (via D) and lower (via B) path
contributions, which are mutually exclusive.
Thus, in the probabilistic model TP we come, using DEF-P, to the realistic conclusion that in general P(E|C) P(E), i.e., C is a separate cause of E, unless c1 = a1
= β = 1. Under the latter ‘exceptional’ deterministic condition (A and B deterministically always on and B deterministically always poisoning), which corresponds
to the original diagram under TN , C is not a separate cause; then {A, C} are joint
causes, as one can also calculate using DEF-P.50 This verdict is in agreement with
DEF-CF applied to the original diagram under the deterministic interpretation TN ,
which led to {A+, C+} being joint causes (cf. Sect. 6, RR2). The original diagram
TN corresponds to TP with c1 = a1 = β = 1. So in this case DEF-P leads to the
correct result obtained with DEF-CF: C and A are not separate but joint causes.
50 One calculates that P(E = 1|C = 0, A = 0) = 0

P(E = 1).

181

We interpreted this in Chapter 5 in legal terms: Assassin and Backup mechanically
sealed their fate, so that both are guilty.
In a similar manner one calculates that in the probabilistic scenario A (Backup)
is a cause of E unless β = 0 or c1 = 1 or a1 = 0, which is again coherent. For
instance, if C always fires, E always fires independently of A; and the reliability β
= 0 means that A (Backup) never puts the poison. In the case that c1 = 1 and a1
and β some value between 0 and 1, say 0.5, DEF-P leads to the natural conclusion
that only C (Assassin) is a separate cause. This is also what DEF-CF gave under the
‘commonsense’ interpretation TC of the deterministic diagram (recall Sect. 6). Thus
TP seems a more natural model than TN ; TP appears to better capture typical human
reflexes of conspiring accomplices.
This can also be seen by using the measure for the causal influence of X on Y
proposed in Sect. 5:
|P(Y |X ) − P (Y )|.

Inf(X → Y) =
Y

X

One finds for instance that:
Inf(C → E) = 2 c1 (1 − β a1 ).
This seems again coherent. For instance, the causal influence of C on E is the
higher, the higher c1 is and the lower the chance is that B fires.
However, the above claims need to be consolidated by further research, notably by
testing DEF-P on more realistic stochastic models, and by comparing it to what other
theories (Bayesian accounts, causal modelling,…) predict. The goal of this appendix
is solely to illustrate DEF-P, and to suggest that further work on stochastic neuron
diagrams might be interesting for testing DEF-P as the final solution to probabilistic
causation.

Chapter 6

On Absolute Free Will

Abstract In this chapter I investigate the question of whether absolute free will
exists: the human capacity to make choices independently of external influences.
To answer this question, one needs to take physics and philosophy of physics into
account. Within this framework, it follows from rather straightforward arguments that
absolute free will cannot exist. I then prepare the next chapter where I defend the idea
that from a socio-humanitarian perspective ‘relative’ free will can exist, a capacity
necessary for moral responsibility. This notion is compatible with neuroscience
but makes abstraction of physics. These two chapters constitute my bi-perspectival
account of free will.

6.1 Introduction
The question whether humans have free will is an all-time classic of philosophy; a
staggering number of famous minds from virtually all philosophical and scientific
disciplines have expressed their opinion about it. No doubt that most of us feel
free often, or most of the time; and few people doubt that we have some freedom,
some capacity to choose between different actions—say between lifting now my left
hand, rather than my right one. And yet a great number of philosophers, physicists,
neurobiologists, psychologists etc. have dared to question this belief in free will—
even if this belief seems a key precondition not only of our legal system and our
habits of blame and praise, but perhaps also of the very dignity of human life. To
my taste, there is perhaps no philosophical topic with such profound philosophical
ramifications and potential implications—on the theoretical level, but ultimately
also for (a practical philosophy of) society—, and yet so little agreement among
the experts. Therefore, discussing free will in only two chapters of a book seems, a
priori, a risky enterprise. I better, then, specify what the precise goal of these chapters
is. They aim, in the first place, as most of the preceding chapters, at conceptual
clarification using the same method of synthetic problem solving as used throughout
this book. (This applies especially to the next chapter, in which I will define the more
complex notion of relative free will.) Thus, I will try to synthesise definitions using

L. Vervoort, Problem Solving in Philosophy,
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-17756-8_6

186

6 On Absolute Free Will

expertise from (analytic) philosophy starting from Aristotle, and to construct a model
that can hopefully solve more problems and answer more questions than competing
models with a comparable scope. I do not claim to have studied all competing models/
theories, and I obviously do not pretend to provide a complete theory of free will
answering all questions from all disciplines related to it. My model can be completed
along many axes, notably a neuroscientific one. But at any rate, these two chapters
will be an illustration of the general method of philosophising recommended in this
book.
The problem of free will does not only have a definitional component, but, just
as those of probability and cause, also an ontological one. The most pressing and
interesting question is, of course: Does free will exist? Do we have free will?—
at least sometimes. For such a tremendously contentious problem as free will, it
should be no surprise that my answer is: that depends on how one defines free will.
Of course, we want the strongest possible definition, at least for starters. Now, we
should have learned from the chapter on Gettier’s problem that a proposition as ‘free
will exists’ depends on the conceptual framework, the representation, the theory T*
one considers. Definitions take their meaning within conceptual frameworks—the
Quinean idea we derived in Chapter 3. Similarly, the expression: “I believe to do an
act of free will” depends on how one defines the subject ‘I’, within which perspective.
So here is a key starting idea of this chapter, one that strongly resonates with the
other chapters of this book: there are different perspectives, theories, regarding the
notions of free will and the ‘I’ that feels free. (This idea is akin to one defended by List
(2019), even if we infer different conclusions of it, cf. next chapter.) Now, not only
are there different perspectives, I claim that to have a full view on free will one needs
to use at least two conceptual frameworks: the perspective based on physics and its
philosophy, and the perspective from social theories (which are closer to common
sense, and ideally include or are at least compatible with neuroscience). I will argue
later why also the second perspective is needed. Thus, I will develop a bi-perspectival
account of free will, one that rests on the assumption that a full philosophical theory
contains two perspectives that are complementary (and that seem, a priori, even to
lead to contradictory conclusions).
My ‘complementarity of perspectives’ is a notion inspired by the famous complementarity introduced by Niels Bohr in the philosophy of quantum physics (Bohr,
1948). Since Bohr’s philosophical ideas are debated (and often considered vague),
I will attempt my own interpretation of his complementarity. Bohr advanced the
powerful idea that (values of) properties of quantum systems are inextricably related
to the measurement context; different experiments reveal different properties; and
these experiments are complementary in that they cannot be done simultaneously1
on a given system but yet jointly describe the system fully. (Recall how I interpreted
these claims within my frequency model of probability, cf. Chapter 4, R4 in Sect. 5).
It seems that Bohr thought of complementarity in a broader sense: not only in physics
but in many other disciplines complementary concepts and perspectives are needed
1 The fact that certain measurements cannot be done simultaneously is formally described by the

commutation relations of quantum mechanics (cf. R4 in Chapter 4).

6.2 Does Absolute Free Will Exist?

187

for a full description of a phenomenon. This is Bohr’s conception of complementarity
as I understand it; let me specify mine in a bit more detail.
To answer existence questions, ideally, one considers the best possible, or rather
the strongest available theory; and an (accepted) concept defined within, or compatible2 with, such a theory. Regarding the free will problem, one can ask the ontological
question “does free will exist?” within T1 = natural science (the collection of ‘best’
neurobiological, physical, etc. theories) and its philosophy. But one can also ask
it within a global ‘best’ theory that describes society, the human psyche, legal and
moral practices, etc.—call this collection of theories (T2) a complete social theory of
the human, or a socio-humanitarian theory, for lack of a better name. It is understood
here that such a theory makes abstraction of physics (or at least of what physics tells
us about free will); common sense is also such a perspective that is usually unencumbered by advanced physics and its philosophy. I believe that both perspectives
T1 and T2 are complementary and needed in the free will debate.
The reader should not be distracted by the above lines: in fine they just say that
‘free will’ can have different meanings; let us look at them, and then decide in which
sense free will can exist. I will start in this chapter with the notion of free will as
illuminated by natural science including physics. In the next chapter I will mainly
look at free will from a socio-humanitarian point of view.

6.2 Does Absolute Free Will Exist?
Both within natural science, socio-humanitarian theories, and common sense, one
might try to define free will along the following line—as is often done in all these
quarters:
Free will is the capacity to choose between different possible actions undetermined
by past external events (or at least partially undetermined by such events).
I will call this the ‘absolute’ definition of free will. Such a conception posits the
existence of spontaneous, self-generated actions, or better of self-generated decisions
leading to actions, actions/decisions that are not (fully) determined by past events
external to the mind of the free-willed subject. Let us now see whether this definition
makes sense from a scientific point of view, in other words, whether such an absolute
free will can exist. I will give two arguments, first a slightly elaborate one, then a
much simpler one. (The broad line of these arguments is well known; I can only
hope to provide some extra clarity.) Science is the perspective we should privilege
according to synthetic philosophy, if it can be used at all; its verdict, if any, should
be closest to truth.

2 As an example: the notion of cause is not really defined within physics proper; one does not really

need it in the theory; yet one can construct, within the philosophy of physics, a definition that is
compatible with physics: see our chapter on cause.

188

6 On Absolute Free Will

According to modern neuroscience, actions and decisions originate in the brain,
and these decisions are ‘materialised’ by neuron circuits, which are biological and
hence ultimately physical systems. Physical systems are described by properties
(variables, see e.g. the chapter on causation). Then, let us assume that the above definition makes sense, and consider a subject S who can (sometimes) choose between
decisions d1, d2, d3,… leading, respectively, to actions a1, a2, a3…. Since the
common-sense definition speaks of undetermined decisions, let us, to verify the
soundness of the definition, inquire what could determine, a priori, these possible
decisions or choices. Such determinants can generally be classified in two groups:
on the one hand there might be external influences (present or past), denoted {e1, e2,
e3, …}, such as external stimuli, encounters with other people, past education, etc.
All these can influence decisions. On the other hand, the definition is a priori compatible with the existence of internal, self-generated influences (past or present)—here
one might think of spontaneous thoughts or perhaps internal stimuli, let us denote
them as {s1, s2, s3, …}. Such internal, self-generated influences are supposed to be
undetermined by past or present events that took place outside the subject S.
Since the ultimate theory is supposed to be a physical theory (whether accessible to
humans or not), I may consider the physical properties/variables that are the physical
pendants of the {di}, {ei} and {si}. For instance, expressed as physical variables,
each of the di may represent the electrical activity of the neuronal circuit(s) that is
(are) associated with decision i (whether in the following the variables are singletons
or n-tuples plays no role: the formalism below is exactly the same in both cases3 ).
Similarly, in a physical description {s1, s2, s3, …} are variables that describe selfgenerated internal brain processes/events (materialised again in neuronal circuits),
and {e1, e2, e3, …} represent past or present external influences.
Within physics, to find out what determines the possible decisions {d1, d2, d3,
…}, and therefore the possible actions {a1, a2, a3,…}, I may consider the following
probability:
P(d 1, d 2, . . . .|s1, s2, . . . , e1, e2, . . . .) ,
or, if one prefers, each of the P(di|s1, s2, . . . , e1, e2, . . . .) ∀ i, which is the probability that a certain decision/action di/ai is chosen by S given the determining
factors {s1, s2, …, e1, e2, …}. (Note that one does not need to make a distinction between the di-variables and their corresponding ai-variables: for a judicious
choice of variables one can assume that P(a1, a2, . . . .|s1, s2, . . . , e1, e2, . . . .) =
P(d 1, d 2, . . . .|s1, s2, . . . , e1, e2, . . . .) .) Note also that a probabilistic description
is general: it encompasses both probabilistic and deterministic variables; the deterministic case is a special variant of the probabilistic case (cf. the chapter on probability). A deterministic description would be appropriate if the considered decisionmaking of S is deterministic—either because the physical universe would ultimately
be deterministic, or because S is very determined to choose only one of the di/ai.
3 This is so because in probability theory the expression P(X |Y ) can be defined when X and Y are

single variables or sets/n-tuples.

6.2 Does Absolute Free Will Exist?

189

Now, the absolute definition assumes that a free-willed subject S can, at least
sometimes, choose between possible decisions/actions {d1, d2, d3, …}, and that
these are not only determined by past external influences {e1, e2, e3, …}, but also by
self-generated {s1, s2, s3,…}—at least one of such. In probabilistic language, this
exactly means that the probability of choosing {d1, d2, d3, …} depends on the {s1,
s2, s3, …}, i.e.
P(d 1, d 2, . . . .|s1, s2, . . . , e1, e2, . . . .)

P(d 1, d 2, . . . .|e1, e2, . . . .) .

(6.1)

Let us simplify notation: our absolute definition, assuming it corresponds to something real, implies that there are decisions/actions d such that they are determined by
one or more self-generated influences s, so that e does not screen off s:
P(d |s, e)

P(d |e) .

(6.2)

Inequality (6.1) and the equivalent (6.2) immediately follow from the definition
of probabilistic dependence (see preceding chapters). But the conclusion is now
immediate: according to physics, there are no self-generated events/properties, there
are no events that would be fully determined within S. No physical events pop out
of nothing: one of the most widely accepted, and massively corroborated, principles
of physics, mentioned already by the epicurean atomist Lucretius in his De Rerum
Natura (1st C. BC). In sum, there are no influences/events/variables ‘s’ and decisions
‘d’ that make (6.1) or (6.2) true; hence our initial assumption that absolute free will
exists cannot be true.
[One might contest: surely there are internal spontaneous thoughts that have only
internal determinants, say prior percepts, feelings, thoughts, …. But, granted this
were a legitimate description, also such internal prior events cannot be undetermined;
they in turn cannot pop out of nothing. (This is particularly clear if one recalls that
mental events are, physically speaking, electrochemical brain processes, changes in
currents: these must come from somewhere.) That means that there must be (assuming
for a moment that internal spontaneous influences s exist) an (internal) variable s1
such that P(s s1 ) = P(s). And this implies, mathematically, that also P(d s1 , e)
P(d |e) , which is an instance of (6.2), and which follows from the assumption that
absolute free will exists and that d is an absolutely free decision. According to a
well-known recursive argument, this reasoning can be applied to earlier stages of
determination involving variables s2 , s3 , …, sN each occurring at earlier times and
determining their predecessors, so that one ultimately arrives at P(d sN , e) P(d |e) ,
where sN are events that occurred before S’s birth. Ergo, our starting assumption
leads to the contradicting conclusion that any decision must be determined by only
external events, occurring certainly outside of S’s mind. These (zillions of) external
events are, physically and mathematically speaking, the ultimate determinants of any
choice—even if we can of course not retrace them.]
Some might object: but there could be quantum events at the origin of a decision—
these are random, uncaused, according to the standard interpretation of quantum
mechanics. Even if this were the case, such quantum events and the systems in

190

6 On Absolute Free Will

which they occur—say a collection of electrons—also come from somewhere. They
depend on, i.e. they are correlated with, many variables: their environment (the wave
function that describes them is determined by the environment, recall e.g. Bohr’s
position above). For instance, they depend on macroscopic variables describing the
neurons and other matter with which they interact, and hence with the past state of
these systems. Again one arrives, by a similar reasoning as above, at the conclusion
that even quantum events in the brain are ultimately depending on external events.
(Moreover, quantum events are random, and real free choices are, in the usual sense,
voluntary and conscious, and therefore certainly not random. This is a further argument showing that quantum randomness cannot be at the origin of free will, against
what is often thought.)
In conclusion, from a physical point of view, there are no self-generated choices,
choices that have no origin outside of a subject’s mind. Decisions and choices are
always necessarily determined only by events that ultimately lie outside S’s mind.
That is what neurobiology and especially physics tell us. The conclusion is clear: the
absolute view of free will—which is also the common-sense view—cannot be sound.
This is an unfathomable idea—a dangerous one according to many! And yet I
believe it is among the most important conclusions the mind can come to, even
if reason seems to stand here in blatant opposition to common sense, to what we
feel, and to the general regimentation of modern societies. It is a paradigm case of
an idea that needs prolonged, careful professional efforts to be understood; and of
which the consequences could have, if understood and applied by society, enormous
impact. There are philosophers who have taken up the grand challenge to reflect on
the consequences, and have shown the direction in which to go. The great Spinoza
is the first who comes to my mind. In his Ethics he developed a systematic theory,
based on the absolute view, which coherently answers countless questions from so
many philosophical chapters—a framework that has therefore a greater chance of
correctness than accounts of smaller scope. But several other philosophers, all free
will sceptics, deserve to be mentioned (a partial list of contemporary philosophers
is Strawson, 1986; Pereboom, 1997; Ch. 18, 2014; Smilansky, 2000; Waller, 2011;
Caruso, 2012; Vilhauer, 2012). I will briefly comment on the implications of the
absolute perspective in the next chapter.
Of course, the above argument could have been much shorter (we could simply
have noted from the start that self-generation is a physical heresy), but I found it
useful to spell it out in some detail. Note that the only non-trivial assumptions that
I made are (1) scientific realism, in particular physicalism/physical reductionism,
the idea that ultimate reality is physical and can be described as usual by variables,
and (2) the ‘nihil ex nihilo’ principle. (Remark: of course, no-one can seriously
believe that humans can construct a physical theory ‘of literally everything’ that
would allow us to predict, on the basis of the properties of the ultimate constituents
of the universe, all the thoughts and decisions that go on in a given human’s brain.
And yet, all these thoughts, however ethereal, are embedded in physical systems and
these material pendants of our thoughts are therefore ultimately, in principle (not in
practice!), describable by physical theory, by the tools of physics. The latter very
widespread hypothesis, which I have to assume to remain within the best available

6.2 Does Absolute Free Will Exist?

191

scientific knowledge and therefore the MCT, is all I need, besides the ‘nihil ex nihilo’
principle.)
Actually, there is a very short argument, which should in fine be equivalent to
the above one. It goes as follows. According to the scientific realism I must adopt,
any action starts from, is controlled by, neurobiological processes in the brain, hence
ultimately by physical processes (even if we cannot reduce all of neurobiology to
physics in practice). Humans are ultimately physical systems, and physical systems
are governed by the laws of physics, originating at the Big Bang. However counterintuitive and unpoetic it may sound to many, humans are therefore bound by the
necessary laws of physics. It plays no role whether these laws are deterministic or
probabilistic: via these laws, whether deterministic or probabilistic, we are ‘correlated’4 with the very far past, we entirely depend on it, in every respect. Still in other
words, our every feeling, thought and choice is generated by physical events that lie
in the far past. As regards choice-making, on the purely physical level, there is strictly
no difference between humans, falling stones, and quantum objects as electrons: we
follow a path generated by the past; just as stones and electrons do not self-generate
their histories, we do not—because we are as physical as they are. Ergo, from the
physics point of view, the concept of self-generated and undetermined, absolutely
free choices and actions, the concept of free will that seems such a cornerstone of
humanity, must be an illusion. With some drama, one might say: it is the universe
that chooses for us.
We have obtained an argument against absolute free will that reminds us, of course,
of arguments proposed by many other philosophers. In the contemporary literature,
one often encounters a version that is called the ‘Consequence Argument’, due to
van Inwagen (1983). However, van Inwagen’s argument rejects free will on the basis
of determinism—as do almost all free will sceptics. My above argument substantially generalises, so strengthens, the Consequence Argument, since it only assumes
physicalism/physical reductionism, which encompasses deterministic and indeterministic systems. My stronger consequence argument is thus the following, simply
replacing determinism by physicalism in van Inwagen’s rationale: “If physicalism
[the hypothesis that the building blocks of everything in the universe are physical]
is true, then our acts are the consequences of the laws of nature and events in the
remote past. But it is not up to us what went on before we were born, and neither is
it up to us what the laws of nature are. Therefore, the consequences of these things
(including our present acts) are not up to us.” My argument locates the origin of
the tension with free will in the fact that our acts originate in, are generated by, the
physical world outside and anterior to us; it denies that we are the ultimate source of
our actions.
Interestingly, this dramatic conclusion based on physics is very similar to the
conclusion that neuroscientists have derived from various detailed neuroscientific
findings (cf. e.g. Walter, 2001; Soon et al., 2008 and see next chapter). This should not
4 Note that statistical correlations may well exist in a given system, even if they are entirely unde-

tectable. Measuring/detecting correlations is subject to measurement uncertainties, limitations of
experimentation. For an illustration in billiards, see (Nikolaev and Vervoort 2023).

192

6 On Absolute Free Will

surprise us if there is coherency between natural sciences, all of which philosophers
and scientists believe to be ultimately reducible to physics. (For the same reason, the
physics-based argument is stronger.) But it is pleasing to contemplate that the web
of knowledge is coherent.

6.3 Does a Relative Form of Free Will Exist? Where Does
the Illusion of Free Will Come From?
This is all I will say here about the physics-based perspective, within which absolute
free will does not exist. But it seems—and now we move in less certain territory—that
there exists a type of free will from the perspective of socio-humanitarian theories, if
one makes abstraction of physics—resonating with what common sense would tell.
I will in this Section go over some grounds, notably from neuroscience, that seem to
explain why such a relative free will could exist (a notion to be defined precisely in
the next chapter), and where (the illusion of) free will comes from. I will do this in
a very sketchy way; these reasons are surely known by philosophers and scientists,
especially those who reject the reality of absolute free will; and they can be discussed
in much greater detail in the light of neuroscience (see e.g. List, 2019; Walter, 2001
and references therein). I mention them here to justify the work of the next chapter,
which is to define relative or ‘socio-humanitarian’ free will.
From the common-sense and felt perspective, the conclusion that absolute free
will is impossible is of course entirely counterintuitive, nay unbelievable—to the
point that, when one defends this idea in the presence of staunch believers in free
will, one may find oneself sound quixotic. It seems that in my mind/head/brain, I can
freely envisage so many options, I could take so many different decisions, I can guide
my life and have influence on the environment and other people. I can write my life
story, as the saying goes. Sure, but all that is not in contradiction with being subject
to the laws of physics; which must still be at the basis of all such capacities. All
such capacities and the way we perceive them must be emergent from the uniquely
complex system that a brain is—so complex that we only have a vague idea of what
is going on in it. Analogously, the famous nematode Caenorhabditis elegans, having
about 300 neurons, and especially somewhat more developed worms, seem to guide
their movements and seem to have choices in going left or right; and from a certain
point of view this is the case; and yet it is a scientific triviality to state that they
are ‘only’ physical systems subject to the laws of physics. The impression I have
that ‘I’ can choose, depends on what one considers (and feels) the ‘I’ to be. More
precisely, the proposition “The subject S can sometimes choose freely” depends on
the theories with which one looks at these concepts, including that of subject. In
the physical sense, the ‘I’ is a collection of atoms of which the temporal and spatial
boundaries are defined by human convention, and for which the concept of free will
makes little sense, as argued above. But from the perspective of what I know, grasp,
feel about my ‘I’, with my very limited knowledge about what really goes on, ‘I’ can

6.3 Does a Relative Form of Free Will Exist? Where Does the Illusion …

193

spontaneously do a whole lot. At first sight, it is beyond doubt that ‘I’ can choose
and that it is ‘I’ who chooses—not the universe! Because I necessarily have a very
limited view of what the ‘I’ really is; and yet, this limited view is the only one that
counts—almost always, except when philosophising.5 So yes, the ‘I’ is (part of) a
well-identified physical body—having a national ID—, with a very personal brain,
with a sensorimotor system that can make this body act, and with neural networks in
the prefrontal cortex that embed the personal beliefs, the personal goals, the personal
memories etc. that are activated when I must act—actions that I therefore perceive as
my very personal actions. (But again, this is not in contradiction, rather to the contrary,
with the fact that these brain mechanisms must obey the laws of the universe. If they
did not, they would be erratic.)
Similarly, some actions demand deliberation, when different possibilities seem
to offer themselves to me. In my head, I can represent a given real-world situation
and different choices I can make in this situation, by using models, ensembles of
hypotheses, theories that apply to these choices and the situation: I can play around
with hypothetical consequences of these choices (hypothetical consequences that
are, on the abstract logical-epistemological level, inferences from the models I use
to represent the situation and the choices), and evaluate these consequences, leading
to a final choice and action. (It is this key idea that will be at the basis of my definition
of relative free will, cf. next chapter.) Surely, to all of us this deliberative thinking
has a strikingly ethereal and free character, I seem to be able to steer it in all kinds
of directions: surely because it seems to only involve my ‘mind’, not my body,
nothing physical—I can do it without moving, just sitting in the armchair. But this
disembodied-ness can only be an impression, an illusion; which might well be at the
origin of the ineradicable belief in free will. As said, the intricate links between, on
the one hand, ‘mental’ processes we call ‘deliberating’, ‘choosing’ etc. and, on the
other, the underlying neurobiological mechanisms only begin to be explored in the
fascinating and relatively new science of the brain—neuroscience.
What is also useful to remember is that the emergence of life from inert matter is
still a mystery; no surprise then that the emergence of consciousness and free will
from inert matter are even a greater mystery. Over millions of years of evolution,
neuron cells have learned, through natural selection, to remember and to store what
we call ‘knowledge’, ‘beliefs’, ‘reasons’, ‘preferences’ etc. in sophisticated neural
circuits; and brains have learned to activate these circuits when their owners are
confronted with what they perceive as possible future pathways. The faculty of
thinking can be seen as an emergent property of a biological supersystem, acquired
via natural selection, and ultimately based on the laws of physics. All this is not new.
All this being said, there is of course a bona-fide sense in which subject S
makes choices based on her neural circuits embedding her beliefs, preferences etc.
Concomitantly, there is a sense in which the subject acts upon and hence influences
the external world—even if part of the larger whole. (Even in a strictly physical
sense it is of course legitimate to consider that it is subject S that interacts in this and
5 And when using philosophical insights to build ethical hypotheses, and hypotheses for a better,

more just society (cf. the last section of the next chapter).

194

6 On Absolute Free Will

that way with the environment and produces changes in it.) It seems therefore that
concepts as agency make sense and correspond to something real. Therefore, I believe
that it makes sense to define a capacity that one could still call free will (so that we all
know what we are talking about) and that corresponds to this all-pervasive impression, this unshakeable belief we have to be free-willed. This impression emerges
from something real, after all, since people do seem to denote roughly the same
thing with the words ‘free will’. As I announced at the beginning of the chapter, such
a definition, then, is the definition within a general socio-humanitarian perspective
and informed common sense, which make abstraction of physics and its philosophy.
I believe it is useful to define such a relative free will, because it is such a vital ingredient of legal practice, of daily habits of blame, praise, punishment, and perhaps even
of (the philosophy of) happiness. Indeed, this concept does a lot of work, it solves
many problems: it will appear to be intimately linked to other key concepts, notably
consciousness, control, agency, responsibility.
However, I will immediately add that a full theory of free will should include
both perspectives: the absolute and the relative view are complementary and need,
ideally, both be used for handling the deepest problems. These deepest problems are
ontological and have an ethical dimension, so go far beyond definitions, and cannot
be treated in any serious detail in these chapters. Some examples are: How to take
the absence of absolute free will into account in our ethical, legal etc. philosophies
and practices? (I believe that society needs a great change in mindset here.) To what
extent are people praiseworthy and blameworthy? Do people need to be punished
(since in an absolute sense they had to do what they did)? What is the sense of
personal responsibility? etc. I will briefly comment on these problems at the end of
the next chapter and in the Epilogue.
