Chapter 7

On Relative Free Will. The CMT Model

Abstract It makes sense to study what ‘free will’ means in daily conversations,
law, and practical philosophy—even if it is a shaky concept. Thus, to complete my
bi-perspectival model of free will, I propose a compatibilist account of relative free
will that attempts: (1) to provide a synthesis of a variety of well-known models,
capable of addressing problems of the latter; (2) to account for the fact that free
will comes in degrees; (3) to interface with neurobiology. I argue that (relative) free
will comes in degrees, as suggested by neurobiology. A concept that can precisely
‘measure’ the variability of free will is—once more—that of ‘theory*’, or rather, the
capacity to make assumptions/hypotheses and apply them. This model highlights
that free-willed actions are consciously monitored by the agent, through beliefs,
assumptions, generally theories*—hence the CMT model (for Conscious-throughMonitoring-through-Theories*). I end by adjoining the absolute view to the relative
one, and sketch some implications of this bi-perspectival account.

7.1 Introduction
In the specialised philosophical literature on the free will debate, two widespread
positions are ‘hard determinism’ and ‘libertarianism’ (cf. Fischer et al., 2007; Kane,
2005, 2012; Mele, 2009; O’Connor & Franklin, 2022; Pereboom, 1997, 2014; Walter,
2001; Watson, 2003; List, 2019). Hard determinists reject free will on the hypothesis
that all events, including our choices, are determined (fixed) by the laws of nature
and the past; libertarians accept free will as a given and reject determinism. Clearly,
hard determinism largely corresponds to the physical reductionism I adopted in the
previous chapter. However, as argued there, strictly speaking I believe the dichotomy
is not between determinism and libertarianism, but between physics / physicalism
and libertarianism: my argument against absolute free will is in essence only based
on invoking the laws of physics, whether deterministic or probabilistic. Fundamental
randomness in nature (as posited by the usual interpretation of quantum mechanics)
is of no more help for absolute free will than genuine determinism. But for the
discussion in the following sections we can equate determinism and physicalism;

© The Author(s) 2026
L. Vervoort, Problem Solving in Philosophy,
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-17756-8_7

197

198

7 On Relative Free Will. The CMT Model

we do not really need to make the difference, since we study free will from now on
mainly from a socio-humanitarian angle.
Many professional philosophers are neither hard determinists nor libertarians but
‘compatibilists’, maybe even a majority as claimed in (Pereboom, 1997, p. 242):
according to this position, determinism and free will are not in contradiction. My
own model of free will could be termed compatibilist, in the sense that I believe that
even in a physical universe in which there is no absolute free will, there is something
that can be called relative or socio-humanitarian free will. But it is better to call
this view bi-perspectival: according to it, the absolute and relative perspectives are
complementary and both are needed for a fuller theory of free will. Absolute free will
as the capacity to choose undetermined by past events does not exist; but there exists
a capacity that is a placeholder, and that intervenes in legal, social, and humanitarian
theories, and in common-sense conversations.
It deserves to be mentioned from the start that I consider human free will, not
animal free will if such a thing would exist. More precisely, I focus on the free will that
is, in philosophy, very often considered to be a necessary condition for moral responsibility. (It is interesting in itself that philosophers have usually looked at the question
of free will from the perspective of moral responsibility. Our model developed below
suggests that the essential and more fundamental correlate is consciousness, rather
than moral responsibility. Note that this common bias among philosophers is well in
agreement with the claim that free will is a socio-humanitarian notion.)
Our goal is to construct a ‘minimal’ model in the sense that we aim to identify
the minimal set of necessary and sufficient conditions to term an act ‘free-willed’
or ‘free’.1 In this endeavour we use classic tools of analytic philosophy. We might
not be very typical in our attempt to synthesize theories of free will. With synthesis,
we mean, as in the rest of this book, the attempt (or its result) of showing how
different established theories can be derived from one and the same unified model;
more realistically, how the essential ingredients of different accounts resort under
one umbrella model. In the free will debate, millennia old, analytic philosophy has
invested such enormous efforts and has made, especially the last fifty to sixty years,
such progress in conceptual clarity, that efforts at synthesis seem now well justified.
This is so on the condition, of course, that the resulting model can solve problems
where isolated accounts remain silent or unconvincing. We insist that our model
should be judged on its results, namely the fact that it can tackle problems that
have been identified in competing modern theories, as shown in Sect. 6. Each of
the ingredients or components of our model will appear necessary to address these
problems.
Our most conspicuous influences come from A. J. Ayer (1954/1997), and, especially, M. Mahner and M. Bunge (1997), as explained in Sects. 3 and 4. Mahner and
Bunge develop an advanced biophilosophical account of consciousness that resonates
strongly with neuroscience, and is therefore well in the scope of synthetic philosophy.
1 From now on I reproduce parts of Vervoort and Blusiewicz (2020a); I will therefore mostly use

the plural ‘we’. I have made many add-ons and modifications, especially in Sects. 5–8, for instance
to make the link with other chapters of this book.

7.2 Background Assumptions. A Preliminary Observation: Free Will …

199

All three authors construct theories of which essential ideas can be traced back to such
fore-runners of the philosophy of free will as Aristotle and Aquinas. In Sect. 5 we will
show in detail how these models can be combined into our favourite model, which
we term the CMT-model of free will (for ‘Conscious-through-Monitoring-throughTheories*’). We intentionally construct the account in a detailed, step-by-step way,
to further illustrate the method of synthetic philosophy.
Our naturalized approach was also inspired by, and can be compared to, the work
of philosopher and neuroscientist Henrik Walter (2001), who proposes a component
theory of maximal libertarian free will: a theory with essentially three requirements
that a libertarian would demand of a ‘free’ act (in short, alternativism, intelligibility
and agency). Walter does not only support his claims by advanced neuroscientific
findings, but has also done an extensive literature review to show that his component
theory covers a wide spectrum of the most relevant modern theories and issues on
free will; hence the relevance of benchmarking our model to Walter’s.2
We will argue that one advantage of the CMT-model is that it captures a feature
of free will we believe is essential, namely that it comes in degrees. The variability
of free will has been hinted to by neurobiologists (e.g. Brembs, 2011), and analysed
by philosophers (O’Connor, 2009); we will present our own arguments in Sect. 2.
Our model suggests a qualitative ‘measure’ for this variability of free will (Sect. 5).
The most important results are gathered in Sect. 6, where we show that this model
can synthetically interpret key properties of recent accounts (and accordingly solve
their problems), notably those of Frankfurt (1969, 1988), Wolf (1990), Fischer and
Ravizza (1998), Walter (2001), List (2019) and other compatibilist models reviewed
in O’Connor and Franklin (2022). In Sect. 7 we briefly sketch how our model could be
instrumental for some scientific disciplines, in particular neurobiology and computer
science (AI). Finally, in Sect. 8, the conclusion of Chapters 6 and 7, I comment on
how the relative perspective must be completed with the absolute one.

7.2 Background Assumptions. A Preliminary Observation:
Free Will Comes in Degrees
Our background assumptions are those adopted in the rest of this book, but let us
mention them explicitly to make this chapter self-contained. First of all, we assume
that our mental activity, our thoughts, choices, feelings etc., have a neurological,
and ultimately chemical-physical basis in the brain: mental states are embedded in
2 Walter himself is not a libertarian; in his book he tests the three ingredients of libertarian free

will against natural science, in particular neuroscience. He argues that neuroscience does not really
support alternativism (the hypothesis that ‘at the atomic scale’, i.e. taking all physical facts into
account, a human brain has, at any given time, real alternatives to choose between—cf. Walter,
2001). So Walter is not in favor of strong libertarianism but shows that versions of intelligibility
and agency do find support in neuroscience; these concepts will be seen to have their pendants in
our minimal model. In this chapter we assume from the start that alternativism is eliminated, for
reasons explained in the previous chapter.

200

7 On Relative Free Will. The CMT Model

neural (super)networks, mental events/acts are brain processes (many relevant neuroscientific insights can be found e.g. in Walter, 2001; many essential implications for
philosophy are studied e.g. in Mahner & Bunge, 1997 and List, 2019). Based on the
famous Libet experiment and several other neuroscientific experiments (showing for
instance that ‘free’ choices can be predicted up to ten seconds before the subject’s
conscious action, cf. Soon et al., 2008 and Sect. 7), many, but certainly not all, neuroscientists favour a deterministic interpretation in the free will debate. We arrived at a
similar physicalist conclusion in the previous chapter, from a different but concurring
angle, forcing us to look for a compatibilist model.
Technically speaking, our goal is to construct a definition of a ‘free-willed’ act
by a human, in the form of necessary and sufficient conditions. Hence we aim at
identifying the conditions C1, C2, C3,… such that ‘Person P does act A of his own
free will’ if and only if C1, C2, C3,… are satisfied. The Ci are all necessary conditions
and they are jointly sufficient to imply free will. As announced, and remembering
Occam’s razor, we are looking for the minimal, most economic set {C1, C2, C3,…}.
But we would also like our model to say something about a feature of free will
that seems important, namely its variability. Consider your favourite dog, Snoopy,
playing in the garden, and suddenly seeing the neighbour’s cat appear. You shout:
Snoopy, stay put! For a fraction of a second Snoopy remains motionless, throws a
guilty glance at you, and then hurries after the cat. Has Snoopy free will? Could
Snoopy have done otherwise, has he alternatives? Perhaps in a certain sense he has
(cf. the previous chapter), but most philosophers agree that free will, and certainly
the free will needed for moral responsibility, is essentially a human thing (if it exists
at all). But granted, at the same time it seems quite intuitive to consider that Snoopy
possesses an embryonic sort of free will. Has a new-born child free will? A foetus?
No, most would say. When, then, does a child start having free will?
If adults can have free will, and if it is linked to, or emerges from, brain activity—a
brain activity that grows continuously more complex when a foetus and new-born
child evolve into an adult—one naturally arrives at the conclusion that free will comes
in continuous degrees. This seems an innocuous conclusion within a neuroscientific
perspective, which has been noted by philosophers3 (O’Connor, 2009). Another way
to understand this variability of free will is to realize that animals have continuously
evolved from bacteria over invertebrates, fish, amphibians, reptiles, small mammals
to primitive human species to modern Homo sapiens, and that somewhere in this
evolution, paralleling a steady increase in brain complexity, free will must have
appeared, not suddenly, but evolving in the same continuous way as the brain did.
Since this hypothesis will be instrumental in the following, we term it the ‘degreethesis’. Simply put: free will comes in degrees; in other words, the mental capacity of
free will is something that admits of (continuous) variations or gradations, something
that a human (and generally speaking an animal) can have to greater or lesser extent—
moreover an extent that can vary with time within an agent (O’Connor, 2009).
3 O’Connor (2009) arrives at the conclusion of variability of free will by an independent route,

notably by observing that agents can be, to greater or lesser extent, consciously aware of the factors
guiding their choices. This observation can well be fitted to our final model developed in Sect. 5.

7.3 A Basic Definition of Free Will (Ayer Model)

201

7.3 A Basic Definition of Free Will (Ayer Model)
Let us now start with our main task and look for the simplest non-trivial compatibilist
model of free will; this simplest model can however not solve certain problems and
will have to be completed in a following stage. A considerable part of the academic
debate on free will has turned around the ‘principle of alternative possibilities’ (PAP)
(Frankfurt, 1969; Kane, 2005). According to the PAP, a person P does act A of his/
her own free will only if P could have acted otherwise than doing A (the latter
condition is a necessary condition for free will). Most libertarians embrace the PAP
as evidence that free will excludes determinism. But it is well known that there are
considerable problems with the characterisation of free will provided by the PAP.
Notably, Frankfurt has provided well-known counterexamples: cases in which P
could not have acted otherwise, while P still seems to act of free will4 ; hence the
PAP would be a wrong characterisation of relative free will (Frankfurt, 1969, 1988).
These arguments have been criticised in turn (e.g. Widerker & McKenna, 2003);
an overview of the most recent developments in this lively debate can be found in
O’Connor and Franklin (2022). We believe it is fair to say that this matter is left
undecided in the literature.
A classic compatibilist attempt to defuse the worries related to the PAP was given
in a well-known article by A. J. Ayer (1954/1997), following a long tradition tracing
back to Aristotle (Nicomachean Ethics, Book III), Hobbes, and Locke. Ayer’s article
famously argues that the capacity to choose as invoked in the PAP may well be
guaranteed by the simple requirement that subject P acts without constraint. Thus,
in its simplest expression, an act of free will is, for Ayer, an act that is not under
constraint. In some more detail, we can extract following analytic definition of free
will from Ayer’s article:
DEF-A:‘P acts of his own free will’ iff
i. P is not compelled by other persons;
ii. there is no habitual ascendancy over P by other persons; and
iii. P acts voluntarily.
Ayer leaves the concept of ‘voluntary act’, again a notion that goes back to Aristotle, imprecise; therefore we will have to come back to it in a moment. But from the
text it follows that at least one ingredient of ‘voluntary’ is: not being under constraint
of an abnormal mental condition such as a mania, a neurosis, etc. (Ayer, 1954/1997).
Can an act that is not constrained and thus free according to (i)–(iii) also be free
in the sense of the PAP, in other words, could the unconstrained P also ‘have done
otherwise’? Ayer answers in the affirmative; he construes ‘P could have acted otherwise’ as follows: A) P was not constrained in the sense above (no-one compelled P
and the act was done in a ‘normal’ psychological condition) and B) P would have
acted otherwise if P had so chosen.
4 In any case, with moral responsibility.

202

7 On Relative Free Will. The CMT Model

Sure, this construal is far from universally accepted (cf. the review in O’Connor &
Franklin, , §2.2); but what is essential for us is that it appears that DEF-A can be
supplemented i2022n order to address worries of much more sophisticated recent
theories, as argued in Sect. 6. Therefore, we take DEF-A as a minimal analytic starting
point. (Even if this is not essential at this point, one could then also adopt Ayer’s
construal of ‘could…’ as ‘would… if…’ as plausible, also because it is compatible
with determinism (Ayer, 1954/1997) and physicalism. Note that the same holds
for clause A): P can be unconstrained in the quite common sense (i)–(iii) even if
everything is determined/physical. In Ayer’s words, free will should be contrasted
with constraint, not determinism.) For the time being we retain: there are respectable
arguments that an act of (relative) free will can be characterized in terms of absence
of constraint in the sense (i)–(iii), with the proviso that we would like to know more
about what ‘voluntary’ means in (iii).
Still, it is clear for other reasons that DEF-A cannot be the whole story. We
can repeat an argument we already invoked above: free will should be something
more than just ‘unconstrained will that has the ability of choosing between options’,
because it seems that even animals could have this type of will—recall Snoopy the
playful dog. But as most philosophers we ascribe relative free will only to humans—
always within a relative, socio-humanitarian perspective, in which free will and
morality are related.
What, then, is the feature that distinguishes humans most clearly from animals?
There is a long philosophical tradition related to this question, but it is presumably
uncontroversial to identify consciousness, the capacity to think, rationality, as the key
element(s) of distinction. Then, something of this should enter the definition of free
will. And indeed, consciousness and/or rationality seem implicit in Ayer’s concept of
voluntariness, as an ingredient of free will, as also follows from early analyses of the
concept of voluntariness by Aristotle and Aquinas (cf. Pereboom, 1997, Ch. 1 and
Ch. 5). We therefore should find a model for this essential but admittedly somewhat
vague concept.

7.4 Voluntariness and Consciousness: Mahner–Bunge
Model
To analyse voluntariness, the most developed naturalised model we are aware of is
proposed in Mahner and Bunge (1997)—‘naturalised’ meaning: in agreement with
science, in particular (neuro)biology. In their Foundations of Biophilosophy (1997)
the authors present a penetrating and wide-ranging approach aiming at providing an
axiomatic theory of key concepts of biology including psychobiology. Their approach
and methods are eminently science-compatible, and their background assumptions
coincide with ours (cf. Section 2 and below). In Chapter 6, on psychobiology, Mahner
and Bunge (MB henceforth) propose an analysis of concepts as mind, mind–body
interaction, consciousness, self, voluntariness, free will etc. In principle we only need

7.4 Voluntariness and Consciousness: Mahner–Bunge Model

203

their definitions of voluntariness and consciousness in order to complete Ayer’s DEFA, but we will give here a succinct overview of other notions too as conceptualised by
MB: this can complete our own sketchy versions in the previous chapter. Sure, MB’s
axiomatic and partly formalized approach is only one possible theoretical framework
for the above concepts; but their hypotheses and theory are systemic and heuristically
powerful. Again, the ultimate justification of the final model we will construct lies in
its problem-solving capacity—and in its relevance for science (Sects. 6 and 7). We
will only retain the essence of MB’s theory, and occasionally make small changes as
indicated.
As said, MB closely link the mind with the neuronal states and networks in the
human brain; mind and soul are not conceived of as immaterial, spiritual, perhaps
immortal entities. In this theory the mind of an animal is construed as the union,
the set of all mental processes of its brain.5 Mahner and Bunge propose following
definition (MB, 1997, p. 205):
DEF-MB-1. Let P denote a plastic neuronal supersystem of animal b of species
K. Then the mind of b during the period τ is the union of all the mental processes
(specific functions πs ) that components of P, i.e., plastic neuronal systems n,
engage in during τ. More precisely,
M(b, τ) = Un πs (n, τ).
The other key concepts, plastic neuronal (super)system, mental process, mental
function, are also defined in Mahner and Bunge (MB 1997, Ch. 6). However simple,
this theory allows one to address some of the old, paradigmatic problems of the philosophy of mind. One such question is: where is the mind located? Strictly speaking,
the mind is nowhere, since it is a set, hence a conceptual object; only brains, whether
minding or not, are somewhere (MB p. 207). Further, according to this model there
can be no real mind–body dualism, thus avoiding an old stumbling block of philosophy, neither mind–body interaction—as opposed to brain-body interaction. MB
explain in following passage (MB 1997 p. 206):
There can be no mind-matter interaction because – unlike individual mental processes and
brains – mind and matter are sets, hence conceptual objects. However, it does make sense to
speak of ‘mental-bodily interactions’ provided this expression is taken to abbreviate ‘interactions among plastic neuronal systems, on the one hand, and either committed neuronal
systems or bodily systems that are not part of the Central Neuronal System on the other’.
Thus, there are interactions between sensory and motor areas, between ideational neuronal
systems and external receptors, between the cortical and subcortical regions of the brain,
between the brain and the endocrine and immune system, and so on. Because mental events
are neural events, and because the causal relation is defined for pairs of events in concrete
systems (recall Sect. 1.9), we have:
Corollary 6.5.: Mental events can cause nonmental events in the same body, and v.v.
5 Of course, in this context the notion of set, a concept of formal logic, is not the most user-friendly

one, at any rate other less formal conceptualisations are also possible. We do not really need the
notion of mind, but give it as illustration.

204

7 On Relative Free Will. The CMT Model

Consequently, disturbances of nonmental biofunctions may influence mental states and,
conversely, mental events such as acts of will may influence nonmental bodily states. This
is what neurochemistry, neurology, psychiatry, psychosomatic medicine, psychoneuropharmacology, education, and propaganda are all about.

In a similar vein and building on the above assumptions one can then define consciousness, termed the highest of all brain functions by Bunge and Mahner (MB 1997,
p. 209). First they define (we only slightly modify their phrasing):
DEF-MB-2. A conscious mental process / choice / act (an act conceived as a
mental process, or linked to a mental process) is a mental process / choice / act
that is monitored (recorded, analysed, controlled, or kept track of) by some other
mental activity in the same brain.
Simply put: for a mental process or act to be conscious, it must be thought about by
a higher part of the brain—a thesis that seems corroborated by neuroscience.6 And
further (MB 1997, p. 209):
DEF-MB-3. The consciousness of an animal b is the set of all the states (or, rather,
processes) of the brain of b in which b is conscious of some perception or thought
in b itself.
(This is not a circular definition thanks to DEF-MB-2.) MB explain:
According to this convention, an animal can only be conscious of some of its own higher
mental processes: not just feeling, sensing, and doing, but also thinking of what it perceives
or thinks. An animal conscious of mental process x (in itself) possibly undergoes (either in
parallel or in quick succession) two different mental processes: x – the object mental process
or content of its consciousness, and thinking about x – i.e., being conscious about x. (MB
1997 p. 208-209)

It seems natural to think of this pair of mental processes as embedded in two different
neural circuits: one embedding process x, and one embedding thinking about x. Using
DEF-MB-2, one can now construct a definition of the voluntary act as invoked for
instance by Aristotle, Aquinas and Ayer (MB 1997, p. 210).
DEF-MB-4. An animal act is voluntary (or intentional) iff it is a conscious
purposeful act.
‘Purposeful’ is left undefined, but is self-explaining; and, for that matter, it seems less
essential to us since it seems that a choice or conscious act can always be associated
with a purpose. And finally (MB 1997, p. 211):
6 Neuroscientific findings show that the cognitive center of the brain, the prefrontal cortex, is

connected to large parts of the brain and seems to function as the ‘integrator’ of information
embedded in very many other mental processes. This is the neurobiological intuition that lies at the
basis of MB’s intuition that consciousness of x is related to ‘thinking about x by a higher level part
of the brain’—say the prefrontal cortex.

7.4 Voluntariness and Consciousness: Mahner–Bunge Model

205

DEF-MB-5: An animal acts of its own free will iff
i. its action is voluntary; and
ii. it has free choice of its goal(s)—i.e., is under no programmed or external
compulsion to attain the chosen goal.
In essence, we find in clause (ii) the absence of constraint that is the main condition
for Ayer. And ‘voluntary’ in clause (i) and in DEF-A (iii) is now explained by
DEF-MB-4: it means conscious and purposeful.
Thus, one finds that Mahner, Bunge and Ayer (MBA) ultimately derive a very
similar definition of free will. Rather than just showing the final result, we have
followed MB in a step-wise manner, to highlight that their definition is based on a solid
psychobiological theory. Most importantly, we will show below that an upgraded
MBA-definition can well be related to a majority of compatibilist theories on free
will. Let us for the moment omit the notion of purpose in voluntary, since it seems
less essential and can be seen to be contained in the notion of choice or choice-act
(so we stipulate it is implicit in the definition below). Then one can unify Ayer’s and
MB’s models as follows.
DEF-MBA. Act A by animal b is ‘free-willed’ or ‘free’ (is made of b’s own free
will)
iff
i. the act A is unconstrained (no programmed or external compulsion), and
ii. the act A is conscious in that the act (linked to a mental process) is monitored
(recorded, analysed, controlled, or kept track of) by some other mental activity
in the brain of b.
In this way we have specified, with MBA, that no-constraint is essentially absence of
constraint by external agents and by (externally or internally) programmed influences,
where internal constraints can be manias, neuroses, etc. We can now define not only
‘free acts’ but also ‘free will’, namely as the capacity to perform free-willed actions
in the sense of DEF-MBA.
It is worth mentioning that DEF-MBA luckily is in a long historical lineage.
Notably, it comes close to Aristotle’s view on free will, but it is also meaningfully
linked to Kant’s (see further). Aristotle conceived of free will as a capacity to make
choices that are unconstrained and not made of ignorance (cf. Pereboom, 1997, Ch.
1). The latter idea can well be linked to clause (ii) of DEF-MBA, as we will detail
in a moment. Thus, we see appearing in this model of free will the component of
consciousness that we intuitively suspected from the start to be an ingredient of free
will—simply by recognizing or assuming that ‘real’ free will cannot be attributed to
lower animals and is typically a human thing.

206

7 On Relative Free Will. The CMT Model

A second essential, science-based hypothesis we started from is that free will
comes in degrees. We posited this degree-thesis essentially by recognizing that there
must be a (continuous) evolution in free will from primitive humans to modern
humans, and from a new-born child to a knowledgeable adult. The next question
to ask is then: which concepts in DEF-MBA admit of degree? Constraint, but more
conspicuously the notions of analysis and control in clause (ii).7
In the next section we look in more detail into this variability. We will introduce
a concept that captures how one monitors a free act (on the logical-epistemological
level), providing by the same token an interpretation of the variability of free will.

7.5 Elaboration of the MBA-Model: The CMT-Model
If free will comes in degrees, a natural question is: can one then define a measure
of the variability of free will? A related question that brings this matter sharply to
the point is: can we define a maximal form of free will—maximal in the sense of
optimal, most adequate? The second question might seem complex and touchy; it is
rather absent from the modern debate, but it was very present in the views on free
will of Spinoza, Kant and many other historical philosophers. We will use the second
question as a heuristic tool to try to answer the first one.
To link this problem to recent debates, let us look at a case mentioned in (Griffith,
2013 p. 33), a case that pervades the contemporary debate under different guises.
Suppose a young girl, Trina, lives in a closed community that taught her since her
early childhood that stealing from people outside the community is praiseworthy, the
thing to do. Suppose that on her first day at school Trina happily puts her worldview
to practice and steals things from her young schoolmates. Does Trina do these acts
of her own free will? As argued by Griffith, in this case many people would accept
that Trina was brainwashed (to some degree), and that she is not really blameworthy
because she did not know any better. Thus, many believe that Trina has no real
free will. This is also what the CMT-model says: while stealing, Trina can surely
be conscious of it (she may perform some more or less conscious analysis of some
of her deeds, there may be a little voice in her head saying: “stealing from these
girls is cool”), but at the same time there is a programmed constraint acting in the
sense that she was brainwashed by parents and community. So it is clause (i) that
implies that her act of stealing is not free-willed. But again, it seems clear that the
notion of degree of free will is helpful here: one could say that Trina’s behaviour
is free to some degree, but not an optimal one. She is constrained by some (harsh
or mild) form of brainwashing; and, especially, she monitors, analyses, controls her
deeds by a presumably sub-optimal hypothesis or worldview, namely that stealing
is praiseworthy. To make a link with what follows: she does not seem to rely on the

7 In general, the ‘being conscious’ of clause (ii) can vary; this comes close, or can at least interestingly

be compared, to the account of O’Connor (2009).

7.5 Elaboration of the MBA-Model: The CMT-Model

207

optimal assumptions to monitor, analyse and control her deeds—assuming there is
any conscious reflection involved at all.
Now to our question whether a maximal/optimal form of free will could be
defined: DEF-MBA does suggest a possibility to qualify optimal/adequate free will.
Answering this question amounts to construing the optimal forms of analysis and
control that consciousness, our brain, can perform when monitoring an act. Using
insights gained in previous chapters, we believe the most synthetic concept to identify such optimal analysis and control is that of ‘representation’ or more precisely
‘theory’—the very concept that has been immensely useful in the preceding chapters! We again use ‘theory’ here in a very broad sense, including scientific and
academic theories (ethical, philosophical, sociological, political, physical, …) but
more generally also belief systems, including every-day beliefs and assumptions/
hypotheses; (coherent) bodies of information; worldviews; etc. In Chapter 1 the
notation ‘theory*’ was introduced to refer to theories in this broad sense.
The link with analysis and control is then not far to seek: it may be said that one
always analyses and controls a conscious act, choice or decision with reference to, or
within, a theory*. This seems obvious when making decisions that need the input of
expert intellectual or theoretical knowledge. But even when making a banal choice
on an idle Thursday evening, say between whether to go to the cinema or rather
to visit a friend, one ‘analyses’ or ‘contemplates’ both alternatives within certain
beliefs, using certain assumptions—for instance assumptions about the satisfaction
each activity will provide. Sure, the analysis may be barely conscious and ultra-rapid
in this example; but we already agreed that being conscious comes in degrees (more
examples are given below.) In any case, in order to identify optimal analysis and
optimal free will we better look not at banal cases but at ethically or intellectually
demanding ones. And then it seems quite clear what optimal, most adequate analysis
means: namely analysis within the ‘optimal theory*’—the best theory* that we have
or have not at hand; the best-informed or most adequate assumptions on which to base
our free choice or decision. Sure, in many cases it is not clear what the best theory*
is, but then, in many cases it is. (Recall from the chapters on Gettier’s problem
and causation that the notion of ‘best possible’ or ‘best available’ theory is very
instrumental and natural even if it usually is an idealisation.8 ) In sum, according to the
above analysis maximal free will is free will using the optimal beliefs, assumptions,
theories*; free will that is based on using the optimal theories and assumptions in our
decision making. In an ethical context: the best moral theories and principles. (Note
8 In cases when the ‘optimal theory’ is unknown, one may consider ‘optimal theory’ as an idealised,

hypothetical concept, something as a hypothetical extrapolation of existing provisional theories. In
natural science for instance, it is current practice to talk about ‘future better theories’ (such as
the elusive Theory-of-Everything); in physics mathematical theorems are formulated regarding the
features of these ‘more optimal’ future theories, such as Bell’s theorem. In sum, there seems little
doubt that this notion of optimal theory is operationally useful: one often can well know which
theory or assumptions are better than others (for a given end); the optimal theory (in the absolute
sense) corresponds to the extrapolated, hypothetical end product of this progress. Philosophy and
natural science are full of this abstract concept, even if we surely will never know the ultimate
theories.

208

7 On Relative Free Will. The CMT Model

that there is a whole multidisciplinary field dedicated to optimal decision making:
decision theory (e.g. Berger, 2013).)
We note that this conclusion closely fits to what Kant thought about free will in his
Foundations of the Metaphysics of Morals (1786/1983). Here Kant states, notably:
“a free will and a will under moral laws become one and the same” (Kant, 1786/
1983 BA98). Thus for Kant, real free will is, in essence, will under moral law. In
our parlance: will in accordance with (monitored, controlled by) adequate moral
theories*.
Let us give a few more examples of how assumptions/beliefs/theories intervene
in free and therefore conscious choices (from Vervoort & Blusiewicz, 2020b). When
freely deciding which suit to choose to go to work, I may ‘assume’ or I may ‘use the
belief’ or ‘be in the belief’ that suit A is more appropriate for today’s work than suit
B. Or, when deciding of my own free will to help my neighbour, if I have Kantian
inclinations, I may base my decision on the view that altruism is a source of fulfilment; or I may base it on the belief that the act will be rewarded by something more
mundane—say a babble plus beer. The more intellectually demanding the context
is, the more elaborate the assumptions I adopt: in some cases these better be part of
a well-grounded theory. For instance, when a spaceship commander decides in an
unforeseen situation to freely press the full power button at a precise time, he hopefully does so on the basis of assumptions that take serious mechanical calculations
into account. If he does not, and hits the button in a panicky reflex, one hesitates to call
his act genuinely free—in agreement with almost all philosophical interpretations.
There is another perspective from which to understand the close connection
between the notions of free will/consciousness and that of theory*. If being conscious
of x involves thinking about x, then there seems to be an unavoidable link with truth.
Thinking that has no connection at all with (approximate) truth is erratic, and therefore at odds with genuine free will, supposed to be compatible with moral responsibility and goal-oriented agency. Next, we have seen in the chapter on epistemology
and Gettier’s problem that the truth of propositions is inextricably perspective- or
theory*-dependent. There is no more or less adequate thinking that does not logically,
epistemologically, and presumably ontologically (see Sect. 7) ‘need’ (the manipulation of) coherent sets of hypotheses. In a slogan: there is no thinking that is not
based on theories*. Many might agree that this is, in hindsight, a truism—but one
with surprisingly far-reaching consequences, as developed in this book.
We were trying, initially, to identify a measure for the variability of free will. The
answer will be clear by now: what varies in our capacity to perform actions that are
free according to DEF-MBA is the adequacy of the theories* we use to monitor these
actions. In other words, the assumptions and belief systems involved in monitoring a
free act are more or less adequate for guiding these acts. It is in this sense that one can
say that Trina in the above example does not seem to use the optimal assumptions to
monitor and control her deeds.
In conclusion, we will adopt the MBA-model with the extra assumption that, in
clause (ii), the monitoring (analysing, controlling) of the conscious brain involves
theories*, and that these theories* have an adequacy that admits of a degree. For the
reasons explained, we only attribute free will to humans, not to animals in general,

7.5 Elaboration of the MBA-Model: The CMT-Model

209

so we introduce in the definition the notion of ‘agent S’. We call our enhanced MBAmodel the CMT-model of free will. It defines free will as the capacity to perform
actions that are Conscious-through-Monitoring-through-Theories*, and is condensed
in following definition:
DEF-CMT. Act A by agent S is ‘free-willed’ or ‘free’ (is made of S’s own free
will)
iff
i. the act A is unconstrained (no programmed or external compulsion), and
ii. the act A is conscious in that the act (linked to a mental process) is monitored
(recorded, analysed, controlled, or kept track of) by some other mental activity
in the brain of S. The latter monitoring occurs through representations, i.e.
theories*, which have a varying degree of adequacy (cf. text).
Note that our model of free will is condensed and minimal; it is also a minimalistic model of consciousness via clause (ii). Compared to the MBA-model, DEFCMT conceptualises, from a logical-epistemological point of view, how, by which
means, internal mental monitoring is done. By the same token, it allows us to interpret the variability and other debated properties of free will, as shown in the next
section. Experts of the highly debated, and often considered elusive, concept of
consciousness—studied in philosophy, neurobiology, cognitive science, computer
science, etc.—will hopefully appreciate the simplicity of the model, condensed in
clause (ii). They might also recognise that DEF-CMT can be classified as a variant
of the well-known higher-order-thought (HOT) theory of consciousness (Rosenthal,
2005). Thus, even if we came from a very different direction (and were initially
not aware of HOT-theories), we ended up with a definition that lies in a respectable
tradition. This is one more justification of the use of the notion of ‘theory*’.
Minimalism is particularly instrumental when one wishes to study a concept
as free will or consciousness in different disciplines, say neuroscience or artificial
intelligence (AI), as is chiefly done in Sect. 7 and (Vervoort & Blusiewicz, 2020b;
Vervoort et al., 2021). For example, Vervoort et al. (2021) investigate the conditions under which artificial intelligence could, one day, become conscious. Starting
from DEF-CMT, framed with notions that make sense in computer science, these
authors propose more or less concrete routes for further interdisciplinary research
on enhancing the cognitive skills of future AI.
In the following section we argue that the CMT-account can subsume other recent
theories of free will, and solve problems of the latter. We focus on compatibilist
accounts as ours. Clearly, for some problems the CMT-model needs to be used
in combination with the perplexing conclusion of the previous chapter—the nonexistence of absolute free will. The fact that both perspectives are needed for certain
key problems is condensed in the idea of complementarity.

210

7 On Relative Free Will. The CMT Model

7.6 CMT-Model Compared to Other Compatibilist
Theories
R1. Frankfurt’s theory. A first well-known theory to consider is Frankfurt’s ‘hierarchical mesh theory’ of free will (Frankfurt, 1969,1988). In a nutshell, according
to Frankfurt an action or choice A is free-willed if it is object of, if it meshes with, a
‘second-order volition’ or desire—a higher desire about the first-order desire to do
A. Then A is really (rationally) desired, in agreement with one’s real self, with one’s
second-order (rational) desires; in other words, A flows from the ‘will one wants’—a
reflective capacity animals likely do not have.
A priori it seems clear that this model resonates well with our model: it suffices to
note that second-order volitions are part of the general beliefs, worldviews etc. agents
use to guide and control their life and actions. Action A meshes with a second-order
volition in that it is consciously monitored (assessed, analysed etc.) with help of a
worldview, a belief system, assumptions of life, in other words theories*. So there
surely seems place for a partial overlap between both models. However, it is well
known that cases as Trina’s, the brainwashed girl, are a threat to Frankfurt’s theory (for
a recent overview and references, see e.g. Griffith, 2013, Ch. 4; O’Connor & Franklin,
2022). Trina may well act in accordance with higher volitions, really believe in what
she does, and therefore be entirely free according to Frankfurt’s model—a conclusion most people would disagree with. The CMT model solves this problem: Trina
is brainwashed and therefore not unconstrained (condition (i)); and she monitors
(assesses) her deeds through questionable, likely inadequate beliefs (condition (ii)).
One could say she has a limited form of free will. Next, there is also a well-known
infinite-regress problem threatening Frankfurt’s theory (why stop at second-order
and not include higher-order volitions ?) This problem is absent from our model for
obvious reasons.
R2. Wolf’s theory. A next interesting account is Wolf’s ‘Reason View’ of free
will (1990). Wolf’s is also a mesh theory, but whereas in Frankfurt’s model free
will is a subjective matter (an act is free as long as there is a mesh between the
agent’s choices and his personal, subjective, higher-level desires), Wolf adds that
these personal desires should also have a ‘connection with the world outside’—they
should have an objective dimension, they should ‘connect with the True and Good’
(Wolf, 1990). So, in order to have free will, one should have the right, objective
reasons to do things, reasons that connect to the True and Good.
The key ingredient of this account can well be subsumed by ours. Our model says
that an action should be consciously monitored, guided by a (sufficiently) adequate
theory*, ethical or other. This can be understood, in Wolf’s parlance, as expressing
that the agent should have adequate reasons for her or his act, reasons that ‘connect
with the True and Good’, in the sense that they are involving, embedded in, backedup by, adequate theories*. Note that ‘adequate theory’ is a more instrumental and
precise concept than ‘adequate reasons’, especially if one wishes to interface with
(computer) science, as explained in Sect. 7 and (Vervoort & Blusiewicz, 2020b;
Vervoort et al., 2021); and we avoid the somewhat metaphorical ‘connection with

7.6 CMT-Model Compared to Other Compatibilist Theories

211

the True and Good’.9 Furthermore, it has been objected to Wolf’s theory that agents
can be manipulated or programmed into accessing the True and Good (e.g. Griffith,
2013, Ch. 4). We avoid this problem via clause (i) in DEF-CMT.
In sum, in spirit there is an obvious link between Wolf’s theory and ours. It may
be that relying on ‘theories’ and ‘consciousness’ as we do, rather than on ‘reasons’
(Wolf) or ‘intelligibility / rationality’ as for instance Walter does (2001), is in last
analysis a matter of taste (and logical construction of the theory). Still, we submit
that the variability of free will, our essential starting assumption, is conceptualised
most precisely via the concept of theory*.
R3. Fischer and Ravizza’s theory. A much debated and sophisticated theory is
Fischer and Ravizza’s ‘Reasons-Responsiveness View’, putting an emphasis on the
‘guidance-control’ involved in a free act (Fischer & Ravizza, 1998; Fischer et al.,
2007). We will here summarise the essence of this theory in a condensed manner. In a
nutshell, an agent has free will if her actions and choices are sensitive to, respond to,
reasons, where it is emphasized that this reasons-responsiveness should not rely on
luck, once in a while, and that it is not responsiveness under compulsion or neurotic
disorder. Rather, the agent should be responsive to reasons through a systematic
(cognitive) mechanism that ensures guidance-control over her actions.10
To make the link with the CMT-model, first note that the concept of conscious
control is explicitly part of our basic starting model, the MBA-definition (clause (ii)).
The conscious monitoring of an act via adequate beliefs thus seems to imply reasonsresponsiveness and guidance-control (or it could be construed to imply this). So,
there is a partial overlap of the theories. However, Fischer’s and Ravizza’s ReasonsResponsiveness view is subject to the same criticism as Frankfurt’s theory: it implies
that the brainwashed girl Trina steals of her free will, since she may well act from
a practical and un-excessive reasoning mechanism. This is a worry we are clearly
immune against, cf. the argument given above.
Modern theories of free will pay much attention to the notion of control. As
said, our model tells us what kind of control it is, or how this control is realised
from a logical-epistemological point of view—the point of view that is needed for
philosophical problem solving.
R4. Other compatibilist accounts discussed in O’Connor and Franklin (2022).
Sourcehood. It is instructive to study the recent review article (O’Connor & Franklin,
2022), providing a thorough overview of key ideas and positions in the contemporary
free will debate, including of their historical lineage and interconnections. We submit
they can all nicely be interpreted within the bi-perspectival account presented in this
and the previous chapter. Let us go over the most relevant of them.
9 Instead of ‘connection (with the True and Good)’ we use the notion of ‘monitoring (via theories*)’,

which can be given a firm neurobiological interpretation: the monitoring is materialized by two
neural circuits being physically connected (cf. above and Sect. 7).
10 In slightly more detail, free-willed acts should be governed by a moderately reasons-responsive
brain mechanism (say, enacting deliberation), i.e. one that is reasons-receptive and weakly reasonsreactive. Fischer and Ravizza (1998) introduce these criteria with the aim to exclude excessively
dogmatic and phobic acts from being free-willed.

212

7 On Relative Free Will. The CMT Model

The authors recall that nowadays two of the most commonly cited features of free
will are the ‘freedom-to-do-otherwise’ and ‘sourcehood’ (O’Connor & Franklin,
2022, § 2.3–2.4). Sourcehood-accounts have been motivated or inspired, notably,
by the Frankfurt-style thought experiments mentioned in Sect. 3, and by the work
by Fischer and Ravizza. First, according to the account proposed in the previous
chapter, in the absolute sense I have no genuine freedom to do otherwise. But in
the relative sense, seen from the perspective that is surely most vivid to us, I often
do have (the impression to have) the possibility to choose between, say, greeting
acquaintances I meet on the street, or menacing them with a knife (!). In my abstract,
imagined world-in-thoughts: of course. I have this (theoretical) possibility to choose
when I can imagine, i.e. represent, different options, different (sequences of) events
starting from my actions, and evaluate these options; and both the representation and
the evaluation involve theories*—even if these can be extremely banal and barely
conscious (but fully unconscious reflex-like actions are not really free). When I then
choose one option, it is ‘I’ who chooses, seen from the perspective that I can grasp. In
this relative but extremely vivid sense, I am the source of my actions, of my greeting
instead of menacing. And the control I exercise is mediated by, or derives from, a
form of cognitive understanding I have of what happens to me. In this sense, one
could say that free will derives from adequate representing, or understanding (of
what happens to me). But again, in a complementary, truer sense, the absolute one,
my choices are determined, correlated with countless preceding events in a chain
that starts entirely outside me. For example: I do not menace people, because I am
educated, conditioned, by education, society, law, etc.; and all the innumerable factors
of education, society, law etc. that have influenced me in my ‘choice’ not to menace
are materialised by events in the real world; and these are all necessarily correlated
with events occurring long before my birth. My capacity to do otherwise is, as a
matter of principle, only a theoretical, metaphorical one, based on my capacity to
represent the world.
Thus, one sees that the concepts of freedom-to-do-otherwise, sourcehood, and
control are intimately related on our account: they can be interpreted in a synthetic
move, sketchily presented above and at the end of the previous chapter. Competing
compatibilist accounts are reviewed in O’Connor and Franklin (2022, § 2.2–2.4).
The authors pay much attention to, notably, the case of phobic agents debated in
the recent literature. They conclude: “it seems compatibilists have yet to furnish an
analysis of the freedom to do otherwise that implies that phobics clearly lack the
ability to choose or do otherwise that is relevant to moral responsibility and yet some
merely determined agents have this ability” (O’Connor & Franklin, 2022, § 2.2).
The solution provided by DEF-CMT to this problem seems clear. Much as in the
case of the brainwashed Trina, both clauses (i) and (ii) prescribe the solution; they
do so in a coherent manner. Most relevant is clause (i): phobic agents are under inner
compulsion and therefore not really free—even if this comes with a degree. (And
if they are not really free, they are not really morally responsible, since free will is
necessary for moral responsibility.) Clause (ii) is coherent with this conclusion, and
adds something about the quality of the limited free will phobic agents have: phobia
imposes representations of reality that are usually considered of a less adequate type.

7.6 CMT-Model Compared to Other Compatibilist Theories

213

Non-phobic agents are equally unfree in the absolute sense, but are monitoring their
acts with theories* that are considered, comparatively, more adequate (or that could
be so); and they are not under inner compulsion. One might compare this simple
solution to the more complicated ones proposed by authors as Vihvelin (2004) and
others reviewed in O’Connor and Franklin (2022, § 2.2).
Similarly, published accounts dealing with the ‘Manipulation Argument’ are
reviewed and found wanting. An example of this argument is Mele’s ‘Zygote Argument’ (2006, Ch. 7), involving a goddess who manipulates the zygote of person
S1 to make sure that S1’s child, S2, will kill some specific person S3 when adult.
Suppose S2, as a result of a moderately reasons-responsive mechanism à la Fischer
and Ravizza and on the basis of motivations with which S2 is identified (as demanded
by ‘identification accounts’, a special class of sourcehood-accounts11 ) does commit
the programmed murder. Many judge that S2 is not free nor morally responsible
for murdering S3 even though this agent satisfies both the reasons-responsiveness
and identification criteria: a problem for a variety of compatibilist models that hinge
on these criteria. O’Connor and Franklin state (2022, § 2.4): “The general problem
raised by this line of argument is that whatever proposed compatibilist conditions
for an agent S’s being free with respect to, and morally responsible for, some action
ϕ, it will seem that agents can be manipulated into satisfying these conditions with
respect to ϕ and, yet, precisely because they are manipulated into satisfying these
conditions, their freedom and responsibility seem undermined.” As a consequence,
authors as Fischer and Ravizza have later tried to upgrade their model by reinforcing
their free will, or sourcehood, criteria with an extra clause, stipulating that a free
agent should take or have taken responsibility for the act under scrutiny. But “[t]he
problem with this reply is that we can easily imagine [the goddess] creating [S2] so
that his murdering [S3] is a result not only of a moderately reasons-responsive mechanism, but also a mechanism for which he has taken responsibility” (O’Connor &
Franklin, 2022, § 2.4)
As above, DEF-CMT solves cases as these via clause (i): S2 is manifestly manipulated and therefore not free. It is as simple as that. The number of problems that
can be solved by a bi-conditional (and bi-perspectival) account is large, so we hope
that philosophers will agree with us that this seems a promising, synthetic way out
of the mentioned conundrums.
R5. Walter’s work. The neuroscientist and philosopher H. Walter (2001) has
proposed a 3-component theory of libertarian free will, so it cannot directly be
compared to ours. But since this theory is based on neuroscientific investigations by
Walter’s and other teams, it is still revealing to have a look at the similarities and differences. A summary is given in Table 1.1., p. 43 (Walter, 2001). Each main ingredient
(briefly, alternativism; intelligibility / rationality; and agency / origination) can be
understood according to Walter’s classification in a minimal, moderate or maximal
11 There is a school of philosophers that tries to stabilise classical compatibilism via the requirement

of self-determination: a free act should be self-determined (rather than determined or undetermined),
i.e. brought about by motives with which the agent is identified (for a list of representatives, cf.
O’Connor & Franklin, 2022, § 2.4).

214

7 On Relative Free Will. The CMT Model

version. Comparison shows in a straightforward manner that the CMT-model, if
we append to it our thesis of the non-existence of absolute free will, corresponds to
Walter’s alternativism in its minimal interpretation, plus intelligibility in its moderate
interpretation. Our model does not explicitly include agency, but we define the free
act of an agent, and therefore agency can be understood as implicit in this notion of
agent and, especially, as made explicit by our ‘consciousness’ clause (ii). In other
words, we can define genuine agency as the capacity of a subject to act freely in the
sense of DEF-CMT. Along these lines, we have discussed in the previous chapter
and under R4 above how agent-based origination / sourcehood can be interpreted in
a universe that is, ultimately, ‘only physical’. (The reader will have understood that
we use shortcuts as ‘there is nothing but the physical’ with a tremendous grain of
salt: from the physical so many spheres or disciplines or problem-spaces emerge, say
the psychological sphere, or the social one—which each need their domain-specific
representations, theories, to be more or less understandable.)
Readers interested in neuroscience will enjoy discovering how Walter—as one
representative of a number of neuroscientists—connects philosophical concepts to
contemporary neuroscience. In the footsteps of scientist-philosophers as Mahner
and Bunge, his work belongs to those rare but very welcome efforts of genuinely
interdisciplinary research.
R6. List’s theory. Finally, it is instructive to compare our bi-perspectival view to
the sophisticated and scientifically well-informed compatibilist account by C. List
(2019) (which will be represented here in an utterly sketchy manner). List starts from
the assumption that following three conditions are sufficient for free will, in short:
intentional agency, alternative possibilities, and causal control. His essential move
for arguing that physical reductionism is not undermining these three conditions is
to advance that the latter belong to other epistemic ‘levels’ than physics and hence
cannot be reduced. The best theories we have to describe and explain for instance
intentional agency and causal control are such scientific theories as psychology and
neuroscience, and according to these the mentioned conditions for free will do very
well exist. Since philosophy should adopt the best scientific theories at hand, it
logically follows that free will exists.
I partially agree: relative free will exists. Indeed, one will note quite some similarities with my bi-perspectival account—notably List’s starting point to look at things
from different theoretical perspectives or levels –, with the difference that I include
physicalism in the maximally coherent beliefs to adopt. True, it is debatable whether
physicalism is a scientific thesis in the strict sense; but it is a thesis that answers
immensely important (philosophical) questions for which non-physicalism remains
powerless or unconvincing—such as: on what other elementary constituents neurobiological systems would be based, if not on physical ones? Therefore, even if I
agree with List that on a practical level neuroscience and psychology and any of the
socio-humanitarian sciences can clearly not be reduced to, described by, physics,
physical reductionism still holds as a philosophical principle; we must assume that
in the absolute all facts of the psyche and of society are in principle consequences
of the laws of physics. Adopting reductionism leads then quasi-immediately to my

7.7 Proposal of a Few Interdisciplinary Lines of Research, Mainly About …

215

absolute view on free will, which is absent from List’s theory. But this elaborate
theory can surely enrich my minimalistic relative account, the CMT-model.

7.7 Proposal of a Few Interdisciplinary Lines of Research,
Mainly About Consciousness, Natural and Artificial
Before concluding, let us briefly indicate some avenues of research suggested by
the CMT-model in neuroscience and computer science. I conjecture that this model
could be instrumental in tackling neuroscientific questions related to consciousness—
considered an essential but at the same time highly elusive concept in neuroscience
(Stern, 2017).
I think here in the first place of the process of monitoring by a neuronal superstructure, presumably in the prefrontal cortex, that should represent a theory*. This
abstract, logical-epistemological notion plays such a central role of problem-solving
in almost all chapters of this book, that I suspect it must have a rather immediate realworld, i.e. neurobiological, correlate. From some point of view, this is an obvious
assumption; but whereas theories-in-the-mind have a very ethereal touch, in the
brain they might be more solid, structured, lawful than meets the eye—and it is this
lawfulness that might be interesting to study empirically. Perhaps it is interesting and
feasible to search for the neuronal correlates for rudimentary theories*—say, law-like
assumptions/hypotheses—in primates. One guesses that they are related to memorycircuits, or to the ‘mirror neurons’ that have become fashionable lately. Indeed, neuroscientists have associated these mirror neurons with the ‘theory of mind’ of primates,
the cognitive base for recognising the ‘self’ and ‘the other’ (Gallese & Goldman,
1998). In the vocabulary of this book, if primates have rudimentary psychological
beliefs, i.e. a rudimentary psychological theory*, altering the corresponding neurons
could dramatically change their behaviour; and some of these neurons might be
involved in any basic cognitive behaviour. In sum, it is tempting to conjecture that
any behaviour in any animal that points to embryonic cognitive capacities should
be associated with (presumably dedicated) neuronal networks or mechanisms that
materialise sets of hypotheses / theories*. Perhaps this umbrella concept can serve
as a guide and synthetic problem-solver in neuroscience too. At this point this is of
course not more than a conjecture.
Next, one can analyse the well-known Libet experiment through the lens of our
account, as is done in (Vervoort & Blusiewicz, 2020b). Libet’s experiment, in which
an electroencephalography (EEG) scan of a test person is made, records the socalled ‘readiness potential’: an electrical signal generated by the firing neurons of
the scanned brain. Every voluntary action, such as moving a hand, induces such a
slowly rising electrical potential. During the experiment, the test persons are asked
to spontaneously flex their hand, whenever they feel like it, and note the time when
they make this ‘free’ decision. The actual time of movement can be measured electronically with high precision; both times coincide reasonably well. Libet’s quite

216

7 On Relative Free Will. The CMT Model

surprising and famous result was that the brain activity as recorded by the EEG
started almost half a second before the time at which the test person consciously
decided to move. Libet and most neuroscientists interpret this result as showing that
it is the brain that decides, rather than a free mind. More precisely: unconscious
brain processes are at work before the conscious impression of choosing manifests
itself; it is not the mind that commands the body, but rather the other way around.
Of course, this interpretation is not unanimous, but even more spectacular results
have been reported since. For instance, using fMRI (functional magnetic resonance
imaging), Soon et al. (2008) reported that ‘free’ choices of test persons (namely the
choice to press a left or a right button) could be predicted up to 10 s (!) before the test
person consciously made the decision to pick one or the other button. The authors
state: “We found that the outcome of a decision can be encoded in brain activity
of prefrontal and parietal cortex up to 10 s before it enters awareness. This delay
presumably reflects the operation of a network of high-level control areas that begin
to prepare an upcoming decision long before it enters awareness” (Soon et al., 2008).
Such neuroscientific findings corroborate our theoretical views in a few respects,
and can be interpreted with our model. First of all, the verdict that absolute free
will does not exist seems to resonate with the experimental detection of unconscious processes that would generate conscious decisions. If everything is ultimately
physical, then high-level neurobiological processes, e.g. associated with conscious
decisions, must emerge from physical constituents and laws; and this emergence
seems not only a logical-epistemological truth, but an almost tangible neurobiological process unfolding over time-scales of the order of a second in the brain. (One
could also see here, it seems, an illustration of how causation on the physical level,
going from basic components, say atoms, to more complex ones, say crystals,12 is
paralleled by causation on the neurobiological level, which also goes from more
basic elements, say individual neurons, to more complex neuronal superstructures,
say those involved in consciousness.) At any rate, if everything is physical, consciousness must be correlated with, caused by, preceding unconscious mechanisms; and
this may well be what one observes in the experiments. Now, even if there is no
absolute free will, the CMT account stipulates that in the experiments by Libet and
Soon et al. the decision events13 are nevertheless free in the relative sense, since these
decisions are unconstrained and conscious, by construction of the experiments. At
the same time, these choices / decisions are clearly of a rather banal type, involving
utterly minimalistic hypotheses (such as: ‘at some point I am supposed to press a

12 The laws describing elementary systems are usually believed to explain, or to ‘generate’, the

laws describing more complex systems. In short, the laws of the component systems ‘cause’ the
laws of the composed systems. It seems that what is said here in a metaphorical way can well be
made precise using the notion of cause discussed in Chapter 5. The first step is to acknowledge that
a more precise statement is: the properties X of the elementary constituents of matter are described
by laws etc. To these properties or variables X one can then apply the formalism developed in the
chapter on causation.
13 Recall that the CMT-model allows one to test whether individual acts, or events, are made of free
will.

7.7 Proposal of a Few Interdisciplinary Lines of Research, Mainly About …

217

button’ and ‘this object here is a button’). It might be interesting to perform experiments in which decisions involve more elaborate cognitive ingredients. In sum, on
the CMT model, the test person in Libet’s and similar experiments is free at the
moment of conscious choice, even if this conscious and free act is determined by
previous causes—as the neurobiological data might suggest.
In artificial intelligence (AI) research, a much debated question is: can future
computers and robots simulate or emulate consciousness (and/or free will)? If
possible at all, the CMT model suggests that one of the key properties a computer /
AI should have to emulate consciousness is the capacity to ‘use’ higher-order theories—and this includes the capacity to adequately apply theories to (all) real-world
situations and to act accordingly. Some will conclude that we are very far from
this possibility (but see Vervoort et al., 2023 for ways to monitor progress, via a
test for theory-based, or hypothetical-deductive, reasoning in AI). This suggests the
following line of research: can machines learn to acquire and use theories*, and
which types and how? Vervoort et al. (2021) present first ideas, and the outline of an
interdisciplinary research program. Interestingly, recently cognitive scientists have
also come to the conclusion that mastering theories is a key goal for AI. In the words
of Lake et al., (2017, abstract):
We review progress in cognitive science suggesting that truly human-like learning and
thinking machines will have to reach beyond current engineering trends in both what they
learn and how they learn it. Specifically, we argue that these machines should (1) build
causal models of the world that support explanation and understanding, rather than merely
solving pattern recognition problems; (2) ground learning in intuitive theories of physics and
psychology to support and enrich the knowledge that is learned; and (3) harness compositionality and learning-to-learn to rapidly acquire and generalize knowledge to new tasks and
situations.

And Lake et al., (2017, p. 2):
The alternative approach [alternative to pattern recognition] treats models of the world as
primary, where learning is the process of model-building. Cognition is about using these
models to understand the world, to explain what we see, to imagine what could have happened
that didn’t, or what could be true that isn’t, and then planning actions to make it so. The
difference between pattern recognition and model-building, between prediction and explanation, is central to our view of human intelligence. Just as scientists seek to explain nature,
not simply predict it, we see human thought as fundamentally a model-building activity.

These ideas obviously align with ideas developed in this book. Actually, a few teams
of physicists have started exploring whether artificial neural nets can abstract basic
physical theories from data, and use these theories for prediction (two examples are
discussed in (Vervoort et al., 2021)). However, the regularities that these deep learning
models can detect and extract from data are not really ‘theories’ in the strict sense,
but rather specific laws, say Newton’s law of gravity, as applied to certain numerical
domains. This is still very far from abstracting and mastering Newton’s full theory of
mechanics—a huge step in generality and predictive power. But if in the future AIs
can be built that master theories of physics, psychology, sociology, ethics, axiology,14
14 Robots and AI in general might well be taught a ‘theory of their values and goals’: know what

their objectives are, know the value-hierarchy; they might also have the practical/common-sense

218

7 On Relative Free Will. The CMT Model

etc., then I do believe that such AIs would possess rational consciousness (Vervoort
et al., 2021), in accordance with DEF-CMT. Of course, sentience, or feeling/emotionbased consciousness, is something else: AI does not feel, even if some types of AI
(chatbots) can already very well simulate, in words, having feelings.

7.8 Conclusion of Chapters 6 and 7
In these last two chapters I developed a bi-perspectival account of free will, one
that starts from recognising that free will can have different meanings, or, more
precisely: that free will can be understood from two perspectives, an absolute one
(that includes all our best theories, including physics and its interpretation) and
a relative, socio-humanitarian one (that we can grasp incomparably much better).
Within each of these perspectives, free will has a different meaning; I proposed the
definition of free will that is best compatible with each perspective. In Chapter 6, I
considered the absolute perspective, and arrived at an astonishing conclusion, one
that is known since antiquity but that continues to stupefy and disconcert many
people, including philosophers and scientists. This conclusion is that genuine free
will, as the capacity to really have options of choice, is an illusion. I argued that this
conclusion follows from the most solid theories we have, so should be considered as
part of the maximally coherent network of ideas, the MCT. In other words, for this
extraordinarily counterintuitive conclusion to be wrong, there must be something
wrong in some of our most basic, most fundamental principles of science or logic—
and this is much more unlikely than that our folk views on free will are incorrect.
In Chapter 7 I developed a definition of the type of free will (or, more generally,
the human capacity or condition) that is necessary for attributing agency and moral
responsibility to a human—the type of free will that people usually consider relevant
in philosophy, in our social and psychological attitudes, in legal systems, in daily
life.
My relative account, heavily drawing on well-known analytic models and on
the work by Mahner and Bunge (1997), stipulates that a free act is unconstrained,
and consciously monitored through sets of hypotheses—called theories*. It thus
highlights the rational aspect of free will. The model is compatible with the fact
that free will comes in degrees, a hypothesis that is natural within a biophilosophical
approach. Note that my model is independent of whether the universe is deterministic
or indeterministic. To fix ideas, let us look a last time at a concrete example. According
to the bi-perspectival account the following three facts can be true at the same time:

knowledge/theories to implement these. The actions and in particular the goals of such advanced AI
hopefully will be under benevolent human supervision. But there is a risk that this will not be the
case (Bostrom, 2014). Note also that having a theory of what one knows (and is) amounts, on our
view, to (rational) self-consciousness. I do not see principled reasons why AI could not be taught
to master such a set of theories.

7.8 Conclusion of Chapters 6 and 7

219

(i) a murder may be committed by an act of free will (in that the act was unconstrained and accompanied by a more or less conscious reflection based on some
beliefs, assumptions etc.);
(ii) but the free will of the murderer is likely to be of a corrupted type (her moral
theory* is likely not adequate);
(iii) in any case, the murderer had to commit the murder, in the sense that
the murderer’s decision resulted from external events that are, scientifically
speaking, ultimately beyond any agent’s control.
The theory stipulates that claims (i) and (ii) should not be adopted without also
considering (iii), which follows from the absolute perspective; (iii) should seriously
be taken into account—more on this in a moment.
This view can be considered compatibilist, although it is more precise to consider
it bi-perspectival: there are two meaningful conceptions that are complementary.
Which of these complementary perspectives to adopt depends on the context; for
the hardest (notably philosophical) problems both need to be used. I do not really
assume that free will and determinism both exist; I claim that a form of free will,
relative free will, exists at many instances in many people even in a physical universe.
Recall that on the absolute account, free will is not only eliminated by determinism,
but by the much more general and widely accepted thesis of physical reductionism,
or physicalism. Determinism is a more debateable hypothesis than physicalism; the
latter must be accepted within the MCT.
Some philosophers may contend that it is better to reject free will altogether (e.g.
Pereboom, 2014; Waller, 2011), and to not introduce a superfluous notion as relative
free will. I well sympathise with this view, and I am not entirely sure what the best
direction is. But it seems that ultimately both approaches can converge, and that the
choice is only a pragmatic matter.
My ideas lie, of course, in a long philosophical tradition—free will has fascinated
thinkers since antiquity. I will here not attempt a historical overview [a synthetic
account of key ideas of historical thinkers can be found in O’Connor and Franklin
(2022, § 1)]. Among the antique thinkers to whom my view connects best, I have
mentioned several times Aristotle (with connections to Plato, of course, when it
comes to the notion of the quality or adequacy of free will)—I should mention many
more. But if I would have to choose two thinkers to whom I feel particularly close,
I would mention Chrysippus and Spinoza.
I find it remarkable15 that already the Stoics and Epicureans had intuited that
all stuff of the world, including human souls, must obey natural laws. The Stoic
Chrysippus (c. 279–c. 206 BC) is one marvellous exponent of the ancient line of
thinkers who came to recognise that everything that happens lies in a network of
cause-effect relations, put less metaphorically: that everything happens necessarily
15 I find it revealing that ancient philosophers could intuit, based on logical thinking and elementary

(pre-)scientific observations, what physics and neuroscience have confirmed two thousand years
later. On a speculative level, this seems to me evidence that the universe is logical (much remains
to be said here!). It also corroborates the idea that philosophy can work in harmony with science,
and function as proto-science.

220

7 On Relative Free Will. The CMT Model

in a way that is correlated with the past. And yet, Chrysippus thought that our actions
can be ‘up to us’ in a certain sense—here my account is again very close to his in
several respects. Chrysippus thought that choices are up to us when they are codetermined by convictions that make up our personality, and by deliberation based
on the options we imagine. So, our choices are determined, but what matters for moral
responsibility is in what manner they are determined (O’Connor & Franklin, 2022, §
1). I wholeheartedly agree. I will end this book with commenting on Spinoza’s view
on free will in the Epilogue, Chapter 9.
An essential ingredient of my view on free will is that both the absolute and
relative perspective must be used when one wishes to interpret this world (more or
less) adequately, when one wants to deal with society or propose ideas to improve it. I
believe that philosophers should participate actively to this grand effort. I believe that
the common-sense view on free will is not enough; and that, however unfathomable,
the absolute perspective has urgent relevance. I would go as far as contending that
an appropriate view on free will can help us in tackling some of the challenges of
our time, enormous as they are.
Now, this book is dedicated to problems for which it is possible to come to
well-founded and thoroughly argued solutions—say, the problem of defining the
concept of free will. To my regret, I cannot propose here a well-founded theory of
all the psychological, societal, etc. implications of my bi-perspectival view; of how
to deal with the extraordinary idea that we do not really have free will. As several
philosophers have excellently argued before me, I believe the consequences for our
views on moral responsibility, ethics, law, retributive punishment, praise, blame,
desert, reward, happiness, etc. are considerable (to name only a few, Strawson, 1986;
Pereboom, 1997, Ch. 18; 2001; Waller, 2011; Caruso, 2012; Vilhauer, 2012). Even
if I have not worked out a coherent theory—which would take another book or
book series –, I feel it is important to mention at least a few intuitions (often in line
with those of the mentioned philosophers). I will do this in a pointillist manner in
the Epilogue (Chapter 9§2), which is dedicated to conjectures that resonate with
Chapters 1–7 without being fully developed. I hope this exercise will show that
scientific philosophy can provide a basis for addressing practical problems with
potentially immediate importance for society.
