Chapter 8

General Conclusion

Abstract I review a few results of the book to highlight that synthetic philosophy
is theory-based philosophy, in which theory relativism plays a key problem solving
role. The potential for a fruitful use of AI in synthetic problem solving is recalled, in
philosophy, and possibly in other disciplines of the humanities and social sciences.

In this book I have proposed a philosophy that starts from one basic hypothesis or
postulate—obvious to many—, namely that science and a science-compatible philosophy are possible. Of course, scientists and philosophers often accept this hypothesis
as an evident, immediate given; it is only for defusing radical scepticism that one
even needs to state it (and promote it as an explicit postulate). Yet it appears that this
assumption, innocuous though it may seem, has surprisingly weighty implications
and ramifications.
The history of science has shown that science works—on the logical level—by
electing some principles as basic, by constructing theories around these basic principles, and by deriving predictions, answers and solutions from or within these theories,
using, essentially, logic or mathematics.1 History has also shown that there is sometimes a hierarchy between theories (within one problem space): it happens that effective approximate theories (often called models) are derived from more fundamental
theories; or, the other way around, that several approximate theories are synthesised
into one more fundamental theory. In a given epoch, the most fundamental theories
are not derived from theories at an even higher level of abstraction; it is not guaranteed
that they ever will be. One of the most concrete and quasi-quantifiable characteristics
of such fundamental theories is that they allow one to answer in their problem space
a maximal number of questions in a coherent manner. Having made from the start
the assumption that a science-compatible philosophy is possible, this state of affairs
in science suggests that also in philosophy the quest for the MCT, or the maximally
coherent web if one prefers, is the best guarantee for finding solid answers; and that
this MCT can be hunted for by searching for the sub-theories (resorting under it)
that solve the most problems in their sub-spaces; the MCT is then the final synthesis.
1 Sure, on the practical level, on the level of discovery, the basic postulates can be said to be inspired,

or induced, by empirical observation and experimentation – ultimately.
© The Author(s) 2026
L. Vervoort, Problem Solving in Philosophy,
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-17756-8_8

223

224

8 General Conclusion

One other consequence for philosophy, at least for synthetic philosophy, is that some
principles should be accepted as basic, unprovable. That is how science and scientific
philosophy work.
The problem of induction in science, studied in Chapter 2, can be phrased thus,
in two more or less equivalent ways:
(i) There is no proof, no justification for (the universal, stable-in-time character of)
laws (allowing us to use them for prediction), or
(ii) There is no proof, no justification for the fact that any given theory of natural
science can work, i.e. can have predictive (and explanatory) power.
The principle of induction can be phrased thus:
(i) There are laws of nature (with a universal, stable-in-time character), or
(ii) Science can work (by inducing laws from observation).
Hence, the problem of induction is obviously immediately solved or dissolved if one
accepts the principle of induction as a basic principle of philosophy of science: one
of these most fundamental principles that cannot be proven, i.e. derived from a more
general theory. Accordingly, following the other basic tenet of synthetic philosophy
just recalled, namely that scientific and philosophical validity of principles and theories is measured by their problem-solving power, adopting the principle of induction
solves manifestly more problems and questions than rejecting it.2 I therefore believe
it is part of the MCT, and elected it as a first principle of synthetic philosophy.
There indeed seem to be good reasons to adopt the principle as basic, in the sense
of maximally general, not derivable from other principles. The principle of induction
is not a law of science proper, so should be one of meta-science. And, to the best of my
knowledge, in the works of philosophers there is no hint of an idea that could point
to a more general philosophical principle characterising (the existence of) all laws
of nature than the statement “there are scientific laws”. “There are scientific laws of
nature”, or if one prefers, “laws of nature (or scientific law statements describing laws
of nature) can be induced, generalised, starting from empirical research” has then
a legitimate status of first principle, to use Aristotle’s term. From this perspective
the claim that there is no justification of the existence of scientific laws and their
universal, stable character, is irrational—even when acknowledging that these law
statements are defeasible.
Thus, the two intimately related concepts of law and theory, as they are well understood in science and philosophy of science, stand at the core of synthetic philosophy.
Is it then a surprise that the notion of theory, or of coherent set of hypotheses (‘T*’),
or, slightly more generally and metaphorically, of perspective or representation, plays
such a crucial role in this book? The idea that all concepts and problems (of science
and philosophy) receive their meaning within a perspective, more precisely a theory,
appears to have a remarkable problem-solving capacity in philosophy—a claim that
2 Examples of problems/questions that can coherently be solved/answered: the problem of induc-

tion; the question why different experimenters can observe the same numerical results when doing
the same experiments; all particular instances of the general question: “Why does this scientific
prediction come true?”.

8 General Conclusion

225

is extra remarkable in view of the blatant obviousness this idea has for many of us.
All chapters of this book can be seen as an elaboration or illustration of this idea.
In Chapter 3, on Gettier’s problem, we laid an independent basis for this thesis,
initially coming from a linguistic angle, by arguing that there is a fundamental
representation-relativity in the concepts of truth, knowledge and justification. Such a
principle of epistemological representation-relativity appeared to be the most natural
inference from a solution to Gettier’s problem and other epistemological problems.
Initially, I somewhat restricted this principle to the use and meaning of the expressions ‘to know’, ‘proposition p is true’ and ‘justified’ (to do justice to language habits
and to solve Gettier’s problem). But the great problem-solving capacity of this principle, confirmed in the subsequent chapters, left me no other choice than to adopt it as
the key to disclose the fundamental nature of truth and knowledge and justification.
It says us what truth and knowledge and justification really are. Concomitantly, I
adopted a relativistic account of truth (a mixed coherence—correspondence view), in
which the concept of absolute/objective truth can be recovered as a natural limit case.
This account appeared to resonate deeply with Quine’s holism. It also led to a view
on the essence of knowledge that can pleasingly be condensed in simple phrases,
such as: knowing implies assuming, and the atoms of knowledge are theories.
This is not all. I believe that this principle has amassed so much credentials by now
that it allows us to infer, without much scruples, other conclusions about cognitionrelated matters, for instance about the nature of understanding and explanation (but
I will leave this as a topic for further work). To give one more example: I think
we can now assert rather confidently that truly powerful AI, AGI (artificial general
intelligence), must be AI that masters theory-based reasoning (in all domains of
interest, cf. Vervoort et al., 2023).
Chapter 4, on the interpretation of probability, did not immediately use the notion
of representation-relativity; nevertheless, keeping theory-relativism in mind was
essential here too. If all theses and propositions are theory-relative, then also the
question of what probability is. If the question is asked from the perspective of mathematics, the answer is simple: probability is what satisfies Kolmogorov’s axioms.
But in this book we are interested in the interpretation of probability beyond mathematics. I argued that one cannot do justice to this utterly subtle problem if one does
not acknowledge that probability theory is, above all, a physical theory. It describes
ensembles of real-world systems, satisfying certain conditions (cf. Chapter 4). (And
it does so arguably in the most general, the most abstract way possible, which makes
the link with the concept of cause—recall DEF-P in Chapter 5—and, especially,
with the principle of causality—that other utterly general principle, cf. Chapter 9—
perhaps less surprising. This points to a fascinating topic for further research: unify
probability theory and the principle of causation.) Simply realising that probability
theory can be seen as a physical theory was the source of the solution to most
of its problems—to the least, it inspired these solutions. In sum, theory-relativism
reminded us also in this chapter that it is essential, when starting to study a problem,
to identify the right perspective from which to address it.
Chapter 5, on the notion of cause, illustrated the utility of representation-relativity
in a particularly conspicuous manner. Positing the theory-relativity of causal claims

226

8 General Conclusion

appeared to be the tool for resolving a wide variety of questions and conundrums
documented in the literature. Problems related to context-relativity (Ch. 5, RR1),
early (Ch. 5, RR2) and late (Ch. 5, RR4) preemption, double prevention (Ch. 5,
RR3), overdetermination (Ch. 5, RR5), tampering (Ch. 5, RR6), transitivity (Ch.
5, RR6), token-versus-type causation (Ch. 5, RR7), black-box cases (Ch. 5, RR8)
could all be solved using one and the same assumption of theory-relativity. It seems
to me that if one principle can solve a series of problems that have hitherto been dealt
with in a disconnected manner, its soundness becomes rather inevitable.
One might wonder: is representation-relativity of causal claims really the same
representation-relativity as the one encountered in Chapter 3 on Gettier’s epistemological problem? It should be, as a simple argument shows. The representationrelativity of ‘true’ (and propositional truth) means that ‘p is true’ can only definitely
be evaluated via a certain T*; without such a T* the sentence has no definite meaning.
Now, a causal claim as ‘X is the cause of Y’ is logically equivalent to ‘It is true that
X is the cause of Y’. Since the latter proposition is T*-relative, the causal claim itself
must be. In sum, in principle we could have anticipated such a perspectival aspect of
causal attribution from the start. Actually, I had not made this anticipation, but I rediscovered representation-relativity in the exercise of problem solving. In any case,
it does not come as a surprise; and the results of Chapters 3 and 5 corroborate each
other. Recall that I emphasised in Chapter 5 that representation-relativity characterises causal identification (and psychological and epistemological-logical aspects
of causation), rather than causation as an ontological property of the universe. Within
scientific realism, one better assumes that the causes, identified with an as-adequateas-possible T*, correspond to something real, independent of the human mind; that
reality is not generated by a theorising mind.
In Chapters 6 and 7 on free will representation-relativity played an essential role
too, in many respects. By now, this should be obvious: these chapters deal in the
first place with the definition of a concept; and concepts (of science and philosophy)
find their meaning in conceptual frameworks. I submitted that free will is perhaps
best studied via a complementary, bi-perspectival approach. Indeed, the rejection
of free will that seems an unavoidable conclusion within a physical perspective,
is so unfathomable and harshly counterintuitive that it seems justified to save the
concept within a socio-humanitarian perspective. Without such a concept it may be
difficult to constructively dialogue with researchers in practical philosophy and with
people and society at large, for instance when the notion of personal responsibility
is at stake. This complementary view implies, when thinking practically, a subtle act
of balancing between perspectives, a balancing of which I did not clearly define the
principle (although I suspect it turns around global wellbeing, admittedly a somewhat
vague idea for the time being).
Again, the notion of theory* plays not only a heuristic role, as in the chapters on
the problem of induction and probability, but intervenes also directly in the definition
of free will, as in that of cause. (Relative) free will is, on my account, a capacity that
is essentially cognitive; and therefore mediated through sets of hypotheses, theories*
(in the wide sense)—as any cognition is. In hindsight, the main steps of the argument
are straightforward, and can be summarised thus: the key condition for free will is

8 General Conclusion

227

consciousness; we can only be conscious or aware of X if we have an (even utterly
vague) idea of what X is; i.e. if we know at least something about X (minimally, that
it is there); and knowledge is mediated through theories*. This is the essence of the
CMT account of relative free will.
In closing, I would like to repeat a conclusion already drawn in Chapter 1, namely
that the method promoted here, problem-solving-through-unification, or unificationthrough-problem-solving, has a genuine potential for being enhanced by AI, present
and future. The synthetic method often starts with establishing a cartography of
problems existing in a given problem-space. Most of the relevant problems have
extensively been dealt with in the philosophical literature; and since a few years
Large Language Models (LLMs) have been developed that ‘have read’ large parts, and
perhaps most, of the literature. At any rate, such LLMs can in principle be trained with
the complete philosophical literature, or a great part of it (which opens the interesting
possibility of training dedicated models). These computational tools work, at their
basis, by statistical text completion, implying that they can in principle identify
the problems (in a given space) that are statistically considered most relevant in the
philosophical literature, moreover in a phrasing that is adequate with high probability
(relative to the screened corpus). Hence, they can in any cognitive space discussed
in the literature provide a cartography of problems that has a very high probability
of relevance; an example is given in Appendix 1.1. This AI-generated cartography
can then be a very solid tool for further—provisionally human—synthesis. Such
LLMs can also directly propose solutions to problems. For the time being, these
solutions should not be expected to be synthetic, new, creative ones—solutions that
significantly extend the web of knowledge (to start with, LLMs are not yet really
‘reasoning’, cf. Arkoudas, 2023; Vervoort et al., 2023). But that may well change in
the near future. Therefore two conclusions impose themselves. First, the potential
for constructive interaction between philosophy and AI is huge. And note that it
is tempting to conjecture that the method we proposed for philosophical problem
solving can also be used, mutatis mutandis, in other disciplines of the humanities
and social sciences. This would especially be so if these disciplines are relatively
mature, i.e. theory-based and oriented towards problem solving. Second, very soon
we will need synthetic philosophy to assess the outputs of AI. If we do not have
synthetic theories at our disposal, the intellectual authority might change sides at
some point.
True, for the time being the reader may estimate that the use philosophers can
make of AI is still rather modest. (For sure, I did not showcase spectacular results
obtained thanks to AI.) When commercial LLMs became efficient, this book was
almost finished, so I have not presented here full-scale systematic studies of using
them as an aid for problem solving, let alone studies with statistical relevance. But
I believe that such more systematic work is now possible and that it should be
highly revealing. Such large-scale studies could for instance compare the cartographies given by different LLMs, or compare AI-generated cartographies with humangenerated ones, based on polls among experts. My own not very systematic tests (e.g.
in Chapter 2, Appendix 2.1) have convinced me that such comparisons should, to

228

8 General Conclusion

high likelihood, show a great convergence between models, which is further evidence
that AI-generated cartographies should indeed provide a solid basis for synthesis.
Another conclusion of this book is that synthetic philosophy should well be able
to constructively interact with science, in a bi-directional way. This seems rather
obvious if knowledge is conceived of as a coherent web spanning all cognitive
fields. Surely there is a typical dynamics. In the first stage, philosophy looks at
what mainstream scientific results tell us about a certain problem space, in order
to start grounding philosophical theses. Here are a few obvious examples of such
widely accepted scientific findings relevant for philosophy:
(i) Quantum systems are essentially probabilistic and a-causal;
(ii) Cognition happens in interconnected neural networks in the brain;
(iii) There are few social law statements.
Now, once philosophy has inferred a synthetic theory about the related philosophical
problem-space (say about (i) causation or (ii) consciousness or (iii) the nature of
scientific knowledge and laws), it may then well propose new interdisciplinary ideas,
coherent with the synthetic theory found, ideas that can guide further scientific
research. The coherence requirement of synthetic philosophical theories implies that
their interdisciplinary inferences or conjectures, even if speculative, should have
at least a reasonable chance of scientific relevance. Examples of such conjectures,
backed-up by synthetic philosophy, could be:
(i) Quantum systems might be causal after all; theories that causally complete
quantum mechanics are a worthy topic of investigation.
(ii) Consciousness emerges from a special kind of neuronal interconnection,
involving a hypotheses-generating network;
(iii) There must be many more social laws, after all.
Note that especially claims (i) and (iii) seem, at least at first sight, in tension with
mainstream science. On the other hand, the case can be made that theses (i–iii) have
massive support from a synthetic point of view. So, I submit that the virtuous path
may be: science → philosophy → science. I tried to propose a few examples of
how philosophy can inspire science in this book; a further (perhaps more ambitious)
example, essentially idea (i) above, is considered in the Epilogue, based on what we
learned about the archetypical problem of causation in Chapter 5.
In sum, I believe that a dose of optimism is warranted as to the guiding role
philosophy can play for foundational scientific problems.
A last conclusion from Chapter 1 that I would like to repeat is that in principle
all philosophical branches can be part of the coherent web, and be the object of
synthetic investigation. For such an integration to succeed, these branches should
be made coherent among themselves and with science. To paraphrase and extend
Ladyman and Ross’s verdict (2007, p. 28), synthetic philosophy is the enterprise of
elucidating coherence networks across the sciences and all branches of philosophy.
Indeed, the MCT-postulate, felicitously having a semi-quantitative nature, operates
for all chapters of philosophy—even if it is surely applicable with greater confidence
in disciplines closer to established science. For instance, I see no principled reason

References

229

why a unified ethical theory could not be constructed, starting from the cartography of
problems attached to ethical proto-theories (virtue theories, deontological theories,
utilitarianism, …). I believe that such a construction could proceed, in essence, by
counting solutions provided by candidate master theories, and possibly by using
sciences as neurobiology and neuropsychology to partly justify these solutions, in
general to inform problem-solving. Sure, such an effort seems considerably more
onerous than the investigations done in this book. A few hints of how synthetic
philosophy could be relevant for very practical problems are given in the Epilogue.
Again, the same methodology might well apply to other disciplines of the human
and social sciences.
As a final conclusion, in what seems a new era of artificial intelligence, with its
huge possibilities but also potential risks—such as an uncontrolled multiplication of
fake information—, I believe it is crucial that philosophers maintain an unshaken faith
in the accessibility of truth. Philosophers have a large responsibility in shaping the
global perception about truth-finding (Boghossian, 2006); and fear of knowledge does
not help. Therefore, in the challenging times in which we live, I believe philosophers
have a key role to play in showing how difficult problems can be solved: the planet
needs their contribution. True, things are somewhat subtle: objective truth seems only
asymptotically reachable, relatively, via perspectives; but these can be assessed for
their objective robustness, possibly using AI as an aid. I therefore hope that others
will apply the method of synthetic philosophy to other, or the same, problems of
philosophy, and beyond. A possible start might be to hunt for weaknesses of my
provisional solutions, and to propose a new synthesis.
