Chapter 9

Epilogue: Lines of Further Research

Abstract In this chapter I extract more speculative claims from the preceding chapters. The aim of this exercise is to show that synthetic philosophy, even if dealing
in the first place with theoretical issues, inevitably interfaces with problems of practical importance (Sect. 2), as well as with scientific ones (Sect. 1). Section 1 aims at
rehabilitating the principle of causation and at showing that it offers a most synthetic
solution to Bell’s theorem. Section 2 dips a toe in the intricate and intriguing universe
of questions related to human agency in a causal world. The theses in this chapter are
not derived with the same rigour as most of the preceding ones; they are therefore
not necessarily part of the MCT. Yet, since they are based on synthetic results, I
believe they are, to the least, interesting topics for further research. Most arguments
are presented in a succinct manner.

9.1 On the Principle of Causation
In Chapter 5 on cause, we started by noting that many key philosophical questions
hinge on the concept of cause. The question that immediately comes to mind is:
are all events caused, do all events have causes—or are some events spontaneous,
uncaused, a-causal, undetermined? In other words, is the principle of causation true?
In the wake of the shatteringly negative verdicts of giants of philosophy and physics
as Hume, Mach, Russell, Bohr, Heisenberg, Pauli and many more, few contemporary
philosophers seem keen on defending a principle that once was a queen of natural
philosophy.
Yet it seems, if we are right that a unified concept of cause exists, that the queen
is happily back in the race of fundamental principles. (I ask the reader to measure
for a moment the dramatic touch in this revival race. In several communities, say
in quantum physics and in many important schools of philosophy of science, the
queen is deemed dead and utterly uncongenial to boot.) Now, the existence of a
synthetic definition amounts to a genuine, be it not conclusive, argument in favour
of (1) the ‘reality’ of causes—more precisely, of the idea that causes correspond
to something real—and, especially, (2) the truth of the principle of causation—the

© The Author(s) 2026
L. Vervoort, Problem Solving in Philosophy,
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-17756-8_9

231

232

9 Epilogue: Lines of Further Research

idea that the universe operates according to laws of cause and effect. Hypothesis (1)
is trivial within our realist background assumptions. Realists assume that coherent
theories, for instance about the notion of cause, have the best chances to correspond
to something real. But (2) has become very unpopular it seems.
Let me define the principle of causation/causality as follows, as is often done: “All
events have causes in accordance with necessary laws of nature”; or “Every effect is
produced through lawful necessity by a cause” (Norton, 2003; other versions exist,
cf. e.g. Norton, 2003; Bunge, 2009). With the help of the modified counterfactual
definition of cause of Chapter 5 (DEF-CF), it is clear what this principle means, also
the ‘necessity’ invoked: namely, that anything occurring in our universe is caused
by other events, following the laws of nature; and that these other events, the causes,
are necessary in the most basic logical sense that if they would not occur, the effects
would not occur (in the same way). (We added that the optimal causal identification
is done by the optimal theory giving us the laws, or rather, the provisionally optimal
law statements. More generally, causal identification is representation-relative, even
if causation is not.)
My argument in Chapter 5 for the reality of the causal principle was straightforward enough. If we make the most likely assumptions, namely (i) that everything
happens according to the laws of nature (the laws of physics, which are either deterministic or probabilistic), and (ii) that a synthetic account of cause, based on DEF-CF
(DEF-F and DEF-P), shows that the laws of nature inherently embody cause-effect
relations, it immediately follows that everything happens according to laws of cause
and effect. As argued at the end of Chapter 5, these laws, both deterministic and
probabilistic, can well be understood as having a character of necessity. For this and
other reasons explained in the following, I believe that it is high time to rehabilitate
the causal principle; that it says something essential about things in themselves.
Clearly, my view is in opposition with old but vivacious and very respected schools
of empiricist, positivist and other philosophers, Hume, Kant, Mach, Russell, Nagel,
van Fraassen, Cartwright, Norton, Ladyman and Ross, to name a few. In a highly
influential and often-quoted passage, Russell asserted (1912, p. 1): “All philosophers,
of every school, imagine that causation is one of the fundamental axioms or postulates
of science, yet, oddly enough, in advanced sciences such as gravitational astronomy,
the word ‘cause’ never occurs. […] The law of causality, I believe, like much that
passes muster among philosophers, is a relic of a bygone age, surviving like the
monarchy, only because it is erroneously supposed to do no harm.” In a modern
version, Norton states: “What I do deny is that the task of science is to find the
particular expressions of some fundamental causal principle in the domain of each
of the sciences” (Norton, 2003, p. 1).
But I believe that Russell and Norton make the same category mistake. It suffices
to note that the principle of causality is a principle of metascience, of philosophy,
not immediately of science. I believe this remark defuses much of their arguments.
Sure, the laws of nature, or their statements, are the building blocks of science, not
the causal principle; but it does belong to philosophy to characterise these laws,
and to analyse which properties they all share. And in this respect, the principle
of causality is utterly instrumental and seems universal—as countless philosophers

9.1 On the Principle of Causation

233

have intuited since antiquity. Put differently, the task of the sciences is to find the
laws and regularities of their respective disciplines. These laws describe reality on
the most fundamental level.1 But, 1) these laws allow us to identify causes in a unified
manner (recall from Chapter 5 how causes can be deduced from, notably, the laws
of physics); and, especially, 2) it is the task of philosophy to recognise what the
lawful ontology expressed by these laws amounts to. Notably: the necessitarianism,
expressed in the causal principle, that these laws entail and the consequences of such
a counterintuitive but eminently relevant worldview (recall Chapters 6 and 7 on free
will). In sum, if one rejects the causal principle as a metaprinciple, one is at high risk
of rejecting essential philosophical theses and theories.
This being said, it seems that authors as Russell, Norton and others underestimate
the importance of the notion of cause even in physics proper. I have already mentioned
the use of cause in Bell’s theorem in Chapter 5§3, and I will return to it below. Next, in
relativity theory it is accepted that effects can only be influenced by causes in their past
light cones, and that causes cannot have effects outside their forward light cones. The
notions of cause and effect are used in a pre-theoretic way, but they comply with the
definition proposed in Chapter 5 (which is nothing else than a synthetic reformulation
of intuition). In some more detail, in relativity theory it is of fundamental importance
that causes happen before their effects; if not, logical paradoxes as the grandfather
paradox would follow; and such logical paradoxes are considered impossible (this
result is part of any book on relativity theory). Interestingly, the logical verdict is
(I believe righteously2 ) accepted as the ultimate ground for judging about physical
matters. That causes do not happen after their effects (for all frames of reference)
is a consequence of the assumption that causal influences do not travel faster than
light. In this sense, causation/causality3 plays an essential role in relativity theory
in discarding faster-than-light influences; the non-existence of such superluminal
causal influences has solid empirical support (e.g. Mattingly, 2005). In sum, physics
does give a prominent place to the notion of cause, after all. I believe philosophy
should do so a fortiori.
1 It is not really necessary here to make a distinction between the laws of fundamental physics

(relativity theory, quantum mechanics and the yet elusive theory unifying them), the laws of ‘less
fundamental’ physics and the laws of other sciences. Indeed, as soon as these laws are backed-up
by theories, my definition of cause allows identifying causes derived from these laws. This is a
pleasant consequence of unification.
2 It seems that logic can be seen not only as a theory of correct reasoning, but also as the most
fundamental theory of physics, or, rather, of ‘what happens in the universe’. Reasoning (as applied
to matters in the real world) can be called ‘correct’ if there is an appropriate link with reality,
after all. Furthermore, logical consistency is shared by all our bona fide theories, be it physical or
mathematical. These ideas resonate with (Russell, (1912/2001, Ch. 8).
3 More precisely, ‘relativistic causality’ as it is usually called is the hypothesis that observed
preferred orders of physical processes (say, first the throwing, then the shattering) are observerindependent (for inertial observers)—so are not altered by transformation between inertial frames.
This hypothesis is withheld in general relativity, and leads to the rejection of superluminal
signalling. I recommend as further reading the analysis of L. Motle (of 25.01.2011) in the following
Stack Exchange discussion: https://physics.stackexchange.com/questions/3871/is-causality-a-for
malised-concept-in-physics (accessed 10.08.2024).

234

9 Epilogue: Lines of Further Research

The ambitious and interesting analysis of causation by Ladyman and Ross (2007,
Chapter 5) leads them to a view that comes close to Norton’s, as they acknowledge themselves (2007, p. 272). Interestingly, they make a distinction between
fundamental physics and special sciences, which include non-fundamental physics.
(Ladyman and Ross take ‘serious candidates for quantum gravity’ to constitute fundamental physics. Since these theories aim at unifying general relativity and quantum
(field) physics, but are not yet constructed in any final form, I include quantum
and relativity theory in the fundamental theories—for definiteness, and as is usually
done.) Referring, notably, to work by Earman and Roberts (1999), Ladyman and
Ross state:
However, we think that Earman and Roberts are correct in their claim that fundamental
physics discovers something of a kind that special sciences don’t; and we call this kind
of something a universal real pattern. We argued in the preceding sections of this chapter
for Russell’s and Redhead’s thesis that these real patterns are not causal. They are instead
structural, in the sense articulated in Chapters 2 and 3. In light of our agreement with Kincaid
about what special sciences do, this means there are, according to us, two respects in which
the achievements of fundamental physics differ from the achievements of special sciences:
the former are exceptionless, and they do not consist in the identification of causal factors
(2007, p. 283).

And a bit further:
Saying that special sciences furnish causal explanations, while fundamental physics aims
at discovering universal laws [as Earman and Roberts (1999) do], comes very close to the
view we articulated in 5.4. Special sciences, according to us, use causal ideas as heuristics
for locating real patterns. Fundamental physics is in the business of describing the structural
properties of the whole universe. These properties are not causal relations. Special sciences
and fundamental physics are thus mainly different from each other in a way we find it very
natural to express by saying: fundamental physics aims at laws, whereas special sciences
identify causal factors (2007, p. 286).

(Note, first, that one can of course make an immediate link between our scientific
realism equipped with theory relativism and Ladyman and Ross’s ‘ontic structural
realism’: our best theories reveal the laws of nature, and therefore ‘structure’ in
this obvious sense.) Pace Ladyman and Ross, the account of causation provided in
Chapter 5 shows that one does not need to distinguish between fundamental and
non-fundamental science, and even everyday discourse, when it comes to analysing
causation. These all lie in an epistemic continuum, as do the laws and regularities on
which they are based. A unified approach is possible thanks to the notion of theoryrelativity: these various cognitive contexts come with their theories*, and it is these
theories* that dictate what the causes are according to DEF-CF. Of course, in the
sciences these theories are based on laws, fundamental and less fundamental (a qualification that strongly depends on the historical epoch, for that matter); so it is these
laws that will rule the causal attribution. The key point is this: the recipe immanent
in DEF-CF works well also for laws presently considered as fundamental—take for
instance Einstein’s field equations of general relativity. These equations relate the
local spacetime curvature (described by the Einstein tensor) with the local distribution
of matter and energy (described by the stress–energy tensor); they can be written as a

9.1 On the Principle of Causation

235

set of nonlinear partial differential equations, i.e. deterministic equations. Although
exact solutions to these equations can only be found under simplifying assumptions,
they show that any relativistic variable X occurring in the equations is a deterministic function of other properties Y. Hence DEF-F or DEF-CF show that the mutual
co-variation that is the essential criterion of causation is well present.
True, Ladyman and Ross make an interesting point, when they remind us that
in fundamental laws the time-asymmetry typical for (mundane) causation is absent
(2007, §5.4). They refer to theories for quantum gravity as String Theory or Loop
Quantum Gravity. These theories are, however, unfinished, which makes the discussion somewhat undefined; but we can equally well refer to relativity theory, quantum
mechanics or electromagnetism. To go back to the above example of general relativity, the dependence between relativistic variables X and Y is perhaps most generally interpreted as a mutual influence, so mutual causation, as captured by Wheeler’s
slogan “Spacetime tells matter how to move; matter tells spacetime how to curve”
(recall the discussion in Chapter 5§3). To be clear, the potential worry against my
account raised by Ladyman and Ross’s interpretation is this: DEF-F and DEF-CF
might not be applicable to general relativity and other fundamental theories in that a
classic time-clause (“causes come before their effects”) may not be trivial to apply
here. What comes first, spacetime curvature or matter-energy?
Now, as already concluded in Chapter 5§3, if the time-clause only stipulates that
effects should not come before their causes, the definitions allow for simultaneous
mutual causation—which solves the problem. And more importantly, to solve the
equations in practice, one needs defined initial conditions, and these do impose a
temporal asymmetry (which is not deducible from the laws/equations themselves).
For instance, given an initial distribution of matter and energy, as well as the initial/
boundary conditions, the field equations determine the future evolution of the gravitational field and the motion of objects within that field. So it is natural, at least
possible, to conclude that the given energy and matter distribution causes the evolution of the field and the motion of the objects under study. It is also natural to say
that “the collision of black holes causes gravitational waves”, etc.
Again, the time-clause surely is not the essential ingredient of causation (so many
things occur before so many other things without being causes, after all). Finally,
let me repeat that the notion of causality is important in general relativity too: the
principle of ‘relativistic causality’, mentioned above in the context of special relativity, remains valid in general relativity and ensures that superluminal influences are
excluded—as observed.
I believe we can conclude from this excursion that the time-clause does more
work in pragmatic contexts, or in folk applications of the notion of cause, when
the cause-inquirer is interested in solving practical problems or asks agency-related
questions—such as the eternal: “Who or what did this?” In fundamental physics
one wishes to identify (discover, derive) the pertinent equations and to apply and
solve them; one is usually not obsessed by the practical question “what causes this?”,
neither by the philosophical question “is there a cause?”. But, once again, that should
not withhold us from doing philosophy and note the connection of necessity between
properties, things, phenomena described by these laws, whether fundamental or less

236

9 Epilogue: Lines of Further Research

fundamental. That is, I believe, the essential message contained in the principle of
causality.
Let me emphasise that the causal principle is not the same as the principle of determinism, claiming that everything is caused in a deterministic manner (for reviews,
see Earman, 1986; Hoefer, 2024). Philosophers of physics widely agree that physical
systems and events are either deterministic or probabilistic; but it is undecided and a
matter of debate whether physical events, systems, and variables ultimately all belong
to one of these two categories. Among physicists there is perhaps still a lingering
‘orthodox’ position stating that the ultimate building blocks of reality are probabilistic
(because quantum); but, as said, things are much subtler upon deeper scrutiny (e.g.
Earman, 1986; Nikolaev & Vervoort, 2023; Vervoort, 2013, 2019; Wuethrich, 2011).
Now, importantly, the above-mentioned principle of causation very well applies also
to probabilistic systems. Laws of nature can be deterministic or probabilistic; and I
have argued that the notion of probabilistic cause can surely be made solid. Thus, the
principle can be made more general in this way: “All events have causes in accordance
with necessary laws of nature, whether these laws are deterministic or probabilistic”. As argued in Chapter 5, there is necessity in probabilistic laws of nature too: in
probabilistic systems it is collections of sufficiently similar systems that necessarily
have to obey the laws of probability theory (cf. Chapter 4). Concomitantly, series
of stochastic events (generated by a ρ-system) cannot be fundamentally erratic or
wildly indeterministic: they too have to show frequency stabilisation, which is an
iron law just as scientifically testable as any deterministic law of physics—even if the
probabilities extracted from the frequency measurements come with an unavoidable
uncertainty, as almost any experimental property. From probabilistic laws, say the
laws of quantum mechanics, one can extract causal relations using DEF-P or its ilk,
P(Y ), and (ii) there is no earlier
in essence: X is a cause of Y iff {(i) P(Y |X )
‘off-screener’} (see an illustration below). Here too one sees the necessity: the way
Y and X occur must follow regularities. X is necessary for Y in this sense: if X would
occur differently, i.e. with a different probability distribution, Y would, in general,
occur differently (that is the meaning of (i)). And accordingly, many and perhaps all
stochastic systems studied in physics are characterised by ensemble-equations that
are perfectly deterministic: think of the equations of Schrödinger, Fokker-Planck,
Liouville, etc.
Of course, in a spirit of synthesis, one immediately wonders whether these
two kinds of necessity, respectively characterising deterministic and probabilistic
systems, are not one and the same thing. Sure, we now enter into speculative territory, but this necessity in nature seems, to me, to be an utterly fundamental property:
it seems to make probability theory and logic possible (as disciplines that describe
reality). It seems to be a prerequisite not only of the universal nature of the laws
of physics but also of the applicability ‘to reality’ of the theories of probability and
logic. I have argued elsewhere that probability theory is, in a sense, the most fundamental physical theory we have (Nikolaev & Vervoort, 2023; Vervoort, 2019). If
this feature of necessity is so fundamental, it is likely a unifying principle. It is then
tempting to assume that probabilistic systems are ultimately deterministic ‘under
the surface’—in deterministic systems the necessity has its simplest expression. The

9.1 On the Principle of Causation

237

necessary, rule-based nature of the behaviour of collections of ρ-systems would then
derive from the deterministic necessity in the individual components; this would
seem to be the philosophically most pleasing conclusion, complying with Occam’s
razor—there is only one category of events and systems, namely deterministic ones.
But this matter may well be undecidable; physical science seems not to give enough
guidance. At any rate, it seems that the more science-compatible question is whether
nature complies with the general principle of causality, comprising deterministic
and probabilistic causation, rather than the question whether nature is ultimately
deterministic or irreducibly probabilistic.
Kant famously saw in the principle of causality a mere tool for organising our
experience; a product of the synthetic faculty of imagination, rendering experience
possible.4 In his steps many philosophers considered and still consider the principle
at best as a heuristic maxim, a (more or less unavoidable) rule to guide scientific
research (“search for the causes, in order to find the laws”). E. Nagel for instance
claims that the principle is a “maxim for inquiry rather than a statement with definite
empirical content” (1961, p. 320). But again, within scientific realism one quickly
realises that such an interpretation does not seem to go to the core of things. Yes, the
principle is also a heuristic rule of great validity, but for a good reason: because it
corresponds to something real. If not, why would it be of such heuristic value, why
would it organise our perceptions so well? I believe it is time to recognise the failure
of philosophical theories that do not adequately answer this question, and the many
questions related to it.
Surely the most cogent attack against the idea that everything has causes, comes
from the interpretation of quantum mechanics, especially the interpretation that is
often called the ‘orthodox’ or ‘Copenhagen’ interpretation, originally due to Niels
Bohr and Werner Heisenberg. Famously, both these fathers of quantum mechanics
were eager to deny that causation plays a role in quantum theory (e.g. Jammer, 1974).
I will give here a very condensed review of my position on this matter, and I ask the
reader to remember that I stroll here far outside the scope of this book. In the next
paragraphs my goal is to show (to readers interested in the philosophy of physics)
that the philosophical results of the previous chapters have an intimate link with
topical research in physics, and suggest new interdisciplinary research projects.
For starters, two short remarks. First, few experts in the foundations and philosophy of quantum mechanics still believe that the Copenhagen interpretation has
prevalence over other interpretations; the weaknesses of this interpretation have now
been studied in detail (cf. e.g. Becker, 2018). And second, there is an interpretation, or
rather an equivalent re-formulation of quantum theory, namely the de Broglie-Bohm
theory, which explicitly assigns causes to quantum events (Bacciagaluppi & Valentini, 2009; Holland, 1995). This theory is sometimes called the causal interpretation
of quantum mechanics.

4 It is noteworthy that Kant dedicates a remarkable number of pages of the Critique of Pure Reason—

I estimate more than a hundred—to his claim stating that although causality is not in the real
(noumenal) world, we necessarily reason as if it were. This idea hurts the realists’ feelings.

238

9 Epilogue: Lines of Further Research

Now a little more substance. The debate of whether (hidden) causes exist for
quantum events is equivalent to the ‘hidden-variables’ question—i.e. the question whether (yet unknown) physical properties could exist that explain quantum
theory. The hidden-variables question is most clearly analysed in Bell’s theorem (cf.
Chapter 5§3). The meaning of this theorem can be summarised thus, according to the
standard view: only nonlocal hidden variables could exist (and reproduce quantum
mechanics). But nonlocal causes violate Lorentz-invariance and are thus in tension
with relativity theory, as explicitly emphasised by John Bell5 ; they are therefore
considered unlikely by many. (The de Broglie–Bohm theory is considered almost
always as nonlocal, in agreement with the standard interpretation of Bell’s theorem:
the causes it assigns to quantum events involve nonlocal interaction. And indeed,
although this is a topic of ongoing research, there are difficulties to make the de
Broglie-Bohm theory relativistic. Yet, I am not sure whether this strongly nonlocal
nature is the correct conclusion, or rather an overhasty interpretation of the rather
unintuitive mathematics, as briefly explained below.)
Now, the interpretation of Bell’s theorem I just gave is the standard one. But it
is highly debated (e.g. Myrvold et al., 2024; Vervoort, 2013). I admit that I suspect
that this standard interpretation is erroneous (unless one understands ‘locality’ in a
different way than is usually done)—ultimately always for synthetic reasons. In order
to come to the standard view that “only nonlocal hidden variables could exist”, one
has to make an extra assumption that is usually left implicit because considered trivial,
an assumption often called ‘free will’ (!). In technical terms this is the assumption that
there is no correlation between the hidden causes (called λ) of the quantum properties
and the detector settings (say α) in experiments. I will call this the ‘no-correlation’
argument or hypothesis. Bell and many people have claimed that assuming that there
would be correlations (due to common causes6 ) between λ and α, would violate
the assumption of free will. How could there be common causes between some
particle property λ and detector setting α, if the value of α can be freely chosen by
an experimenter? That seems to amount to a cosmic conspiracy in the eyes of many.
But is this argument based on free will sound? I believe that, to counter it, we
can invoke exactly the same reasoning we used to invalidate absolute free will in
Chapter 6. If everything is ultimately material, physical, then also the choice α in
the head of some experimenter is part of the physical. The neuronal processes that
embody the choice cannot pop out of nothing, and must in fine be correlated with
what happened at the Big Bang, according to an identical argument as we made
in Chapter 6. The same must be the case for the properties λ. Both α and λ must
5 Indeed, this is how Bell concludes his seminal paper of 1964: “In a theory in which parameters are

added to quantum mechanics to determine the results of individual measurements, without changing
the statistical predictions, there must be a mechanism whereby the setting of one measuring device
can influence the reading of another instrument, however remote. Moreover, the signal involved
must propagate instantaneously, so that such a theory could not be Lorentz invariant” (Bell, 1964).
6 I do not consider the possibility of retro-causation (and other more exotic possibilities), as in the
rest of this book. Indeed, if our conclusions in Chapter 5 are correct, the philosophical problems
connected with causation can be solved while assuming good old forward causation. Retro-causation
is defended for instance in Price (1996).

9.1 On the Principle of Causation

239

therefore be caused by, correlated with, somehow connected with, the variables that
describe the Big Bang (in full agreement with our concept of cause, DEF-CF or
DEF-P). Ergo, there is a correlation, a connection, between λ and α, after all. This
ab-initio correlation can, of course, not be calculated (nor measured). We do not have
a Theory-of-Absolutely-Everything at hand, but it is still natural to assume that such
a theory exists in principle. Then it is also only natural to assume that the events
happening at the Big Bang (or, if one prefers, the properties of the elusive ultimate
theory describing it) are the common causes of λ and α (this idea is elaborated in
somewhat more detail in Nikolaev & Vervoort, 2023; Papatryfonos & Vervoort, 2025,
Sect. 4). Note that it is good practice in physics to assume the existence of properties
even if we do not have access to them. That is how statistical mechanics and statistical
thermodynamics can be constructed: these theories assume the existence of particles
having positions and velocities, even if we do not know them.
(At this point, let me risk the following side-remark: the solution I propose here
seems straightforward to me. To the point that I sometimes suspect that people
have overlooked it for psychological reasons. There is a tenacious mistrust in the
physics community of anything that is non-calculable, of anything that looks more
philosophical than physical. Indeed, the assumption that everything is correlated with
what happened at the Big Bang is not strictly part of physics; one cannot calculate
these correlations; it is philosophical, if one wishes. And yet, it is an utterly natural
hypothesis, explicitly or implicitly made by scientists and philosophers all the time.
So, to me, there is a puzzling element in this debate. At the same time, I am well
aware this matter is unusually subtle; so a dose of agnosticism seems advisable.)
Now, if one rejects the free-will or no-correlation assumption one rejects the
standard interpretation of Bell’s theorem: then there can be causes λ of quantum
properties after all, not only nonlocal ones. The causal principle can then well be
upheld, Q. E. D. One can also say: the causal principle gets further corroboration
by offering a solution also to this problem of the interpretation of Bell’s theorem.
The interpretation that I have briefly sketched here is often called ‘superdeterminism’
(because it posits that freely-chosen variables have causes retracing to the Big Bang);
but it is actually simply a (consequence of a) modern variant of the causal principle.
Note also that I do not need the assumption that the λ are deterministic variables;
they may be probabilistic. Therefore I prefer the term ‘supercorrelation’ as coined
in (Vervoort, 2013) or ‘contextuality’ (cf. Papatryfonos & Vervoort, 2025, Sect. 4).
In sum, within my interpretation, Bell’s theorem is a consequence of physical reductionism, or, equivalently, of the principle of causality, and expresses the
universal connectedness of things, rather than the nonlocality of quantum mechanics
and nature. What is mind-boggling about the theorem is then that it allows us to detect
this ab-initio connectedness of (almost) everything. (The idea that the principle of
causality implies universal connectedness, i.e. universal dependence, is not new and
can for instance be found in Mach’s writings,7 see a condensed history in Bunge
7 Mach wrote (around 1883): “The business of physical science is the reconstruction of facts in

thought, or the abstract quantitative expression of facts. The rules which we form for these reconstructions are the laws of nature. In the conviction that such rules are possible lies the law of

240

9 Epilogue: Lines of Further Research

(2009), pp. 252–253.) It is important to note that this interpretation does not rely on
nonlocality. Relying on the causal principle has the great advantage of doing away
with this cumbersome notion, which is in immediate tension with relativity theory.8
Many people have come to believe that Bell’s theorem and the experimental results
show that quantum theory itself (and therefore nature) is nonlocal. This seems a stark
inference of Bell’s theorem, even if we consider its orthodox interpretation, which
only states that local hidden variables are impossible, not that nature is therefore
nonlocal (for a position embracing nonlocality, cf. Maudlin, 2011). What gives extra
credit to my interpretation, is that the alleged nonlocality of quantum mechanics and
of de Broglie-Bohm theory is utterly mysterious: it cannot be used to superluminally
influence, to change the quantum statistics of some system at a space-like separated
interval, as is well-known (e.g. Myrvold et al., 2024, §8.2). What kind of nonlocality
is this then? It is instructive to realise that even Bell changed his view on nonlocality (Myrvold et al., 2024, §8.3). Despite more than half a century of research,
it is still not clear whether the ‘nonlocality’ of quantum physics induces an insuperable incompatibility between the two pillars of physics. On my view, it doesn’t.
On my view, nonlocality can be explained and replaced by a notion with a clear and
bona-fide meaning, namely universal connectedness or non-separability (or universal
correlation), which, in turn, is a direct consequence of the causal principle. On this
interpretation, the alleged nonlocality of quantum mechanics and de Broglie-Bohm
theory would amount to an optical illusion of sorts. But let us remain cautious: only
a unification of both theories should provide the final verdict.
A causal interpretation of Bell’s theorem has important logical consequences,
obviously for philosophy, but also for physics. In philosophy, embracing the causal
principle led, to my taste, to one of the most powerful philosophical systems of
all time, namely Spinoza’s philosophy (cf. some comments in the next section). In
physics, it has a clear heuristic role: it incites to look for new local hidden-variable
theories, compatible with relativity theory. If the unification of quantum mechanics
and general relativity theory remains the Graal of physics, then the result that local
hidden-variable theories are possible after all, is obviously good news—since it
seems difficult to unify a local theory as general relativity with a nonlocal one.
What I have in mind here are effective hidden-variable theories that violate the free
will or no-correlation condition (and hence bypass Bell’s no-go theorem) while this
violation can be explained by the real but hidden and intractable common causes
describing the Big Bang or other early times (more in Nikolaev & Vervoort, 2023;
Papatryfonos & Vervoort, 2025). There are several candidate theories that, even if
unfinished, seem to comply with this scheme. The de Broglie–Bohm theory (Holland,
1995), the ‘double-solution’ theory of de Broglie (Jamet & Drezet, 2024) and theories à la ‘stochastic electrodynamics’ (De la Peña et al., 2015) might indicate an
causality. The law of causality simply asserts that the phenomena of nature are dependent on one
another” (cited by Bunge (2009), pp. 252–253).
8 The reasoning behind the derivation of Bell’s inequality makes it clear that it can only be violated
either by nonlocal, i.e. superluminal, hidden variables or hidden variables violating no-correlation
(perhaps there are more exotic options, but they seem unlikely). Again, nonlocality means: involving
superluminal influences.

9.1 On the Principle of Causation

241

interesting direction to take. These theories hypothesise the existence of a new property, a (hidden) pilot-wave or background field, that is supposed to explain quantum
mechanics. This extra field does depend on the detector settings (see e.g. Holland,
1995, p. 78–79), so that Bell’s no-go theorem is indeed circumvented. And, as said, I
doubt that this violation of the no-correlation assumption should be called nonlocal or
superdeterministic—at most ‘contextual’ or ‘soft-superdeterministic’ (Nikolaev &
Vervoort, 2023; Papatryfonos & Vervoort, 2025), a harmless variant. Such effective
theories can be perfectly physical (de Broglie-Bohm theory surely is) and explain
quantum mechanics (perhaps the de Broglie-Bohm theory can be elaborated to bring
this explanation to its logical end), while they are, on my hypothesis, justified by a
hidden ‘superdeterministic’ substratum that we only need as a heuristic or explanatory assumption—or, of course, for our philosophical understanding. Again, in a
similar way classical mechanical particles and classical mechanics are presupposed
in order to explain and construct statistical thermodynamics, even if the individual
mechanical properties are intractable. Other hints that de Broglie–Bohm theory
is in fact local come from the so-called hydrodynamic interpretation of quantum
mechanics (Vervoort & Gingras, 2015).
It seems to me that this same scheme of reduction also applies to other theories
aiming at deriving quantum mechanics via violation of no-correlation (Donadi &
Hossenfelder, 2022; ‘t Hooft, 2016, 2021). Especially relevant is the ‘Cellular
Automaton’ theory of Nobel laureate G. ‘t Hooft, (2016, 2021), who has invested
sustained efforts in constructing a theory that could, ultimately, unify quantum
mechanics and general relativity. The Cellular Automaton theory is always deemed
superdeterministic; but of course no attempt is made in this theory to derive the
choices of experimenters starting from the degrees of freedom of the Big Bang!
Again, it seems to me that the superdeterminism of this theory is entirely physical
and harmless, against charges made in the literature (these charges are answered
in e.g. Andreoletti & Vervoort, 2022; Hossenfelder & Palmer, 2020). One is left
wondering whether the negative connotations that many physicists and philosophers
still entertain about superdeterminism have unduly diminished the interest for this
essential work by ‘t Hooft and others.
But let us return to the principle of causation. The main upshot of the above excursion in quantum mechanics is that the causal principle can well be maintained, also
taking quantum theory into account. It is not that the principle needs to be saved with
artificial assumptions, in a nostalgic move to save an old principle. There are positive
reasons: the principle not only incites to do further research in quantum physics as
just argued9 ; I believe it has also a greater explanatory power than its competitors—
even if restricting the discussion to physics and its philosophy. If correct, this would
be the main reason why it deserves to be accepted as a plausible candidate, and indeed
the best provisional solution. I believe the causal principle can not only explain the
mysterious ‘nonlocality’ of quantum mechanics along the lines sketched above; it can

9 The competing assumption, so the ‘no-hidden-variables’ assumption, seems to be an obstacle to

the unification of quantum mechanics and general relativity.

242

9 Epilogue: Lines of Further Research

also answer foundational questions of probability theory. This would bring a particularly weighty argument into the balance. Indeed, above I have suggested at a few
occasions that probability theory is, in a sense, the most fundamental physical theory
we have. It has a broader scope than quantum mechanics and classical statistical
theories, since all these theories comply with probability theory. Moreover, general
relativity theory is a special case of a probabilistic theory with probabilities 0 or 1.
Now, according to the general view on theory-building and problem-solving exposed
in Chapter 1, any meta-principle that can coherently answer questions raised by this
encompassing theory, should, a priori, be of a unique unifying strength. And indeed,
there are fundamental questions raised by probability theory that, to the best of my
knowledge, can only be answered by the causal principle. I will just mention these
questions here, but not repeat my answers (cf. Nikolaev & Vervoort, 2023; Vervoort,
2019). The first question is: How to interpret probability and statistical correlation
in a coherent manner, valid for quantum and classical systems? The second question is: How can one explain the ubiquitous occurrence of the Gaussian probability
distribution in classical and quantum systems? As far as I know, if one rejects the
causal principle one cannot answer these fundamental questions—just as it becomes
difficult to interpret Bell’s theorem.
(The above considerations also suggest following idea that deserves, I believe,
further investigation: could it be that nonlocality, superdeterminism, contextuality,
and retrocausality are just superficially different sides of the same multifaceted coin:
different conceptual interpretations of counterintuitive mathematical theories and
theorems? This idea is entertained in Papatryfonos and Vervoort (2025), where it is
argued that causality offers the better synthetic prospects.)
Before concluding, I would add one less important point. We just had a look at the
question of whether quantum variables could be caused by, could be emerging from,
deeper-lying properties—a question we scrutinised from the point of view of some
alleged best theory, some absolute angle, as is always done (this led to the abstract
notion of hidden-variable theory and Bell’s analysis). But, interestingly, one can even
answer the question by looking from the angle of quantum physics itself (which
is often believed to be more provisional). Indeed, in Chapter 5 it was shown that
causal identification is in principle representation-relative. So, the question “Does
every event have a cause?” could in principle be understood relative to some specific
theory, say quantum theory—even if is clear that normally the best possible theory
is targeted. (If we would append the Copenhagen interpretation to quantum theory,
the doctrinal answer would be ‘no’.) Now, even within quantum mechanics itself it
is well possible to attribute causes to quantum events—causes that are in agreement
with DEF-P of Chapter 5. Quantum events are probabilistic, and we have seen in
Chapter 4 that probabilities are expressed as functions of the measurement context,
possibly representative of some natural environment. More precisely, it was argued
there that any probability can be understood as a conditional probability, conditional
on the variables describing the (measurement or natural) context, cf. Chapter 4, §3 and
§5 R3. This immediately implies that the variables describing this context qualify
as causes, according to DEF-P. Let us look at an example. The Bell-correlation
P(s1, s2|a, b) —the paradigmatic example that is studied to show that the spins or

9.2 Agency in a Causal World. Consequences of the Non-existence …

243

polarisations (s1, s2) have no causes!—can well be understood as expressing that
the detector settings (a, b) are causes of (s1, s2). Indeed, (a, b) are set before the
measured values (s1, s2); and within quantum mechanics there are no off-screening
common causes between (s1, s2) and (a, b). Hence, by DEF-P, (a, b) are causes of
(s1, s2) according to quantum mechanics! This verdict does not hurt our intuition:
the measurement specified by (a, b) involves an interaction with the particles and is
thus one of the causes of the measurement result. A similar reasoning can be held for
any quantum event, characterised by a quantum probability. In sum, if one accepts
DEF-P, even within quantum mechanics the (probabilistic variant of the) principle
of causality is valid, against what is often believed.
This concludes my short and surely incomplete defence of the principle of causation/causality. I have to admit that the arguments amassed above in favour of the
principle leave me with a sense of bewilderment. There is a strong current against the
principle, prevailing notably in philosophy of science and (philosophy of) quantum
physics,10 where causality is almost universally rejected. Yet, I can only repeat that
this principle is an immediate consequence of two assumptions that seem utterly
cogent by now: the existence of a synthetic definition of cause, and physicalism,
the assumption that the ultimate building blocks of the universe are physical—a
hypothesis that can hardly be surpassed in generality and acceptance. Unless I overlooked something essential, this gives the principle of causality an air of inevitability
as a philosophical principle. As said, for its explanatory power in philosophy, one
of my favourite references is Spinoza’s philosophy, briefly commented on in the
next section. But in physics too, the principle has explanatory and in any case
heuristic potential. One can imagine several lines of interdisciplinary research, some
mentioned above, which would make little sense without assuming the principle. The
‘no causality’ or no-hidden-variables hypothesis appears remarkably arid in comparison. It seems to dictate that physics ends with quantum mechanics, that quantum
mechanics cannot be reduced to a more fundamental theory. But until now science
has refused to die.

9.2 Agency in a Causal World. Consequences
of the Non-existence of Absolute Free Will
In this section I consider some lines of further research starting from the findings
obtained in Chapters 6 and 7 on free will. There I argued that any human decision and
choice is dependent on—i.e., caused by, necessitated by—previous external causes.
I argued that this conclusion is a direct consequence of physicalism.

10 As only one example, in some recent quantum experiments it seems that the cause-effect order can

be reversed or is indefinite (Goswami et al., 2018). But these results are disputed (Vilasini & Renner,
2024). Recall that the time-clause in my definitions allows for simultaneous mutual causation; and
it is not the most essential component; there is a more fundamental component.

244

9 Epilogue: Lines of Further Research

As is well known, the non-existence of absolute free will seems to have profound
consequences for a variety of chapters of practical philosophy, ethics and philosophy
of law, related to moral responsibility, the practice of blame and punishment, etc.
For my quick survey in this Epilogue, I will start by noting that most of the latter
disciplines and concepts concern or have an intimate relation with the concept of
happiness, so with human feelings—or so they can be construed. So does a principle
I wish to promote here, even if cursorily; a thesis I believe to be of great importance.
This well-known principle, a version of utilitarianism, states that the ultimate criterion to judge ethical acts is the happiness or wellbeing they procure. (I will not go in
any depth into the meaning and possible implementation of the maxim.) Exercising
blame or praise, imposing punishment, etc. have impact on happiness, wellbeing of
individuals and society. Therefore, when one wishes to connect a theoretical analysis of the concept of free will to the best theories for such humanitarian and social
concepts (responsibility, praise, punishment,…) and their respective disciplines, one
needs to take into account psychological, feeling-related facts. In other words, what
one derives of a theoretical model of free will (I tried to provide the philosophical
core), must be linked to, and have relevance for, psychological, feeling-related realities and their conceptualisation, which are studied in practical philosophy and human
and social sciences. This means that my bi-perspectival model must be integrated
in, made relevant for, psychological and social facts that are not fully theoretically
understood neither fully rational, but governed in large part by feelings and emotions.
I conjecture that the principle that allows, ultimately, to make this integration—this
next step of synthesis—is that of eudaimonia or happiness maximisation.11 I submit
that integrating the absolute view on free will in practical philosophy should and
can be done with as ultimate guiding rule, or aim, the maximisation of global and
individual eudaimonia or wellbeing.
At any rate, it seems to me that this constraint of practical philosophy, this feelingladenness of psycho-social reality, explains why the relative view on free will remains
most important for most practical issues; and why the absolute view can only help
in moderating, rather than revolutionising, more classic views based on the relative
perspective. Let me repeat: the felt experience of having choices is very real. As we
all know, feelings, emotions, perceptions, leave often a much stronger impression
of ‘realness’ than theoretically known things. Eating this pie feels decidedly good,
even if I know it is not beneficial for my health. Thus, it seems we should not hope
for excising or radically changing a (well-informed) view of relative free will in
practical philosophy, neither in our usual attitudes and habits of life. Perhaps one
can see the (gradual) incorporation of the absolute view as an ideal that advanced
societies should strive to. And I dare to add, as a tribute to philosophers as the Stoics

11 Recall that in natural science, general principles are a frequent, maybe the ultimate, motor for

theory-synthesis. For instance, Einstein developed his relativity theory by applying symmetry principles to the lower-level Newtonian mechanics. Thus he crafted a theory with wider applicability
starting from a more restricted one, by making the latter obey some general principles. That seems
an inspiring approach also in philosophy.

9.2 Agency in a Causal World. Consequences of the Non-existence …

245

and Spinoza: not only societies, but also individuals, if eudaimonia, wellbeing, or at
least peace of mind,12 is what they are after.
Indeed, as several philosophers have argued, the absolute view incites us, for
instance, to be moderate in praise and blame; to refrain from excessive punishment; to
ban capital punishment; to reorient retributive punishment as much as possible to reeducation (as some countries already do); to replace hatred by understanding; etc. (As
said, in this Epilogue I will express my opinion in a sketchy way. All these ideas have
been explored by many philosophers; all these ideas are obvious to very many citizens
all over the world; and all these ideas are unbelievably wrong to others. My point
is that they are linked to fundamental synthetic theses, so that they could probably
be studied using the tools of synthetic philosophy.) Thus, the absolute view teaches
tolerance, towards others and ourselves—not to find excuses for the inexcusable,
which would in the long run decrease wellbeing, but to find some solace when one
faces the fait accompli, or when all reasonable efforts have had adverse effects. Notice
that, in this way, our view professes nothing more than what ancestral wisdom surely
has taught since prehistoric times to humans in countless places. Mild forms of
blame and punishment might be justified for the well-known reasons of deterrence.
In this context, let me refer for instance to D. Pereboom’s lucid analysis of how hard
determinism can well be compatible with normal interpersonal relationships and
morality (Pereboom, 1997, Ch. 18). This is how Pereboom’s classic article ends—I
hope the close resonance with the view expressed above and in Chapters 6 and 7 is
clear:
Given that free will of some sort is required for moral responsibility, then libertarianism,
soft determinism [compatibilism], and hard determinism, as typically conceived, are jointly
exhaustive positions […]. Yet each has a consequence that is difficult to accept. If libertarianism were true, then we would expect events to occur that are incompatible with what our
physical theories predict to be overwhelmingly likely. If soft determinism were true, then
agents would deserve blame for their wrongdoing even though their actions were produced by
processes beyond their control. If hard determinism were true, agents would not be morally
responsible—agents would never deserve blame for even the most cold-blooded and calmly
executed evil actions. I have argued that hard determinism could be the easiest view to accept.
Hard determinism need not be of the hardest sort. It need not subvert the commitment to
doing what is right, and although it does undermine some of our reactive attitudes [such as
deep resentment for wrong-doing], secure analogues of these attitudes are all one requires
for good interpersonal relationships. Consequently, of the three positions, hard determinism
might well be the most attractive, and it is surely worthy of more serious consideration than
it has been accorded.

In the economic and political sphere too the implications are, potentially, of enormous
weight, if they can be enacted effectively—and it will be obvious that this a fantastic
challenge. But is this a reason not to work on it? The absolute view teaches that all
things and events are correlated with the events at the Big Bang. There are therefore,
in a sense, no events and choices really up to us, really originating in us. And at
the same time all events are, for the same reason, interconnected, correlated among
12 In times of hardship, happiness is not the ideal that can realistically be strived to, but peace of

mind might be.

246

9 Epilogue: Lines of Further Research

each other13 : an idea that was already part of ancient, notably oriental and Greek,
philosophies, for instance certain schools of Buddhism. These ideas suggest that
there is a sound perspective from which earthly success and wealth are the fruits of
the working of the universe—chance, one might say—, and only in a relative sense
of the actions of successful and wealthy people. The universal causal determination
of the absolute view is manifesting itself in everything, but in this case most visibly
in the good genes, the good upbringing, the good friends, the good brain, etc.—at
least one of these—that the successful and wealthy enjoyed having at some point
in their lives. As all things related to free will, success and wealth should also be
approached in a bi-perspectival way. On the political and economic level, it seems
to me that the conclusion can only be that advanced societies should strive to more
equity (perfect equality seems not realistic14 ), in order to compensate for misfortune.
And this should be done by the most effective means, for instance by a more just
taxation than is now in vigour (Piketty, 2014).
I do not need to remind the reader that our world urgently needs solutions: we face
enormous challenges—think of the problems due to the global climate change, which
I believe are intimately related to the inequity problem. Everything here is a matter
of balance, where eudaimonia maximisation seems to me to be the ultimate guiding
principle. I conjecture that future AI (deep learning / machine learning) can become
extremely useful in assisting us in making strategic decisions, including in politics
and economics; I submit that it could do so by applying the principle of eudaimonia
maximisation. Here is one more ambitious line of further research, perhaps already
started by some. Of course, whether those in power will accept to be guided by AI
in political and other decisions is a wholly different matter.
Along the same lines, to those who find the idea of physical reductionism
depressing, I would say the following. The partial control we have over our lives,
relative free will, is very real: it is real to our feelings, even if it is not to our theoretical
understanding. The felt sense is, in the practice of life, surely the most important. So,
by all means, let us continue living pro-actively, with the motivating feeling of being
the authors of our existences. After all: our decisions and actions do make differences
in the world—that is difficult to deny. And they make differences in wellbeing, the
key guiding principle; therefore they are, in principle, assessable, ‘measurable’.15
Thus, there are quite objective indicators that can tell us if we are on the right track
13 Correlations can, in practice, most of the time not be detected, but under certain conditions they

can; and they can even be detected in a numerically precise way (see examples in Nikolaev &
Vervoort, 2023).
14 Here is one example of how more equity can be promoted in a very practical way. The author
has worked for a well-known German company, Carl Zeiss, where, until about twenty years ago,
the highest salary (of the best paid employee, the director) could be at most ten times higher than
the lowest salary. Carl Zeiss, a paragon of German technological innovation, was constantly elected
as one of the best companies to work for in Germany. Alas, contemporary economic discourse has
convinced people that the admirable ‘factor of ten’ rule is not realistic anymore in modern times.
15 For practical philosophers who wish to test their ideas empirically, it is pleasant to know that
happiness, peace of mind, etc. can be assessed, ‘measured’ by various experimental methods of
psychology, neurobiology, molecular biology, etc. So, for instance, the hypothesis of Spinoza and
the Buddhists that altruistic actions are, on average, increasing even the authors’ wellbeing—an

9.2 Agency in a Causal World. Consequences of the Non-existence …

247

with these actions: we can obtain feedback in different guises. As said, I believe the
ultimate touchstone is our happiness, or its correlates, say peace of mind, more realistic in times of hardship. My view is therefore all but fatalistic; fatalism likely does
not bring us much good. Much to the contrary, we saw that our free will can grow,
can be made more effective, notably by learning, by rendering the perspectives with
which we look at the world and ourselves sounder. Contrary to what some philosophers have claimed, in a solid bi-perspectival view hope is warranted: the hope that
I will grow as a human, for instance by educating myself, by learning. And the hope
that the universe, or fate, will be reasonably favourable to me.
Of course, there is a kind of subtle balancing advocated here between two perspectives. But that is also done in science—recall Bohr’s complementarity –; and every
parent and every teacher knows that some constructive forms of illusion are helpful,
nay needed. Just as in science, in a more elaborated theory, in a next stage of synthesis,
this balancing might be given an entirely solid basis.
As a tribute to Spinoza, it seems to me that I can hardly do better than to cite
O’Connor and Franklin from their condensed summary of Spinoza’s view on free
will—the philosopher who has given, in his Ethics (1677 [1992]), perhaps the most
systematic philosophy based on determinism—or what I would call physicalism in
modern terms. Here is the apt summary (O’Connor & Franklin, 2022, § 1.2):
[Spinoza] endorses a strong form of necessitarianism in which everything is categorically
necessary as opposed to the conditional necessity embraced by most compatibilists, and
he contends that there is no room in such a world for divine or creaturely free will. Thus,
Spinoza is a free will skeptic. Interestingly, Spinoza is also keen to deny that the nonexistence
of free will has the dire implications often assumed. As noted above, many in the modern
period saw belief in free will and an afterlife in which God rewards the just and punishes
the wicked as necessary to motivate us to act morally. According to Spinoza, far from this
being necessary to motivate us to be moral, it actually distorts our pursuit of morality. True
moral living, Spinoza thinks, sees virtue as its own reward (Part V, Prop. 42). Moreover,
while free will is a chimera, humans are still capable of freedom or self-determination. Such
self-determination, which admits of degrees on Spinoza’s view, arises when our emotions are
determined by true ideas about the nature of reality. The emotional lives of the free persons
are ones in which ‘we desire nothing but that which must be, nor, in an absolute sense, can
we find contentment in anything but truth. And so, in so far as we rightly understand these
matters, the endeavor of the better part of us is in harmony with the order of the whole of
Nature’ (Part IV, Appendix).

I wholeheartedly agree. Spinoza does not dwell on ‘possible worlds’ and the idea
that ‘things could have been otherwise’, or that ‘(the laws of) nature could have
been otherwise’. We live in this world, ruled by causal laws that embed or imply
necessity, as I argued in the previous section; and it is not conducive to insight—nor
to eudaimonia (!), according to Spinoza—to assume contingency in events or laws.
I doubt that the popular assumption that ‘laws are contingent’ solves problems in
a maximally coherent way. (Sure, laws can be said to be logically contingent; they
could be different from a purely logical point of view; but such a hypothesis has
no bearing at all on the universe as it is.) To the contrary, as the reader will have
idea counterintuitive to some –, should be empirically testable. Of course, this demands tightly
controlled, complex experiments, which seem not frequently done.

248

9 Epilogue: Lines of Further Research

understood, it seems to me that Spinoza’s necessitarianism offers coherent answers
to a multitude of problems in a most parsimonious manner. It is simple and radical,
and does not introduce redundant categories and hypotheses.
To end on a bi-perspectival note: things happen as they must; but we can, and
often should, be part of the action.
